# Changelog 7.0

Every line is indexed in [CHANGELOG.md](../CHANGELOG.md).

## [Unreleased]

7.0 is a major release: the queue's, and the foundation every 7.x builds on. Every
edge runs one execution contract with its layers, HTTP speaks the `http` crate's
types on a server of nestrs's own, the lifecycle is ordered and open, a resource
opens once per named instance, and a driver, a port or a package has a seam of its
own. A 6.1 project upgrades in one pass: the ordered checklist is
[Upgrading from 6.x to 7.0](https://nestrs.dev/upgrading/), and a queue on Redis
also moves its keys, as [Upgrading queues from 6.x](https://nestrs.dev/queue/upgrading/)
lays out. Below is everything a 6.1 caller, driver author or deployment can see,
and why, grouped by what it makes true; what breaks says so where it is described,
and the upgrading guide holds its migration.

- **Security first**

### rustls 0.23.45 — a TLS 1.3 handshake message across a key change is refused

RUSTSEC-2026-0285 (GHSA-2mjx-qc3c-rqvc, published 2026-09-14): rustls 0.23.44
accepted TLS 1.3 handshake messages sent at the wrong encryption level when they
followed a key-changing message in the same record, which RFC 8446 §5.1 requires be
refused with `unexpected_message`. Every lockfile the repository owns — the
framework's, the demo's and the benchmark's — carried 0.23.44 and now resolves
0.23.45.

A library's lockfile is not published, so **an application built on nest-rs resolves
rustls on its own: `cargo update -p rustls` is the whole fix**, and the
`rustls = "0.23"` requirement already admits it. `nestrs` does not depend on rustls,
so `cargo install --locked nest-rs-cli` was never exposed. The advisory sat in the
tree for eleven days with nothing noticing; the tree is now checked against the
advisory database on every change to it and before every release, and GitHub's
Dependabot watches the default branch's lockfiles for vulnerabilities in between.

### A security header set under its 6.x key fails the boot naming its 7.0 key

7.0 reads each security header under `<PREFIX>_HTTP__SECURITY_HEADERS_<FIELD>`. A
deployment keeping 6.x's flat key (`<PREFIX>_HTTP__CONTENT_SECURITY_POLICY`, `HSTS`,
`PERMISSIONS_POLICY`, …) heard one `warn` and lost the header it set: a
Content-Security-Policy, Permissions-Policy or Cross-Origin-Embedder-Policy not sent
at all. **Breaking: each of the nine flat keys fails the boot** as a retired key,
`ConfigError::Retired` — `<PREFIX>_HTTP__HSTS is read by nothing since 7.0 — set
<PREFIX>_HTTP__SECURITY_HEADERS_HSTS: it was a security header's 6.x key, and the
header it set would not be sent as set` — naming the variable set and the key it
became, never the value. `CORS_EXPOSED`, `CORS_MAX_AGE` and the bare
`SECURITY_HEADERS` keep the `warn`, in the same words: losing them leaves a policy
more closed than the one set.

What fails if you get it wrong: a deployment setting `<PREFIX>_HTTP__HSTS` (or any
header's flat key) does not boot until the variable moves to its
`SECURITY_HEADERS_` name.

### A refusal never quotes what the client sent

`ParseArray<T>` refused a list with `contains an invalid item: `<item>``, and a
`ValidationPipe` failing `must_match` carried the other field's value in its
details — the password a confirmation did not match. Both reached the HTTP 400
body, the WS error frame and its `warn` line, a GraphQL or MCP error, and the
queue's dead-letter record and line. `ParseArray` now names the item's position
and the expected type, as `Parse<T>` already did (`item 2 must be a valid u64`).

**Breaking: a validation failure is said by its field, its rule and its message,
never the rule's parameters**, in a reply's `errors`, a boot error and a log
line alike: `value` is the rejected input, `must_match`'s `other` the other
field's, and a bound such as `max = self.limit` can read another field. A client
that read `errors.<field>[].params.min` reads `code`; a rule that should tell
the client its bound says so in its `message`.

An error carrying a `validator` failure — `ServiceError::Validation`, or an
application's own error wrapping one — was said on every line and frame that
renders it in `validator`'s wording, which lists the rejected input among the
rule's parameters. The rule's code is kept and its parameters are dropped
(`password: Validation error: length`), whether the failure is the error's
source or spelled in its sentence (`{0}`, `transparent`).

### A GraphQL operation runs its guards whatever it returns

`#[operations]` emitted the guard chain only for an operation whose return type
read as a `Result`, so under a deny-all `#[use_guards]` on the resolver, or an
app-wide guard, an operation returning `i32`, `Vec<T>` or a bare `impl Stream`
subscription served its data in 6.1. Every wrapper the macro emits now answers a
`Result` — a bare `T` becomes `async_graphql::Result<T>` — so a denial is a field
error on every query, mutation, subscription, `#[entity]` resolver and field
resolver. **Breaking for anything relying on the gap:** such an operation now
needs the posture its guards ask for. A bare return beside `#[authorize]`, beside
a `Piped<P>` argument, and on an `#[entity]` is accepted now; those refusals
existed only because of the gap.

A `#[field_resolver]` runs its resolver's `#[use_guards]` and its own on every
call, and leaves the app-wide pool to the request's root field
(`GraphqlSite::Field`). It used to run nothing unless its method declared a guard,
and then the pool once per parent row.

### `rediss://` is verified TLS

- **A queue runs against a Valkey speaking TLS itself, every connection
  verified** — the push, the settle and the worker's blocking read on a
  connection of its own — and the same Valkey under an authority the client was
  not given fails the boot at once.
- **A `rediss://` URL encrypts every connection the client opens**, the ones it
  reopens behind its callers included, and verifies the certificate for the
  URL's host through rustls, against the system's authorities — read once at
  boot and handed to the client, which would otherwise read the store again on
  every connection, blocking the runtime while it does; `SSL_CERT_FILE` and
  `SSL_CERT_DIR` point the read elsewhere — or the authorities in
  `<PREFIX>_REDIS__TLS_CA_CERT` (inline PEM, or `_FILE`), which replace them.
  `TLS_CERT` / `TLS_KEY` present a client certificate — both or neither.
- **Verification cannot be switched off.** `#insecure` fails the boot
  (`RedisError::UnverifiedTls`), and so does every TLS setting that cannot work,
  naming what to change: material beside a `redis://` URL (`PlaintextUrl`), and
  material no handshake could use, a host no certificate can name or a handshake
  refused the same way on every attempt (`TlsRefused`).
- **A certificate refused after the boot is reported once, at `warn`**, until a
  command answers again — a renewal gone wrong is named rather than read as an
  outage. It is learnt from a handshake of the connection's own as soon as a
  command fails on the socket — the drop itself — rather than once the client's
  reconnect retries are spent, seconds later, every caller cut at its budget
  meanwhile; one such handshake per connect budget at most, so a Redis that is
  down is not sent one per failing command.
- The process-wide rustls crypto provider is installed once when the app chose
  none, as the HTTP transport's listener installs it, since a tree compiling both providers
  otherwise panics on its first handshake. `redis` gains the features that carry
  it — `tokio-rustls-comp` and `tls-rustls-webpki-roots`, whose roots are never
  read but whose absence would make the client read the system's store per
  connection — and `tls-rustls-insecure` stays off, so nothing could honour
  `#insecure`.

### Every connection the framework opens verifies TLS against the system's authorities

- **Breaking: Postgres's TLS is verified or off.** A URL naming no `sslmode`
  connects with `verify-full` — the server's certificate checked, and its name
  against the host — where sqlx's default, `prefer`, encrypted without checking
  either. `sslmode=disable` is plaintext, written where it is meant; `allow`,
  `prefer`, `require` and `verify-ca` fail the boot naming
  `<PREFIX>_SEAORM__URL`, never quoting it. The certificate chains to the
  system's authorities unless `sslrootcert` names a file; libpq's
  `sslrootcert=system` says the same. A project `nestrs new` generates writes
  `sslmode=disable` beside its local, plaintext Postgres.
- **Breaking: `SeaOrmConfig::connect_options()` returns a `Result`** and is the one
  constructor of a Postgres connection: the app's pool, `connect_from_env`, a tool or a
  test of your own, and `EphemeralDatabase` all open through it, so each refuses what the
  boot refuses — an empty URL, an `sslmode` that encrypts without verifying (`allow`,
  `prefer`, `require`, `verify-ca`), and a `connect_timeout` outside its range, which
  a config built in code never had checked — each naming its variable:
  `<PREFIX>_SEAORM__URL`, never quoted, or `<PREFIX>_SEAORM__CONNECT_TIMEOUT_SECS` for the
  budget. Until now the refusal lived in the pool's private path alone: a caller of
  the public `connect_options` kept the URL's unverified mode, and sqlx then accepted any
  certificate and any host name.
- **`EphemeralDatabase` verifies TLS.** It built connect options of its own that set no
  `sslmode`, so sqlx's `prefer` applied to every e2e suite built on it: encrypted without
  checking the certificate, and plaintext — credentials included — with a server that
  declined TLS. It now opens through `connect_options`, verifying as the app does, and a
  test double presenting a certificate nothing trusts, or declining TLS, gets no startup
  message. `nest-rs-testing`'s `orm` feature now links `nest-rs-seaorm`, which an app
  enabling `seaorm` already had.
- **Breaking: Valkey's connection trusts the system's authorities** where it
  trusted the Mozilla roots compiled into the client, so an authority installed
  in the system's store — an enterprise's private one — is trusted like any
  other; an image shipping no store trusts nothing until it installs one or
  names an authority in `<PREFIX>_REDIS__TLS_CA_CERT`.
- **Storage names an authority as Valkey does**: `StorageConfig::tls`
  (`StorageTls`), read from `<PREFIX>_STORAGE__TLS_CA_CERT` or its `_FILE`,
  replaces the system's authorities for the endpoint — refused holding no
  certificate, or beside an `http://` endpoint. `StorageConfig::default()`'s
  endpoint is `https://rustfs:9000`.
- **Added: `nest_rs_config::system_authorities`**, the system's authorities as
  PEM, read once per process, and **`nest_rs_testing::TestAuthority`** (feature
  `tls`), which issues in process the certificates a TLS test double presents
  or a client trusts — on rcgen, the rustls project's generator.

### HTTP serves on nestrs's own accept loop: a connection cap, an HTTP/2 stream cap

The HTTP transport handed every socket to poem's server, which accepted
without a limit, let one HTTP/2 connection run any number of streams at once,
dropped an accept error and tried again at once — spinning on `EMFILE` when the
process ran out of descriptors — and re-raised a panic from a connection's task.
poem's `Server` has no hook for any of it, so the transport now accepts its
connections itself, on hyper. No public type or signature changes.

- **At most `<PREFIX>_HTTP__MAX_CONCURRENT_CONNECTIONS` connections are open at
  once, 10 000 by default.** Past it a new connection waits in the kernel's
  listen backlog until one closes — it sees latency, then the kernel's refusal
  once the backlog is full, never a reset of a connection it holds — and one
  `warn` on `nest_rs::http`, `connection cap reached; new connections wait in
  the listen backlog`, names the variable; an `info` follows once a place is
  free. A socket upgraded to a WebSocket counts until it closes. Whole numbers
  from 1 to 1 048 576 (Linux's default `fs.nr_open`), refused outside naming the
  variable, from the environment and from `HttpConfig::max_concurrent_connections`
  alike.
- **An HTTP/2 connection runs at most 200 streams at once**, advertised in
  `SETTINGS_MAX_CONCURRENT_STREAMS` (RFC 9113 §5.1.2) where poem advertised no
  limit; a client resetting streams faster than they are answered is sent
  `GOAWAY` past 20 (CVE-2023-44487), as before.
- **An HTTP/1 request head larger than 64 KiB is answered `431 Request Header
  Fields Too Large`** (RFC 6585 §5); hyper's default buffer let each slow head
  hold about 400 KiB.
- **An accept that fails for want of descriptors or buffers backs off**, 5 ms
  doubling to one second until an accept succeeds, with one `warn`, `accept
  failed; backing off`, per episode; a connection its peer gave up on before it
  was accepted is skipped at `debug`.
- **A panic in a connection, or in one of an HTTP/2 connection's streams, is
  contained, and the server keeps accepting:** a connection's panic drops that
  connection, filed as one `error` on `nest_rs::http`, `a connection task
  panicked`; a stream's resets that stream alone and its connection keeps
  serving the others, filed as `an HTTP/2 stream task panicked`. Each line
  carries the panic's message.
- **TLS runs on tokio-rustls**, offering HTTP/2 then HTTP/1.1 as before; a
  renewed certificate is swapped into the running listener as before, its files
  now read off the async threads. The listener still installs aws-lc-rs as the
  process's crypto provider when the app has chosen none, so a client built
  later from the default finds the same one.
- Every accepted socket is sent with `TCP_NODELAY`, Go's, nginx's and Kestrel's
  default.
- **The boot line `transport listening` is filed on `nest_rs::http`** — at
  `debug`, once the socket is bound, with the address it bound; `app ready`
  says the address at `info` — and poem's own lines on `poem::server` — `listening`,
  `server started`, `initiate graceful shutdown`, `wait for all connections to
  close.`, `server stopped` — are no longer filed: a boot reads `transport
  listening`, a stop `shutdown signal received` on `nest_rs::app`, then the
  drain's own line when the window leaves a connection open.

Not yet bounded: until the head and idle deadlines land (HTTP-02), a
connection holds its place under the cap for as long as it stays open — one
that never finishes its first head, a TLS handshake that never completes, a
kept-alive connection left idle after its last answer, and a peer that vanished
without closing its socket alike. Under poem each cost only a descriptor; here
10 000 of them, harmless or hostile, stop the replica accepting until some
close.

### Every phase of an HTTP connection has a deadline

Since the transport accepts its own connections, each holds a place under
`<PREFIX>_HTTP__MAX_CONCURRENT_CONNECTIONS` until it ends — and none had a
deadline. A client that opened a connection and sent nothing, a head trickled a
byte at a time, a TLS handshake never finished, a kept-alive connection left
idle after its last answer, and a peer that stopped reading while the server
wrote each held its place, its task and its buffers for as long as the client
liked: 10 000 of them, harmless or hostile, stopped the replica accepting.
hyper's own head timer could not close the first case — it arms one deadline
over a head and an idle connection alike, and never over the version sniff —
so the transport now holds each phase itself.

- **The first request head is due `<PREFIX>_HTTP__HEADER_READ_TIMEOUT_SECS`
  after accept, 30 s by default**, with a TLS handshake and HTTP/2's preface
  inside it; on HTTP/1 a later head is due as long after its first byte. Past
  it the connection is closed. Whole seconds from 1 to 300.
- **A kept-alive connection with nothing in flight waits
  `<PREFIX>_HTTP__IDLE_TIMEOUT_SECS` for its next request, 75 s by default**,
  counted from the end of its last answer; then an HTTP/1 connection is closed
  and an HTTP/2 one is sent `GOAWAY`. A request in flight — its handler, then
  its response body — has no connection deadline: its own budget bounds it.
  Whole seconds from 1 to 3600. The boot line `transport listening` carries
  `idle_timeout_secs`.
- **A write the peer takes nothing of fails after
  `<PREFIX>_HTTP__SEND_TIMEOUT_SECS`, 60 s by default**, counted from the
  moment it starts waiting and cleared by any write that goes through, and the
  connection is dropped — a download, an event stream, a WebSocket or a
  graphql-ws socket alike, so a slow reader that keeps taking bytes is never
  cut. On HTTP/2 a stream the peer grants no window waits with nothing pending
  on the socket: that stream is reset (`RST_STREAM` `CANCEL`) at the same
  deadline, and its connection serves its other streams on. A body answered in
  one chunk is handed to HTTP/2 64 KiB at a time, so it stays in flight, under
  this deadline and out of the idle one, until its last piece; the last
  400 KiB or so are HTTP/2's to send once the body has ended, under the idle
  deadline. An `#[sse]` stream's ceiling bounded only its emission, and its
  docs said to bound idle sockets at a proxy; they no longer do.
- **Every cut at the send deadline files one `warn`, `the peer took nothing
  within the send deadline; it is cut off`**, on its edge's target:
  `nest_rs::http` for a connection (with `send_timeout_ms` and the `error`) or
  an HTTP/2 stream (with `send_timeout_ms`), `nest_rs::ws` for a gateway's
  socket (with `conn_id`), whose disconnect hook runs, and `nest_rs::graphql`
  for a graphql-ws socket, whose `graphql.subscription` line files `cancelled`
  rather than `ok`, since the server ended it.
- Each of the three is refused at `0` and past its ceiling, naming the
  variable, from the environment and from the pinned `HttpConfig` field alike
  (`header_read_timeout`, `idle_timeout`, `send_timeout`); `0` turns none of
  them off.
- **The connection cap's `warn` is filed once a connection waits on the cap,
  and its `info`, `connection cap no longer reached; accepting again`, once
  none does any more.** At a full cap the next connection is accepted and held
  unserved, the rest waiting in the listen backlog; the `warn` was filed
  whenever the cap was full, a connection waiting or not, and the `info` never
  at a cap of one. A busy spell now files one `warn` and one `info`, however
  many connections it held back, and connections that never wait file neither.

The per-request cost: an `Arc` clone and a counter increment when hyper hands
the request over, and a clock read, two stores and a decrement at its body's
end; on a kept-alive HTTP/1 connection, a clock read at the next head's first
byte and two relaxed loads per read; on HTTP/2, an allocation per stream, the
stream's task-local swapped in at each poll, and two relaxed stores per frame —
a clock read and a timer only for a stream that waits on its window. No timer is
set per request: each connection's watch wakes at most every
`min(HEADER_READ_TIMEOUT_SECS, IDLE_TIMEOUT_SECS)`, 30 s by default.

### A large response body no longer stays in memory on the connection that sent it

poem's server copied each frame of an HTTP/1 response body whole into the
connection's write buffer, and the buffer kept that size until the connection
closed. A handler answering with one large in-memory body — `Body::from` a
`Vec<u8>`, a file read whole — left that much memory on every kept-alive
connection it answered, and any client could drive it. The transport now hands
hyper at most 64 KiB of a body at a time, split without a copy, on HTTP/1 as on
HTTP/2, so a connection's write buffer stays within about 128 KiB whatever the
body. A fixed-length body's bytes on the wire are unchanged; a streamed body
goes out in chunks of at most 64 KiB.

### A config's `Default` opens nothing in a deployment

A `#[config]` type's `Default` is the production-safe value now, and a development
convenience in it is a [`Surface`](#a-config-has-one-base-and-a-development-opening-resolves-where-it-is-used)
left to the profile. Three did not hold to it, and the docs taught the form that kept two
of them open: a config pinned over `..Default::default()` carried the opening into
production.

- **Breaking: `StorageConfig::default()` holds no credential, and a deployment refuses
  plain HTTP.** `allow_http` is `Surface::ByProfile`, open under a development or test
  profile alone, and the dev container's pair (`nestrs`/`nestrs`) left the framework: no
  profile names a store or its pair
  ([Development surfaces, emits and seeds follow the declared profile alone](#development-surfaces-emits-and-seeds-follow-the-declared-profile-alone)). A
  config pinned over `..Default::default()` now refuses, in every profile, a missing pair
  naming `<PREFIX>_STORAGE__ACCESS_KEY`, and in a deployed or undeclared process an
  `http://` endpoint naming `<PREFIX>_STORAGE__ALLOW_HTTP`; it used to authenticate with
  the public pair in production.
- **Breaking: `OpenApiConfig::default()` serves no documentation in a deployment**
  (`enabled: Surface::ByProfile`). It serves it in development and test only, as the bare
  import always did; a config pinned over `..OpenApiConfig::default()` served `/api` and
  `/api-json` publicly in production with only a `warn`, against the documentation's own
  exposure decision. A deployment now opens them only by saying so: `enabled:
  Surface::Open` in the pin, or `<PREFIX>_OPENAPI__ENABLED=true`.
- **A pin closes nothing it does not name, and opens nothing in a deployment.** A pinned
  config reads the process environment and never `.env`, so a pin over `Default` takes the
  storage pair from the deployment even in development.

### Server-Timing is off outside development unless a deployment turns it on

`ServerTimingModule` stamped every response with the time each backend took — the
sub-steps a handler records name them (`covers;desc="object-store lookup"`) — in every
profile, and on the refusals too: a `401`, `403` or `429` told the caller it turned away
how long the authentication path or the rate check worked. The W3C Server Timing draft
(§4, *Privacy and Security*) warns that the header can expose application and
infrastructure information, and lets a server give its metrics to authenticated callers
"and nothing at all to all others". The header is now a development surface, as the
OpenAPI documentation is.

- **Breaking: the bare import stamps nothing outside the development and test
  profiles.** Under `<PREFIX>_ENV=staging` or `production`, a deployment turns it on with
  `<PREFIX>_SERVER_TIMING__ENABLED=true`, and the boot says so once at `warn` on
  `nest_rs::server_timing`, naming the `environment`. The import line is unchanged.
- **`ServerTimingModule::for_root(config)`** is new, taking a `ServerTimingConfig` (one
  field, `enabled`) or `None`, as every configured module does; its alias is
  `ServerTimingSetup`. `ServerTimingConfig::default()` leaves the header to the profile,
  `enabled: Surface::ByProfile`: on in development and test, off elsewhere. A pinned
  `Surface::Open` opens it in every profile, with the same `warn` outside development, and
  the variable still outranks the pin.
- **A `401`, `403`, `407` or `429` never carries `Server-Timing`**, whatever the profile
  and the switch: a guard's denial, an interceptor's, a handler's own refusal, the rate
  limiter's. The rule reads the status line: on `/graphql` and `/mcp`, a refusal answered
  inside a `200` (a GraphQL `UNAUTHENTICATED`, `FORBIDDEN` or `RATE_LIMITED` error, an MCP
  JSON-RPC error) keeps its timing wherever the header is on — the docs page lists it
  under *Limits*.
- **The boot decides once, at register.** Switched off, the module attaches no wrap, so
  the transport keeps its fused path and a request pays nothing for it; a handler
  reading `Timings` with `.unwrap_or_default()`, the documented form, runs unchanged and
  records into an accumulator nobody reads.

### An error nobody meant for the client is answered opaquely, and its chain logged, on every edge

A handler's failure reached its client in its own words on two edges, and was logged
nowhere on a third. A GraphQL resolver's `Err` became the client's `message` through
async-graphql's blanket `From<T: Display>`, so `Err(anyhow!("insert failed:
(email)=(a@b.c)"))` shipped the database's sentence; a WebSocket handler's `Err`
became its frame's `error`, its chain filed at `warn`; an HTTP handler's `Err`
answered as a `500` whose text the normalizer dropped and whose chain no line held.
Every edge now renders a handler's error by one rule:

- **its own deliberate error, as built** — an `async_graphql::Error` you built, a
  `WsError`, an HTTP `ResponseError` (`ProblemDetails`, `ServiceError`, your own);
- **an error implementing `ToProblem`, as the problem it says** — new in
  `nest_rs::core`: `Problem` (a status in `400..=599`, a `Code`, a client-safe
  detail, a wait), `Code` (SCREAMING_SNAKE, checked where it is declared in a
  `const`: `Code::new("conflict")` and `Problem::new(200, …)` do not compile),
  `code` (the framework's nine: `UNAUTHENTICATED`, `FORBIDDEN`,
  `INSUFFICIENT_SCOPE`, `RATE_LIMITED`, `UNAVAILABLE`, `INVALID_ARGUMENT`,
  `NOT_FOUND`, `CONFLICT`, `INTERNAL`) and `ToProblem` itself. A `Problem` answers
  as an RFC 9457 document with a `code` member and `Retry-After` on HTTP
  (`ProblemDetails::from_problem`), `message` and `extensions.code` on GraphQL
  (`nest_rs::graphql::problem_error`), `error` and `errors.code` in a WebSocket
  frame (`WsError::from_problem`), `message` and `data.code` in an MCP JSON-RPC
  error (`nest_rs::mcp::problem_error`). Its detail never reaches a client on a
  `5xx`: a `503` says `"service unavailable"`, any other `5xx` `"internal error"`;
  a `4xx` without one says its code in words (`not found`).
- **the framework's vocabulary found in the chain** — a `Problem`, a pipe
  rejection, a decode failure said without its value — in its own form;
- **anything else, opaquely**: `"internal error"` (`OPAQUE_CLIENT_MESSAGE`), under
  the `INTERNAL` code on GraphQL, and **one `error` line** on the edge's target
  (`nest_rs::graphql`, `nest_rs::ws`, `nest_rs::http`) carrying the whole chain.

A `5xx` problem answers the same sentence an opaque failure does, so the chain
behind it is filed the same way: one `error` line on the edge's target whenever the
error is more than the bare `Problem` (a domain error whose `to_problem` says `503`,
a `Problem` an anyhow chain carries under its context). A bare `Problem` withholds
nothing and files nothing, as a bare status does on HTTP; its detail, never sent on
a `5xx`, is never logged either — what failed belongs in the error's chain.

GraphQL and WebSocket read a handler's error by its type, where the decorator names
it. HTTP answers an error's own `ResponseError` as built (every `ToProblem` error is
one, `Problem` included, so `?` on it answers through `nest_rs::http::HttpError`), and
any other error a route returns opaquely. An MCP operation keeps
returning `McpError`: `problem_error(&e)` converts a `ToProblem` error, `.opaque()?`
anything else.

`ServiceError` implements `ToProblem` (`NotFound` → `NOT_FOUND`, `Conflict` →
`CONFLICT`, `Forbidden` → `FORBIDDEN`, `Invalid` and `Validation` →
`INVALID_ARGUMENT`, each with its sentence; `Db`, `Masking`, `Internal` → opaque),
so a resolver or a gateway returning a service's result answers as an HTTP route
does. The GraphQL by-id binding's opaque failure now carries `INTERNAL`, the one
code every opaque failure carries, where it said `INTERNAL_SERVER_ERROR`.

**Breaking:** a client that read an error's text on GraphQL or WebSockets now reads
`"internal error"` unless the error is deliberate or implements `ToProblem`; a
WebSocket handler's opaque failure, and the chain behind a `5xx` problem, are filed
at `error`, no longer at `warn`.

### A guard's refusal reads the same on every edge, and a 5xx never carries server text

A per-message WebSocket guard's denial reached the client as its own text, so
`Denial::internal("redis at 10.0.3.7 refused")` named the server's address, a rate
limit lost `retryAfterSeconds` and a scope denial `requiredScopes`; GraphQL, MCP and
WebSocket sent an unavailable dependency's free text, which HTTP has always kept to
itself. One policy now holds on the four edges: a `4xx` denial carries its reason;
an unavailable dependency answers `"service unavailable"`
(`nest_rs::core::UNAVAILABLE_CLIENT_MESSAGE`, new) with its class and its wait; an
internal denial answers `"internal error"` (GraphQL said `"internal server error"`).
On the edges with no status line a denial's class is the `code` a `Problem` answers
under — `extensions.code` on GraphQL, `data.code` on MCP, `data.errors.code` on
WebSocket (`"FORBIDDEN"`, `"RATE_LIMITED"`, `"UNAVAILABLE"`, …) — beside the
lowercase `reason` MCP and WebSocket already carried, so a client branches on one
member whichever refused it. A WebSocket denial frame carries `errors.code` and
`errors.reason`, and `retryAfterSeconds` or `requiredScopes` when the denial names
them, and the denial is filed once at `warn` on `nest_rs::layers` (`guard`,
`conn_id`, `event`, `status`), as on the other edges, where the gateway filed the
guard's text on `nest_rs::ws`. HTTP's denial document is unchanged: its status and
its RFC 6750 challenge say the class.

**Breaking:** a client that parsed a `503`'s text over GraphQL, MCP or WebSocket
loses it — it was never a contract. `nest_rs::ws::WsMessageCheck::check` and
`EventLayerTable::check` return `Result<(), WsError>` where they returned
`Result<(), String>`: a hand-written `WsMessageCheck` refuses with
`Err(WsError::new(..))`, or `denial_to_ws_error(denial)` for a `Denial`.

### A panic is one structured, redacted event

Every panic first ran Rust's default hook, which writes `thread '…' panicked at …:
<payload>` to stderr — the payload unredacted, and `.unwrap()` on a failed decode puts
the value it could not read into it. Catching the unwind afterwards did not take the
line back: the redaction the framework applied to its own `error` came after the leak.
`#[nest_rs::main]` now installs a panic hook before `main`'s first line.

- **A panic a unit of work contains is said once, by that unit.** An edge, a listener,
  a job, a tick, a lifecycle hook or after-commit work catches its own unwind through
  `nest_rs::core::panic::contain`, and the hook stays quiet for a panic raised while it
  runs: the unit's `outcome="panic"` line and its `error` are the record. That `error`
  now also carries `panic_location`, the `file:line:col` the panic happened at. So do
  the tasks the framework joins and reports: a transport, a scheduled job's loop, a
  WebSocket connection's writer.
- **A panic no unit contains is one `error` on `nest_rs::app`**, `panicked where no unit
  of work contains it`, with `panic` (the payload through `panic_message`, serde's
  quoted values cut), `panic_location`, `thread`, and `backtrace` when `RUST_BACKTRACE`
  asks for one: a task the app spawned, a value a cancelled unit drops, `main` itself,
  an HTTP handler. JSON when `<PREFIX>_LOG_FORMAT` says so, like every other line.
- **A joined task's panic no longer travels as tokio's sentence**, `task N panicked with
  message "<payload>"`, which carried the payload unredacted. `App::run`'s error for a
  transport that panicked is ``transport `<name>` panicked``, so `main`'s `Error:` line on
  stderr names the transport and not the payload; `nest_rs::core::error_message` says a
  `JoinError` in a chain as `task N panicked`. The WebSocket writer's line is now
  `writer task panicked` at `error` (it was `writer task failed` at `warn`), with the
  connection's `conn_id`.
- **Before any subscriber exists** — while the `.env` cascade loads, or in a binary that
  installs none — the hook writes one line to stderr instead,
  `nestrs: panicked at <file:line:col>: <message>`, with the same redacted message.
- **`nest_rs::core::panic::contain(fut)`** is new: it runs `fut`, catches its unwind,
  and returns `Result<F::Output, Box<dyn Any + Send>>`, as
  `AssertUnwindSafe(fut).catch_unwind()` did — a crate that contains a unit of its own
  goes through it, so the hook leaves that panic to the unit's line.
  `nest_rs::core::panic::LOCATION_FIELD` names the new field, beside `panic::FIELD`.
- The hook replaces the previous one without chaining it; a test that installs its own
  hook still replaces this one. Under `panic = "abort"` it still runs first, so it is
  the only record.

### The edge reads no request body: a refused request is answered unread, and a body that stalls answers `408`

The transport edge buffered a request body that declared no length — a chunked
upload — whole, up to the cap, before routing, before any guard and before the
request timeout existed. One anonymous chunked request could hold a task and up
to 2 MiB for as long as it liked; a guard's refusal waited for the body it was
about to refuse, and a client that sent `Expect: 100-continue` was told to go
ahead first. And a body that stopped arriving was bounded by nothing once the
request timeout was off.

- **Nothing reads a body before its reader.** The edge wraps every body that is
  not empty once, and whoever needs it — an extractor (`Json`, `Form`,
  `RawBody`, `Multipart`), a guard, the global body pipes, GraphQL's
  POST decoder, MCP — reads it after routing and the guards. A refused request
  is answered before a byte of its body is taken, and hyper never writes `100
  Continue` for it, since it writes that on the first read.
- **The cap is counted as the body is read**, wherever its length cannot be
  trusted (none declared, or compression in front of the edge); a declared
  `Content-Length` past it is still refused unread. Past it the read fails and
  the request is answered `413`, whatever the reader made of the failure — a
  problem document saying `the body is larger than <cap> bytes`. A read the
  edge stopped, past the cap or stalled, is the client's doing: the global body
  pipes no longer file it as their own `global pipe: failed to read body` `warn`.
- **A body that sends nothing for `<PREFIX>_HTTP__BODY_READ_TIMEOUT_SECS`, 10 s
  by default, once something reads it, is answered `408 Request Timeout`**
  (RFC 9110 §15.5.9): on HTTP/1 with `Connection: close`, since the unread rest
  leaves the connection unusable; on HTTP/2 that stream alone ends and its
  connection serves on. The bound counts the gap between two frames, so an
  upload that keeps sending is never cut by it. Whole seconds from 1 to 3600,
  refused outside — `0` included — from the environment and from the pinned
  `HttpConfig::body_read_timeout` alike. The default is half the request
  timeout's, below it on purpose: that budget counts from the request's
  arrival, so a gap set at or past it never fires first, and a stalled body is
  answered the budget's `503` instead.
- **The request timeout counts from the request's arrival**, its body's reading
  included, so a body trickled a byte at a time is cut at
  `<PREFIX>_HTTP__REQUEST_TIMEOUT_SECS` like any request, `503` with
  `Retry-After`. It used to count from the first time the handler waited. The
  access line's duration starts at the same instant.
- **The docs stop teaching a webhook signature verified in a handler**, an
  authentication decision outside a guard: the HTTP extractors page sends a
  webhook to a guard, and the guards page shows one reading the body under the
  cap, verifying a Standard Webhooks HMAC-SHA256 in constant time within a
  five-minute replay window, and setting the bytes back for the handler.

The per-request cost, only for a request with a body: one box around it and one
`Arc` for its verdict; a timer is allocated only when the body is waited on. A
bodiless request pays nothing new while the access line is on, the default: the
budget counts from the instant the line already reads. With
`<PREFIX>_HTTP__ACCESS_LOG=false` and a budget, the edge reads the clock once
per request, where it used to only when a handler waited.

### `/graphql` and `/mcp` run every global guard

A registered authz bridge silently replaced the app's global guards at the two
in-band endpoints. With `use_guards_global([guard::<ApiKeyGuard>()])` and
`AuthzGraphqlModule` imported, a request carrying no API key reached every GraphQL
operation and every MCP tool: the bridge ran its own `AuthnGuard` and `AuthzGuard`,
and the pool never ran. And `/graphql` stamped every request with the `#[public]`
marker, so any guard admitting on `is_public()` admitted every GraphQL request.

- **The global pool always runs at the request** on `/graphql` (its POST and its
  graphql-ws upgrade) and on `/mcp`. The bridges are gone: the guards a module
  declares for either endpoint join the pool, each run once (*`/graphql` and
  `/mcp` take their guards from the modules that declare them*). With no guard at
  all, `/mcp` stays deny-all and `/graphql` says it runs unguarded at `warn`.
- **`/graphql` is `Posture::InBand`**, not `#[public]`: one endpoint whose
  operations each declare their own posture. `cx.reflector().posture()` reads
  it — the posture a mount stamped, else `Public` under the `#[public]`
  marker, else `Gated` — and `is_public()` is `false` on `/graphql`. `AuthnGuard`
  admits a request with no credential on `Public` and `InBand` alike, and on
  `InBand` still refuses a credential that does not verify: a bad bearer on
  `/graphql` answers `401` with `error="invalid_token"` instead of running as an
  anonymous caller. `AbilityGuard` builds the visitor ability on both. `/mcp`
  carries no posture and stays `Gated`.

**Breaking:** an HTTP-only global guard — an API key, an IP allow-list — now
gates `/graphql` and `/mcp`: a request it refuses
answers its `401`/`403` where it answered `200`. A hand-written guard admitting
anonymous callers on `is_public()` refuses anonymous GraphQL requests until it
reads the posture:

```rust
if matches!(cx.reflector().posture(), Posture::Public | Posture::InBand) {
    return Ok(());
}
```

### No sentence quotes a configured value or a peer's payload

A deployment's configuration, a client's frame and a provider's answer are somebody's
data: a boot error, a log line and a reply describe them and never quote them. Several
framework sentences still did.

- **A URL is shown through `nest_rs::config::endpoint_shown`** — scheme, host, port and
  path; never the userinfo, query or fragment a credential travels in, and "an
  unparsable URL" for a value that does not parse. The OAuth client, the JWK Set
  client, the OTLP exporter (its boot refusal, its exporter-build error, its `info`
  line and `OpenTelemetryConfig`'s `Debug`) and storage (its boot refusal,
  `StorageError::PlaintextEndpoint` and `StorageConfig`'s `Debug`) all show their
  endpoint that way. The OTLP endpoint is parsed once when the config resolves, and a
  value that does not parse fails the boot naming the variable that set it
  (`OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT`, [below](#the-app-is-named-after-its-binary-and-telemetry-reads-the-opentelemetry-specifications-variables)).
- **A config that fails its rules is said by field and rule** — `OAuthClient::new` and
  a social provider's pinned config went through `validator`'s own wording, which lists
  each rule's parameters, the rejected value among them. A URL in an
  `OAuthClientConfig` that does not parse is refused when the client is built, naming
  its variable, the field and the parser's reason: "`<PREFIX>_OAUTH__CLIENT__AUTH_URL`
  (or `<PREFIX>_OAUTH__CLIENT__AUTH_URL_FILE`), or `OAuthClientConfig::auth_url` in
  code, does not parse as a URL: invalid port number".
- **Breaking: `Setting::shown` is gone, and `Setting::parse` never passes a value
  through the parser's reason**, inline or read from a file: a refusal names the
  variable and what was expected — "does not parse as u16", "expected a boolean (1/0,
  true/false, yes/no, on/off)", "is not a valid HTTP header name". An entry of a list is
  named by its place: `<PREFIX>_HTTP__TRUSTED_PROXIES`' "entry 2 of the list is not an
  IP address", `<PREFIX>_AUTHN__ALGORITHMS`' "entry 1 of the list names an algorithm
  this verifier does not run", and the protected resource's issuers, scopes and bearer
  methods the same way; a refused resource URI names its variable.
- **A graphql-ws frame that does not decode closes the socket `1002` with the fixed
  reason "not a graphql-ws message"**, where it carried serde's sentence, which quotes
  the client's payload; serde's report goes to one `debug` line, said through
  `DecodeError`. Every close reason the socket sends is cut to 123 bytes on a character
  boundary (RFC 6455 §5.5).
- **A multipart GraphQL request whose `operations` or `map` part does not decode is
  answered naming the part** — "the `map` part: invalid type: a string, expected a
  sequence at line 1 column 21" — never the part.
- `nest-rs-testing`'s socket builder names a header whose value cannot be sent,
  never the value: a test header is often `Authorization`.
- Writing a config of your own: a refusal goes through `Setting::refuse` with a reason
  saying what was expected, a URL through `endpoint_shown`, a `validator` failure
  through `ConfigError::validation`, and a required value no tier set through the new
  `ConfigError::unset(namespace, key, why)` — "must be set, inline or through
  `<KEY>_FILE`", then why there is no default — which Redis, seaorm and storage now
  use. A sentence naming every way a value is given comes from
  `var_name_or_field(namespace, key, field)` or, for a value `<KEY>_FILE` may hold,
  `spellings_or_field` — "`<VAR>`, or `Type::field` in code" — beside `var_name` and
  `spellings`.

### The OAuth client and the OTLP exporter follow the outbound policy

Every client the framework opens now follows one policy: TLS through `ClientTls`, https
wherever the protocol's standard requires it, no redirect followed, bounds of its own,
and a URL shown only through `endpoint_shown`. The JWK Set client already did; the OAuth
client checked its URLs for blankness alone and trusted reqwest's built-in roots, and the
OTLP exporter dialled through a client the SDK built with no TLS setting of ours.

- **Breaking: an OAuth provider endpoint is `https`.** `<PREFIX>_OAUTH__CLIENT__AUTH_URL`,
  `TOKEN_URL` and `USERINFO_URL` over plain http fail the boot naming the variable and
  the RFC (6749 §3.1 and §3.2, 6750 §5.3) — they carry the user's sign-in, the client
  secret and the code, and the bearer token. `REDIRECT_URL` is `https` or `http` on
  `localhost`, `127.0.0.1` or `[::1]` (RFC 8252 §7.3), so a local
  `http://localhost:3000/callback` keeps working. A social provider's `REDIRECT_URL`
  follows the same rule, and its refusal names `<PREFIX>_SOCIAL__<PROVIDER>__REDIRECT_URL`;
  a provider of your own refuses its redirect the same way through the new
  `nest_rs::oauth::client::refuse_plaintext_redirect(url, setting)`, naming its own
  variable.
- **Breaking: `OAuthClient::fetch` refuses a URL that is not `https`** before anything is
  sent: the call carries the user's access token.
- **Breaking: `OAuthClientConfig` gains `tls: ClientTls`** —
  `<PREFIX>_OAUTH__CLIENT__TLS_CA_CERT` trusts an identity provider a private authority
  signed, and `TLS_CERT` / `TLS_KEY` present a client certificate to one requiring it,
  each with its `_FILE` spelling. The social providers trust the system's authorities.
- **Breaking: `OpenTelemetryConfig` gains `tls: ClientTls`**, which the specification's
  `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_CERTIFICATE`, `CLIENT_CERTIFICATE` and `CLIENT_KEY` files override
  ([below](#the-app-is-named-after-its-binary-and-telemetry-reads-the-opentelemetry-specifications-variables)). The crate now builds the
  exporters' HTTP clients itself, one per signal: verified against those authorities or
  the system's, a 3 s bound on reaching the collector, each export cut at the SDK's own
  export timeout (`OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_<SIGNAL>_TIMEOUT`, then `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_TIMEOUT`,
  in milliseconds, then 10 s), no redirect, and https alone unless the endpoint is
  `http://`, which the OTLP specification lets declare plaintext — a collector beside the
  app stays the common case. A timeout that is not a whole number of milliseconds fails
  the boot naming its variable ([below](#no-opentelemetry-variable-endpoint-or-header-is-passed-over-in-silence-and-a-piped-console-is-not-coloured)), and a zero is no limit, the processor's bound cutting the export ([below](#no-opentelemetry-value-leaves-an-app-exporting-nothing-or-under-a-name-it-did-not-write-without-a-word)). TLS material beside an `http://` endpoint fails the boot
  naming both settings, and an endpoint whose scheme is neither fails naming the
  variable. Https no longer depends on another crate enabling reqwest's TLS.

### A process that declares no environment runs as production

`<PREFIX>_ENV` chooses the `.env` files and arms every development convenience, and a
process that left it unset ran as development: it read `.env.development`, served the
OpenAPI documentation and, in the demo, the GraphQL playground, allowed storage's plain HTTP
with the dev container's public pair, and dialled Redis's plaintext loopback. A forgotten
variable is not a visible line.

- **Breaking: an unset, empty or unrecognised `<PREFIX>_ENV` is `Production`.**
  `Environment::from_env()` reads it so, `Environment::default()` is `Production`, and an
  undeclared process reads `.env.production.local`, `.env.local`, `.env.production` and
  `.env` — never `.env.development`. Each development convenience waits for a declared
  `development` (`dev`) or `test`: the documentation stays closed, storage refuses plain HTTP
  and has no credential, Server-Timing stamps nothing, and Redis has no URL. An app reading
  any config says so once at boot, at `info` on `nest_rs::config` — `no
  environment declared; running as production`, with `declared = false` — and each refusal
  names its variable. `Environment::declared()` is unchanged. `nestrs doctor` reads the
  files an undeclared app reads.
- **An unrecognised `<PREFIX>_ENV` is said once, by name, never by its value**: the stderr
  warning quoted the value and was repeated by every reader; a misspelt `prodution` loaded
  the development cascade in production, and now runs as production.
- **Breaking: no profile holds a Redis URL.** The plaintext loopback `redis://127.0.0.1/`
  left the framework: the profile's `.env` file names the server
  ([Development surfaces, emits and seeds follow the declared profile alone](#development-surfaces-emits-and-seeds-follow-the-declared-profile-alone)).
  An import with no `<PREFIX>_REDIS__URL` now fails the boot naming the
  variable, in every profile, and says that a pinned config reads it from the deployment,
  not `.env`; it used to dial the loopback in plaintext in staging and production. A blank `<PREFIX>_REDIS__URL` is refused under the spelling that set it.
- **`Environment::is_deployed()`** is new: `true` for `Staging` and `Production`, where a
  `Surface::ByProfile` stays closed. Storage, OpenAPI, GraphQL and Server-Timing key on it
  through that value, and a config of your own with a development convenience does the
  same.
- **The managed paths declare their profile**: `nestrs run dev` (the scaffold's and the
  demo's `dev` recipes) declares `development`, the demo's `db` recipes too, and the test
  harness `test`. `nestrs run start` declares nothing and runs as production.

### Development surfaces, emits and seeds follow the declared profile alone

A development convenience is armed by a declared `<PREFIX>_ENV=development` or `test`,
through a `Surface` left to the profile, and by nothing else. GraphQL did not follow it, the console
followed the cargo build profile, two resource configs named this repository's dev
container, and the demo seeded synthetic jobs in every profile.

- **Breaking: the bare `GraphqlModule` serves the playground and answers introspection
  under a declared development or test profile**, as OpenAPI's documentation and
  Server-Timing are served. Both stayed off in every profile, so an app opened them by
  hand; the demo opened the playground and left introspection off, a page with no schema to
  read. A deployment that opens one (`<PREFIX>_GRAPHQL__PLAYGROUND=true`,
  `<PREFIX>_GRAPHQL__INTROSPECTION=true`, or `Surface::Open` pinned) is honoured and said
  once at `warn` on `nest_rs::graphql`, naming the `environment`. A suite booting the
  module under the test profile now gets the page on `GET /graphql` where it got `405`:
  pin `playground: Surface::Closed` to assert the closed endpoint.
- **Breaking: `disable_introspection` is `introspection`**, its opposite
  (`<PREFIX>_GRAPHQL__DISABLE_INTROSPECTION=false` is `<PREFIX>_GRAPHQL__INTROSPECTION=true`):
  a development surface's key says it serves, as `PLAYGROUND` and
  `<PREFIX>_OPENAPI__ENABLED` do. The old key is read by nothing, and the boot says so at
  `warn`.
- **Breaking: one key pair writes both committed artifacts.** `GraphqlConfig`'s
  `emit_sdl` / `schema_path` (`<PREFIX>_GRAPHQL__EMIT_SDL` / `__SCHEMA_PATH`) and
  `OpenApiConfig`'s `emit_document` / `document_path` (`<PREFIX>_OPENAPI__EMIT_DOCUMENT` /
  `__DOCUMENT_PATH`) are `emit` / `emit_path` on both (`<PREFIX>_GRAPHQL__EMIT`,
  `__EMIT_PATH`; `<PREFIX>_OPENAPI__EMIT`, `__EMIT_PATH`). `emit` is open under a declared
  development profile alone — under test, parallel suites would rewrite a committed
  file — and a relative `emit_path` is resolved against the
  directory of the crate `#[nest_rs::main]` expanded in, whatever directory the process
  runs from, so each app of a workspace writes its own `schema.graphql` and
  `openapi.json`. An absolute path is written as given; a process no `#[nest_rs::main]`
  started resolves a relative one against its working directory, as before. Each emit
  copies what its endpoint serves: the SDL whatever the playground says, the OpenAPI
  document only while `<PREFIX>_OPENAPI__ENABLED` serves it — an open `emit` with the
  documentation closed writes nothing, and the boot says so at `warn` on `nest_rs::openapi`.
- **Breaking: no profile names an address or a credential.** `RedisConfig` no longer
  dials `redis://127.0.0.1/` in development and test — a plaintext loopback the TLS-only
  dev container never serves — and `StorageConfig` no longer names `s3://nestrs` at
  `https://rustfs:9000` with the `nestrs`/`nestrs` pair, a host a
  scaffolded app's `compose.yml` never starts. Both now refuse an unset `URL` (and storage
  an unset pair) by name in every profile, as SeaORM always did: the profile's `.env`
  file names them, as `nestrs new` writes `compose.yml`'s Postgres and Redis into
  `.env.development` and `.env.test`. `StorageConfig` still allows plain HTTP in development
  and test.
- **Breaking: the console's format follows the declared profile** — text under
  development and test, JSON otherwise, a deployment and an undeclared process alike —
  where `LogFormat` read `debug_assertions`: a release build under
  `development` wrote JSON, a debug build in production text. The kernel reads the profile
  through the config crate the binary links when it builds the console, so a test process
  and a binary calling `load_cascade` follow it too. `<PREFIX>_LOG_FORMAT` still decides
  when set, and `nestrs new`'s `.env.development` no longer restates `text`.
- **The demo** moved its database, Redis and storage settings out of the `.env` every
  profile reads into `.env.development` and `.env.test`, dropped its five GraphQL and
  OpenAPI lines and its `NESTRS_LOG_FORMAT=text`, and seeds its synthetic transcode jobs
  under a declared development or test profile alone (`AudioConfig::synthetic_seed` is a
  `Surface`). `nestrs run start` under the production profile names its services on the
  process, as a deployment does.

### `#[authorize]`'s class gate runs before the route's own interceptors and filters

An HTTP route's class gate ran when its handler's arguments were extracted, inside
every interceptor and filter the route declared. A route-scoped caching
interceptor answered a caller `#[authorize(Read, Secret)]` refused, from its
store, and a route-scoped filter saw the gate's `403` as an error it could map to
a success. The posture a developer declared in one visible line was not the
access decision.

- **The class gate runs with the guards**: right after them, whose ability it
  reads, before the route's pipes, and outside every interceptor, filter and
  exception filter the route or its controller declares. A refused caller gets
  the gate's `403` (or `insufficient_scope` challenge, or the `500` of a missing
  ability guard) and nothing the route binds runs; the denial is a response, as
  a guard's is, so no filter sees it, route-scoped or global. The same `warn`
  files it.
- **Every spelling is gated there**: `#[authorize(A, E)]`, every `#[crud]`
  operation (now declared through `#[authorize]`), and the public-reads
  `Authorize<A, E>` parameter beside `#[public]`, selected by type like the
  response mask — an alias or a re-export gates too.
- **`RouteResponseShaper::GATE`** (new, provided, `None` by default) is the class
  gate a shaper type declares; `Authorize<A, S>` declares its own. `Bind<A, S>`
  declares none: its per-row check stays where it loads the row.

**Breaking:** a route-scoped interceptor or filter no longer runs for a caller the
route's posture refuses, and no longer observes that refusal; a global filter no
longer sees it either, since the refusal is a response where it was an error.

### A database error's text never reaches a client, a log line or a dead letter

Postgres words a refusal around the input it refused (`invalid input syntax for type
uuid: "…"`), sqlx's error displays that sentence and sea-orm's `DbErr` displays it
again, so a value a client sent reached every framework log line that rendered the
error, every queue dead-letter reason, and — on a GraphQL `#[crud]` operation — the
client itself: a `#[crud]` query over a table the database refused answered `200` with
`relation "…" does not exist` as its `message`, with no code.

`nest_rs::core::SafeLink` (new) is how a driver says its errors without the values
they quote: an entry submitted once per driver crate
(`nest_rs::core::inventory::submit! { SafeLink::new(say) }`) whose `say` answers a
sentence for a link of its own. `error_message` and `DecodeError::redact` ask every
sayer about each link of a chain before its `Display`, say a claimed link by its
sentence and read nothing below it, and say it the same way wherever a wrapper spells
it whole (`{0}`, the queue's job error, anyhow's own box). `nest-rs-seaorm` submits
one for `DbErr`, sea-orm's `RuntimeErr` and sqlx's error: a server error is said as
`database error <SQLSTATE>` plus the constraint, table and column the server named
(`database error 23505 on constraint "users_email_key", table "users"`); a variant
whose text can hold a value (`Type`, `Json`, `TryIntoErr`'s cause, sea-orm's internal
error, sqlx's decode, protocol and configuration errors) by its kind alone; the
others, which name a pool's state, a count or a schema name, keep their sentence.
Every `error = %…` field the framework writes reads `error_message`, so this holds on
every edge, in every dead-letter reason, and in `DecodeError::redact`'s replies.

A GraphQL `#[crud]` operation now answers the class a client branches on: a unique
violation `CONFLICT` (`"conflict"`), a row gone before the write `NOT_FOUND`, a
create its row-level re-check rolled back and a denied row `FORBIDDEN` (the denied row
answered a code-less `"forbidden"`), anything else `"internal error"` under
`INTERNAL`, its error filed once at `error` on `nest_rs::orm` (`crud operation
failed`), as the HTTP twin files it. A mutation whose statement the database
refuses answers its class too, since each mutation root field runs in a savepoint
of its own (*A GraphQL operation is a unit of work, and each mutation root field a
savepoint*).

**Breaking, for operators:** a framework log line that rendered a database error now
says it by SQLSTATE and the names the server reported, never by the server's message:
a search for a server's sentence in the logs no longer finds it; the database's own
log keeps the statement. `ServiceError`'s and `#[crud]`'s `500` lines on
`nest_rs::orm` carry the error under `error`, where they wrote `detail`. A GraphQL
`#[crud]` client reading a failure's text now reads its code.

### A browser's `Origin` is checked at both WebSocket upgrades and at MCP, CORS on or off

A browser consults CORS before it lets a page read a response, never before it
opens a WebSocket, and never before a request simple enough to need no
preflight. With no CORS policy configured — the default — a page on any site
could open a gateway socket or a graphql-ws socket under its visitor's cookies
(RFC 6455 §10.2, cross-site WebSocket hijacking), or post to `/mcp`, whose
transport requires a server to validate `Origin` on every incoming connection.
The transport's CORS layer refused a foreign `Origin` only once origins were
listed.

- **A WebSocket gateway's upgrade, the graphql-ws upgrade on the GraphQL path
  and every `/mcp` request now check `Origin` themselves, CORS on or off.** A
  request with no `Origin` — any client but a browser — is unaffected. With
  no CORS policy, one carrying the request's own origin is served; any other
  is answered `403` with a problem document (`code` `FORBIDDEN`, which names
  no origin) before a socket opens — before the guard on `/graphql` and
  `/mcp`, after a gateway's upgrade guards.
- **Once `<PREFIX>_HTTP__CORS_ORIGINS` is set**, the transport's CORS layer
  answers first, as before, and serves only the origins it lists: the
  server's own is listed too when a page it serves opens a socket or calls
  MCP. Its `403` files no `warn`. Admitting the own origin there unlisted
  would let a DNS-rebinding page, whose `Host` names its own domain, pass on
  every route.
- **The request's own origin**, which counts only with CORS off, is the
  scheme it arrived over and its `Host` (HTTP/2's `:authority` when it has
  none) — or, from a peer listed in
  `<PREFIX>_HTTP__TRUSTED_PROXIES` alone, the `proto` and `host` that
  `Forwarded` or `X-Forwarded-Proto` / `X-Forwarded-Host` give. Origins compare
  per RFC 6454: scheme and host without case, a default port written or not.
  `Origin: null` is admitted only where `null` is listed, or `*`.
- **Each refusal of the check files one `warn`, `browser origin refused`**,
  on the edge's target (`nest_rs::ws`, `nest_rs::graphql`, `nest_rs::mcp`),
  with `origin`, the header sent, and `expected`, the origin the server took
  for its own — which tells an operator a proxy rewriting `Host` is not
  trusted yet.
- MCP's `allowed_hosts` still answers DNS rebinding; rmcp's own
  `allowed_origins` stays empty, since it cannot say "the request's own
  origin".

### The CORS allow-list is read once, as origins, and `null` or an entry no browser sends fails the boot

`HttpCors::origins` (`<PREFIX>_HTTP__CORS_ORIGINS`) had two readers that read it two ways. The
CORS layer compared a request's `Origin` with each entry's bytes; the browser-origin check of the
WebSocket and graphql-ws upgrades and of `/mcp` parsed each entry as an origin, dropped in silence
one it could not parse, and read the container's configuration rather than the transport's. So
`https://app.example/` (a trailing slash) admitted no browser on either, `https://APP.example`
admitted a page's socket and refused its `fetch`, an internationalized host was refused unless
the list held its raw UTF-8, which no browser sends, and a listed `null` admitted every sandboxed
iframe and `data:` document on any site, on both.

- **One list, parsed once at boot, read by both.** A request's `Origin` is parsed the same way
  and matched as an origin (RFC 6454 §5): scheme and host without case, the scheme's default port
  implied, an IPv4 host as the URL standard reads it, so `https://App.example:443` admits a page
  on `https://app.example` and `https://127.1` one on `https://127.0.0.1`, on a route and on an
  upgrade alike. The answer still echoes the request's own `Origin` bytes, which the browser
  checks. What a browser sends is matched on its bytes before any parse.
- **An entry that is no origin fails the boot**: a path (a trailing `/` included), a query, a
  fragment, userinfo, no host, a malformed IP literal or port, a host that ends in a number and is
  no IPv4 address (`https://app.123`), a percent-encoded host, a space or control character, a
  character outside ASCII — write an internationalized host in its `xn--` form, as browsers send
  it.
- **`null` fails the boot**, saying why — every sandboxed iframe, `data:` document and redirect
  across origins sends it, whatever site it runs on — and pointing at `*`, which says what it
  opens and which `credentials` refuses. A `null` a browser sends is refused by the CORS layer and
  the upgrades under any list but `*`; the browser-origin refusal's `hint` now says so.
- **Each refusal names the list and the entry's place, never the entry**:
  `invalid CORS config: entry 2 of `origins` (<PREFIX>_HTTP__CORS_ORIGINS) is not an origin,
  `scheme://host[:port]`: it carries a path, …`. `methods`, `headers` and `exposed_headers` say a
  bad entry the same way (`entry 1 of `methods` (…) is not a method, a token`), where they quoted
  it, and an entry beside a `*` is read too. A forbidden method is named by its place beside the
  method (`is `TRACE`, a forbidden method`).
- **A policy listing no origin installs no CORS layer**, whatever else it sets. `for_root` and the
  environment already read an empty `origins` as no layer; a seeded `HttpConfig`
  (`App::builder().provide`) or one handed to `HttpTransport::from_config` with an empty `origins`
  had installed a layer admitting every origin, with credentials when it set them.

### The graphql-ws socket is bounded in size, in its wait for `connection_init` and in operations at once

A graphql-ws socket buffered up to tungstenite's 64 MiB per message, held an
upgraded socket that never sent `connection_init` for its whole four-hour
lifetime, ran any number of operations at once, and let a `subscribe` reusing
a running id silently replace it — where graphql-ws requires `4409`. One
client could hold memory and tasks without bound. Three keys join
`<PREFIX>_GRAPHQL__*`, and three fields `GraphqlConfig`:

- **`<PREFIX>_GRAPHQL__MAX_MESSAGE_BYTES`, 64 KiB by default** (as
  `<PREFIX>_WS__MAX_MESSAGE_BYTES`), held at the protocol layer: a larger frame
  is never buffered, and the socket closes `1009` (Message Too Big), as the
  WebSocket gateway closes at its own cap.
- **`<PREFIX>_GRAPHQL__CONNECTION_INIT_TIMEOUT_SECS`, 3 s by default** (the
  graphql-ws reference server's wait), whole seconds from 1 to 60, never off:
  a socket with no `connection_init` by then closes
  `4408 Connection initialisation timeout`, for the legacy
  `subscriptions-transport-ws` protocol too.
- **`<PREFIX>_GRAPHQL__MAX_SOCKET_OPERATIONS`, 100 by default**, `0` for
  unlimited: a `subscribe` past it is answered the protocol's `error` message
  for its id (`too many operations on this socket`), and the socket and its
  running operations stay.
- **A `subscribe` reusing the id of an operation still running closes the
  socket `4409 Subscriber already exists`**; the reason does not echo the id.
- A `4408` or `4409` close files the socket's `graphql.subscription` line
  `error`.

### A WebSocket message and an MCP operation each have a deadline

A socket answers one message at a time, and nothing bounded the message: a
handler that never answered stalled its socket until the connection's lifetime
ran out — four hours by default — or the shutdown window. An MCP operation
fared no better: rmcp answers a session's request, and a stateless one from a
client older than `2026-07-28`, with an event stream whose head leaves at once,
so the HTTP request timeout, which ends at the head, never reached the tool
behind it. Only a `2026-07-28` stateless request, which holds its head until
the operation's first message, was cut by the request timeout.

- **`WsConfig::message_timeout`**, read from `<PREFIX>_WS__MESSAGE_TIMEOUT_SECS`,
  bounds one message — its guards, pipes and handler — from its decoded
  envelope. Past it the message's work is dropped and its unit of work rolled
  back, the client gets `{"event": "<event>", "data": {"error": "the message
  was not answered in time", "code": "UNAVAILABLE", "reason": "timeout"}}`, and
  the socket goes on serving. The `ws.message` line files
  `error`, and one `warn` on `nest_rs::ws` — `websocket message not answered
  within its deadline; it is cut` — names `conn_id`, `event`,
  `timeout_secs` and `reason="timeout"`.
- **`McpConfig::operation_timeout`**, read from
  `<PREFIX>_MCP__OPERATION_TIMEOUT_SECS`, bounds every request operation —
  `tools/call`, `prompts/get`, `resources/read` and the rest — but
  `subscriptions/listen`, which has no end of its own. Past it the operation is
  dropped and rolled back, and the client is answered a JSON-RPC internal error
  (`-32603`, `the operation was not answered in time`) whose `data` is
  `{"code": "UNAVAILABLE", "reason": "timeout"}`. The `mcp.operation` line
  files `error`, and one `warn` on `nest_rs::mcp` — `mcp operation not answered
  within its deadline; it is cut` — names `method`, `operation`,
  `operation_timeout_secs` and `reason="timeout"`.
- Both default to **20 seconds**, the request timeout's, inside the shutdown
  window; both read whole seconds from 1 to 3600, `0` for none, refused outside
  from the environment and from the pinned struct alike, with the request
  timeout's sentences (`nest_rs::http::UNIT_TIMEOUT_BOUNDS`, the range the
  three share). The deadline cuts the
  body inside the unit of work, so a commit is never cut.
- A deadline past `<PREFIX>_HTTP__SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_SECS`, or off, files one
  boot `warn` per gateway or endpoint — on `nest_rs::ws`, `a websocket unit's
  deadline outlasts the shutdown window: one admitted just before a stop can be
  cut unanswered`, with `unit`, `timeout_secs` and `shutdown_timeout_secs` (*A
  connection hook has a deadline of its own*); `the operation timeout outlasts the shutdown window:
  an operation admitted just before a stop can be cut unanswered` on
  `nest_rs::mcp`, the same with `operation_timeout_secs`. Not refused.

The set: an edge's per-unit wait is `<unit>_timeout` in its config, read from
`<PREFIX>_<EDGE>__<UNIT>_TIMEOUT_SECS` — `HTTP__REQUEST_TIMEOUT_SECS`,
`WS__MESSAGE_TIMEOUT_SECS`, `MCP__OPERATION_TIMEOUT_SECS`. Not bounded yet: a
GraphQL operation on the graphql-ws socket, which has a count bound and no
deadline, and a gateway's `on_connect` and `on_disconnect` hooks, which are
awaited without one — a stalled `on_connect` holds its socket before the read
loop and the lifetime ceiling are armed.

### A provider a handler cannot reach answers an opaque `500`, the type filed at `error`

A request-scoped provider a `Scoped<T>` could not resolve answered a `500`
whose body named the Rust type (`no provider registered for
`app::billing::Ledger``), and `Bind<A, S>` said which part of its wiring was
missing; the transport's normalizer withheld those bodies behind the edge, but
any reader of the error saw them. Both now answer `internal error`, the opaque
message every edge uses, and file the type and what was missing at `error` on
`nest_rs::http` (`Scoped`) or `nest_rs::orm` (`Bind`), field `provider`.

`Scoped<T>` and `Bind<A, S>` implement `nest_rs::core::ResolvesProvider`, so the
boot can check the provider each one resolves; the check runs once `#[routes]`
probes its handlers' parameters, in a later section.

### A problem's `detail` is only ever the framework's own sentence

A raw `4xx` text body a layer or a library wrote on a mount was turned into
the problem document's `detail`, so a value the client sent, quoted back by a
library, reached the wire. The `detail` now comes only from a sentence the
framework wrote — a body its renderer produced, or a constant sentence it
checked quotes nothing (rmcp's transport refusals); any other raw `4xx` text
answers its status's problem document with no `detail`.

### MCP sessions are capped per mount and expire after a configured idle time

A client older than `2026-07-28` opens an MCP session with `initialize`, and the
session outlives the HTTP connection that opened it: the transport's connection
cap never counted it. A caller past the endpoint's guard could open sessions in
a loop, each holding a worker task and its channels until five minutes of
idleness — rmcp's default, which nestrs did not set, so an rmcp release could
move it. And `<PREFIX>_MCP__SSE_RETRY_SECS` never reached a session's streams:
they advertised rmcp's own `retry: 3000` whatever the variable said.

- **`McpConfig::max_concurrent_sessions`**, read from
  `<PREFIX>_MCP__MAX_CONCURRENT_SESSIONS`, caps the live sessions one endpoint
  holds — default **1 024**, from 1 to 1 048 576. Past it an `initialize` is answered
  `503` with `Retry-After: 5` and a problem document whose `code` is
  `UNAVAILABLE`, and one `warn` on `nest_rs::mcp` — `mcp session refused: the
  mount holds its maximum` — names `max_concurrent_sessions`. A session already
  open is served as before, and a `2026-07-28` request, which opens no session,
  is never refused for it. A session restored from a `SessionStore` takes a
  place like one opened here. An idle session costs about 110 KiB, so the
  default holds about 110 MiB at most.
- **`McpConfig::session_idle_timeout`**, read from
  `<PREFIX>_MCP__SESSION_IDLE_TIMEOUT_SECS`, closes a session that goes that
  long without a message and frees its place — default **300 seconds**, whole
  seconds from 1 to 3600, never off. Its client's next request gets `404` and
  opens a new session.
- **The wait for `initialize`** after a session opens is 60 seconds, set in
  code so an rmcp release cannot move it.
- **`<PREFIX>_MCP__SSE_RETRY_SECS` now reaches every stream of a session**,
  `0` included, which advertises no `retry:`.

The set: a bound on what a client holds is `max_concurrent_<thing>` and
`<thing>_idle_timeout` beside its siblings — `HTTP__MAX_CONCURRENT_CONNECTIONS`,
`HTTP__IDLE_TIMEOUT_SECS`, `MCP__MAX_CONCURRENT_SESSIONS`,
`MCP__SESSION_IDLE_TIMEOUT_SECS`. Two `initialize`s racing for the last place
are both admitted by the check that answers `503`, and the session manager
refuses the second; a session restored from a `SessionStore` past the cap is
refused there too. Both answer the cap's `503`, with `Retry-After: 5` and the
`UNAVAILABLE` problem document, and file the cap's one `warn` on
`nest_rs::mcp`. A request waiting on another's restore of the same session
still reads rmcp's `404` when the cap refuses that restore: rmcp hands a
waiting request only whether the restore succeeded. Its client opens a new
session, which the cap refuses `503`.

### An MCP session is served to the principal that opened it alone

A client older than `2026-07-28` names its MCP session on every request with
`Mcp-Session-Id`. Each request was authenticated by its own bearer, but nothing
tied the session to who opened it: a caller holding a valid token of its own
and another user's session id was served inside that session — its `POST`s
answered, its standalone stream opened or resumed with the server messages
meant for the owner, and a `DELETE` closing it. MCP's security best practices
ask a server to bind a session to the user it serves.

- **A session is bound at `initialize`** to the principal the request was
  served for: the actor id and the tenant the authentication guard read off
  the verified credential (`PrincipalIdentity::actor_id` and `tenant_id`).
- **Any other principal's request on it** — a `POST`, the standalone `GET`
  stream, a resume with `Last-Event-ID`, a `DELETE` — is answered exactly as a
  session the endpoint does not hold: `404`, `Not Found: Session not found`,
  so the answer never says whether the session exists. One `warn` on
  `nest_rs::mcp`, `mcp session refused: another principal opened it`, names
  the `call` that refused it, never the session or its owner. Nothing is
  closed.
- **A `DELETE` of a session the endpoint does not hold** is answered `404`, as
  the transport specification answers a terminated session; rmcp answered it
  `202`.
- **A session restored from a `SessionStore` keeps its principal**: the
  endpoint stores it in the handshake's `_meta` under `dev.nestrs/principal`,
  checks it before another instance restores the session or deletes it, and
  hands rmcp the client's handshake without it. A session stored by an earlier
  release, without that key, is refused to every caller, at `warn` as `mcp
  session refused: stored without its principal`.
- **An anonymous session binds the anonymous principal**: a caller presenting
  a credential does not reach it, nor an anonymous caller an authenticated
  principal's. A public endpoint (`McpModule::for_root(McpAccess::Public)`) runs
  no guard at its request, so all its sessions are anonymous and served to
  whoever names them, as before.

### An MCP session opens only for a principal naming its subject

The binding above reads the actor id and the tenant of the principal that opened a session.
A principal the endpoint's guards admit with no actor id — a machine token carrying no
`sub`, a guard that admits without authenticating — was told apart by its tenant alone: two
such clients in one tenant, or in none, were one principal, each served the other's sessions
(its `POST`s, its standalone stream and resume, its `DELETE`), and with no tenant such a
client also reached the anonymous principal's. A tenant is not unique to a client, and MCP's
security best practices bind a session to user-specific information.

- **On a gated endpoint, a session is bound to the subject**: the `actor_id` your
  `PrincipalIdentity` returns, the tenant still beside it. A principal naming no subject
  opens no session: its `initialize` is answered `403` with a problem document
  (`"code":"FORBIDDEN"`, a `detail` saying why) and no `Mcp-Session-Id`, and one `warn` on
  `nest_rs::mcp`, `mcp session refused: its principal names no subject`, carries a `hint`
  and never the credential or the tenant. A full endpoint answers it the same `403`, never
  the cap's `503`.
- **Return the credential's subject from `actor_id`.** RFC 9068 §2.2 requires `sub` in a
  JWT access token — the client's own id for a client acting on its own behalf — so a
  conformant token always names one. A client that has none still calls statelessly, on
  protocol `2026-07-28`, which opens no session; in a suite,
  `nest_rs::testing::mcp::call_tool_statelessly` calls a tool that way.
- **A public endpoint** (`McpModule::for_root(McpAccess::Public)`) is unchanged: no guard
  runs at its request, every caller is anonymous, and the session id is a session's only key.
  So each session it stores is bound to the anonymous principal, which names no subject: a
  gated endpoint reading the same `SessionStore` — the app once it closes its endpoint, or
  another app sharing the store — adopts none of them, said at `warn` as `mcp session
  refused: stored for a principal naming no subject`, and their clients open new sessions.
- **A task is reached only through the session that started it**: the endpoint builds each
  session's hosts, so a `TaskManager` a host holds in a field is that session's. A
  `TaskManager` an app shares across sessions through a provider is the app's own state, and
  its `tasks/*` methods decide who may reach each task.

### A token is accepted only by the resource it was minted for, and only from an issuer the resource advertises

`AuthnConfig::audience` was both the `aud` an issuer stamped on every token it minted and
the `aud` a verifier required, so every app trusting one issuer had to share one audience,
and a token minted for one resource was accepted by all of them: the RFC 8707 binding
`OAuthResourceModule` made mandatory held for no app, and the demo's `api` and `live`
accepted the tokens minted for its `assistant`. And no resource server compared a token's
`iss` with the authorization servers it advertises, which RFC 9068 §4 requires.

- **An issuer mints each access token for one resource**: `JwtService::sign_for(resource,
  &claims)` makes the resource the token's `aud`, whatever audience the issuer requires of
  its own; `sign` keeps that own audience, for what an app mints for itself (its OAuth
  transaction cookie, a development token).
- **A token request names its resource** (RFC 8707 §2): `AccessTokenRequest::resource`
  reads every `resource` parameter, the one a request may repeat. A `RegisteredClient`
  lists the `resources` its tokens may be minted for, and
  `AuthenticatedClient::audience_for(&request.resource)` chooses the token's audience —
  the one requested when the client is registered for it, its only one when the request
  names none — and refuses anything else as `TokenError::InvalidTarget`, `400
  invalid_target`: an unregistered resource, none among several, two at once.
- **`OAuthResourceModule` binds the verifier to the resource it advertises**: a token is
  accepted only when its `aud` is `RESOURCE` and its `iss` exactly matches one of
  `AUTHORIZATION_SERVERS`. `<PREFIX>_AUTHN__AUDIENCE` is no longer required there, and a
  set one that differs is the one required, warned as before; a set `<PREFIX>_AUTHN__ISSUER`
  narrows the issuers to itself and must be one of them, or the boot fails naming both
  variables; `ALLOW_ANY_AUDIENCE` beside the module fails the boot. A handshake keeps the
  app's own audience and issuer, so the OAuth client of an app serving a resource still
  reads its transactions.
- **The MCP endpoint answers the resource's host**: `<PREFIX>_MCP__ALLOWED_HOSTS` keeps its
  loopback default, and the host `RESOURCE` names is admitted beside it, unless the list was
  emptied on purpose.
- **The demo**: `auth` mints every access token with `sign_for` — `/token` for the resource
  the client asks for among its registration, `/login` for the `resource` it names or the
  first of `<PREFIX>_SIGN_IN__RESOURCES`, the social callback for the one its authorize leg
  named, or that first — and
  stamps its issuer URL, `http://localhost:3001`, which the assistant accepts as the one
  authorization server it advertises; the assistant sets no audience of its own. Its chart
  gives each app its own origin as `AUTHN__AUDIENCE` and the signing key to `auth` alone, in
  a Secret of its own (chart 0.4.0). Run locally, each app reads its own `apps/<app>/.env`
  ([an app's own directory](#an-apps-own-directory-holds-a-layer-of-the-env-cascade)): the
  api requires `http://localhost:3002`, the live app `:3004`, `auth` `:3001`, and the
  signing key sits in `apps/auth/.env` alone.
- **Breaking:** `RegisteredClient::new` and `AuthenticatedClient::new` take the resources
  before the payload, and a registry read from configuration lists `resources` for each
  client. Under `OAuthResourceModule`, a token naming no `iss`, or another than an
  advertised authorization server, is refused. The migration is the upgrading guide's.

### Discovery refuses a container no app sealed, instead of serving everything linked

Every reader of a link-time registry — the queue's `#[process]` methods, the scheduler's
ticks, the event bus's listeners, the health indicators, and GraphQL's resolvers — filtered
its entries against the reachability set the boot seals into the container, and read a
