container carrying none as "everything is in scope": one built by hand with
`Container::builder()` mounted every resolver, consumed for every `#[process]` method, ran
every tick, wired every listener and probed every indicator linked into the binary, whatever
its modules imported. GraphQL's loaders and context seeds failed closed instead, silently or
at `warn`.

- **`ReachableProviders::of(container, reader)`** returns the set the boot sealed, or
  `UngatedDiscoveryError` naming `reader` — what the caller discovers, as its inert-host
  report already names it: `#[process] method`, `scheduled method`, `#[on_event] method`,
  `indicator`, and on GraphQL `GraphQL resolver`, `#[dataloader] method` and
  `GraphQL context seed`. `ReachableProviders::reaches(&self, provider)` is a method; there
  is no `Option` to read as "everything".
- **Every reader refuses at a step that fails the boot**: `QueueWorker::configure`,
  `Scheduler::configure`, the events and health wiring steps, and GraphQL's wiring step;
  GraphQL's mount, which has no `Result`, files the refusal on its container and mounts
  nothing, and the boot fails on it once the transports are configured.
- **`nest_rs::core::UngatedDiscoveryError`** joins the kernel's boot refusals:
  "`#[process] method`s are discovered from a container that carries no reachability set;
  build it through App::new or App::builder, whose boot seals the set its modules reach".
- **Only the boot builds the set**: `ReachableProviders`' field is private, and a test
  boots through `TestApp`, `HeadlessApp` or `App::builder`, which seal it.

`App::new`, `App::builder()` and `TestApp` always seed the set: an app sees no change.

**Breaking:** `ReachableProviders::reaches(Option<&Self>, id)` is gone, and so is building
the set by its tuple field; a container built by hand that a transport or a wiring
discovers from is refused.

### A library's events never put a client's payload or a secret on a line

The framework's subscribers — the console `#[nest_rs::main]` installs, `install_console`,
`OpenTelemetry::init`/`init_with` and the test subscriber — write every library's events,
and some of those quote what a client sent. rmcp did so at the default `info` filter, 3.5 and 3.8 alike:
each client's `initialize` params (its name, version and capabilities), every notification
it sent (progress messages included) and every cancel reason, so **every app serving
`#[mcp]` wrote client values into its logs out of the box**; at `warn` its `Host` and
`MCP-Protocol-Version` headers, its JSON-RPC ids and the error data of each JSON-RPC error,
at `error` the session id a client named when the session store fails, and at `debug` and
`trace` each tool call's arguments and reply. An audit of every library the framework wires
found five more: h2 writes a client's PING data at `warn` and its GOAWAY debug data at
`debug`, rustls a client's SNI at `warn` and its ClientHello at `trace`, tungstenite a close
frame's reason at `debug` and every message at `trace`, sqlx-postgres a malformed `.pgpass`
line — password included — and an unknown connection parameter's value at `warn`, and the
OTLP exporter its request's URL, whose userinfo or query may carry a collector's
credential, and the collector's error body at `debug`.

Each of them is now clamped below the level it quotes at, narrowing the filter
`RUST_LOG` (or `OpenTelemetryConfig::log_filter`) sets, so a bare
`debug` or `trace` writes none of it either, and a stricter filter (`warn`, `off`, one
naming a single target) keeps its own level: a clamp never opens what the filter closes.
One `/mcp` denial now files one line, the framework's: rmcp's own `response error` was a
second one, at `warn`. A request whose `Host` is outside `<PREFIX>_MCP__ALLOWED_HOSTS` was
said only by rmcp's own `warn`, which quotes the `Host`; the mount now files it,
`reason="host_not_allowed"`, naming the variable and never the value. rmcp's own lines were
also the only report of a failed MCP session call and of a failure of the app's
`SessionStore`, and named the session: the mount now says each on `nest_rs::mcp` by its
kind — `mcp session manager failed` (`error`, with the `call` that failed), `mcp stream not
resumed: answered with an empty stream` (`warn`), `mcp session store failed` (`error` for a
load rmcp answers `500`, `warn` for a store or a delete) — and a session refused past the
cap by the manager files the cap's own `warn`. The `500` rmcp answers a failed session call
no longer carries the session id a client named.

The same audit found the framework's own line quoting it: the HTTP edge's `connection
ended on an error` (`debug`, `nest_rs::http`) said the error a connection ended on, and
h2 spells a GOAWAY's debug data — bytes the peer chose — into it. A GOAWAY error is now
said by its reason alone, wherever an error chain is rendered.

### The framework's own lines and replies no longer quote a value the client chose

Every line the framework files about a request now carries the server's values only. Before
7.0's release, five of them repeated something a client had written, at `warn` or on every
request at `info`:

- **The browser-origin refusal** — the `warn` a gateway's upgrade, the graphql-ws upgrade and
  `/mcp` file when they refuse a browser's `Origin` — named the `Origin` sent and the origin the
  server took for its own, read off the request's `Host` or a trusted proxy's `Forwarded
  host=` / `X-Forwarded-Host`. Both are the client's to write. It now says why by kind:
  `reason` (`scheme_differs`, `host_differs`, `port_differs`, `host_missing`, `origin_null`,
  `origin_repeated`, `origin_not_serialized`, `origin_not_visible_ascii`), the sent origin's
  `scheme` (`http`, `https`, `other`), `trusted_proxy`, and a `hint` naming the settings that
  would admit it (`<PREFIX>_HTTP__CORS_ORIGINS`, and `<PREFIX>_HTTP__TRUSTED_PROXIES` while
  the peer is no listed proxy).
- **The access line** (`http.request` on `nest_rs::operation`) filed the client's
  `User-Agent` on every request, and the method as the client spelled it, so an extension
  method was a token the client chose. `user_agent` is gone, and `method` is one of the
  methods OpenTelemetry's HTTP conventions know — RFC 9110's, `PATCH` and `QUERY` — or one a
  route registers, spelled exactly, and `_OTHER` for any other.
- **A WebSocket message's lines** (`ws.message`, its deadline and serialisation `warn`s) named
  the event as sent, and an event no handler serves was quoted again by its `warn` and by the
  error frame's text. They name the event a handler declares, or `_OTHER`; the frame says
  `unknown event`, still on the event the message named.
- **A GraphQL operation's line** (`graphql.operation`, and the `warn` of an operation cut at the
  request deadline) carried the operation's name from the client's document. The line names
  the operation's type; the root fields' `graphql.field` lines say what ran.
- **An MCP operation's line** (`mcp.operation`, its deadline `warn` and its panic line) named a
  custom method, any tool a `tools/call` named, a prompt name, a resource URI and a task id as
  the client sent them. It names the protocol's method or `_OTHER`, and a tool by the name a
  host declares or `_OTHER`; a prompt, a resource or a task stays off it.

The throttler's `rate limit exceeded` warn named the raw path of a request the router matched
nothing for; it names the matched template, or no route.

Replies a library writes no longer hand a client's names back either. rmcp refused a prompt no
host declares as `prompt '<name>' not found`, a custom method no host serves with the method as
its message, and a request its transport turns away by quoting the `MCP-Protocol-Version`,
`Mcp-Method` or `Mcp-Name` it carried; async-graphql refused an `operationName` its document
holds no operation of by quoting it. `/mcp` answers `prompt not found` (the prompts still listed
under `available_prompts`), a JSON-RPC error in its code's own words with its id and code kept,
and any other transport refusal as its status's problem document; `/graphql` answers `Unknown
operation named by operationName`. On a path several `#[mcp]` hosts share, a `prompts/get` now
reaches the host serving the prompt: the first host's refusal used to end the route.

The request spans keep what the telemetry conventions require or recommend, as a stated
exception, since a tracing backend reads those names and the span goes to the deployment's own
collector: `url.path`, `user_agent.original` and, for a method outside the known set,
`http.request.method_original` on the HTTP request span; `graphql.operation.name` on a GraphQL
operation's; the method and the tool, prompt or resource an MCP operation named. A header the
HTTP span records beyond that set is recorded only once it parsed: a `traceparent` the
transport did not continue as the four hex fields it parsed to (a malformed one was recorded
whole), an `X-Request-Id` from a trusted proxy only in the shape a proxy mints it — a UUID, or
16 to 64 hex digits, as nginx's `$request_id` is (any value of at most 200 bytes was
recorded). An MCP span no longer records a task id, which no convention names.

### GraphQL and MCP replies no longer hand a client's values back

Before 7.0's release, a library's refusal could still quote, in the reply, what the request
carried:

- **GraphQL quoted what a variable held.** async-graphql's validation and input coercion
  name the value they refuse, and the framework passed it on: an enum's value
  (`enumeration type "Role" does not contain the value "…"`), an input object's key the
  type does not declare (`unknown field "…" of type "PostFilter"`), the value a scalar
  refused (`Expected input type "Int", found "…".`), and a scalar's own message, which may
  quote it (`Failed to parse "UUID": …`). These are values from the request's `variables`,
  or written in the document, and they reached the reply on `POST` and over the graphql-ws
  socket alike. Each is now said by kind: `does not contain the value sent`, `an unknown
  field of type "PostFilter"`, `Expected input type "Int", found a string.`, and a
  scalar's message as every edge reports a decode failure — serde's sentence without its
  value, any other as `a value its type does not accept`. A name the document spells — a
  field, an argument, a fragment, a variable — stays in the message, as the GraphQL
  specification's own errors name it: the document is the sender's own, and no line
  carries the message.
- **MCP quoted a task id.** rmcp's `TaskManager`, the helper a host writing `tasks/*` uses,
  refuses a task it does not hold as `unknown task: <id>`; `/mcp` answers `unknown task`
  (`-32602` kept).
- **MCP grew without bound on a name a client chose.** rmcp 3.5 looked up the schema of
  the tool a `tools/call` names whenever its `MCP-Protocol-Version` is `2026-07-28` or later,
  building a whole handler to ask, and kept every answer, a miss included, keyed by the name
  sent: any caller past the endpoint's guards grew the process for good with one new name per
  request, a session not even needed. 7.0 builds on rmcp 3.8, which looks the schema up per
  request and keeps nothing (*Dependencies*). A call naming a tool no host declares is answered
  by the handler, as at every protocol version — `tool not found`, `-32602`, under
  `NOT_FOUND` — and files its `mcp.operation` line (`tools/call`, `_OTHER`, `error`).

What a refusal keeps is now exact: rmcp's transport refusals worded without anything the
request carried keep their sentence for the client — `Not Acceptable: Client must accept
both application/json and text/event-stream`, `Bad Request: Session ID is required`, `Not
Found: Session not found`, `Payload Too Large: request body exceeds 4194304 bytes`, the
`_meta` keys a `2026-07-28` request lacks — as the problem's `detail` or the JSON-RPC
message, where the previous change had left a bare status. Anything else stays by kind.
Two values a reply still carries are stated: an MCP refusal of an unsupported protocol
version keeps `data.requested`, the version the client asked for, since the specification
requires that shape; and a GraphQL error names the document's own names.

### A service holds no connection: `Repo` runs every query, filtered or through a named escape

`Repo::conn()` handed a service the unit's raw executor, a SeaORM connection, so a custom
read could run `Repo::scoped(..)`'s select on it. The same handle ran any query:
`Users::find().all(&Repo::<Users>::conn()?)` read every row of every tenant, and
`active.insert(&conn)` wrote past the create check — through no escape the data-layer rule
names. Every scoped read made a service hold it, the docs taught an ability-less read
through it, and a downcast of the port's own reader, `nest_rs::database::current_executor()`,
to the SeaORM executor reached the same handle without it.

`Repo` now hands out no connection:

- `Repo::scoped`, `with_tombstones`, `unscoped` and `unscoped_by_id` return a
  `RepoSelect<E>` (new). It takes SeaORM's own `QueryFilter`, `QueryOrder` and
  `QuerySelect` builders, and runs on the ambient executor through `all()`, `one()`,
  `count()` and `exists()`, failing closed outside a unit of work as every `Repo` call
  does.

  ```rust
  let rows = Repo::<Users>::scoped(Action::Read)
      .filter(user::Column::Name.is_in(names))
      .all()
      .await?;
  ```

- `Repo::insert_unscoped(active)` and `resolve_unique_slug::<E>(column, source, fallback,
  extra)` read the ambient executor, as `update` and `delete` always did.
- The handle a unit installs is private to `nest-rs-seaorm`: a downcast of
  `nest_rs::database::current_executor()` finds nothing a service can run a statement on.
  A driver keeps its own handle private the same way (*Writing a database driver*).
- The SeaORM executor is `SeaOrmExecutor`, named for its driver beside the port's
  `nest_rs::database::Executor`. It no longer implements the port itself: the handle a unit
  installs wraps it, and a service test runs its body in a unit of the app's binding
  (`TestApp::unit`).
- `nest_rs::database` holds the one read API: seaorm's `with_executor`,
  `with_request_executor`, `with_job_executor`, `current_executor`, `ExecutorScope` and
  `current_executor_scope` are gone, and the port's installers are the framework's alone
  (*No function hands a unit a wider ability or executor scope than it runs under*).

A relation's dataloader batch and `CrudService`'s reads run on the same select, and the
keyset page on the ambient executor under the same filter. A query that drops the ability
goes through a method named for it, whose rustdoc states its bar, so auditing the escapes is
one search per name. One way past the filter is left to review: each of SeaORM's builder
traits hands out the statement as `&mut` through `query()`, and a statement replaced, cleared
or `union`ed there runs unfiltered — a `.query()` on a `Repo` select is read as an escape.

No installer widens the unit it runs in: inside a request, or a body continuing one, a job
install runs under the request scope, since re-installing the unit's own handle under the job
scope turned a read that failed closed — a route with no ability guard, a reply no mask shapes
— into an unscoped one.

**Breaking:** `Repo::conn()` leaves the public API with no replacement: a custom read is
`Repo::scoped(action)` and SeaORM's builders, an ability-less one `Repo::unscoped`, an
insert `Creatable::create_from_active` or `Repo::insert_unscoped(active)`. `all`, `one`,
`count` and `exists` on a `Repo` select take no connection; `insert_unscoped` and
`resolve_unique_slug` lose theirs; `nest_rs::seaorm::Executor` is `SeaOrmExecutor`; the
seaorm read functions are `nest_rs::database`'s, and no public function installs an
executor of its own. A downcast of the port's reader to the SeaORM executor compiles and answers
`None`. `RepoSelect` offers no projection (`into_tuple`, `into_model`) and no joined fetch of
a related entity (`find_also_related`): a related row comes through its own service's
loader, under its own filter.

### No function hands a unit a wider ability or executor scope than it runs under

`nest_rs::authz::with_ability(ability, fut)` installed whatever ability it was handed, in
place of the one the unit carried, so a service could run a request's reads under rules it
built itself, past the guard that authorized the request;
`nest_rs::core::with_ambient(Arc::new(ability), fut)` did the same under the kernel's name.
And outside every unit — a
task spawned off a request included — `nest_rs::database::with_job_executor` granted the job
scope's unscoped reads to whatever handle it was handed. The data-layer rule named both as
escapes, held by review alone.

Two public ways in remained beside them: `nest_rs::database::run_unit`, the edge port, tagged
a unit inside a request with the job scope its spec declared, and a capture re-entered
inside another unit (`RequestContinuation::scope` or `enter`, `TaskContext::carry`) was
installed whole in place of that unit's context — a job's as a request's, one caller's in
another's request. And an ability intersection skipped a condition with no part, which
sea-query renders as FALSE (an empty `any`) or NOT TRUE (a negated empty `all`): a grant
admitting no row read as admitting every row the other side admits.

- **`with_ability` only narrows.** Inside a unit holding an ability, the body runs under what
  both allow: a subject granted where both grant it, a row both conditions admit, a column
  both expose, either side's denial denying. In a job, which holds none and reads unscoped,
  the body runs under the ability given — a job acting for a principal. Anywhere else, a
  request no ability guard authorized or outside every unit, it installs nothing, and a read
  that failed closed still does.
- **The ability guard is the one place an ability comes from.** The ability is ambient
  through a type only `nest-rs-authz` builds, so neither `with_ambient` nor a layer's
  `cx.attach` installs one: `Ability` no longer implements `AmbientValue`.
- **No public function installs an executor of its own.** `with_request_executor` and
  `with_job_executor` are the port's plumbing, for the framework's suites and a driver; a
  unit is the one an edge opens, and a service test's the testing harness's,
  `TestApp::unit(ability, …)` or `system_unit(…)`.
- **A unit never opens wider than the unit it opens in.** `run_unit` tags a job unit opened
  inside a request with the request's scope. Outside every unit the spec's scope stands —
  that is how a queue attempt, a tick and a third-party consumer open theirs — so a job unit
  a feature opens on a task of its own reads unscoped: the data-layer rule names it as an
  escape, found by grepping `ExecutorScope::Job`.
- **A capture re-entered inside another unit never widens it.** `AmbientValue` has
  `reenter`, beside `carry`: each value a capture holds is what its `reenter` returns where
  the capture is re-entered — the value itself by default. A job's scope runs as the
  request's inside a request, and a captured ability narrows to the one the running unit
  reads under — nothing in a request reading under none, a route no mask shapes included —
  and a captured tenant inside a unit served for another tenant is dropped. Where no unit
  runs (a spawned task, a body polled after its handler) a capture continues its own unit,
  every value kept.
- **An intersection keeps every condition of both sides**, an empty one included, so a grant
  admitting no row admits none in SQL as in the instance check.
- **The test harness subscribes as a caller with `ability(..)`**:
  `app.graphql_socket().ability(ability)` replaces `attach(ability)`, which keeps any other
  value a guard attaches (`testing` with `authz`).

**Breaking:** `nest_rs::database::{with_request_executor, with_job_executor}` no longer
resolve; a service test runs its body through `TestApp::unit` or `system_unit`. An `Ability`
passed to `with_ambient` or `cx.attach` no longer compiles: the caller's ability comes from
`AbilityGuard` over an `AbilityFactory`. `with_ability` keeps its signature and installs
nothing where the unit holds no ability and is not a job. `GraphqlSocketBuilder::attach(ability)`
is `ability(ability)`. A `run_unit` declaring `ExecutorScope::Job` inside a request runs under
the request's scope, as does a job's capture re-entered there.

### A denial that holds for every row refuses at the class gate, and no scope withholds a denial

The class gate read grants only: `Ability::can_class` asked whether any grant matched, and a
denial, even one with no `when` and so matching every row, reached it as nothing. Under
CASL's canonical `can(Manage, E).when(org)` beside `cannot(Create, E)`, `#[crud]`'s create
ran its `INSERT` for a caller the policy denies outright: a taken unique value answered
`409` across tenants, an unfillable NOT NULL column a `500` filed at `error`, and only an
insert that landed reached the re-check's `403`. A list under `cannot(Read)` answered
`200 []`, a by-id read told an existing row (`403`) from a missing one (`404`), and a
hand-written `#[authorize(Create, E)]` body ran whatever it did outside `Repo`. Beside it, a
denial took `requires_scope` like a grant, and a token without the scope dropped the
denial: a client asking for fewer scopes got more than one asking for more, against
RFC 6749 §3.3.

- **A denial no `when` narrows refuses at the class gate**, keyed on the action or on
  `Manage`, on every edge (HTTP, GraphQL, WebSocket, MCP), before any interceptor, pipe or
  handler runs: `can_class` is any grant and no such denial. The answer is a
  plain `403`, `forbidden`, never the `insufficient_scope` challenge, filed once at `warn`
  with `reason=class_denied`; `missing_scopes` names nothing, since no scope lifts a
  denial. A denial a `when` narrows stays with the rows, in the query pre-filter and the
  instance check.
- **`Creatable::create` sends no insert no row could pass**: a caller whose ability refuses
  `Create` on the class — no grant, or a denial no `when` narrows — gets
  `RecordNotInserted` before the statement, logged `access denied — no row of the entity is
  in the caller's scope` at `warn` on `nest_rs::orm`, whatever posture the unit runs
  under.
- **A denial is a rule of its own kind**: `cannot` returns `RuleSpec<'_, E, Cannot>`,
  `can` a `RuleSpec<'_, S>` (its kind `Can` by default), and `requires_scope` and `fields`
  exist on a grant alone. A denial holds whatever the token carries and hides rows, never
  columns, so `cannot(..).requires_scope(..)` and `cannot(..).fields(..)` — one dropped
  for a narrower token, the other ignored — no longer compile. `nest_rs::authz::{Can,
  Cannot}` are new.

**Breaking:** a `cannot` no `when` narrows, beside a broader grant, refuses its action with
`403` where the request ran; `cannot(..).requires_scope(..)` and `cannot(..).fields(..)`
no longer compile (`E0599`); `RuleSpec` takes a third parameter, its kind, `Can` by
default.

### A `when` that holds for every row narrows nothing, and a write no row could pass runs no hook

A denial whose `when` was `TRUE` for every row by its shape — `p.all([])`,
`Predicate::Always`, a disjunction holding one — kept its `when`, so the class gate read it
as conditional and let the caller through to the rows: a `#[crud]` create still sent its
`INSERT`. And a write no class gate saw (a handler gated on another subject) ran the
entity's `before_save` before its `UPDATE` matched no row, or its `INSERT` was rolled back.

- **A rule whose `when` holds for every row is committed as the rule no `when` narrows**,
  so such a denial refuses at the class gate like one with no `when`: `403`, `forbidden`,
  `reason=class_denied`. The reading is sound, not complete: a shape a column or `NULL`'s
  third value decides stays conditional. Its SQL renders `TRUE` wherever it sits inside a
  larger predicate.
- **`Repo::update`, `Repo::soft_delete` and `Creatable::create` refuse a caller no row could
  pass for** — no grant, a denial no `when` narrows, or a request holding no ability, as on a
  route gated on a `#[subject]`, whose ability `UnmaskedReply` withholds — with what a row
  outside its scope answers, `RecordNotUpdated` or `RecordNotInserted`, before the entity's
  `before_save` runs or a statement is sent. A job, which holds none, stays unscoped.
  `Repo::delete` runs no hook, and its statement still carries the denial in its `WHERE`.

**Breaking:** a `cannot` whose `when` holds for every row, beside a broader grant, refuses
its action with `403` where the request ran.

### An HTTP route no guard covers refuses the boot unless it says `#[public]`

A route with no guard — not the app's global pool, not a `#[use_guards]` on its
controller or itself — and no `#[public]` booted with one `warn`, `unguarded
routes detected`, and served every caller. The opening was an absence, where every
other edge refuses an operation that declares no posture at compile time, and a
feature's controllers needed their guards declared twice, once in `main` and once
on the provider, to stay closed in an app that forgot the first.

- **The boot refuses such a route**, naming every one in a single error —
  `GET /users (list), …` — with both remedies: bind a guard (`#[use_guards(...)]`
  on the controller or the route, or the app's global pool), or mark the route
  open on purpose with `#[public]`. A route declaring `#[authorize(A, E)]` and no
  guard is refused too: no guard attaches the ability its class gate reads, so it
  could only ever answer `500`.
- **A route a guard covers needs no posture of its own**: the guard decides, as
  a token endpoint behind its client-authentication guard does.
- Declaring the guards on the provider stays the convention, because it lets a
  feature travel to an app with no global pool; forgetting them there is now a
  failed start rather than an open route.
- `nestrs g http` and `g ws` bind `#[use_guards(AuthnGuard, AuthzGuard)]` over a
  plain `g feature` port too, import the transport's authz module, and scaffold
  the auth adapter when the workspace has none, as `g resource` does: a
  generated route or socket is guarded, and `#[public]` is a line you write.

**Breaking:** an app that booted with `unguarded routes detected` refuses to boot,
naming the routes. Bind a guard, or mark each route that is open on purpose
`#[public]`.

### `#[public]` means a caller with no credential is admitted, on every edge

On HTTP `#[public]` makes an authentication guard admit a visitor; on a WebSocket
message and an MCP operation it meant only "no gate and no mask", and every message
had to say it or not compile. So the demo's chat messages, behind an upgrade
`AuthnGuard` runs, read `#[public]`, `nestrs g ws` wrote it, and a reviewer grepping
for what is open read false openings — the mislabel the route posture was made to
refuse.

- **A WebSocket message and an MCP operation take HTTP's grammar.** One under its
  gateway's `#[use_guards]`, the app's pool or a guard of its own, and one under a
  gated endpoint the pool or `EndpointGuards` cover, needs no posture; `#[authorize]`
  adds the gate and the mask.
- **Breaking: a message no guard covers says `#[public]` or refuses the boot**, as a
  route does, naming every such message and each remedy. The compile error is gone,
  since `#[messages]` cannot see the app's pool, and the transport's `unguarded
  self-mount edges detected` warn with it: a gateway running no guard at its upgrade
  and with no `#[public]` message refuses too, whatever guards its messages bind, since
  any caller connects and its hooks run, and so does a hand-built `Posture::Gated`
  self-mount running no guard, which served every caller.
- **Breaking: `#[public]` that can open nothing refuses the boot**, on WebSockets and
  MCP: behind a guard declaring `GuardPhase::Authentication` or `Authorization` at the
  request carrying the unit, and on a gated endpoint denying every request. Behind a
  guard that identifies no one, a rate limit, it stays the opening it says.
- **A `#[public]` unit no ability reached reads the visitor's rules at its
  `AbilityGuard`**, on every edge, as a `#[public]` route does: a tool of an endpoint
  `McpAccess::Public` opens serves under the pool holding `AuthzGuard`, and a gated
  unit still fails closed.
- **An operation of an endpoint `McpAccess::Public` opens declares its posture or
  refuses the boot**, whatever guards stand beside it: no guard runs at its request,
  so the posture is what refuses a visitor. GraphQL keeps its compile error, for the
  same reason.
- `nestrs g ws` stops writing `#[public]`, the header `nestrs g mcp` writes says what
  the word means, and the demo's chat and notifications gateways drop it.

### A guard's `require` answers by cause, and a guard writes one decision for every edge

`cx.require::<T>()` answered a `500` wherever `T` was missing, so a guard reading the
principal on both a route and a GraphQL field wrote two bodies: `/graphql` admits a
visitor, and only the guard could tell that from a wiring gap.

- **The principal a guard declares through `Guard::expected_principal` answers by
  cause.** Absent at a unit it is a caller a guard attaching it admitted without one,
  the caller's `401` on every edge, filed at `warn` with `reason = "anonymous_caller"`.
  A credential a `#[public]` route set aside answers the `401` it earned, its RFC 6750
  `error` included, as an `Ambient` parameter does (`reason = "rejected_credential"`).
  Any other value's absence stays the wiring `500`.
- **Breaking: the boot refuses a chain nothing in which attaches that principal**, a
  route's, and at the wiring step a GraphQL field's, a message's and an MCP operation's
  with the request carrying them, read off `produced_principal`. An authentication
  guard of your own declares it beside a guard requiring its principal. A guard doing
  without the principal says `PrincipalClaim::of::<T>().or_visitor()`, as `AbilityGuard`
  does, and nothing attaching it stays a `warn`.
- The demo's `PostAuthorGuard` writes one `check` and declares
  `impl<V: UnitView> GuardFor<V>` once; a visitor's `publishPost` answers
  `UNAUTHENTICATED`, where it answered `FORBIDDEN`.
- **`PostAuthor` is gone from the demo, and the publication row leaves
  `insert_unscoped`** for `Creatable`: users are granted `Create` on the audit row with
  `actor_id` pinned to their own id, so the grant fills the actor and the re-check holds
  it, and no parameter threads it. Chat's user-only rule is the capability
  `#[subject] Posting`, granted under `sub.user_id()`, and `ModerationGuard` goes: a
  client's message answers `forbidden`, filed by the class gate.

### An MCP `#[resource]` binds its subject with `#[authorize(A, bind = S)]`

A resource reading one row hand-called `CrudService::access`, a runtime `Action` beside
the attribute's marker, and matched `Access` in three arms. `#[authorize(A, bind = S)]`
now hands its `Authorized<A, E>` parameter the row its template's one variable names,
through the load a route runs, after the class gate. A row the caller may not have,
one that does not exist and an id that is no v7 answer *Resource not found* with no
body run, the one answer the protocol's page already asked for. A template naming
several variables is refused at compile time, and a message, a tool, a prompt and a
listing keep refusing the key: they name no id. `S` joins the boot's access contract,
as on a route. A load the service could not run answers as a route's does, a statement
cancelled at its bound `UNAVAILABLE` at `warn`, as a GraphQL field's now does too, and
anything else opaquely, its chain filed once: the `by-id` line of `Unwired` on a route
is now one constant, `a bound subject cannot be loaded`, its `error` field saying why.

### A masked WebSocket reply is read by its type, not by the spelling of `Result`

`#[authorize(Action, Entity)]` on a message returning anything but a literal `Result`
did not compile, since the mask read the spelling. It now reads what the frame reads,
`ReplyValue`, so a bare value and a `Result` behind an alias mask what they answer, and
the refusal is gone. A mask's failure is an error frame whatever the method returns.
A reply that does not serialize is refused at the mask's bound.

### No guard admits a unit it never decided: `check` has no default

`Guard::check` admitted by default, and `check_http` runs it, so the two entries never
had to be written: an empty `impl Guard for X {}` passed every request and every unit,
and a guard declaring `GuardFor<GraphqlView>`, `GuardFor<WsView>` or `GuardFor<McpView>`
that wrote only `check_http` compiled, read as a protection beside a resolver, a message
or a tool, and admitted every one of them.

- **`check` is a required method.** A guard writes its decision in it — `check_http`
  still runs it on an HTTP request unless the guard overrides it — so a guard missing it
  is ``E0046: not all trait items implemented, missing: `check` ``, on every edge alike.
- **A guard whose decision is the request's alone says so**: `AuthnGuard` and
  `ClientAuthenticationGuard` admit, in `check`, the units the request they
  authenticated carries — the mirror of a unit-only guard admitting the request in
  `check_http`, through a zero-sized future, so the pool admits each in-band unit
  without allocating, as the default did.
- `GuardFor<V>`'s diagnostic says what the declaration now proves: that the guard's
  decision was written for `V`'s units.

**Breaking:** an `impl Guard` without `check` no longer compiles. An HTTP-only guard
bound on routes moves its `check_http` body into `check`, which `check_http` runs; one
in the global pool — its decision the request's alone, as an authentication guard's —
keeps `check_http` and writes
`async fn check(&self, _cx: &mut UnitContext<'_>) -> Result<(), Denial> { Ok(()) }`.
What fails if you get it wrong: an HTTP-reading `check` in the global pool runs at every
GraphQL field, WebSocket message and MCP operation, where `HttpView::of(cx)?` refuses each
with the opaque `500`, filed at `error` for every refusal and once per site.

### A route binds its subject with `#[authorize(A, bind = S)]`, and posture is never a parameter type

A route could declare who may call it through a parameter's type: a
`Bind<A, S>` parameter armed the response mask and authorized the row with no
`#[authorize]` beside it, so a search for the attribute missed the route, and a
hand-written `Authorize<A, E>` armed the class gate on any route. GraphQL
already took `#[authorize(A, bind = S)]` with an `Authorized<A, E>` parameter;
HTTP refused that grammar, and resolvers had a third spelling, the
`bind::<A, S>(ctx, &id)` helper.

- **One grammar on both edges.** `#[routes]` takes `#[authorize(A, bind = S)]`
  — the entity derived from `S::Entity`, or written beside the action. The
  attribute runs the class gate with the guards and arms the mask; the row the
  route's one path parameter names then loads through `S::access` (`400` for an
  id that is not a UUID v7, `404` absent, `403` denied) into the handler's
  `Authorized<A, E>` parameter, which carries the row and never the posture.
  `bind` with no such parameter, the parameter with no `bind`, two of them, a
  bound route whose path — its controller's included — names other than one
  parameter, and an entity written beside `bind` other than the service's, on
  either edge, fail to compile; the service is checked at boot as a
  `Scoped<S>` is.
- **A hand-written `Authorize<A, E>` is the public-reads spelling**, beside
  `#[public]` alone: elsewhere it is refused at compile time, and at boot when
  an alias hides its name.
- **`#[crud]` documents a generated operation**:
  `api(get(summary = "…", description = "…"))` gives a generated route the
  OpenAPI text a hand-written one carries in `#[api]`. An operation `ops`
  leaves out or the block writes by hand, or the key on a resolver, is refused
  by name, and so is `paginate` beside a hand-written list, on both edges: a
  key nothing generated reads is never dropped.
- **`#[crud]`'s by-id operations under a parameterised controller path fail
  to compile.** `get`, `update` and `delete` read the id from their route's one
  path parameter, so under `#[controller(path = "/orgs/{org}/users")]` each
  answered `500` on every request; `#[controller]` now counts its path's
  parameters for `#[routes]` and `#[crud]` to assert.
- The users exemplar drops its `get` and `user` overrides, which added nothing
  over `#[crud]` but their text, and the posts exemplar binds `publish` on both
  edges with the attribute. Sections above that name `Bind<A, S>` describe it
  before this change.

**Breaking:** `nest_rs::seaorm::Bind` is gone: declare
`#[authorize(A, bind = S)]` on the route and take the row as an
`Authorized<A, E>`. `nest_rs::seaorm::graphql::{bind, bind_required}` are gone:
declare `#[authorize(A, bind = S)]` on the operation. A hand-written
`Authorize<A, E>` on a route that is not `#[public]` is refused: declare
`#[authorize(A, E)]`. `nest_rs::authz::http::AbilityShaping` is private.
`paginate` on a `#[crud]` block writing its list by hand is refused: drop it.
A `#[crud]` block generating a by-id operation under a controller path naming
a parameter is refused: write those operations by hand, or mount the resource
under a path naming none.

### A secret is redacted by its type: `nest_rs::core::Secret`

Keeping a secret out of a log line was written by hand, type by type. Eight
framework types listed every field in a `Debug` of their own to print `<redacted>`
for one — `SeaOrmConfig`, `RedisConfig`, `StorageConfig`, `ThrottlerConfig`,
`TlsIdentity`, `RegisteredClient`, `AccessTokenResponse`, `SocialProfile` — and a
ninth printed `HttpTls`'s key by its size. `AuthnConfig`, `JwtKey`, `JwtOptions`,
`OAuthClientConfig`, `TokenSet` and the GitHub and Google configs kept their
secrets out by having no `Debug` at all, so no struct of an app holding one could
derive it. The demo's user entity restated its nine columns in two `Debug`
impls to hide its password hash, whose newtype serialized the raw hash whenever
the row did.

`nest_rs::core::Secret<T>` (new) holds a value no format prints: its `Debug`
writes `<redacted>`, it has no `Display`, it serializes as the string
`"<redacted>"`, and `expose_secret()` is the one read, at the call that needs the
value. It deserializes the value itself, so a configuration or a row decodes into
one; `==` between two byte-like secrets runs in constant time; a
`#[validate(length(…))]` rule judges the value and names the placeholder in its
refusal. Under `nest-rs-seaorm` it is a column type over its value's own, so an
entity writes `pub password_hash: Option<Secret<String>>` and derives `Debug`.
`#[expose]` refuses a `Secret` column at compile time, naming the field: a secret
never crosses the wire, and an input would write one from it. A hidden `Secret`
column is rebuilt for a masked reply from its value's placeholder — the empty
string for a `Secret<String>` — and a `Secret<Vec<u8>>` from its
`#[wire_default]`, without which `#[expose]` refuses the column; a hidden column
is never read off the wire, so a `"<redacted>"` a body carries is never read back.

Every framework secret is now one: `SeaOrmConfig::url` and `RedisConfig::url`,
which may carry a password; `StorageConfig::access_key` and `secret_key`;
`ThrottlerConfig::pseudonym_key`; `AuthnConfig::secret` and `private_key`, and
`JwtKey::Hmac` and `JwtKey::Pem::private_pem` behind them;
`OAuthClientConfig::client_secret` and the GitHub and Google configs' own;
`RegisteredClient::client_secret`; `AccessTokenResponse::access_token`;
`TokenSet::access_token`, `id_token` and `refresh_token`; `SocialProfile::email`
and `name`, personal data a provider reported; and the private keys inside
`TlsIdentity` and `HttpTls`. Each type holding one has a `Debug`, the ones
that had none included; where it also holds a URL — `OAuthClientConfig`'s
endpoints, `AuthnConfig::jwks_uri` and `JwtKey::Jwks`'s, the GitHub and Google
redirects — `Debug` shows it through `nest_rs::config::endpoint_shown`, its
userinfo and query left out. secrecy, the crate this would otherwise be, has
had no release since 0.10.3 (2024-10-09), past the twelve-month bar.

**Breaking:** each field above is `Secret<String>` (`Option<Secret<String>>` where it
was an `Option`): a literal still reads `"…".into()`, a `String` takes `.into()`,
and a read takes `.expose_secret()`. `SocialProfile::email.as_deref()` is
`email.as_ref().map(|e| e.expose_secret().as_str())`. A `custom(function = …)`
validation rule on such a field receives `&Secret<String>`. `#[expose]` on a field
whose type names `Secret` no longer compiles. A struct serialized with a `Secret` in
it writes `"<redacted>"`, never the value: a row or a config stored that way and
read back holds the placeholder.

### An MCP resource is declared under `#[tools]`, like a tool

A host serving resources had no decorator to declare them, so it wrote rmcp's
whole `ServerHandler` by hand — a `get_info` restating its capabilities,
`list_resources` and `read_resource` parsing its URIs, the refused row turned
into a `-32002` — and its tools and prompts moved under rmcp's own
`#[tool_router]`, where `#[tool(description = …)]` compiles with no posture: no
class gate, no mask, no layer. The demo's posts host was that shape, its
`list_posts` and `draft_follow_up` ungated.

`#[tools]` (changed) takes two more roles. `#[resource(template =
"post://{id}")]` (new) reads the URIs an RFC 6570 level-1 template writes: each
`{variable}` is a parameter of the method, percent-decoded and parsed with
`FromStr`, and `resources/templates/list` advertises the template with its
`name` (the method's unless stated), `title`, `description` (the doc comment
when none is stated) and the media type its answer is read as — `Json<T>` as
`application/json`, a `String` as `text/plain`, a `ReadResourceResult` as
built. Wrapped in `Option`, `None` is a resource the caller may not read.
`#[resources]` (new) answers `resources/list` with a `Vec<Resource>`, one per
host. Each is a site like a tool's — `#[authorize(Action, Entity)]` or
`#[public]`, guards, exception filters, interceptors, `Scoped<T>` — and the
mask reads a resource's `Option<Json<T>>`; a listing's entries are URIs and
names, so a gated `#[resources]` writes `unmasked`. The `resources` capability
is derived from the two roles. A URI is offered to the templates in declaration
order; one no template writes, a variable that does not parse and a `None` all
answer MCP's *Resource not found* under the `NOT_FOUND` class, quoting no URI.
Two hosts on one path reading one template fail the boot, as a tool name
claimed twice does. `McpOperationKind` gains `Resource` and `Resources`, and a
guard's `McpView` names a resource's template, never the values its URI
carries.

What `#[resource]` refuses at compile time: an operator or a modifier in the
template (`{+path}`, `{id:3}`), two variables with no literal between them, a
variable no parameter takes, a parameter no variable names, one wrapped in
`Parameters<…>` or a pipe as a tool's argument is, a template written twice in
one host, a second `#[resources]`, a listing beside no `#[resource]`, an answer
that is no resource's body, and a masked `ReadResourceResult`, bare or in its
`Option`. A host serving completion, logging levels, subscriptions or tasks
still writes rmcp's `ServerHandler` by hand.

**Breaking: the mask refuses at compile time what it could only fail at every
call.** A masked `#[prompt]`, whose messages are no row whatever type carries
them, and a masked tool answering `ContentBlock` or `InputRequiredResult`,
beside the `CallToolResult` and `CallToolResponse` it already refused, each
compiled, then answered every caller `-32603` and filed
`irreconcilable_wire_value`; each now does not compile, naming
`#[authorize(Action, Entity, unmasked)]`.

### A `Model` serializes nowhere: `#[expose]` writes the mask's round trip, and a hidden column it cannot rebuild does not compile

A response mask rebuilt each row through serde on the entity's `Model`, so every masked entity
wrote `model_attrs(derive(PartialEq, Serialize, Deserialize))` and its crate a `serde` line —
a decorator forcing a manifest line. A `Model` that serialized was then accepted by `Json(model)`
and `WsReply::reply(&model)`, its hidden columns (`role`, `deleted_at`) included, on a route no
mask shaped. A hidden column of a type with no placeholder — a tenant `Uuid`, a timestamp —
compiled, and every masked reply carrying its row answered `500`, although `#[expose]` saw the
field. And `Ability::mask` serialized the whole `Model`: under a grant with no `.fields`, its
value carried every hidden column but a `Secret`.

- **Breaking: the `Model` derives no serde.** `#[expose]` writes the round trip a mask reads a
  row through, column by column, each through its own serde impls, in a sealed seam of
  `nest-rs-authz`, and refuses a `Serialize` or `Deserialize` derived on the row — in `#[derive]`
  or sea-orm's `model_attrs` and `model_ex_attrs` — at the derive, naming the exposed type a reply
  answers and the inputs a write takes. Delete `model_attrs(derive(…, Serialize, Deserialize))`
  and the `use serde` beside it, and the features crate's `serde` line where nothing else names
  serde. A handler answering a raw `Model` no longer compiles: it answers the exposed type,
  `Json(User::from(&model))`. A serde impl `#[expose]` cannot read — written by hand, or derived
  above the decorator — is an opening of its own. `nestrs g entity` and `g resource` write no
  serde, and `g entity` adds no `serde` line.
- **Breaking: a hidden column with no placeholder is refused at the field**, naming
  `#[wire_default]`, `#[wire_default(expr)]` and `#[expose]` (a `Secret` the first two alone): a
  `String`, `Option`, `bool`, number or `Secret` of one takes its own; any other needs one. A
  hidden primary key is refused naming `#[expose]`: a masked reply reads a row's identity off the
  wire and never invents one.
- **Breaking: `nest_rs::authz::WireModelDefaults` is gone.** The round trip, the exposed keys and
  the columns the rebuild invents are `#[expose]`'s alone; `Ability::mask`, `mask_many`,
  `masked_reply` and every `#[authorize(Action, Entity)]` mask take an
  `#[expose]`d entity, and the rustc error says so.
- **Fixed: `Ability::mask` writes the exposed columns alone**, whatever the grant.
- **A hidden column is never read off the wire**: its placeholder stands, whatever the body
  carries under its key — a `"<redacted>"` a byte-like `Secret` could not read back included.
- **A hidden enum column needs no wire derive**: `#[wire_default(Role::User)]` names its
  placeholder, so neither `#[wire_enum]` nor `#[derive(Default)]` is there for the mask.
- **Faster: the mask cuts each wire row in place** to its exposed, granted keys rather than
  writing the rebuilt model again: on a 100-row list of a user-shaped entity in release, the
  mask's own cost went from about 79 µs to 41 µs, beside the 51 µs the shaper spends parsing and
  writing the body.

### A handler's `Result` is known by its type at every edge, never by its name

`#[operations]` took any return type whose name ended in `Result` for one, so a
query returning `SearchResult` — an ordinary `SimpleObject` — did not compile,
while a `Result` renamed on import (`use async_graphql::Result as GqlResult`) or
behind a type alias was taken for a value: its error reached the client as data
and its operation line filed `ok`. `#[routes]`' response shapers rewrote such a
`Result`'s error status into the success code, and `#[tools]` refused it as an
operation that cannot fail. Every edge now decides by type, through the kernel's
`nest_rs_core::Answer` probe (WebSocket already did, through `ReplyValue`): a
payload named `…Result` is a value, and a renamed `Result` fails like a spelled
one — on the line, to the client, with its error's own status. Two readings stay
spelled, each refused with a sentence at the return type: a `#[subscription]`
(async-graphql's derive reads the name), and a masked MCP operation, whose mask
unwraps `Result<Json<T>, …>`.

### WebSockets, GraphQL and MCP each dispatch one key to one method

- **WebSockets.** A `#[messages]` handler keeps every error type it had: the reply
  path reports an `Error` with its whole chain and anything else by its `Display`
  (`ReplyOutcome`, `ErrorReport`). `#[messages]` refuses a second method claiming
  one event or one connection hook, a method carrying two roles, an `#[on_connect]`
  returning a value, a generic method and a receiver other than `&self`. For code
  building replies by hand, `WsReply::from_handler_error` takes the error by value
  and `payload_error` the `serde_json::Error` it reports without its value (see
  *A logged error names every cause beneath it*); `WsScopeError` moves to
  `error.rs`, its path unchanged.
- **GraphQL.** A resolver's field name is written by the macro rather than left to
  async-graphql, by async-graphql's own rule — a new word after `_` and after a
  digit, so `get_2fa` is still served as `get2Fa` and the served SDL is unchanged —
  so two methods naming one field, `a1_b` beside `a_1b` included, are refused at
  compile time with both named. `#[dataloader]` carries a method's
  `#[cfg]` onto the loader it generates and calls the batch by its path, and a
  synchronous subscription or `#[entity]` resolver is accepted. **Fixed:**
  `#[operations]` keeps an `#[expect(…)]` written on a resolver method; it kept
  only `#[allow]`, so the lint the expectation answered fired anyway.
- **MCP: rmcp 3.1 → 3.8.** `ServerInfo` is `ServerConfig` upstream, and
  `nest_rs::mcp::ServerConfig` and `nest_rs::mcp::model::ServerCapabilities` name the two
  at the framework's surface, so the next upstream rename costs one line there rather than
  one per host. A project scaffolded from 6.1 resolves rmcp 3.8 today, whose
  deprecation of `ServerInfo` (since 3.4) turns a `#[tools]` expansion into a `-D warnings`
  failure; 7.0 is its fix. `#[tools]` refuses two methods claiming one tool or
  prompt name. A `#[tool]` or `#[prompt]` doc line written as
  `#[doc = include_str!(…)]` or `concat!(…)` is part of the description sent to
  the model; it was dropped, and a doc written only that way was refused as
  missing. A description that evaluates blank — a
  `#[doc = include_str!("tool.md")]` of an empty file, a `concat!("")`, a `description = CONST` holding only
  whitespace — fails the build with the sentence a missing description gets, as a
  blank literal already did: the compiler evaluates the check where only it can
  read the value.

### The kernel knows the unit a layer runs around: `UnitContext`, `Edge`, `Handler`, `Reflector`, `Posture`, `Peer`

Each edge described its unit its own way — `GraphqlOperationContext`,
`McpOperationContext`, a socket's `(&WsClient, &str, &Value)` — and only HTTP
could read a handler's metadata, through a `Reflector` that read the request's
extensions. The kernel now owns one vocabulary every edge and package composes on,
read off the unit the operation line already names:

- **`Edge`** — the closed edge vocabulary as an enum (`#[non_exhaustive]`):
  `Edge::ALL` in the vocabulary's order, `Edge::as_str` for the word a unit name,
  a span target and a config namespace spell it with. A declared
  `operation_log::Unit` keeps its edge: `nest_rs::http::unit::REQUEST.edge() ==
  Some(Edge::Http)`.
- **`Handler`** — one dispatch site as its decorators declared it, built once and
  shared by every unit the site serves: its unit, host type and name, method, the
  address a client uses, its `Posture`, and its `#[meta]` values on two levels,
  the host's and the method's, built on first read.
- **`Reflector`** (`nest_rs::core::Reflector`) reads a `Handler`: `get::<M>()`
  prefers the method's value over the host's (NestJS's `getAllAndOverride`),
  `get_all::<M>()` yields the method's then the host's, and `posture()` /
  `is_public()` read the declared `Posture` — `Public`, `Gated`, `InBand`
  (`/graphql`'s endpoint) or `System` (a job, a tick, a listener).
- **`UnitContext`** — what a layer knows about its unit: `unit()`, `edge()`
  (NestJS's `getType()`), `handler()`, `reflector()`, `peer()`, `deadline()`, and
  `switch_to::<V>()` / `switch_to_mut::<V>()` for the edge's own `UnitView`
  (NestJS's `switchToHttp()`). It is `Send`, so a layer holds it across an
  `.await`; it is never boxed and carries no container.
- **`Peer`** — who is on the other end, as far as the edge can tell: the client
  address its trusted proxies resolved, the connection a socket unit arrived on.

Nothing dispatches through these types yet: each edge adopts them as its layers
move onto the one pipeline, and `nest_rs::http::Reflector` keeps reading the
request until then.

**Breaking:** `nest_rs::core::operation_log::EDGES`, a `[&str; 7]`, is gone;
`Edge::ALL` replaces it, an enum the compiler holds. `unit!` refuses an edge
outside it in the same place, now naming `nest_rs_core::Edge::ALL`.

### No site runs without a layer it declares, and a guard chain cannot be misordered beside one route

- **`Layer::priority` orders within its site, never across.** The composed chain
  was sorted by priority as a whole, so a handler-scope guard at `-10` ran
  before the global authentication guard whose principal it reads. Each site is
  now ordered on its own, declaration order breaking ties, and the sites run
  global, then controller (or resolver, gateway, host), then handler.
- **A layer a site declares and no imported module provides is never skipped.**
  It was dropped from HTTP routes and GraphQL and MCP operations — the site ran
  without it — and panicked a WebSocket gateway's mount. Now, naming the layer
  and the site (`UnresolvedLayerError`: "`…` is declared on `…`, and no imported
  module provides it — import the module that provides it"):
  - a site composed at boot — a controller, a route, a gateway's upgrade and
    each of its messages — fails the boot;
  - a GraphQL or MCP operation, which composes its chain on its first call,
    refuses that call with an opaque internal error and files one `error` on
    `nest_rs::layers`, `a layer the site declares is provided by no imported
    module`, with `layer` and `site`.
  Through `App::builder()` the access graph already refuses such a declaration;
  this holds for every other composition too.
- **A route's whole guard chain is phase-validated.** Only the global and
  controller scopes were, so `#[use_guards(AuthzGuard, AuthnGuard)]` beside one
  handler booted and answered `500` on every request; such a route now fails the
  boot naming both guards and the route. A `#[force_guards]` replay is checked
  where its guard first runs, so re-running the pool's authentication guard
  beside a handler, after the authorization guard, still boots.
- **A global interceptor or exception filter no imported module provides fails
  the boot of every app.** The check ran only when HTTP configured, so a worker
  or scheduler app booted with the layer silently missing; it now runs at the
  kernel's wiring step.

**Breaking (behaviour):** a layer that relied on a negative priority to run
ahead of a broader site's layers now runs inside its own site; a non-HTTP app
with an unresolvable global interceptor, filter or exception filter, and an app
with a route whose handler scope lists authorization before authentication, no
longer boot. One signature changes: `nest_rs_guards::dispatch::ShapedRoute`, the
endpoint `#[routes]` wraps around a handler, gains a `Refused` variant — the
route whose chains did not compose — so an exhaustive `match` on it needs that
arm.

### A guard an operation composed goes with its app

Every GraphQL and MCP operation site kept its composed guard chain in a
`static`, so a guard's `Drop` never ran and whatever it held — a throttler
store's connection, an ability factory's dependencies — outlived its app; a
test process booting many apps kept every one. The container now holds the
composed chains and the site keeps a weak handle: dropping the app drops them.

### A GraphQL resolver no longer converts a foreign error with `?`

async-graphql's `custom-error-conversion` feature is on, which removes its blanket
`impl<T: Display> From<T> for async_graphql::Error`: with it, a `?` inside a resolver
body turned any error's `Display` into the client's message before the framework
could answer it opaquely. `From<&'static str>` and `From<String>` remain, and
`nest-rs-seaorm` converts `ServiceError` through its `ToProblem`.

```rust
// before — compiles, and ships the database's sentence
async fn user(&self) -> async_graphql::Result<User> { Ok(self.svc.find().await?) }
// after — return the error; its type decides what the client reads
async fn user(&self) -> Result<User, ServiceError> { self.svc.find().await }
```

**Breaking:** a resolver body `?`-converting a foreign error into
`async_graphql::Result` no longer compiles; nor does a resolver returning a
`Result` whose error is a `Display` type that is no `std::error::Error`, nor one
returning a `Result` alias over a foreign error (`type R<T> = Result<T, MyError>`,
which async-graphql reads as a value). The feature is not additive: an app linking
another async-graphql-based crate that relied on the blanket fails to build.

### One ambient context carries what a unit holds, and each value says how far it travels

What a unit of work carried for the code it calls lived in four task-locals across three
crates — the request context in `nest-rs-core`, the executor and its scope in
`nest-rs-database`, the ability in `nest-rs-authz` — each carried by hand wherever a unit
went on in another future, and the carriers disagreed. A streaming body re-entered the
request's trace and nothing else, so a service called from an `#[sse]` stream or a
download found no executor and no ability, and failed closed; a DataLoader batch needed
a `LoaderScope` listed by hand; a new value — a tenant, a deadline, a package's — needed
every edge changed. The kernel now holds one map, inside the request context's one
task-local, and every framework value and every package's value rides it.

- **`AmbientValue`** (`nest_rs::core`) is the visible line that lets a type travel:
  `impl AmbientValue for Locale {}`. Its `carry(self: Arc<Self>, into: Continuation)`
  says what crosses each place a unit continues — the value itself (the default,
  everywhere but a job's envelope), a narrower one, or nothing — and its
  `reenter(self: Arc<Self>)` what a captured value is where its capture is re-entered
  inside another unit (*No function hands a unit a wider ability or executor scope than it
  runs under*).
- **`Continuation`** names those places: `Body` (a streaming response polled after the
  handler returned), `Batch` (a DataLoader batch), `Task` (work handed to `TaskContext`),
  `Commit` (`after_commit` work and an emitted event's listeners), `Connection` (a
  socket's next messages, a graphql-ws operation) and `Envelope` (a job it pushes).
- **`ambient::<T>()`** reads the running unit's `T`; **`with_ambient(value, fut)`** runs
  `fut` with it added. Adding the value already carried installs nothing.
- **`TaskContext::capture(into)`** captures the span and what crosses `into`;
  `TaskContext::current()` is `capture(Continuation::Task)`.
- **`Deadline`** states when a unit must end, on tokio's clock: a `Batch` keeps it, every
  other continuation drops it.
- **`UnitContext::attach(value)`** records a value for the rest of the unit, installed
  once the running stage has passed, so a denied unit installs nothing;
  **`UnitContext::ambient::<T>()`** reads what this stage attached, else what the unit
  carries. No edge builds a `UnitContext` yet: they adopt it as their layers move onto
  the one pipeline.

Where each value goes, by its own `carry`:

- **The executor steps down to the pool** wherever the unit continues past its
  transaction — a streaming body (polled after the commit), a DataLoader batch (shared by
  the request's fields), after-commit work and listeners — and is **dropped** on a task
  handed to `TaskContext`, a socket's next message and a job's envelope, where a write
  opens a unit of its own. A transaction a caller opened itself names no pool, so work
  continuing past it runs with no executor.
- **The ability crosses everywhere but a job's envelope.** A job is system work.
- **A unit opened inside another** (a GraphQL field in its request, a WebSocket message
  in its connection) carries what its parent carries, and a batch captured inside it
  carries the request's values, never one field's.

What now carries it:

- **A streaming body a route returns** runs under what its handler ran under: the
  request's trace and actor, the pool rather than the route's transaction, every
  value a layer added, and the caller's ability — which a route no response mask
  shapes, an `#[sse]` stream among them, withholds: a read the mask would have
  scoped fails closed there, so a stream sending a user's own rows filters them
  in its handler.
- **A DataLoader batch** carries its request's trace, ability and pool with nothing
  bound for it, and runs in the request's span, not the field's that asked first
  (below).
- **A WebSocket gateway and a graphql-ws socket** carry what their upgrade carried into
  every hook, message and operation, never its scope or its transaction.
- **Work held for a commit, an emitted event's listeners among it,** runs under what
  its unit carried into the commit — the pool, the scope, the ability, every value that
  crosses — captured where the work was registered by the executor that holds it. A
  held listener used to see the emitter's trace, plus the executor and ability the
  first-party driver re-installed by hand. A listener with no commit to wait for runs
  inside its emitter, on the emitter's executor, as it did, and now reads every value
  the emitter carries.
- **A task spawned through `TaskContext::carry`** now carries the caller's ability, and
  still no executor.

**Breaking:** `nest_rs::core::RequestContinuation::current()` is gone: it cloned the
running context whole, and with every value in one map a whole clone would hold the
request's transaction. `RequestContinuation::capture(into)` replaces it, carrying what
crosses `into` and nothing else. `nest_rs::database`'s `current_executor()` and
`current_executor_scope()`, and `current_ability()` and `with_ability`, keep their names and
signatures; their values live in the kernel's map.

### One pipeline in core: interceptors and exception filters that name no edge

Interceptors, filters and exception filters were three crates typed over poem, so a
package could wrap an HTTP request and nothing else — not a GraphQL field, a socket
message, an MCP tool or a job — and the other edges refused their attributes by name.
`nest-rs-core` now holds one contract every edge dispatches a unit through: HTTP,
GraphQL, WebSocket, MCP, a job, a tick and a listener each run it (the sections
below), and the three 6.x crates are gone.

- **`Interceptor`** (`nest_rs::core`) runs around one unit on any edge:
  `async fn intercept(&self, cx: &mut UnitContext<'_>, next: Next<'_>) -> Outcome`.
  `Next::run` runs the rest of the unit once — it consumes the `Next`, so a second
  run does not compile — and what the interceptor attached before it is ambient inside
  it, and only there.
- **`Outcome`** is `Result<Settled, Failure>`. `Settled` proves the unit ran or was
  answered: only `Next::run` and an edge's view mint one, so an interceptor that
  neither runs the unit nor answers it does not compile. `Failure` carries the error
  with its whole chain — an `anyhow::Error` keeps the error it holds — `find::<E>()`
  reads any link, and `answered(reply)` records that a layer wrote the reply.
- **A failure stays a failure.** An interceptor answering `Ok` after the rest of the
  unit failed leaves the unit failed, the original error kept and marked answered,
  with one `warn` on `nest_rs::layers` naming the interceptor — so the unit's data
  rolls back whatever a layer told the client.
- **`ExceptionFilter`** answers a failed unit on any edge:
  `type Exception` is an error type found anywhere in the failure's chain — inside a
  `thiserror` enum's source, inside an `anyhow::Error` — or `dyn Error + Send + Sync`
  for every failure, and `catch` returns `Caught::Problem(problem)` (answered in the
  edge's standard form), `Caught::Answered(settled)` (written through the edge's view)
  or `Caught::Declined` (the next filter is tried). The method's filters are tried
  first, then the host's, then the app's; a filter whose exception the failure does
  not carry costs nothing. A guard's denial reaches none.
- **`#[interceptor(stage = unit)]`** (`nest_rs::core`) declares an interceptor a module
  brings around the guards of every unit: a singleton provider built like an
  `#[injectable]` one, so the unit runs the container's own instance, which can inject
  anything its module reaches, and which sees a denial too — an opening written in the
  source. A type also listed by the app runs once. `stage` takes `unit` alone; a bare
  `#[interceptor]` is refused (*An app-wide interceptor at the handler stage is the
  root's*), and so is `priority`: an interceptor orders itself within its stage through
  `Layer::priority`. The boot says each one it registers at `debug`.
- **`Layer::serves(unit)`** says whether a layer has work at a unit, read once when an
  app-wide list is composed for a site, so a layer serving one edge costs the others
  nothing; a layer a decorator binds at a site always runs there.
- **An interceptor or an exception filter is bound by its type**, the specs built for
  it the framework's own (*Tier 1 holds what a developer writes, each item at one
  path*).

The stage order, for every edge that dispatches through it: the unit stage, the guards,
the class gate, then — with what the guards attached made ambient — the exception
filters around the handler stage (the app's interceptors, the modules', the host's, the
method's, first listed outermost) and the handler. A site with nothing bound runs its
handler with no allocation, and one with guards alone runs them inline.

No edge dispatches through it yet: `#[routes]`, `#[operations]`, `#[messages]`,
`#[tools]` and the workers keep today's chains, the attribute `nest_rs::http::interceptor`
keeps mounting at the HTTP edge, and `use_interceptors_global`,
`use_exception_filters_global` and `use_filters_global` keep their HTTP-typed traits.
Each edge moves in its own change, and the three crates go once the last has.

### `#[meta]` on every host and handler, declared once per site

Only an HTTP route took `#[meta(EXPR)]`, and the route copied each value into
every request's extensions — a clone and an insert per value per request, read
back from the same bag guards write principals into, so a guard inserting a
value of that type shadowed the route's own.

- **Every pair takes `#[meta(EXPR)]` on the host and on each dispatched
  method**: `#[controller]`, `#[resolver]`, `#[gateway]` and `#[mcp]` on the
  struct; `#[processor]`, `#[scheduled]` and `#[listeners]` on the impl block
  they decorate; and every route, root field, message, connection hook, tool,
  prompt, job, trigger and listener. A read prefers the method's value of a
  type, and a host value of another type still reads through. Two values
  naming one type at one level are a compile error.
- **Each site declares one `Handler`**, built once: its unit, host, method,
  address and posture, and its values, built on the first read and kept for the
  process.
- **`cx.reflector()` reads the site's declaration** — a `nest_rs::core::Reflector`
  over the unit's `Handler`, on every edge: no value is cloned per request, and
  nothing a layer inserts into the request shadows it. `posture()` and
  `is_public()` read the declared posture; on a request no route declared (a
  self-mount) they read the posture its mount stamped, as before.
- **Every edge reads its site's declaration**: a route, a root field, a message,
  a hook, a tool, a prompt, a job, a trigger and a listener each keep their
  `#[meta]` with their site, and a guard, an interceptor or an exception filter
  there reads it (*A guard has two entries over the unit it checks*).
- A `#[field_resolver]` declares no `#[meta]`, and is refused one: it runs under
  the root field that produced its parent.

**Breaking:** a `#[meta(EXPR)]` value is `Send + Sync + 'static`, no longer
`Clone`, and `EXPR` is evaluated once per process, on the first read, where it
was evaluated at every mount. A guard that read a `#[meta]` type with
`req.extensions().get::<M>()` instead of `cx.reflector()` finds nothing, and
`#[public]` no longer inserts the `Public` marker into a routed request.

### Global pipes register beside their contract, and every edge resolves the pool once

`use_pipes_global` was imported from `nest_rs::guards`, an accident of a
dependency cycle, and the pool behind it was read from the container on every
GraphQL operation and every WebSocket message.

- **The pool lives in `nest_rs::pipes`**, beside `GlobalPipe`, and is declared
  as `GlobalPipes<(…)>` (*A suite boots the composition `main` runs*);
  `nest_rs::guards` and its prelude no longer name it.
- **Each edge resolves the pool once, when it mounts** — the HTTP route,
  `/graphql`'s POST, every WebSocket gateway — and folds the resolved list per
  request; a provider's first build moves from the first request to the boot.
  The pool is composed as every layer family is: a pipe listed twice runs once
  on a `/graphql` POST and on a WebSocket message, as it already did on an HTTP
  body, and `Layer::priority` breaks declaration-order ties there too; each
  graphql-ws `subscribe` folds its variables through it too.
- **A global pipe no imported module provides fails the boot of every app**, at
  the kernel's wiring step (`WiringFailedError`, "the `nest_rs::pipes::global`
  wiring failed", the pipe named in its cause). The check ran only when HTTP
  configured, so an app serving no HTTP booted with the pipe silently dropped.

**Breaking:** the import path above. The hidden slots the guards crate filled
for the other edges are gone (`nest_rs_graphql::__private::GraphqlVariablePipe`,
`nest_rs_ws::__private::{WsDataPipe, resolve_ws_data_pipe}`), with
`nest_rs::ws::WsDataFold`; `nest_rs::ws::gateway_endpoint`, the call
`#[messages]` mounts a gateway through, takes the container in place of the
socket context and the data pipe, and resolves both itself.

### `NoBearerChallenge` is an HTTP marker, and the global guard checks run in every app

- **`NoBearerChallenge` moves to `nest_rs::http::challenge`**, beside the
  `Bearer` challenge it opts a `401` out of. `nest-rs-oauth-server` no longer
  depends on `nest-rs-guards`: the marker was all it used.
- **A global guard no imported module provides, and a global pool listing an
  authorization guard before the authentication guard it reads, fail the boot of
  every app**, at the kernel's wiring step (`WiringFailedError`, "the
  `nest_rs::guards::global` wiring failed", the guards named in its cause). Both
  checks ran only when HTTP configured, so an app serving no HTTP booted past
  them.

**Breaking:** the import path above; and an app refusing a global guard now
fails with `WiringFailedError`, whose own sentence names the wiring — the guard
is in its cause, which `{err:#}` and the boot's report print.

### A guard has two entries over the unit it checks, and declares the units it checks

`Guard` had one method per edge — `check_http(&mut poem::Request)`,
`check_graphql(&GraphqlOperationContext)`, `check_ws_message(&WsClient, &str, &Value)`,
`check_mcp(&McpOperationContext)` — and four marker traits to say which a guard implemented.
It now has two, both over the unit being dispatched, a `UnitContext`:

- **`check_http`** runs on an HTTP request — a route, a WebSocket upgrade, the `/graphql` and
  `/mcp` requests — and reads it through `HttpView::of(cx)`: the request, head and body (set
  the body back for the handler after reading it), and the route it matched.
- **`check`** runs on every other edge's unit — a GraphQL field, a WebSocket message, an MCP
  operation — and reads it through its edge's view: `GraphqlView` (the field, its
  operation, its role), `WsView` (the event, its data, the client), `McpView` (the method,
  the tool or prompt, the host).

On every edge the context answers the same questions: `cx.reflector()` reads what the site
declared — so `#[meta(Throttle::per_minute(1))]` beside a `#[query]`, a
`#[subscribe_message]` or a `#[tool]` now limits it, where only a route's `#[meta]` was read
before — `cx.ambient::<T>()` reads what an earlier guard attached, and `cx.attach(value)`
hands a value to everything after the guard. What a guard attaches is ambient for the rest
of the unit once its whole stage passed: a handler on every edge reads it with `Ambient<T>`
([A handler reads what a guard attached](#a-handler-reads-what-a-guard-attached-as-ambientt-on-every-edge-and-forward_principal-is-gone)).
`check` has no default, and `check_http` runs it unless a guard overrides it
([A guard writes its decision once](#a-guard-writes-its-decision-once-derives-its-layer-and-the-edge-files-its-denials),
[No guard admits a unit it never decided](#no-guard-admits-a-unit-it-never-decided-check-has-no-default)).

`GuardFor<V>` replaces `HttpGuard`, `GraphqlGuard`, `WsGuard` and `McpGuard`: a decorator
binding a guard requires `GuardFor` of the view its site dispatches, and the refusal names
the view. A guard whose one `check` serves every edge declares `impl<V: UnitView>
GuardFor<V>` once; `ThrottlerGuard` does, so `nest-rs-throttler` loses its `graphql`, `mcp`
and `ws` features.

`nest-rs-guards` now depends on `nest-rs-core` alone — no `poem`, no `nest-rs-http`, no edge
behind a feature: each edge runs its own guard stage and owns its view.

Every denial is filed where it happens, the `/graphql` and `/mcp` requests' guard stages
included, which filed none: one line on the guard's target naming the guard and the status.
A guard that could not evaluate the unit says what failed with
`Denial::unavailable(retry_after, reason).with_source(source)`: the line carries the whole
chain as its `error` field, while the client reads the condition alone. The source keeps
each link as the type it is, an `anyhow::Error` unwrapped, so a driver's `SafeLink`
still says its own error without the value it quotes, as `AuthError`, `ServiceError`
and an `#[on_event]` listener's `Err` do. `Denial` implements `Display` and `Error`.

**Breaking:** a guard's entries change signature, the markers become `GuardFor<View>`, and a
value a guard hands on is attached rather than inserted into the request, so its type
implements `AmbientValue` — a `Strategy::Principal` and an `AbilityFactory::Actor` included,
which is why `PrincipalIdentity` is no longer implemented for `()` and `&'static str`.
`Reflector`, `HandlerMetadata`, `GraphqlOperationContext`, `McpOperationContext`,
`EdgePosture` and the prelude's `HttpRequest` are gone; a self-mount declares its posture
with the `Handler` the framework's decorator or module passes to `HttpEndpointMeta::new`,
and `HttpEndpointMeta::{exempt, posture}` go. `Ambient<T>`, HTTP's `Ctx<T>` before, reads
what is ambient and nothing else, so a value an interceptor, a middleware or a test put in the request's extensions is
made ambient instead (`nest_rs::core::with_ambient`). `Denial`'s variants become private
fields: build an unavailable one with `Denial::unavailable`, and its cause with
`.with_source(source)`. `ClientOrigin::of` takes `&nest_rs::http::Request`. The denial
renderers move to their edges under their names — `nest_rs::graphql::denial_to_graphql_error`,
`nest_rs::ws::denial_to_ws_error`, `nest_rs::mcp::denial_to_mcp_error` — and HTTP's two
leave the public API, since they answer poem's types. What the decorators' expansions
called leaves it too: `nest_rs_guards::{GuardEndpoint, GuardExt, GuardAsWsMessageCheck,
dispatch, layer_chain, RouteShaper, GraphqlSite, run_layered_graphql_chain,
run_layered_mcp_chain}` (`layer_chain` stays at `nest_rs::core::layer_chain`),
`nest_rs_http::{GlobalGuardsActive, Public}`, `nest_rs_ws::{WsMessageCheck,
EventLayerTable, gateway_endpoint}`, `nest_rs_graphql::GraphqlFederationGuard` and
`nest_rs_authz::run_ability_chain`. Every row is in
[Upgrading](https://nestrs.dev/upgrading/).

### A guard writes its decision once, derives its `Layer`, and the edge files its denials

A guard wrote its decision twice: `check_http` and `check` both admitted by default, so a
guard declaring `GuardFor<HttpView>` that wrote only `check` let every HTTP request
through, and copies of one decision drifted. Every layer wrote an empty
`impl Layer for X {}` beside a `#[derive(Default)]` its fieldless struct had no use for,
and a guard filing its own denial lines kept `files_its_denials() -> true` in sync by hand
with a `warn!` before each `Err` — one line missed, a denial went unseen; one too many, it
was filed twice.

- **One decision.** `check_http` runs `check` unless the guard overrides it, so a guard
  writes its decision once and it runs on every unit and every request it is bound at. A
  guard overrides `check_http` only where the request differs from the unit: it reads the
  request itself, answers in its protocol's own words, or a value missing at the request
  means something else than at the unit (the demo's `PostAuthorGuard`).
- **The edge files every denial, once, on the guard's target.** `Guard::target` names the
  target (`nest_rs::layers` by default) and the denial says why —
  `Denial::forbidden("…").with_reason("banned_author")`, `.with_source(err)` to keep what
  failed. Every edge files one `guard denied the request` (`… the operation`, `… the
  federation field`, `… the message`) at `warn`, at `error` for a `500`, with one field
  set: `guard`, `unit`, `host`, `address`, `conn_id`, `status`, `reason`, `retry_after` and,
  when the denial keeps a source, `error`, the whole chain from the denial's own text down —
  never that text alone, which the client reads or which may name what no line carries.
  The framework's guards moved: `AuthnGuard` and `ClientAuthenticationGuard` on their
  crates' targets with the strategy's reason (the token endpoint's cause naming the method
  attempted), `AbilityGuard` on `nest_rs::authz`, `ThrottlerGuard` on `nest_rs::throttler`
  with `limit_exceeded`, `store_failed`, `store_timeout` or `store_panicked`, the store
  named in the cause and never the key. A strategy or a store that panics keeps its own
  panic line beside the refusal's.
- **A precondition is required, not decided.** `cx.require::<Claims>()?` reads what an
  earlier guard attached; its absence answers `MissingAmbientError`, a `500` the edge
  files once at `error` naming the guard and the type. A value whose absence is
  legitimate is still read with `cx.ambient::<T>()` and decided.
- **`#[derive(Layer)]`** writes the `Layer` impl, `#[layer(priority = …)]` its priority;
  a layer choosing the units it serves keeps its hand-written impl. `#[injectable]` builds
  a braced fieldless struct as written, as it already built a unit one: no `Default`.

**Breaking:** `Guard::files_its_denials` and `Denial::unavailable_because` are removed
(`Denial::unavailable(…).with_source(…)`), and `Denial` is a struct whose fields are
private: build it with its constructors, tell denials apart with `http_status()` and
