`bearer_error()`. A guard writing only `check` now runs it on HTTP requests too, so one
in the global pool runs at every route, the WebSocket upgrade and the `/graphql` and
`/mcp` requests, failing closed where it reads a view the request does not have: one
whose decision is a unit's alone admits the request in `check_http`. Every guard denial's
line is the edge's, on the guard's target, with the field set above: a query matching
`rate limit exceeded`, `authentication failed`, `client authentication failed`, `ability
guard denied: …`, `throttler store failed; …` or `throttler store did not answer …`
matches `guard denied the …` and its `reason` instead, and one reading `route`, `field`,
`operation`, `event`, `transport` or `connection` reads `address`, `unit` or `conn_id`.

### A handler reads what a guard attached as `Ambient<T>` on every edge, and `forward_principal!` is gone

What a unit's guards attach is ambient for the rest of it, yet a handler read it four
ways: `Ctx<T>` as an HTTP parameter, `ctx.data::<T>()` in a resolver once some module
wrote `forward_principal!(T)` — a line every app with GraphQL and a principal repeated,
and which reached the principal alone — `nest_rs::core::ambient::<T>()` in a message
handler or a tool, and `cx.ambient::<T>()` in a layer. A value a guard bound on a
field attached never reached the resolver, so the demo's `publish_post` derived its
author again from the claims, behind a hand-written `async_graphql::Error` naming the
guard to the client.

- **HTTP's `Ctx<T>` is core's `nest_rs::core::Ambient<T>`, one parameter on every
  edge**, named for the ambient context it reads, beside `AmbientValue`, `ambient()` and
  `cx.ambient()`, so one concept has one word. A route reads it as an extractor, by its type, as before — the `401` an authentication
  guard deferred on a `#[public]` route included. `#[operations]` (root fields and
  `#[field_resolver]`s), `#[messages]` and `#[tools]` (a tool, a prompt, a resource and
  the listing) read it by the last segment of its type, as they read `Scoped<T>`, and
  resolve it in the tail once the guards and the class gate passed, through a function
  returning core's own `Ambient`, so a namesake does not compile; none of them lists it among
  the arguments its protocol describes — no schema argument, no payload, no tool input.
- **An absent value fails the unit, never the client's request**: the guard that
  attaches it did not run there. Each in-band edge answers its opaque internal error
  and files the chain at `error`, naming the type (`MissingAmbientError`, what
  `cx.require::<T>()` answers a guard), as a route answers its `500`.
- **`forward_principal!` is removed**: a resolver takes `claims: Ambient<Claims>`. The gate
  is unchanged — a value is there only once the module-gated guard that produces it ran
  — and `GraphqlContextSeed` stays for the framework's own seeds. `nestrs g graphql`
  writes `AuthzGraphqlModule` without the line.
- **The demo reads every guard's value as a parameter**: the chat gateway's `message`
  and `typing` take the author as `caller: Ambient<Claims>`, and the GraphQL
  `publish_post` answers `Result<Post, PostError>`, the error's own `ToProblem` its
  `CONFLICT`.

**Breaking:** `nest_rs::http::Ctx` is `nest_rs::core::Ambient` (the prelude carries
it); `nest_rs::graphql::forward_principal!` is removed, with its forwarded
`ctx.data::<T>()` — a resolver reading one takes an `Ambient<T>` parameter instead. A
resolver, message handler or tool parameter whose type's last segment is `Ambient` is now
read as `nest_rs::core::Ambient`, never as an argument of the protocol.

### One layer grammar for every decorator pair

Each edge's decorators parsed their layer attributes by hand, and refused the
ones they did not run with one shared "HTTP-only" sentence, or not at all: a
`#[use_guards]` beside a lifecycle hook or a health probe, a `#[force_guards]`
on a controller struct, a `#[use_interceptors]` on a `#[routes]` block all
reached rustc as `cannot find attribute`, the framework's own vocabulary
presented as unknown.

- **One closed table, `nest_rs_codegen::layers`, answers every layer attribute
  at every edge pair, `#[hooks]` and `#[indicators]`**, on the host and on a
  method: `#[use_guards]`, `#[force_guards]`, `#[use_pipes]`,
  `#[use_interceptors]`, `#[use_exception_filters]`, `#[use_filters]` and
  `#[meta]`. Each cell is taken, *not yet* run by its edge — a compile error
  saying it would be a silent no-op — or refused with the fact that makes it
  meaningless. The decorators page carries the table. `#[processor]`,
  `#[scheduled]` and `#[listeners]` read their rows too (*Interceptors and
  exception filters run around a job, a tick and a listener call*); the
  WebSocket `#[on_connect]` / `#[on_disconnect]` hooks read `#[meta]`, refuse
  `#[use_guards]` and `#[use_pipes]`, and refuse interceptors and exception
  filters as cells their edge does not run (*A connection hook has a deadline
  of its own*).
- **New named refusals**: every layer attribute and `#[meta]` beside `#[hooks]`
  ("a lifecycle hook has no caller and no site") and `#[indicators]` ("an
  indicator runs inside the health endpoint, whose layers are the endpoint's");
  `#[use_pipes]` on a GraphQL resolver or operation (bind `Piped<P>` on the
  argument; a type declaring rules is validated there unasked) and on an MCP
  host or tool (inside `Parameters<…>`); `#[use_filters]` at GraphQL, WebSockets
  and MCP, naming `#[use_exception_filters]`, whose `dyn Error + Send + Sync`
  catch replaces it; `#[force_guards]` on a host struct; any layer attribute
  on an edge's operations block, naming the struct it belongs on.
- **`#[force_guards(X)]` alone binds `X` on its method and re-runs it.** It
  replayed only a guard the method also listed in `#[use_guards]`, so the
  documented spelling — the attribute alone — ran the guard once, as if it were
  not there. Listing a guard in both is now refused: `#[force_guards]` says
  both things.

**Breaking:** a guard listed by both `#[use_guards]` and `#[force_guards]` on
one method is a compile error; drop it from `#[use_guards]`. `#[force_guards]`
alone now runs its guard a second time where it ran once. The refusal
sentences of the layer attributes changed wording (they are compile errors
either way).

### A global pipe has one entry, handed the unit and the kind of input

`GlobalPipe` had a method per edge — `transform_body`, `transform_graphql_variables`,
`transform_ws_data` — which no package could extend, and none told a pipe which
route, operation or message it was shaping.

- **`GlobalPipe::transform(&self, cx: &UnitContext<'_>, argument: ArgumentKind,
  input: &mut Value)`** is the one entry, defaulting to leaving the input
  unchanged. `nest_rs::pipes::ArgumentKind` (`Body`, `Variables`, `Data`,
  `#[non_exhaustive]`) says which input it is, read off NestJS's
  `ArgumentMetadata.type`; `cx` is the unit's, as a guard's is — `cx.edge()`,
  `cx.handler()` and its `Reflector`, and the edge's view (`HttpView` for a body
  and for `/graphql` variables, `WsView` for a message's `data`).
- **An operation sent over the graphql-ws socket, a subscription included, runs
  the global pipes over its variables**, as a `/graphql` POST does. It ran none,
  so a validation the pool held was skipped by sending the operation over the
  socket. A refusal answers the protocol's `error` for that operation, which
  never starts; the socket goes on. The view is the head of the request that
  opened the socket.
- A WebSocket message whose event no handler serves meets no global pipe.

**Breaking:** the three methods are gone; a pipe implements `transform` and
matches on `argument`.

### A value breaking its type's rules answers `400` whoever checks it, and a validation entry carries no `null`

The edge refused a value breaking its type's rules with `400`, while a service's
`ServiceError::Validation` answered `422`: one mistake, two statuses, by who checked.
**Breaking: `ServiceError::Validation` answers `400`**, as the edge's own check does
— RFC 9110 §15.5.1's client error, true for a body, a path, a query and a header
alike; `422` stays `ServiceError::Invalid`'s, a well-formed value the service refuses
on business grounds. Both keep `INVALID_ARGUMENT` and the `errors` member. A
validation entry now carries the `message` its rule declares, or none, never
`"message": null`, on every edge.

What fails if you get it wrong: a client keying on `422` for a validation failure a
service raised reads `400`, with the same code and `errors`.

### Every edge validates what it decodes, with no wrapper, and a pipe is one carrier

Validation was a pipe each parameter had to remember: `Valid<Json<T>>` on HTTP,
`Valid<T>` on a GraphQL argument, a WebSocket payload and a job,
`Parameters<Valid<T>>` on MCP. A forgotten wrapper compiled and skipped the rules
the type declared — a create written by hand over a bare `CreateUser` stored the
empty name or the malformed email `#[crud]`'s generated one refused — and `Valid`
and `Piped` each named two types, `nest_rs::http::Valid<E>` and
`nest_rs::pipes::Valid<T>`.

- **Every edge holds each argument it decodes to the `validator` rules its type
  declares**, after the guards and the class gate and before any pipe runs over
  it: a route's extractors (`Json`, `Query`, `Form`, `Path` and `Header` each
  validate as their value does), a GraphQL operation's and a field resolver's
  arguments, a WebSocket payload, an MCP tool's or prompt's `Parameters<T>` and a
  resource's template variables, a job. An `Option` is validated when it holds a
  value, a list item by item; a type declaring no rules costs nothing, the check
  resolving at compile time. A refusal is the edge's pipe refusal, unchanged:
  `400 validation failed` and its `errors` on HTTP, a field error under
  `INVALID_ARGUMENT`, an error frame, `invalid_params`, a job dead-lettered at
  once. `#[crud]`'s create and update take a bare `Json<Create<E>>` /
  `Create<E>`, checked like any other argument.
- **`Piped<P, S = Decoded<P::In>>`, in `nest_rs::pipes`, is the one carrier.** A
  route names the extractor the pipe reads (`Piped<ParseUuid, Path<String>>`); an
  edge that decodes its argument itself writes `Piped<P>`, its wire value the
  pipe's `In`; `Piped(value)` binds the pipe's output on every edge. An extractor
  of your own is a `nest_rs::pipes::Source`. `Piped<P>` on a route is a compile
  error naming the spelling a route takes. `Piped` is no extractor: `#[routes]`
  reads it where the signature spells it out, so one behind a type alias is a
  compile error naming `FromRequest` rather than a parameter skipping its
  source's rules, and a route registered on `HttpRoutes` by hand extracts the
  source, then calls `Piped::<P, E>::apply`.
- **`PipeError::from(ValidationErrors)`** (new) is how a hand-written check
  answers as an edge does: `value.validate().map_err(PipeError::from)`, the
  field map without the submitted values.
- **`#[output]`** (new, `nest_rs::core` and the prelude) derives `Serialize` and
  `JsonSchema` for a DTO an edge only sends; `#[input]` keeps `Deserialize`,
  `Validate` and `deny_unknown_fields` for what an edge reads. The demo's replies
  (`PersonDto`, `PresignedUrlDto`, `TranscodeProgressDto`, `TranscodeState`) take
  it.

**Breaking:** `nest_rs::http::{Valid, Piped, IntoInner}`, `nest_rs::pipes::{Valid,
ValidationPipe}` and `nest_rs::mcp::{Valid, Piped}` are gone: drop the `Valid`
wrapper, import `Piped` from `nest_rs::pipes` and write `Piped<P>` off HTTP, and
implement `Source` where an extractor implemented `IntoInner`. A value breaking
its type's rules that no `Valid` wrapped is now refused at its edge, a service's
own `ServiceError::Validation` (`422`) giving way to the edge's `400` for an
input the edge decoded. [Upgrading](https://nestrs.dev/upgrading/), steps 6, 12
and 13.

### `#[authorize]` is one class gate on every edge

`#[authorize(Action, Entity)]` decided through four functions — HTTP's `Authorize`
extractor, `nest_rs::authz::graphql::authorize`, `ws::authorize` and `mcp::authorize` —
which disagreed on what a refusal said, and three of them existed only behind their edge's
feature, so a package's own decorator could not emit the posture's gate. There is now one,
`nest_rs::authz::class_gate::<Action, Entity>`, ungated by any feature: every decorator
emits it — `#[routes]`, both `#[crud]`s, `#[operations]`, `#[messages]`, `#[tools]` — and
each edge runs it on the unit right after its guards, before any pipe, interceptor or
filter. It coerces to the pipeline's class gate, so a package's decorator emits it for its
own posture.

- **One refusal, one sentence, on every edge.** No grant is `forbidden`, no principal is
  `unauthenticated`, and a grant the token's scopes withhold is RFC 6750 §3.1's own words,
  `the request requires higher privileges than provided by the access token`, where HTTP
  answered `forbidden`, GraphQL `insufficient_scope` and WS and MCP `insufficient scope`.
  The codes are unchanged.
- **Off a `#[public]` site, an anonymous caller is refused on HTTP too**, `401`, whatever the
  visitor's rules grant, as GraphQL, WS and MCP already refused it: a grant written for
  public reads never satisfies an `#[authorize]`. A hand-written `Authorize<A, E>` on a
  `#[public]` route still reads the visitor's grant.
- **A unit no ability reached** — no ability guard ran — is refused opaquely on every edge,
  and files one `error` on `nest_rs::authz`, `authorization denied`, with
  `reason="no_ambient_ability"`, the site's `host` and `method` and the remedy. GraphQL
  told the client to install a bridge on `/graphql`; HTTP filed `missing request Ability —
  route is authorized but no ability guard ran`. Every `authorization denied` line carries
  the site's `host` and `method`.

**Breaking:** `nest_rs::authz::{gate, GateVerdict}` and `nest_rs::authz::{graphql, ws,
mcp}::authorize` are gone; `class_gate` replaces them. `RouteResponseShaper::GATE` is the
pipeline's class gate over the unit's context, no longer a function of poem's request.
`nest-rs-authz` depends on `nest-rs-guards` whatever its features.

### `/graphql` and `/mcp` take their guards from the modules that declare them

`/graphql` and `/mcp` ran their request's guards inside the endpoint, through an
operation guard a module bound as `dyn GraphqlOperationGuard` or `dyn McpOperationGuard`
— a bridge type per guard pair, `GraphqlAbilityBridge<A, G>` and `McpAbilityBridge<A, G>` —
with the global pool run around it by a runner of each edge's own. A module now declares
the guards an endpoint's request runs, as a provider:

```rust
#[module(
    imports = [AuthzModule],
    providers = [EndpointGuards<McpView, (AuthnGuard, AuthzGuard)>],
)]
pub struct AuthzMcpModule;
```

`nest_rs::http::EndpointGuards<V, (G1, …)>` names the endpoint by the view of the units its
request carries, `GraphqlView` or `McpView`, and lists up to eight guards checking an HTTP
request. The HTTP transport composes every self-mount's request stage in one place — the
global pool, a gateway's own guards, the declared guards — and runs it in front of the
endpoint, so no edge runs a request guard of its own.

- **The pool still always runs**, and the declared guards join it where it lists the first
  one they name, else after it; each guard runs once, and the same guards declared by two
  modules run once, in import order. A declaration is a provider, so a module the app does
  not import declares nothing — and an app with no global pool, an assistant serving `/mcp`
  alone, is authenticated by the feature module it imports.
- **A declaration reaches an endpoint, or nothing builds.** `V` is a
  `nest_rs::http::CarriedView`, a view whose units an endpoint's request carries —
  `GraphqlView`, `McpView`, a package's own, which its self-mount declares with
  `HttpEndpointMeta::carries::<V>()` — so `EndpointGuards<WsView, _>` does not compile: a
  gateway's guards are its `#[use_guards]`. A declaration for an endpoint the app does not
  mount — `EndpointGuards<GraphqlView, _>` with no `GraphqlModule` imported — fails the boot
  naming it, since its guards would run nowhere.
- **The stage is phase-checked at boot**, as a route's chain is: endpoint guards that
  authorize before they authenticate fail the boot naming both, where they refused every
  caller.
- **`/mcp` stays deny-all with no guard at its request**, now said once at boot on
  `nest_rs::layers` — `a gated endpoint runs no guard at its request; it denies every
  request` (`path`, `kind`, `units`, `hint`) — and once per refused request, `request
  denied: the endpoint runs no guard at its request`. It is opened in code alone:
  `McpModule::for_root(McpAccess::Public)` runs
  no guard at its request, the global pool's included, and a module declaring guards for it
  beside that opening fails the boot naming the declaration rather than seeing them dropped.
- **A browser origin is checked before any guard** on a gateway's upgrade, where it was
  checked after the gateway's guards, as it already was on the graphql-ws upgrade and
  `/mcp`: a refused origin never pays for authentication. The `403` and the `browser origin
  refused` line are unchanged.
- **`/graphql` with no guard at its request** says so at `warn`, `no guard runs at the
  /graphql request — graphql operations run unguarded`; with guards, `graphql operations
  gated` at `debug` says whether the pool, a declaration or both run there.

**Breaking:** `GraphqlOperationGuard`, `McpOperationGuard`, `AllowAllMcpGuard`,
`nest_rs::authz::graphql::GraphqlAbilityBridge`, `nest_rs::authz::mcp::McpAbilityBridge`
and `nest_rs::mcp::resolve_operation_guard` are gone; the opening is
`McpModule::for_root(McpAccess::Public)`, gated by default. `nest_rs::mcp::{McpMount, endpoint}` move to
`__private`, and `McpMount` takes no guard: `McpMount::deny_all()` is `McpMount::new()`.
`HttpEndpointMeta::{self_guarded, self_guarded_if, guarded_at_the_edge,
edge_access_is_implicit}` and `nest_rs_http::__private::guards_in_band` are replaced by
`HttpEndpointMeta::carries::<V>()` and the request stage the transport composes. The MCP
boot line `mcp operations gated` and its `mode` field are gone. The test harness's
`app.graphql_socket().data(ability)` no longer reaches an `#[authorize]` subscription,
whose gate reads the ambient ability: `ability(ability)` gives the socket what an upgrade's
ability guard attaches, ambient for every operation and forwarded into the context by the
app's seeds.

### HTTP runs on the one pipeline: interceptors and exception filters are core's

Every HTTP unit — a route, a self-mount's request (`/graphql`, `/mcp`, a
WebSocket upgrade, the OpenAPI document), and what the router answers itself
(a `404`, a `405`, a module's fallback) — now runs through the pipeline
`nest_rs::core` declares for every edge, inside the request's unit of work:

1. the unit stage — `#[interceptor(stage = unit)]`;
2. the guard stage — the global, controller and method guards, then the
   route's `#[authorize]` class gate;
3. the exception filters — the method's, the controller's, then the app's;
4. the handler stage — the app's global interceptors, the controller's, the
   method's;
5. the handler — the global pipes over the body, the extractors, the handler,
   the response mask.

A self-mount's request runs the first three, its guard stage the request stage
the transport composes; what the router answers itself — a version the header
or media-type strategy refuses included — runs the unit stage and the
exception filters alone. A route a controller registers on `HttpRoutes` by
hand, outside `#[routes]`, is a route all the same: the app's guard pool, its
exception filters and its interceptors run around it, inside the request's
unit of work.

- **Interceptors are `nest_rs::core::Interceptor`**:
  `async fn intercept(&self, cx: &mut UnitContext<'_>, next: Next<'_>) -> Outcome`,
  the request through `HttpView::of(cx)?.request()`, the reply through
  `response_mut()` once `next.run(cx)` returned, or an answer of its own
  through `respond(..)`. `GlobalInterceptors<(…)>` runs one at the handler stage;
  `#[nest_rs::core::interceptor(stage = unit)]` brings one with its module's import,
  around the guards, on every HTTP unit, refusals and `404`s included.
- **Exception filters are `nest_rs::core::ExceptionFilter`**:
  `async fn catch(&self, err: &Self::Exception, cx: &mut UnitContext<'_>) -> Caught`
  — `Answered`, `Problem` or `Declined` — matching its type anywhere in the
  failure's source chain; `type Exception = dyn Error + Send + Sync` catches
  every failure. A global one now shapes a `404`, a `405` and a self-mount's
  own error too, and the closest scope is tried first. It reads the request
  as a guard does, its headers and extensions whole — the old `Filter`'s
  `RequestSnapshot` is the view's own request — and so does an interceptor
  once `next.run(cx)` returned.
- **What a failure's answer keeps from the client is filed once, by the
  status sent**: a failure an exception filter answers `4xx` files nothing,
  and one answered `5xx` — the filter's reply or its `Problem` — files the
  chain once, at `error`, at `warn` behind a `503`.
- **`App::builder().use_interceptors_global(..)` and
  `.use_exception_filters_global(..)` are `AppBuilder`'s own methods**, taking
  `nest_rs::core::{interceptor, exception_filter}` specs; no extension trait
  to import.
- **A failed unit never commits, answered or not.** A handler's error, or a
  `4xx`/`5xx` it answered, rolls a mutating request back, whatever status an
  exception filter or an interceptor answered — a `200` included.
- **A route refused at boot names itself whole**: a layer no imported module
  provides, or guards out of phase order, fail `configure` naming the route's
  method, its served path and its controller.
- **Every HTTP request is a unit of work of the edge's own.** With
  `SeaOrmDatabaseModule` imported, the transport resolves the binding once at
  mount and runs each request through it, around the guards: a transaction on
  a mutating route, the pool on a safe one and on a `/graphql` or `/mcp`
  request; the router's own answers open none.

**Breaking:**

- A global interceptor — 6.x's `use_interceptors_global` or a module's
  `#[interceptor]`, now `GlobalInterceptors<(…)>` — runs after the guards, around a
  route's handler: it no longer sees a `404`, a `405`, a refusal, or a self-mount's
  request. One that must is declared `#[interceptor(stage = unit)]`.
- `#[use_interceptors]` and `#[use_exception_filters]` take core's
  `Interceptor` and `ExceptionFilter`; the 6.x crates' traits are gone (*The
  6.x layer crates are gone*), and `#[use_filters]` is a compile error on every
  decorator, naming the catch-all exception filter.
- `#[nest_rs::http::interceptor]`, `nest_rs::seaorm::DbContext`,
  `nest_rs::http::{MappedError, CarriedUnits, carried_units}` and the
  `AppBuilderInterceptorsExt`, `AppBuilderExceptionFiltersExt` and
  `AppBuilderFiltersExt` traits are gone.
- A mutating request a filter answered `2xx` after its handler failed rolls
  back, as does a handler answering `404` on purpose.

GraphQL, WebSocket and MCP run the same pipeline (their sections below): no edge
refuses `#[use_interceptors]` or `#[use_exception_filters]` on a handler or its
host, and the app's global interceptors reach every GraphQL root field, WebSocket
message and MCP operation. The one place both are refused is a WebSocket
connection hook, a compile error, which runs no layer.

### Interceptors and exception filters run around a job, a tick and a listener call

A queue attempt, a scheduled tick and an event listener call each ran their
body straight under their unit of work: no interceptor or exception filter
could wrap one, a layer attribute beside `#[process]`, `#[every]`, `#[cron]`,
`#[after]` or `#[on_event]` met the compiler's `cannot find attribute`, and
the app's global interceptors never saw them. Each is now a unit of the one
pipeline a request runs through, inside its transaction:

- **`#[use_interceptors]` and `#[use_exception_filters]`** are taken on a
  `#[processor]`, `#[scheduled]` or `#[listeners]` block and beside each of its
  methods, the block's running outside the method's. A guard there is a
  compile error naming why — `a job, a tick and an event have no caller to
  gate — guard where the work is produced` — and so is a scoped pipe, which a
  job's payload takes on its argument (`Piped<P>`).
- **The app's own interceptors and exception filters run around every job,
  tick and listener call**, outermost, as a module's unit-stage interceptor does.
  One that only means something for a request narrows itself with
  `Layer::serves`, reading the unit (`nest_rs::queue::unit::JOB`,
  `nest_rs::schedule::unit::TICK`, `nest_rs::events::unit::DISPATCH`).
- **A layer reads the unit through its edge's view**: `QueueView` — the
  `attempt()` with the job's id and number, the `processor()`, the backend's
  `backend_id()`; `ScheduleView` — the `job()` as `key = "…"` spells it, the
  `occurrence()` a job firing once claimed, its `replicas()`; `EventsView` —
  the `event()` type and the `listener()`. `cx.deadline()` is the decorator's
  `timeout`.
- **A layer may settle the unit without running it**: `QueueView::acknowledge`
  completes the job (the inbox pattern, keyed on `attempt().job_id()`),
  `ScheduleView::skip` and `EventsView::skip` pass the tick or the call. From
  an exception filter, the same answer settles a failure: the job is
  acknowledged and its writes roll back (`warn`, `job failed; a layer
  acknowledged it, so it settles done and its writes rolled back`); a tick's
  or a listener's failure a layer answered — `skip`, or a filter's client
  problem (a `4xx`) — is said at `warn` rather than `error`, while a `5xx`
  problem stays at `error`.
- **A layer a method binds and nothing provides fails the boot**, at the
  worker's or the scheduler's configure, or the bus's wiring; one a module the
  host's module does not import is refused by the access graph, as an
  `#[inject]` is.

A job attached by hand as a `CronJobMeta` binds no layer of its own and runs
under the scheduler's handler; the app's layers run around it.

### Two `#[meta]` values of one type refuse their site, whatever their expressions spell

The decorators refused two `#[meta]` values of one type at one level only when
each expression named its type (`Throttle(10)`, `Role::Admin`). A value built
through a function hid it: `#[meta(Tag::new())]` written twice on one route
compiled and booted, and the second value was read by nothing.

- **A site refuses two values of one type on its method, or on its host,** by
  the type they resolve to, when it composes: a route as it mounts, a
  `#[processor]`'s, a `#[scheduled]` job's and a `#[listeners]` method's at the
  boot. The boot fails with `nest_rs::core::DuplicateMetaError`, naming the
  type and where (`PostsController::create`, or the host for the host's
  values). The compile error stays where the expression names its type.
- **A site's values are built when it composes**, or on their first read if
  that comes first; they were built on the first read alone.

**Breaking:** an app declaring two values of one type at one level through
expressions that hide the type no longer boots; keep one — a method's own
value already overrides its host's.

### GraphQL runs on the one pipeline: a resolver binds interceptors and exception filters

Each root field of a GraphQL operation — a `#[query]`, a `#[mutation]`, an
`#[entity]`, and the federation fields `_service` and `_entities` — now runs
through the pipeline `nest_rs::core` declares for every edge, as a
`graphql.field` unit nested in its `/graphql` request and its operation's
unit of work:

1. the guard stage — the app's pool, the resolver's and the field's guards,
   each one's `check`, then the field's `#[authorize]` class gate;
2. the exception filters — the field's, the resolver's, then the app's;
3. the handler stage — the app's global interceptors, the resolver's, the
   field's;
4. the field — its argument pipes, the call, the posture's mask.

- **`#[use_interceptors]` and `#[use_exception_filters]` are taken on a
  `#[resolver]` and beside each of its fields**, with core's
  `nest_rs::core::{Interceptor, ExceptionFilter}`. An interceptor reads the
  field through `cx.switch_to::<GraphqlView>()` — its name, its operation's
  type, its role — and `cx.unit()` is `nest_rs::graphql::unit::FIELD`.
- **The app's global interceptors and exception filters reach every root
  field**, as they reach a route's handler. The `/graphql` request itself
  still runs the unit stage and the app's filters alone.
- **An exception filter answers a field's failure with a `Problem`**: the
  field's error carries its sentence on `message` and its code on
  `extensions.code`, the operation's other fields resolve, and what the answer
  withholds is filed by the status it says — a `4xx` files nothing.
- **A `#[field_resolver]` runs only what is declared on it** — its resolver's
  and its own guards, its own interceptors and exception filters — once per
  parent row, never the app's layers nor its resolver's interceptors. **A
  `#[subscription]`'s layers run at its opening**, never per item.
- **Every field's layers are composed at boot**, at the kernel's wiring step:
  a layer a field declares that no imported module provides, or two
  `#[meta]` values of one type on a field, refuse the boot naming the field,
  rather than its first operation.
- **The federation fields are units of their own**: `_service` and
  `_entities` each file a `graphql.field` line of role `federation`, and run
  the app's filters and interceptors around the pool.
- **A field's `Ability` is its unit's**: the response mask and
  `nest_rs::seaorm::graphql::bind` read the ability the field's class gate
  checked — a guard bound on the field included — never one copied into the
  GraphQL context. A subscription's items, which arrive outside its opening's
  unit, are masked against the ability that opening captured.
- **The resolver-membership and entity checks run at the wiring step**, in
  every boot path, under `nest_rs::graphql::schema`: a boot error now reads
  ``the `nest_rs::graphql::schema` wiring failed`` with the check's sentence
  as its cause.
- **A schema keyed outside a subgraph fails the boot, whatever keys it**:
  async-graphql serves `_service` and `_entities` from a key alone, so an
  object a field returns carrying async-graphql's own `#[graphql(entity)]` is
  refused without `GraphqlConfig::federation` as an `#[entity]` is, and that
  attribute beside `#[query]`, `#[mutation]`, `#[subscription]` or
  `#[field_resolver]` is a compile error — write an `#[entity]`. The federation
  fields run under the app's guard pool wherever one is listed, a subgraph or
  not.

### A resolver reaches a request-scoped provider through a `Scoped<T>` parameter

`nest_rs::core::Scoped<T>` is a parameter, as on a route — no schema
argument — on a root field and on a field resolver alike, and its provider is
inside the boot's access contract: one reached through no import fails the
boot with an `AccessGraphError`, one no module provides with a
`MissingDependencyError` naming it. A field that still misses it — on a
graphql-ws socket, which carries no request scope — answers the opaque
`internal error` under `INTERNAL`, the provider's type named only on the
operator's `error` line; a field resolver's injected `&Service` missing its
provider answers the same, where it named the type to the client.

`#[authorize(Action, bind = Service)]` is inside the same contract: a resolver
whose module does not import the bound service's fails the boot with an
`AccessGraphError`. The service resolves through the request's scope, as
`Bind<A, S>`'s does — the container's on a graphql-ws socket — and
`nest_rs::seaorm::graphql::bind` answers a missing provider or a missing
ability with the opaque `internal error` under `INTERNAL`, the type or the
remedy on the operator's `error` line, where it answered the client a wiring
sentence.

**Breaking:**

- `Scoped::<T>::from_context(ctx)` is gone: take a `Scoped<T>` parameter.
- `nest_rs::authz::graphql::ability(ctx)` is `ability()`, reading the field's
  unit; the GraphQL context no longer carries an `Arc<Ability>`, so
  `ctx.data::<Arc<Ability>>()` finds none — call
  `nest_rs::authz::current_ability()`.
- A global interceptor or exception filter now runs around every GraphQL root
  field: one meant for HTTP alone narrows itself with `Layer::serves`
  (`unit.edge() == Some(Edge::Http)`).
- A boot error from the GraphQL checks wraps the check's sentence; read it
  with its chain (`{err:#}`).
- `nest_rs::authz::graphql::masked_item_for` takes the ability it masks
  against: `masked_item_for::<A, E, _>(ctx, &ability, item)`.
- async-graphql's `#[graphql(entity)]` on a `#[query]`, `#[mutation]`,
  `#[subscription]` or `#[field_resolver]` no longer compiles, and an object
  carrying it fails the boot outside a subgraph.

### WebSocket messages run on the one pipeline: a gateway binds interceptors, exception filters and pipes

Each message a gateway serves now runs through the pipeline `nest_rs::core`
declares for every edge, as a `ws.message` unit inside its own unit of work,
cut at its `message_timeout`:

1. the unit stage — the `#[interceptor(stage = unit)]`s its app's modules bring;
2. the guard stage — the app's pool, the message's `#[use_guards]`, each one's
   `check`, then the message's `#[authorize]` class gate;
3. the exception filters — the message's, the gateway's, then the app's;
4. the handler stage — the app's global interceptors, the gateway's, the
   message's;
5. the message — the data pipes, its payload's own pipe, the call, the
   posture's mask.

- **`#[use_interceptors]`, `#[use_exception_filters]` and `#[use_pipes]` are
  taken on a `#[gateway]`**, for each of its messages, **and beside each
  `#[subscribe_message]`**, for that one, with core's
  `nest_rs::core::{Interceptor, ExceptionFilter}` and `nest_rs::pipes`'
  `GlobalPipe` — each layer once per message whichever scopes list it. They
  were compile errors saying the edge did not run them.
- **The app's global interceptors and exception filters run around every
  message.** One that only means something for a request narrows itself with
  `Layer::serves`, reading `nest_rs::ws::unit::MESSAGE`.
- **A layer reads the message through `WsView`** — its `client()`, its
  `event()`, its `data()` before the handler takes it, and its `reply()` once
  the rest ran — and answers it with `WsView::respond`: an interceptor without
  running the rest of the message, an exception filter in place of the frame
  the failure would have answered. An exception filter catches the handler's
  error by its type and answers `Caught::Problem` as
  `{ "error": …, "errors": { "code": … } }`. The message still failed, so what
  it wrote rolls back.
- **A handler's error is filed once its answer is final**: one an exception
  filter answers as a refusal (`4xx`) files nothing, one it answers `5xx` files
  the chain it withholds at `error`.
- **The class gate runs right after the message's guards**, before any
  interceptor, exception filter or pipe; the app's global pipes and
  `#[use_pipes]` fold over a message's `data` after the gate, where they ran
  before it.
- **A message's request scope is shared** while no provider is request- or
  transient-scoped: there is nothing to build, so no scope is opened per
  message.
- **What the upgrade's guards attached reaches every message and connection
  hook by the connection alone** — the principal, the ability, the tenant.
  `SocketContext`, the seam a data context bound to carry them, and `Captured`
  are removed.
- **A gateway's boot check runs on the kernel's wiring step**, in every app
  importing it, whatever it serves: its upgrade's guards, and each message's
  layers and `#[meta]` values, refuse the boot — a layer nothing provides, two
  `#[meta]` values of one type at one level (`DuplicateMetaError`, naming the
  type and the gateway) — where only an app serving HTTP checked them before.
- **The connection hooks take `#[meta]` and refuse a layer by name**: a guard
  (the upgrade's guards admitted the socket) and a pipe (a hook has no payload)
  for good; an interceptor and an exception filter until the hooks run them.
  Each met the compiler's `cannot find attribute`.

`Gateway::dispatch` still calls a handler on its own, as a test calls it —
its posture's class gate, its parameters, its pipes, the call, the mask — and
no guard or layer; a message on a socket runs the pipeline around the
handler, the class gate after its guards.

### A connection hook has a deadline of its own

An `#[on_connect]` or `#[on_disconnect]` hook was awaited without a bound: one
that never ended held its socket before the read loop and the lifetime ceiling
were armed. Each now runs under its own deadline, `WsConfig::connect_timeout`
and `WsConfig::disconnect_timeout`, read from `<PREFIX>_WS__CONNECT_TIMEOUT_SECS`
and `<PREFIX>_WS__DISCONNECT_TIMEOUT_SECS` on the message timeout's range and
with its default, 20 seconds. Past it the hook's work is dropped and rolled
back, its line files `error`, and one `warn` on `nest_rs::ws` names the unit
(`websocket connection hook not finished within its deadline; it is cut`); a
connect hook cut there closes its socket unserved with `1011`, as one that
panicked does, and a disconnect hook lets the close go on. The boot's `warn`
for a deadline the shutdown window cannot hold now covers the three units, one
line each, said `a websocket unit's deadline outlasts the shutdown window: one
admitted just before a stop can be cut unanswered`, with `unit` and
`timeout_secs` fields in place of `message_timeout_secs`; the `warn` of a
message cut at its deadline says it as `timeout_secs` too.

A statement the database cancelled at its own bound and the gateway's own
deadline both answer `code: "UNAVAILABLE"`; only the deadline's frame adds
`reason: "timeout"`, the cancelled statement being the service's problem,
answered as every problem is.

### A message takes `Scoped<T>` as a parameter, inside the access contract

A handler reached a request-scoped provider by calling
`Scoped::<T>::from_context()` in its body, which no boot check could see: a
provider no imported module offered failed at the first message, and its
`WsScopeError::NoProvider` named the Rust type on the client's frame. A
`#[subscribe_message]` method now declares it as a parameter, beside its
`&WsClient` and its payload:

```rust
#[subscribe_message("seq")]
#[public]
async fn seq(&self, seq: Scoped<RequestSeq>) -> u64 {
    seq.value()
}
```

`nest_rs::core::Scoped<T>` implements `nest_rs::core::ResolvesProvider`, so the
boot checks its provider like an `#[inject]` field: one reached through no
import fails with `AccessGraphError`, one no module provides with
`MissingDependencyError` naming it. A miss that still happens at a message
answers the opaque `internal error` frame, the type filed at `error` on
`nest_rs::ws`. `#[messages]` reads the parameter by its name, as it reads the
`&WsClient` by its reference; a `Scoped` of another crate does not compile.

### MCP runs on the one pipeline: a host binds interceptors and exception filters

Every request an MCP client sends — but `subscriptions/listen` and the
notifications — is now one `mcp.operation` unit on the pipeline
`nest_rs::core` declares for every edge, inside the `/mcp` request's context
and a unit of work of its own. Its own site runs the unit stage, once around
whatever the request reaches; a `#[tool]` or a `#[prompt]` then runs its own
site inside it:

1. the guard stage — the app's pool, the host's and the operation's guards,
   each one's `check`, then the operation's `#[authorize]` class gate;
2. the exception filters — the operation's, the host's, then the app's;
3. the handler stage — the app's global interceptors, the host's, the
   operation's;
4. the operation — its pipes, its `Scoped<T>` parameters, the call, the
   posture's mask.

- **`#[use_interceptors]` and `#[use_exception_filters]` are taken on an
  `#[mcp]` host and beside each `#[tool]` and `#[prompt]`**, with core's
  `nest_rs::core::{Interceptor, ExceptionFilter}`, at each scope in the order
  above. A layer reads the operation
  through `cx.switch_to::<McpView>()` — its JSON-RPC method, the router serving
  it, what it names, the host declaring it — and `cx.unit()` is
  `nest_rs::mcp::unit::OPERATION`.
- **The app's global interceptors and exception filters reach every tool call
  and prompt fetch**, as they reach a route's handler; an
  `#[interceptor(stage = unit)]` serving MCP runs once around every request, a
  listing, a handshake and a `tools/call` naming no declared tool included. The `/mcp` request itself still runs the
  unit stage and the app's filters alone.
- **An operation's error is answered by its type**: an operation answering a
  `Result<T, E>` — spelled so, renamed or behind an alias — fails with any `E`
  — its own `McpError` answers as built, a
  `ToProblem` error the problem it says (`data.code`, `invalid_request` for a
  `4xx`, `internal_error` for a `5xx`), any other opaquely, its chain filed at
  `error` — and an exception filter reads it as its type first and may answer
  a `Problem` over it. A filter changes what the client reads, never whether
  the operation's unit of work rolls back.
- **What a layer makes ambient around `/mcp` reaches the operation**: the
  request's span, scope and trace, what its guards attached and what an
  interceptor around it added, carried onto the task rmcp runs the operation
  on, in session and stateless modes alike.
- **Every operation's layers are composed at boot**, at the kernel's wiring
  step, beside the endpoint's own checks (a contested identity, a tool or
  prompt two hosts claim, protocol versions no host shares, an identity no
  host serves), which now run in every app that imports a host, whatever it
  serves: a layer an operation declares that no imported module provides, or
  two `#[meta]` values of one type on one operation, refuse the boot rather
  than the first call. A boot error reads ``the `nest_rs::mcp::endpoints`
  wiring failed`` with the check's sentence as its cause.
- **A refused operation leaves its `/mcp` request untimed**, as before: the
  mark `Server-Timing` reads now crosses onto rmcp's task.
- **A generic `#[mcp]` struct or `#[tools]` impl does not compile**: each
  operation's site is a `static`, which no parameter reaches.

### A host writing rmcp's handler by hand binds no layer on its `#[mcp]`

A host that writes rmcp's `ServerHandler` itself — resources, completion,
rmcp's own `#[tool_router]` — declares no operation for a layer to run on, so
`#[use_guards]`, `#[use_interceptors]`, `#[use_exception_filters]` and
`#[meta]` beside its `#[mcp]` are a compile error at the attribute, naming the
host. They compiled before and ran nowhere: a guard bound there gated
nothing. Guard such a host's endpoint with `EndpointGuards<McpView, G>` from a
module or the app's global guards, or move its tools and prompts under
`#[tools]`, where the host's layers run on each of them.

### A tool reaches a request-scoped provider through a `Scoped<T>` parameter

`nest_rs::core::Scoped<T>` is a `#[tool]` or `#[prompt]` parameter, as on a
route — it is no part of the tool's input schema — resolved from the `/mcp`
request's scope once the operation's guards and class gate admitted the
caller. Its provider is inside the boot's access contract: one reached
through no import fails the boot with an `AccessGraphError`, one no module
provides with a `MissingDependencyError` naming it. An operation that still
misses it — on a container built by hand — answers the opaque
`internal error`, the provider's type named only on the operator's `error`
line, where it named the type to the model.

**Breaking:**

- `McpToolContext`, `Captured`, `OperationValue` and `OperationOutcome` are
  gone: what a tool context captured off the request and installed around an
  operation is an interceptor in `GlobalInterceptors<(…)>` (around the operation,
  after its guards) or an `#[interceptor(stage = unit)]` (around the guards), and a value one makes
  ambient — with `nest_rs::core::with_ambient` or `cx.attach` — reaches the
  tool.
- `Scoped::<T>::from_context()` is gone: take a `Scoped<T>` parameter, or read
  `nest_rs::core::current_request_scope()` in a host writing rmcp's handler by
  hand.
- `nest_rs::mcp::unresolvable_chain` is gone: an operation's layers are
  composed at boot.
- `McpView::method()` returns `&str`, and `kind()` and `host()` are `Option`s:
  the view describes every request, not only a tool call or a prompt fetch.
- `McpView` is no longer `Copy`: it holds the method and the name the request
  addressed. Clone it where a copy was taken.
- A generic `#[mcp]` struct does not compile, as a generic `#[tools]` impl
  does not.
- `#[use_guards]`, `#[use_interceptors]`, `#[use_exception_filters]` or
  `#[meta]` beside an `#[mcp]` whose host has no `#[tools]` impl does not
  compile.
- A global interceptor or exception filter now runs around every MCP tool call
  and prompt fetch: one meant for HTTP alone narrows itself with
  `Layer::serves` (`unit.edge() == Some(Edge::Http)`).
- A boot error from the MCP endpoint checks wraps the check's sentence; read
  it with its chain (`{err:#}`).

### The 6.x layer crates are gone: interceptors and exception filters are core's

`nest-rs-interceptors`, `nest-rs-filters` and `nest-rs-exception-filters`
held NestRS 6's poem-typed layer traits, which nothing in the framework has run
since every edge moved onto the one pipeline. They leave the workspace, and the
umbrella's `interceptors`, `filters` and `exception-filters` features with
them: the umbrella counts **30 capabilities**. An interceptor is
`nest_rs::core::Interceptor`, an exception filter `nest_rs::core::ExceptionFilter`,
and `#[interceptor]` is `nest_rs::core`'s; none takes a feature.

**Breaking:**

- `nest_rs::interceptors`, `nest_rs::filters` and `nest_rs::exception_filters`
  are removed, and a manifest naming the `interceptors`, `filters` or
  `exception-filters` feature of `nest-rs` fails to resolve.
- `LayerKind::Filter` is removed: the kinds are `Guard`, `Interceptor`, `Pipe`
  and `ExceptionFilter`, the four families the pipeline runs.
- `nest_rs::http::HttpBootCheck` is removed. Every boot check runs on the
  kernel's wiring step (`provide_wiring`), in every app importing the module,
  whatever it serves.

### A redundant layer declaration's line names the decorator that re-runs a layer

The `debug` line `redundant layer declaration deduped` on `nest_rs::layers`
told the operator to "use `#[force_*]` to re-run", a decorator family that
does not exist. Its `hint` now reads `broadest site wins; `#[force_guards]`
re-runs a guard`: guards are the one layer with a re-run opt-in.

- **A filter on the old hint** matches the new one only through its
  unchanged `broadest site wins` prefix; the message, the target and the
  other fields did not move.

### Each graphql-ws operation runs in a request scope of its own

A graphql-ws socket ran every operation with no request scope, so a `Scoped<T>` parameter or
a `bind = Service` on a request-scoped provider passed the boot and answered every operation
on the socket with the opaque internal error, where a WebSocket message opens a scope of its
own and an MCP operation shares its request's.

- **A query or a mutation sent over the socket runs its unit in a fresh request scope; a
  subscription opens one with it**, and its stream is polled inside it, so its opening and
  its items share one scope until it completes. The upgrade's scope never crosses onto the
  socket. A `POST`'s operations share the HTTP request's, as before.

### Every opaque server failure on HTTP is one problem document

HTTP's opaque `500` read three ways, depending on which part of the framework answered it: a
`#[crud]` operation and a `ServiceError` answered `code: INTERNAL` and no `detail`; `.opaque()`,
a `Scoped<T>` the request scope could not resolve and a `Bind` whose route was not wired
answered the constant `"internal error"` as `detail` and no `code`; an error the edge could not
read, a bare `500`, a raw-text `500`, a panic, the request deadline's `503` and a guard's `5xx`
denial (authentication's `503` when what its strategy asks does not answer) answered neither. A
client branching on the class read three documents for one failure.

- **Every `5xx` the framework answers without describing it is its class's document**:
  `type: about:blank`, the status's `title`, its `status`, and `code` — `INTERNAL`, or
  `UNAVAILABLE` for a `503` — with no `detail`:
  ```json
  { "type": "about:blank", "title": "Internal Server Error", "status": 500, "code": "INTERNAL" }
  ```
  `ProblemDetails::from_status` carries the code on a `5xx`, and so does every problem built on
  it: `ProblemDetails::internal()`, `from_error`, a `ResponseError`'s default rendering, a raw
  error the edge lifts onto the document, a guard's `5xx` denial (its `Retry-After` kept).
  `.opaque()`, `Scoped<T>` and `Bind` answer
  `ProblemDetails::internal()`, the document a `Problem::new(500, code::INTERNAL)` renders.
- **What the answer withholds is filed as before**: once, at `error`, at `warn` behind a `503`.
- **The OAuth token endpoint keeps RFC 6749's error body**, `{"error": "server_error"}` at
  `500` or `503`: its clients parse that standard, not a problem document.
- **The OpenAPI document names the `code` member** of its `ProblemDetails` schema.
- **Breaking:** an opaque HTTP `500` no longer carries `detail: "internal error"`, and a `5xx`
  problem built from its status carries `code`; a client reads the `code`.

### A `5xx` a `ResponseError` answers is filed once, by the HTTP edge

A handler's own error implementing `ResponseError` and answering a `5xx` was answered opaquely
and filed nowhere: the edge read no chain behind a typed error, and only the operation line's
`status=500` said a request had failed. The framework's own typed errors filed themselves as
they rendered, before an exception filter could answer otherwise, each on its own target and
at its own level.

- **The edge files a typed `5xx`'s whole chain once its answer is final**, as it files every
  other failure it answers: `request failed` at `error`, `request unavailable` at `warn` behind
  a `503`, on `nest_rs::http`, by the status the client was finally sent. A failure an
  exception filter answers with a `4xx` files nothing; one it answers with a `5xx` files the
  chain once. A decode failure in the chain is said without its value.
- **`ServiceError`, `AuthError` and `TokenError` no longer file as they render.** Their
  `5xx` is the edge's line, so one failure is one line, at one level, whoever answered it:
  `ServiceError`'s `service error surfaced as 500` / `503` on `nest_rs::orm`, `AuthError`'s
  `authentication unavailable` at `error` on `nest_rs::authn`, and `TokenError`'s
  `token request failed` / `unavailable` on `nest_rs::oauth::server` are gone from a route's
  answer.
- **A `4xx` stays unfiled by the edge**, and a refused credential's `authentication failed`
  stays `AuthError`'s own line at `warn`, its security record.
- **A bare `Problem`, or a `ProblemDetails` returned as the error itself, withholds nothing**
  and files nothing, as a bare status does. A `ProblemDetails` carried in an `anyhow::Error`
  or handed to `HttpError::new` answers its status's opaque document, so its chain is filed,
  as before.
- **An `AuthError` a handler returns as a ready response** (`IntoResponse`, no `?`), which no
  edge reads a chain from, files its outage at `warn` on `nest_rs::authn`, as the edge files
  every `503`.
- **`#[crud]`'s database failures are unchanged**: an operation's `crud operation failed` /
  `unavailable` line on `nest_rs::orm`, since its problem document keeps no chain.
- **The GraphQL, WebSocket and MCP edges are unchanged**: they read no `ResponseError`, and
  already file every opaque failure where they answer it.
- **`nest-rs-oauth-server` emits nothing on `nest_rs::oauth::server` any more**, and drops its
  `tracing` dependency.

### A failure names its class by the same code on every edge that has room for it

A client branching on a failure's class read it on some answers and not on others. A WebSocket
frame and an MCP error answering a failure the client is not told of carried no code, while the
same edges' problem answers of that class did. On HTTP, a problem built from a status alone
carried a code only on a `5xx`, so the router's `404` for an unmounted route and `Bind`'s `403`
and `404` read differently from `#[crud]`'s, and a guard's denial named no code at all, while
GraphQL, WebSockets and MCP named it. A value that did not decode named `INVALID_ARGUMENT` on
HTTP and in a GraphQL field error, and nothing on WebSockets, on MCP or in GraphQL's refusal of a
body, and a pipe's refusal named it on HTTP alone.

- **Every opaque answer names the `INTERNAL` class.** On WebSockets the frame is
  `{"error": "internal error", "errors": {"code": "INTERNAL"}}`, on MCP the JSON-RPC internal
  error `{"code": -32603, "message": "internal error", "data": {"code": "INTERNAL"}}`, each built
  by one constructor, whoever answered it: an error no rule names, a `ToProblem` saying `None`,
  `.opaque()`, a panic, a request-scoped provider out of reach, a unit that could not begin, a
  site that did not compose, an internal guard denial. MCP's answer to a path no host serves,
  unreachable through `#[mcp]`, is that opaque answer too, in place of a sentence of its own.
- **A problem HTTP builds from a status alone carries the class the status names**:
  `INVALID_ARGUMENT` for a `400` or a `422`, `UNAUTHENTICATED` for a `401`, `FORBIDDEN` for a
  `403`, `NOT_FOUND` for a `404`, `CONFLICT` for a `409`, `RATE_LIMITED` for a `429` — beside
  the opaque document's `UNAVAILABLE` for a `503` and `INTERNAL` for any other `5xx`. That covers
  `ProblemDetails::from_status` and every constructor and rendering built on it: `bad_request()`,
  `not_found()` and the other named statuses, `HttpError::from_status`, `pipe_error`, a
  `ResponseError`'s default rendering, the router's own `404`, a raw-text error the edge lifts
  onto the document, `Bind`'s refusals, `#[crud]`'s `403` and `404`, a page cursor no page
  issued.
- **A guard's denial on HTTP carries the code its class carries on every edge**:
  `UNAUTHENTICATED`, `FORBIDDEN`, `INSUFFICIENT_SCOPE` for a token too narrow (its `403` alone
  would read `FORBIDDEN`), `RATE_LIMITED`, `UNAVAILABLE`, `INTERNAL` — the code GraphQL's
  `extensions.code`, a WebSocket frame's `errors.code` and an MCP error's `data.code` carry for
  the same denial.
- **A value that does not decode names `INVALID_ARGUMENT` wherever its answer has a member for
  it**, as HTTP's `400` and GraphQL's field error already did: on WebSockets under `errors.code`
  for a payload, an envelope or a decode failure a handler's error carries, its sentence kept;
  on MCP under `data.code` for a prompt's arguments and a decode failure an operation's error
  carries, JSON-RPC's `-32602` kept; on GraphQL under `extensions.code` for the `400` answering
  a body that is no GraphQL request, as a resolver's decode failure already answered.
- **A pipe's refusal on GraphQL names `INVALID_ARGUMENT`** under `extensions.code`, beside the
  field map under `extensions.errors`, as HTTP's `400` names it beside `errors`: a resolver's
  argument pipe, `nest_rs::graphql::pipe_error`, and a global pipe over the variables, on a
  `POST` and over the socket.
- **MCP's answer to an operation stopped before it settled** — at the transport's stop, or at
  its client's `notifications/cancelled` — keeps its sentence, `the operation was cancelled
  before it completed`, and names the `UNAVAILABLE` class under `data.code`, as an operation past
  its deadline does: the server could not serve it, and nothing failed. No client reads it on
  these two paths: the transport ends the stream of an operation it stops, and rmcp drops the
  answer to a request its client cancelled.
- **Additive:** every answer keeps its members, and its sentence but one — a path no MCP host
  serves, unreachable through `#[mcp]`, now says `internal error`. A test comparing a whole `4xx`
  problem document, a whole opaque WebSocket frame or MCP error, a WebSocket decode frame, an MCP
  prompt's argument refusal, a GraphQL pipe refusal or GraphQL's refusal of a body that is no
  GraphQL request sees the new `code`.
- **Not covered**, each a limit:
  - a status no class names — `405`, `406`, `408`, `410`, `413` and `415` among them — carries
    no `code` on HTTP, nor do the WebSocket refusals of a message past its cap and of a binary frame; a class
    for each is a new public name;
  - a pipe's refusal on WebSockets and MCP names no class: its `errors` and its `data` are the
    field map itself, where a `code` member would collide with a field named `code`, so naming
    it reshapes that member;
  - an MCP tool's arguments that do not decode are rmcp's `isError` tool result, the
    specification's form for an input the model can correct, whose content is text alone;
  - an in-band address nothing serves — a WebSocket event no handler serves, an MCP tool,
    prompt, task or method no host declares, a GraphQL field the schema lacks — answers its
    protocol's own refusal with no `NOT_FOUND` code, while HTTP's router `404` carries one.

### Every status the framework answers names its class

A `405`, `406`, `408`, `410`, `412`, `413`, `415`, `416` or `426` the framework answered was a
problem document with no `code`, so a client could branch on its `status` alone.

- **Nine codes join `nest_rs::core::code`**, each the status's registered reason phrase
  in upper snake case, as its document's `title` spells it (RFC 9110 §15.5):
  `METHOD_NOT_ALLOWED` (`405`), `NOT_ACCEPTABLE` (`406`), `REQUEST_TIMEOUT` (`408`), `GONE`
  (`410`), `PRECONDITION_FAILED` (`412`), `CONTENT_TOO_LARGE` (`413`), `UNSUPPORTED_MEDIA_TYPE`
  (`415`), `RANGE_NOT_SATISFIABLE` (`416`), `UPGRADE_REQUIRED` (`426`). The code set's rule is
  written where the next member is placed, `code`'s module doc: a class one status answers by
  its own name is that status's reason phrase; the nine existing codes keep their names, each
  read off the standard that names its class (gRPC's canonical names for `INVALID_ARGUMENT`,
  `UNAUTHENTICATED`, `UNAVAILABLE`, `INTERNAL`; RFC 6750 §3.1 for `INSUFFICIENT_SCOPE`; RFC 6585
  §4 for `RATE_LIMITED`).
- **Every writer carries its code with no edit at the writer**, since each reaches
  `ProblemDetails::from_status`: the router's `405` with its `Allow`, static files' `405`, `412`
  and `416`, `/graphql`'s `405` with the playground off, the edge's `408` for a stalled body and
  `413` past the cap, `Body`'s and `RawBody`'s `413`, GraphQL's `413` for a multipart file past
  the cap and a batch past `max_batch_size`, the `415` of `Json`, `Form` and `Multipart`, the
  WebSocket handshake's `426`, rmcp's `405`, `406`, `413` and `415` on the MCP endpoint, and an
  app's own `HttpError::from_status(StatusCode::GONE)`. Additive: a member joins the document,
  none leaves.
- **WebSocket's two in-band refusals name their class**: a binary frame answers
  `{"error": "binary frames are not supported", "code": "UNSUPPORTED_MEDIA_TYPE"}` and a frame
  past `max_message_bytes` `{"error": "message too large", "code": "CONTENT_TOO_LARGE"}`, the
  classes HTTP's `415` and `413` name, as RFC 6455's close codes 1003 and 1009 say.
- **An address no handler serves names `NOT_FOUND` in band**, as HTTP's router `404` does: a
  WebSocket event no handler serves (`{"error": "unknown event", "code": "NOT_FOUND"}`), and on
  MCP a tool, prompt, task or method no host serves (`data: {"code": "NOT_FOUND"}`, a prompt's
  refusal still listing `available_prompts` beside it). A method no host serves is a custom
  one, said `Method not found`, or a protocol method rmcp refuses in its own words, the method's
  name (`resources/read`, `logging/setLevel` on a host serving neither), each keeping its
  `-32601`; a request rmcp's dispatch refuses for a method its protocol version has not (`ping`
  at `2026-07-28`) is answered HTTP `404` under the same class. A tool no host declares carries
  the class at every protocol version — rmcp's `-32602` `tool not found` gains `data: {"code":
  "NOT_FOUND"}` from a lone host's router, the shared path's merge, a host written by hand or an
  endpoint built by hand, the code and the message staying rmcp's and the name sent in no reply;
  its operation files one `mcp.operation` line, `outcome="error"`, the tool named `_OTHER`. A
  refusal a host writes with `data` of its own keeps it, as does a resource's `-32002`, which is
  a host's own answer.
- **A body MCP cannot read as a JSON-RPC message is answered `400`**, as MCP's Streamable HTTP
  transport says of input the server cannot accept, where rmcp answered `415 Unsupported Media
  Type` with nothing said, the answer of a wrong `Content-Type`: a JSON-RPC error carrying no
  `id` — `-32700` Parse error for a body that is not JSON, `-32600` Invalid Request for JSON that
  is no message (an array, a `tools/call` whose `params` is an array, a bare string), JSON-RPC 2.0
  §5.1's codes in their own words — under `data: {"code": "INVALID_ARGUMENT"}`. A body typed
  otherwise keeps rmcp's `415`, `Unsupported Media Type: Content-Type must be
  application/json`, now under `UNSUPPORTED_MEDIA_TYPE`. The access line files `status=400`.

### The class sits beside the field map on WebSocket and MCP

A pipe's refusal named its class beside its field map on HTTP (`code`, `errors`) and GraphQL
(`extensions.code`, `extensions.errors`), and only its field map on WebSocket and MCP, whose one
member carried either the class or the map. `errors` now names the field map and nothing else on
every edge; the class is its sibling.

- **A WebSocket error frame carries its class members in `data`**, beside `error`:
  `{ "error": …, "code": …, "reason"?: …, "requiredScopes"?: […], "retryAfterSeconds"?: …,
  "errors"?: { … } }`. A problem is `{"error": "conflict", "code": "CONFLICT"}`, the opaque frame
  `{"error": "internal error", "code": "INTERNAL"}`, a scope denial `{"error": …, "code":
  "INSUFFICIENT_SCOPE", "reason": "insufficient_scope", "requiredScopes": ["posts:write"]}`, a
  deadline `{"error": "the message was not answered in time", "code": "UNAVAILABLE", "reason":
  "timeout"}`, a decode failure `{"error": "invalid payload for `named` at `name`: …", "code":
  "INVALID_ARGUMENT"}`, and a pipe's refusal ``{"error": "invalid payload for `validated`:
  validation failed", "code": "INVALID_ARGUMENT", "errors": {"text": [ … ]}}``. A frame a handler
  builds with `WsError::new` carries no class, as a GraphQL `Error::new` carries none.
- **An MCP pipe refusal's `data` is `{code, errors}`**: `{"code": -32602, "message": "validation
  failed", "data": {"code": "INVALID_ARGUMENT", "errors": {"latitude": [{"code": "range"}]}}}`,
  and `{"code": "INVALID_ARGUMENT"}` with no field map. JSON-RPC's `-32602` and the message stay;
  every other `data` the framework writes keeps its shape.
- **`WsError` is typed member by member**: `error`, `code`, `reason`, `required_scopes`,
  `retry_after_secs` (on the wire `retryAfterSeconds`, every edge's spelling), and `errors`, the
  field map, its one `serde_json::Value`.
  `WsError::with_details(message, value)` is gone: a `{"code": …}` passed to it would otherwise
  land in the field map with no compile error. `WsError::new(message).with_errors(map)` builds a
  frame carrying a field map.

**Not covered:**

Each a limit:

- **hyper's `431`** (request header fields too large) is written before the edge, bodiless, with
  no document to carry a code.
- **A status nothing in the framework writes** (`402`, `411`, `414`, `417`, `421`, `428`, `451`)
  carries no `code`; the rule places each the day the framework comes to write one.
- **An MCP tool's undecodable arguments** are turned by rmcp 3.8 into an `isError` text result,
  dropping the error's `data` and so its class.
- **rmcp's own JSON-RPC transport refusals** (`HeaderMismatch`, an unsupported protocol version,
  a missing client capability) keep the `data` rmcp writes, with no `code` beside it.
- **A method rmcp's dispatch refuses inside a session's stream carries no class**: on a session
  negotiated before `2026-07-28`, `subscriptions/listen`, and a tasks method on a server
  advertising no tasks, are answered `-32601` with no `data` before any handler runs, and the
  answer rides the session's `200` stream, which the endpoint does not read (rmcp's
  `StreamableHttpService` takes a `ServerHandler`, so no layer sits above its dispatch). The
  same refusal of a `2026-07-28` request is answered `404`, which the endpoint classes.
- **async-graphql's validation errors** — an unknown field or argument among them — carry no
  `extensions.code`: async-graphql writes them, and the class a document the schema refuses
  answers is not decided here.
- **A body MCP cannot read is said by JSON-RPC's words alone**: where a body that is not JSON
  failed (serde's line and column) is not said, and JSON of no message's shape cannot be placed,
  since rmcp reads every message through an untagged enum, which names no path.

### A domain error maps to the wire once, through `ToProblem`, its problem type on every edge

A domain error wrote its mapping twice: `nest_rs::core::ToProblem` for GraphQL, WebSocket and MCP,
and `nest_rs::http::ResponseError` for HTTP, which read no `ToProblem` — two `match`es over one
enum, free to drift; `ServiceError` carried both. A `Problem` held a status, a code, a detail and a
wait, not RFC 9457's `type` and `title`, so a problem type of an app's own took an HTTP-only
exception filter building a `ProblemDetails`, and no other edge's client saw it.

- **A route reads its error by its type**, as every other edge reads a handler's: `#[routes]`
  answers a `Result<T, E>` whose `E` implements `ToProblem` with the problem it says — the opaque
  `500` when it says none — the error kept as the failure's cause for an exception filter and the
  operator's line. So does a route `#[http_code]` or `#[response_header]` shapes, the shaping left
  to a success, and an `#[sse]` route's `Result<SseStream, E>`.
- **An error maps to HTTP once, by types**: every `ToProblem` error is a
  `nest_rs::http::ResponseError` answering its problem, so `?` on one in a body answering an
  `HttpError` answers it too, and a second, hand-written `ResponseError` for it does not compile
  (E0119). `Infallible` is a `ToProblem` that says nothing, so `?` on an infallible result still
  converts. A route's error converting into no `HttpError` — an `anyhow` chain, a library's
  error — answers the opaque `500`, a `Problem` its chain carries answering itself, as on every
  other edge.
- **`Problem::with_type(uri, title)`**, a `const fn`: RFC 9457 §3.1's type, a URI a client keys
  on, checked for a URI reference's characters when it compiles, and its title, together, as the
  RFC pairs them. HTTP answers them as the document's `type` and `title` (unset, `about:blank`
  and the reason phrase, as before), GraphQL as `extensions.type` and `extensions.title`,
  WebSocket and MCP as `data.type` and `data.title`; `Problem::type_uri()` and `title()` read
  them. Unlike the `detail`, they go out on a `5xx`: a type is the developer's constant, never
  what failed.
- **`Problem::with_errors(map)`** carries a field map, every edge's `errors`, withheld on a `5xx`.
  A `ServiceError::Validation`'s problem carries its fields, so GraphQL, WebSocket and MCP answer
  them beside the class, where they answered the class alone.
- **`WsError` gains `problem_type`**, a boxed `WsProblemType { uri, title }` serialized flat as
  `data.type` and `data.title`, so a handler's `Result<_, WsError>` keeps its size.
- **The demo's `PostError` writes one `ToProblem`**, `AlreadyPublished` a `const` problem typed
  `https://nestrs.dev/problems/post-already-published`, and `PostProblemFilter` is gone; its
  `publish` route returns `Result<Json<Post>, PostError>`. `AudioError`, which the client is owed
  no word of, implements neither trait, and the audio routes say `.opaque()?`.

**Breaking:**

- `nest_rs::seaorm::ServiceError`'s HTTP mapping is its `ToProblem`, so `?` on it is unchanged;
  `.status()` and `.as_response()` on one read that one mapping.
- A domain error's `ResponseError` beside its `ToProblem` no longer compiles: delete it, and `?`
  on the error answers its problem.
- `HttpError` no longer converts from an `anyhow::Error` or a `StatusCode`: `?` on an `anyhow`
  result in a body answering an `HttpError` is `.opaque()?`, or the route returns
  `anyhow::Result<T>`, which answers the same opaque `500`; a bare status is
  `HttpError::from_status(status)`, and a route returning `Result<T, StatusCode>` returns
  `Result<T, HttpError>` with it.
- `nest_rs::ws::WsError` gains the public field `problem_type`; it is `#[non_exhaustive]`, so a
  struct literal was already refused.

[Upgrading, step 11](https://nestrs.dev/upgrading/) holds the migration. What fails if you get it
wrong: a `ResponseError` beside a `ToProblem` is ``E0119: conflicting implementations of trait
`ResponseError` ``; a `?` on an `anyhow` error or a `StatusCode` inside a
`nest_rs::http::Result<T>` body is ``E0277: `?` couldn't convert the error``.

### HTTP: a route's path is refused at compile time when it cannot mount, `off` drops one header, and the nested settings are named for HTTP

- **Breaking: the settings `HttpConfig` nests are named for the crate** —
  `TlsConfig` is `HttpTls`, `CorsConfig` is `HttpCors`, `SecurityHeadersConfig` is
  `HttpSecurityHeaders` — since `Config` names a `#[config]` and nothing else.
- **`<PREFIX>_HTTP__SECURITY_HEADERS_<HEADER>=off` drops that one security header**
  whatever its default — `<PREFIX>_HTTP__SECURITY_HEADERS_HSTS=off` — as setting its
  field to `None` does in code.
  A blank value is refused at boot; an empty one is unset, as every variable is.
- **`#[routes]` reads a path with the route grammar the router mounts by**, so a
  path gains its leading `/`, `/q/{id}` and `/q/{other}` are one route, a
  parameter named two ways is refused, a path that cannot mount is refused at
  compile time, and a duplicate —
  in one version, in a shared one, in every version, under true `#[cfg]`s — is
  refused naming both methods. Two verbs on one method and a generic method are
  refused too.
- **`#[http_code]`, `#[response_header]` and `#[redirect]` work written
  path-qualified** (`#[nest_rs::http::http_code(201)]`), and a shaper `#[routes]`
  does not read — outside a `#[routes]` impl, or under an import alias — is a
  compile error, where it was a silent no-op.
- The edge's typed errors move to `error.rs` with their public paths unchanged,
  and the transport never quotes a PEM value it cannot parse.
- **Fixed: an error the route tree answers carries the edge's headers.** The
  router's own `404`, an extractor's `400` and any `Err` escaping the tree were
  rendered after the security-header stamp, so they shipped without
  `X-Content-Type-Options`, `X-Frame-Options` and HSTS. The edge now renders such an
  error as its problem document inside the stamp, keeping the route the span is
  named for, with or without CORS and compression outside it.
- **`HttpFallbackMeta` mounts the router's fallback**: one surface answering, at
  its path and outside the global prefix, every request no route and no
  self-mount claims. Which side answers is decided before routing, from the
  paths the transport mounted — a controller's prefix (as declared and as each
  version mounts it), a self-mount's paths, the global prefix whole, and the routes of a controller at `/` — erring toward the route
  table, so a fallback never shadows a route, and the request reaches either
  side whole. It is public by construction: registering one is the opening. Two
  fail the boot naming both; one under a path someone owns fails it naming the
  owner; a route answering `GET` at its own path is a boot `warn`.
  `literal_mount_path` is the check a mount path a module reads from
  configuration goes through: a canonical path the router matches as written.

### The OpenAPI document types what a generated client reads, and its page loads nothing from elsewhere

The demo's document, run through Redocly's recommended rules and the two client
generators a TypeScript frontend reaches for (Hey API with its TanStack Query
plugin, openapi-typescript), produced clients that compiled and typed less than
the server sends: 7 lint errors and 6 warnings. It now lints with no error and
three warnings, each a health probe answering no 4xx, which is true; every
operation's success and errors are typed. The document stays OpenAPI 3.1.2: the generators read a 3.2
document as 3.1 and drop what only 3.2 says, which
`.claude/decisions/openapi-document-scope.md` records with the rest of what the
document refuses.

- **An `#[expose]`d id or timestamp keeps its format.** The wire type carries a
  `Uuid` or a `DateTimeWithTimeZone` column as a `String`, and its schema said
  only `string`; the field now states `format: uuid` or `format: date-time`, its
  Rust type unchanged. A timestamp is spelled as chrono's own `Serialize` spells
  it, `Z` at UTC, where an unmasked route wrote `+00:00` and a masked one, which
  re-serializes the model, already wrote `Z`.
- **`ProblemDetails` points `type` and `instance` at URI references**, as RFC 9457
  §3.1 defines them: `format: uri` made a validating client refuse the relative
  `instance` the framework writes (`/orders/17`). `status` is bounded to
  `100..=599`.
- **Every operation documents the `500` a handler's `Err` answers, one taking a
  body the `413` its cap answers, and a `401` its `WWW-Authenticate` challenge.**
  No `default` response: it would claim a status a handler writes with a body of
  its own.
- **Added: `#[api(error(503 = ProbeReport))]`** declares an error status a
  handler answers with a JSON body of its own, 4xx or 5xx alone, documented
  beside the framework's problem document for that status. The health probes
  declare their `503` with it.
- **Added: `#[api(deprecated = "YYYY-MM-DD")]` deprecates a route for its
  clients.** The operation is `deprecated: true`, and every answer of the route,
  a guard's refusal included, carries the RFC 9745 `Deprecation: @<unix time>`
  header. **Breaking:** Rust's own `#[deprecated]` on a `#[routes]` handler or a
  `#[controller]` struct is a compile error naming that key: it deprecates the
  method for its Rust callers — the framework alone — and clippy's
  `deprecated_semver` holds its `since` to a version, where the header is a
  date (`.claude/decisions/route-deprecation.md`).
- **Breaking: a paginated `#[crud]` list links its next page in
  `Link: </posts?first=20&after=…>; rel="next"`** (RFC 8288), built on the path
  the caller addressed, where it sent the cursor in `x-next-cursor`, an `X-` field
  RFC 6648 retired. The last page has no `Link`
  (`.claude/decisions/pagination-link-header.md`).
- **Breaking for a generator grouping by tag: a route's default tag is its
  controller's token** (`posts`), the stem its `operationId`s carry, where it was
  the struct's name (`PostsController`) and a `#[crud]` route took its entity's
  (`Post`), so one controller could fall into two groups.
- **Added: the whole Info object.** `OpenApiConfig` gains `summary`,
  `terms_of_service`, `contact` (`OpenApiContact`) and `license`
  (`OpenApiLicense`), read from `<PREFIX>_OPENAPI__SUMMARY`, `__TERMS_OF_SERVICE`,
  `__CONTACT_NAME`/`_URL`/`_EMAIL` and `__LICENSE_NAME`/`_IDENTIFIER`/`_URL`; a
  license named by both an SPDX identifier and a URL, or by either without a
  name, is refused at boot, as OpenAPI 3.1 makes them.
- **`security` is read off the route's guards** (*A route's document lists what
  its guards answer*, below). **`servers` is stated** at the origin root too
  (`/`), so a generator has a base URL to read.
- **The headers sent on a route's behalf are documented**: the list's `Link`, a
  deprecated route's `Deprecation`, and each `#[response_header]` with its value
  (`const`), except `Set-Cookie` or a name sent twice, which no one value
  describes.
- **A component no operation reaches is not published.** A `Query<T>` or
  `Header<T>` payload is expanded into parameters, and its definition was left in
  `components.schemas`, so a generator emitted a type no call used.
- **CORS exposes `Link`, `Location` and `Retry-After` without being told.** None
  is CORS-safelisted, so a frontend on another origin could not read the next
  page, a created resource's address or a rate limit's wait unless the
  deployment listed them in `<PREFIX>_HTTP__CORS_EXPOSED_HEADERS`, which still adds to
  them.
- **The health probes** carry a summary and their `ProbeReport` schema.

**The Swagger UI page loads nothing from elsewhere.** Swagger UI's default
online-validator badge made the browser fetch an image from `validator.swagger.io`
carrying the document's URL whenever the page was served under a host other than
`localhost` — an internal API's address handed to a third party on every visit.
The initializer sets `validatorUrl: "none"`, moves out of the page into
`api/swagger-initializer.js`, and the page carries its own
`Content-Security-Policy`: its own scripts, its own origin, no framing; a
deployment's `<PREFIX>_HTTP__SECURITY_HEADERS_CONTENT_SECURITY_POLICY` still replaces
it. Swagger UI moves from 5.32.6 to 5.33.1.

**Breaking for code building `nest_rs::http::HttpRouteMeta` by hand**:
`#[routes]` builds it, and it gains `error_responses`, `sets_next_link`,
`response_headers` and `deprecation`.

### A route's document lists what its guards answer, read off the guards themselves

The OpenAPI document guessed what a route's guards refuse: `401` and `403` on
every guarded route whatever its guards answered, and `429` only when a
`#[use_guards]` path ended in the word `ThrottlerGuard`. So the documented
global binding, `use_guards_global([guard::<ThrottlerGuard>()])`, an alias
(`use … as Limit`) or a wrapper documented no `429`; a `#[public]` route under
a global throttler none either; the `503` `AuthnGuard` answers on every route
when its strategy could not evaluate a credential appeared nowhere; and a
quota or API-key guard of your own could not get its status into the document
at all.

- **Added: `Guard::denial_statuses(&self, public: bool) -> &'static [u16]`**,
  the statuses `check_http` can refuse a request with on a route that is
  `#[public]` or not, read off the guard's `Err` paths. Implementing it is
  optional: the default, `401` and `403` off a `#[public]` route and nothing on
  one, is what a guard that says nothing was documented with. A `500` is never
  declared — it is a wiring defect, and every route documents it. A quota guard
  refusing with `Denial::rate_limited` declares:

  ```rust
  impl Guard for QuotaGuard {
      fn denial_statuses(&self, _public: bool) -> &'static [u16] {
          &[429]
      }
  }
  ```
- **The framework's guards declare theirs**: `AuthnGuard` a `503` on every
  route and a `401` off a `#[public]` one, `ThrottlerGuard` a `429` on every
  route, `AbilityGuard` none — its one refusal of a request is a wiring `500`.
- **Fixed: a route documents what its guard chain declares** — the app's pool,
