  its controller's and its own guards, as the route runs them, whatever each is
  named. The document reads the chain the route composed when it mounted, never
  a second composition. `429` and `503` carry their `Retry-After`, `401` its
  `WWW-Authenticate` challenge. The app's pool lives on the builder, outside
  the module tree, so an end-to-end suite repeats `main`'s `use_guards_global`
  line on `TestApp::builder()`, or its routes run, and its document reads,
  without those guards.
- **Fixed: `403` is documented where an ability can be refused** — a route a
  mask arms (`#[authorize]`, `Authorize<A, E>`, `Bind<A, S>`) — or where a
  guard declares it, no longer on every guarded route.
- **`security: [bearerAuth]` marks a route whose guards can answer `401`**; a
  route behind a throttler alone requires no credential. `#[public]` still
  states `security: []`, and a route nothing guards still states nothing.
- **`nestrs g auth`'s development-only guard (`nest_rs::authn::DevelopmentOnlyGuard`)
  declares the `403`** it answers on its
  `#[public]` token route, and the demo's post-author and transcode guards
  declare theirs.

**Breaking:** `HttpRouteMeta::throttled()` is removed: a route's guards
declare their statuses, and the document reads the declaration, never a type's
name.

### Static files and single-page apps are served beside the API

- **`StaticFilesModule` serves a front end's build and public files from the
  API's own binary** (`nest_rs::static_files`, feature `static-files`).
  `StaticFilesModule::for_root(StaticFilesConfig { root, .. })` reads the
  directory `<PREFIX>_STATIC_FILES__ROOT` names, resolved at boot;
  `for_root(Embedded::of::<WebAssets>())` serves a folder `#[derive(Embed)]`
  compiles into the binary, its `crate_path = "nest_rs::static_files::rust_embed"`
  keeping `nest-rs` the manifest's one line. The embedded folder is code, with no
  variable, so both travel in one value, `(Embedded::of::<WebAssets>(), config)`.
  A directory set beside an
  embedded folder, and one unset, missing or not a directory, fails the boot
  naming every way to set it, never its value; two embedded folders fail it naming
  both imports. `PATH`, `SPA_FALLBACK` and `MAX_AGE_SECS` complete the namespace,
  each settable in code too; the lifetime runs from a second to a year.
- **The files answer what nothing else claims.** They are the router's fallback
  (`HttpFallbackMeta`, above): every route and self-mount answers first, a path
  under a controller's prefix, a self-mount's path or the global prefix is its
  owner's `404`, and the files sit outside the global prefix — the API at `/api`,
  the app at `/`.
- **What is served is held to a public folder's defaults**: `GET` and `HEAD`
  alone (`405` with `Allow` otherwise), no name beginning with `.` but a leading
  `/.well-known/` (RFC 8615), a path decoded once and strictly (`..`, an encoded
  `/` or `\`, a control character are a `404`, an unreadable escape a `400`),
  every file resolved through its links and kept under the root, regular files
  alone, and no listing. A strong `ETag` and `Last-Modified` answer `304` and
  `412`, one `Range` `206` or `416`, and `If-Range` sends a changed file whole. An
  HTML document is always revalidated. A climb out of the root, and a link leaving
  it, are a `warn` on `nest_rs::static_files` with a `reason` and never the path.
- **`SPA_FALLBACK` answers a browser's navigation, and only that**, with the
  root's `index.html`: a request whose `Accept` names `text/html`, or whose
  `Sec-Fetch-Mode` is `navigate`, for a path no file matches and whose last
  segment names no file. Every other miss is the framework's `404` problem
  document, so an API client never parses a page, and both answers carry
  `Vary: Accept, Sec-Fetch-Mode`.
- **A request is one lookup.** An embedded folder is a table built at boot, each
  file with its `ETag`, type and date ready; a directory is resolved, opened and —
  up to 64 KiB — read in one blocking call, a larger file streamed in 64 KiB
  chunks.
- **rust-embed 8.13 joins the tree**, the market's way to fold a folder into a
  Rust binary, built with `debug-embed` so a debug build serves what a release
  does. A file's type is read off a table of the types IANA registers for a
  front end's files, `application/octet-stream` for the rest, rather than
  through `mime_guess`, whose last release is past the freshness bar.

### `nest_rs::http` has an HTTP vocabulary of its own: `Request`, `Response`, `Body`, `HttpError`, `Endpoint`

nestrs's HTTP surface was poem's: a handler, a guard, an interceptor or a test
named `poem::Request`, `poem::Response` and `poem::Error`, so poem's major was
nestrs's and no other server could sit underneath. `nest_rs::http` now holds
the vocabulary the rest of 7.0 moves onto, written so that it names no server
library:

- **The `http` crate's types, re-exported as their one path**: `Method`,
  `StatusCode`, `Uri`, `Version`, `HeaderMap`, `HeaderName`, `HeaderValue`,
  `Extensions`, `Scheme`, the `header` constants, and the two heads as
  `RequestParts` and `ResponseParts`; `Bytes` beside them.
- **`Request`**: the `http` head, a `Body`, and what the connection knows —
  `peer_addr()`, `local_addr()`, `scheme()`, `original_uri()` (the URI as sent,
  before the edge's trailing-slash trim or a version rewrite), `route()` and
  `path_param(name)` once a router matched it. `into_http()` hands it to a
  library that speaks `http`, and `Request::from` takes it back with nothing
  lost. A `&Request` may cross an `.await` in a `Send` future: a body that is
  not `Sync` is made so without a lock per frame. Its `Debug` names the
  headers, never their values.
- **`Response`** and **`ResponseBuilder`**: the builder takes typed names and
  values only, so a header that does not parse can no longer be dropped
  silently, which poem's builder did.
- **`Body`**: `into_bytes_limit(n)` refuses a declared size past `n` before
  reading anything, and stops at the frame that crosses it.
- **`HttpError`**, the edge's error beside `WsError`, `QueueError` and the
  rest: a status, the error that caused it (`is`, `downcast_ref`, `downcast`,
  and its `source()` for the log) and typed extensions copied onto the response
  it renders, each replacing the response's own value of its type. It renders
  an RFC 9457 problem document directly — a `4xx` detail said without any value
  it quotes, no detail on a `5xx`, `WWW-Authenticate: Bearer` on a `401` —
  where a poem error became text first and was read back. An `anyhow::Error`
  answers an opaque `500` and keeps its whole chain for the log. A type
  implementing **`ResponseError`** converts with `?` and answers its own status;
  `ProblemDetails` is one, and `BodyError` (`TooLarge` 413, `Stalled` 408,
  `AlreadyTaken` 500, `NotUtf8` and `Io` 400) another.
- **`Endpoint`**, `BoxEndpoint` and `endpoint_fn`: what answers a request, as
  an `async fn call(&self, Request) -> Result<Response>`; **`FromRequest`** and
  `RequestBody`: what a handler parameter reads, the body at most once — a
  parameter that may fail reads `Result<T>` and sees the refusal, and there is
  no `Option<T>` reading that would turn a refusal into `None`; **`IntoResponse`**
  and `Result<T>` (whose error defaults to `HttpError`).

`#[routes]`, the guards, the interceptors and `nest_rs::testing` speak this
vocabulary, each move in the sections that follow with its upgrading row. The
names are the ones 6.x users already wrote through poem, so most of those moves
are a path change.

`nest-rs-http` now depends on `http`, `hyper` and `thiserror` directly; each was
already in every application's dependency tree.

### A route is written in OpenAPI's path templating: `#[get("/users/{id}")]`

A route's path was read with poem's grammar — `:id`, `*rest`, a parameter
constrained by a regular expression — so every controller was written in one
router's syntax, the OpenAPI document rewrote it, the span and the rate
limiter's bucket spelled it poem's way, and the next router could not take
over without breaking every app. A route's path is now a **route template**,
OpenAPI's path templating, the spelling the document already printed:

- `{name}` takes one segment and may follow literal text in it (`/@{handle}`);
  nothing follows it in its segment.
- `{*name}` takes the rest of the path, never empty, and comes last.
- `{{` and `}}` are literal braces.

What you write is what is served, logged and documented: the boot's `mounted
route` line, the OpenAPI path, the span's `http.route` and the throttler's
bucket all read `/users/{id}`. A controller's own `path` may hold parameters too
(`/orgs/{org}/members`).

`nest_rs::http::RouteTemplate` reads a template at boot the way `#[routes]`
reads a literal — `parse`, `as_str`, `parameters`, `catch_all`, refusing with a
`RouteTemplateError` in the same words — so a template from config is held to
the same grammar.

**Breaking:**

- `#[get]`, `#[post]`, `#[put]`, `#[patch]`, `#[delete]` and `#[sse]` refuse the
  6.x spellings at compile time, each with the 7.0 one written out —
  ``#[get] `/users/:id`: `:id` is the 6.x parameter syntax — write `/users/{id}` ``,
  and the same for `*rest`. A parameter with a pattern (`:id<\d+>`, `{id:\d+}`) is
  refused: parse it with its type (`Path<u64>`) or a pipe. So are text after a
  parameter in its segment (`/{id}.json`) and a catch-all before the end.
  `#[crud]` mounts `/{id}`.
- `#[controller(path)]` reads the same grammar and holds no catch-all.
  `#[gateway(path)]` and `#[mcp(path)]` are literal: a parameter, a brace, or a
  `:`, `*` or `<` is a compile error.
- A self-mount's path (the GraphQL path from `<PREFIX>_GRAPHQL__PATH`, a
  gateway's), a router fallback's path and the global prefix
  (`<PREFIX>_HTTP__GLOBAL_PREFIX`) are literal, checked at boot: a template fails
  it, naming the owner, where poem used to panic. `literal_mount_path` refuses
  `{` and `}`.
- Two spellings of one address across two controllers (`/parcels/{id}` and
  `/parcels/{key}`) fail the boot naming both handlers; poem mounted them as two
  nodes, and one handler never ran.
- The throttler's HTTP bucket key and its `rate limit exceeded` line carry the
  declared template (`/users/{id}`): a deployment's in-flight counters start
  fresh once, at the upgrade. Counters are ephemeral; nothing is dropped.
- The OpenAPI document now prints a parameter after literal text
  (`/@{handle}`), which it used to leave out; a catch-all and a literal brace
  are still left out with a `warn`.
- `HttpRouteMeta::path` holds the route's template (`/{id}`), where it held
  poem's spelling (`/:id`).

### A handler that panics is answered `500`, and its connection serves on

A handler that unwound took its connection down with it: over HTTP/1 the client
read a reset and no status, and every request it had kept alive on that
connection went with it; over HTTP/2 its stream was reset `CANCEL`. The process
hook filed the panic beside the request's `outcome="panic"` line, and the server
filed a second `error` for the task it caught.

- **The edge contains everything it dispatches** — the handler, its guards,
  pipes, extractors and interceptors, a WebSocket upgrade request — through
  `nest_rs::core::panic::contain`, and answers `500` with the same opaque
  `application/problem+json` document every other `5xx` answers: no `detail`,
  nothing of the payload. The security headers and `traceresponse` are stamped on
  it like on any answer, and the connection serves its next request (HTTP/1.1
  keep-alive, HTTP/2's other streams).
- **One `error` says it**, on `nest_rs::http`: `a handler panicked; answered 500`,
  with the payload under `panic` (through `panic_message`, serde's quoted values
  cut) and `panic_location`. The process hook stays quiet, and neither `a
  connection task panicked` nor `an HTTP/2 stream task panicked` fires: those now
  only name a defect outside the edge. The request's `http.request` line files
  `outcome="panic"` with `status=500` and its `bytes`, and its span carries
  `http.route`, `http.response.status_code=500` and `error.type=panic` — with the
  access log off too.
- **A body that panics while it is written** — an `#[sse]` stream, a download, an
  MCP `GET` stream — ends there with an error, since its framing cannot be
  finished: over HTTP/1 its connection closes, over HTTP/2 its stream alone is
  reset. One `error` says it (`a response body panicked; it ended unfinished`),
  the line files `outcome="panic"` with the `status` it was answering and the
  bytes written, and no other connection is touched.
- **A GraphQL root field that panics over HTTP** still files its `graphql.field`
  `panic` line, and its request is now answered `500` rather than reset.
- **A panic a unit catches itself is said.** Inside a contained unit the hook
  stays quiet and leaves the panic to the unit; one the unit's own code caught
  with `catch_unwind` and never raised again was therefore said by no one, where
  Rust's default hook used to print it. The hook's event — `panicked where no
  unit of work contains it` on `nest_rs::app`, redacted, with `panic_location` and
  `backtrace` when `RUST_BACKTRACE` asks — is now filed for it when the poll that
  caught it ends.

### A header a route sets is its own; the edge's defaults fill what it left unset

The edge stamped every default response header with replace semantics, so a
route could not opt out of a deployment's value: a
`#[response_header("cross-origin-resource-policy", "cross-origin")]` on an
embeddable image was replaced by the deployment's `same-origin`, and a global
`<PREFIX>_HTTP__SECURITY_HEADERS_CONTENT_SECURITY_POLICY` replaced the OpenAPI page's
own, stricter policy, which then broke the Swagger UI.

- **Each default is written only where the response has none**: the security
  headers (`X-Content-Type-Options`, `X-Frame-Options`, `Referrer-Policy`, the
  `Cross-Origin-*` trio, HSTS, `Content-Security-Policy`, `Permissions-Policy`)
  and `Server`. A value a handler, a response decorator or a surface set is kept
  whole; a response without one gets the deployment's, as before. Same cost, one
  lookup per header.
- **The OpenAPI page keeps its `Content-Security-Policy` under a global one**, and
  every other response gets the deployment's. No default CSP ships in 7.0: static
  files serve the app's own documents, and `default-src 'none'` would break every
  single-page app the framework serves; framing stays refused by
  `X-Frame-Options: DENY`.
- A handler that sets an insecure value on purpose keeps it: that is the visible
  line an opening is.

### The HTTP transport is built from `HttpConfig` alone; the imperative mount and its strict switch are gone

`HttpModule::for_root(HttpConfig)` configured the transport, and so did a public
builder beside it: `HttpTransport::new()`, `Default`, and `api_versioning`,
`security_headers`, `fail_secure_strict`, `global_prefix`, `server_header`,
`bind`, `max_body_bytes`, `request_timeout`, `shutdown_timeout`, `cors`,
`compression`, `tls` and `mount`. Two ways to configure one module is what the
`for_root` seam exists to refuse, and `mount` was the one hole the global guard
pool could not reach: an endpoint the transport could neither shape nor read a
posture from, with a strict switch to refuse it under active global guards.

- **`HttpTransport::from_config(&HttpConfig)` is the one constructor**, the call
  `HttpModule::for_root` makes; `take_endpoint` stays for in-process drivers.
  Every setter, `new` and `Default` left the public API.
- **`mount` is gone**, with `<PREFIX>_HTTP__FAIL_SECURE_STRICT` and
  `HttpConfig::fail_secure_strict`, which guarded it alone. A surface beyond
  the route table is a self-mount, an `HttpEndpointMeta` declaring its posture:
  `Guarded` gets the global guard pool at its edge, `Exempt` gates in band or is
  public by design. A deployment still setting the variable boots, and the boot
  names it once at `warn` as a variable no config reads.
- **`VersionSelector` is crate-private**: nothing public took one once
  `api_versioning` went; the strategy is `HttpConfig::versioning`.
- **`TestAppBuilder::http(transport)` is gone.** The harness boots the
  transport the app's own `HttpModule::for_root(cfg)` resolves, as before; a
  suite that needs another value seeds the whole config with
  `TestApp::builder().provide(HttpConfig { .. })`, which the harness boots from
  in place of the module's. A WebSocket suite (`build_ws`) serves that config
  on a reserved local port.
- **`Server: nestrs/<version>` stays behind `HttpConfig::server_header`**; the
  builder's free-form `server_header(&'static str)` had no caller outside its
  own test.

### The transport's one constructor holds every range it reads, and a body-read gap at or past the request timeout is said at boot

- **`HttpTransport::from_config` refuses a `shutdown_timeout` or a
  `request_timeout` outside its range** — 1 to 3600 s, a pinned zero refused,
  `None` the budget's off — naming the variable and the field, as the
  environment read does. A config built in code (a seed, a direct call) never
  met that read: `Duration::MAX` reached the shutdown arithmetic and panicked.
- **`HttpTransport::from_config` refuses a `version_header` that is no HTTP
  header name**, whatever the strategy, naming
  `<PREFIX>_HTTP__VERSION_HEADER` and `HttpConfig::version_header`; so does the
  environment read for a pinned value the variable leaves in place. Under
  `versioning: Header` such a value booted on the URI strategy while the
  OpenAPI document described the header.
- **A `body_read_timeout` at or past the `request_timeout` files one boot
  `warn`** on `nest_rs::http`, `the body-read timeout reaches the request
  timeout: a body that stops arriving is answered the request timeout's 503,
  never 408`, with `body_read_timeout_secs` and `request_timeout_secs`. The
  budget counts from the request's arrival, so such a gap never fires first.
  Nothing is said with the budget off, where the gap is the body's one bound.

### A `*` in a CORS list admits every origin, method and header

`HttpCors`' documentation calls `*` the wildcard, but it was handed to poem as
a literal: `<PREFIX>_HTTP__CORS_ORIGINS=*` refused every cross-origin request
with `403`, and a `*` in `<PREFIX>_HTTP__CORS_METHODS` or `…_HEADERS` refused
every preflight. A `*` in any of the three lists now admits every value. With
`<PREFIX>_HTTP__CORS_CREDENTIALS=true` a `*` still fails the boot, as before.

### The transport above the router speaks nestrs's HTTP vocabulary

The accept loop now hands each request to the assembled stack as a
`nest_rs::http::Request` — its peer, local address, scheme, the URI as sent and
the instant it arrived, with hyper's `OnUpgrade` left in its extensions — and the
edge, the problem normalizer and the response body's carrier all take and answer
nestrs's `Request`, `Response` and `Body`, as do the router, CORS and compression
around the edge.

**Breaking**, for code that drove a transport by hand:

- `HttpTransport::take_endpoint()` returns a `nest_rs::http::BoxEndpoint`.
  `TestApp` keeps its client; a suite that boots a transport itself drives the
  endpoint through `Endpoint::call`.
- `HttpCors::into_middleware` is gone: the transport turns the policy into its
  layer at boot, refusing the same values with the same sentences.
- `nest_rs::http::current_body_limit()` returns `usize`: the transport's cap on a
  request it serves, `RawBody::DEFAULT_LIMIT` off one. The `unwrap_or` a reader
  wrote is gone with the `Option`.
- `nest_rs::http::normalize_error_response` takes and returns
  `nest_rs::http::Response`.

### CORS answers vary on `Origin`, and compression reads `Accept-Encoding` as RFC 9110 writes it

- **Every answer under a CORS policy carries `Vary: Origin`**, a preflight and
  the `403` refusing an origin the policy does not list included. poem's layer said so only for a wildcard pattern, so a shared cache
  could serve one listed origin's answer, `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` and all,
  to another (WHATWG Fetch, *CORS protocol and HTTP caches*).
- **Compression is negotiated by weight** (RFC 9110 §12.5.3): whitespace around
  `;q=` is read, a coding refused with `q=0` is never used, an `identity`
  weighed above every coding answers unencoded, and `zstd` is offered under its
  own token. A response compression could have encoded carries
  `Vary: Accept-Encoding`.
- **An event stream is never compressed**: a compressor held each event back
  until its buffer filled. A `204`, a `304` and a response that already names
  its `Content-Encoding` are left as written too.
- **A partial answer is never compressed**: a `206`, or any response carrying
  `Content-Range`, was encoded while its range still counted the unencoded
  bytes (RFC 9110 §14.4), so a client reassembling a download from ranges —
  `nest_rs::static_files` answers them — read the wrong offsets.
- **An encoded answer's entity tag is weak**: a strong `ETag` names one
  representation's exact bytes, which a content coding changes (RFC 9110
  §8.8.3), and the encoded body went out under the plain one's. Compression
  now sends it `W/"…"`, which `If-None-Match` still matches, and a `304`
  carries the tag and the `Vary: Accept-Encoding` its encoded `200` would
  (§15.4.5), so a cache updates the entry it holds. A weak tag matches no
  `If-Match` or `If-Range`, which compare strongly: a write guarded by the tag
  of an encoded read is refused, and a resumed download restarts whole.

### HTTP/2: a response's tail stays under the send deadline, and its stream task is cut at the window's close

- **The last bytes of an HTTP/2 response no longer go out under the idle
  deadline.** hyper hands h2 a frame as soon as the window has room for a byte of
  it, so up to one 64 KiB piece of a response a peer stopped granting window to
  waited past its stream's task, and its send deadline, unbounded until the
  connection went idle. A body's last byte is now handed on its own, so the
  stream waits for the window on the rest and is reset at `SEND_TIMEOUT_SECS`.
  Each HTTP/2 response with a body carries one more `DATA` frame.
- **A stream's task outliving the shutdown window is cut at its close**, with the
  connections, and the transport waits for it to unwind, at most the half-second
  settle the way down already gives what it stopped; it ran detached until it
  ended on its own. One that still runs then is said once at `warn` on
  `nest_rs::http`, `HTTP/2 stream tasks still ran after their connections were
  cut; they are left to end on their own`, with `streams`.

### The request budget counts from the request's arrival

`<PREFIX>_HTTP__REQUEST_TIMEOUT_SECS` is counted from the instant the accept loop
handed the request over, where hyper had read its head, rather than from the
moment the edge first ran it — with CORS or compression on too, whose layers run
before the edge. The access line's duration counts from the same instant.

### One route table, `HttpRoutes`, which the transport reads whole

Every controller and every surface that mounts beside the controllers — a
GraphQL schema, an MCP endpoint, a WebSocket gateway, the OpenAPI document —
now registers its entries on one table, `nest_rs::http::HttpRoutes`, in
nestrs's route grammar: `routes.route(Method::POST, "/rpc", endpoint)` for one
method, `routes.route_any("/mcp", endpoint)` for a protocol that dispatches on
the method itself. The transport sees each address, each method and who
registered it, so what poem's `Route` left opaque is now checked at boot, and
the server library stays behind the transport: the table owns the method
dispatch, the global prefix, the version rewrite and the fallback, and the
adapter only matches paths.

**Breaking**, for a surface or package that mounts on the transport (an app
mounts through the decorators and modules, which do this for it):

- `Controller::mount(container, routes: &mut HttpRoutes)` and
  `HttpControllerMeta::new(…, |container, routes| …)` register on the table
  instead of returning a poem `Route`.
- `HttpEndpointMeta::new(handler, path, label, |container, routes| { … })`
  registers its entries on the table. An entry sees the URI the client sent,
  the global prefix included, and the template it was registered under
  (`Request::route`, `Request::path_param`).
- **A self-mount registering a path it did not declare fails the boot**,
  naming the surface, the path and what it declared: an entry answers at the
  mount's `path` or below it, a subtree registered as `{path}/{*rest}` — before, the
  closure could mount anywhere, out of reach of every collision check. Every
  entry it registers, below its path included, is served as sent whatever API
  version a caller states: a versioned catch-all never takes it.
- `HttpFallbackMeta::new(path, owner, |container| endpoint)` builds the one
  `BoxEndpoint` handed every request the fallback answers, whole.
- `MethodTable`, `AllowedMethods`, `matched` and `Matched` leave the API: the
  table answers a method an address does not serve with `405` and its `Allow`,
  and records the matched template for every entry.
- `HttpView::route()` returns `Option<&str>`.
- **A self-mount the guard pool gates at its edge runs the pool once routed**,
  on each entry it registered, so a guard reads the template the request
  matched, as on a controller's route. A path under the mount that none of its
  entries answers is the router's `404`, the pool not run — as for any route.
- **The global prefix is applied at registration**: every address in the table
  is the one a client calls. A handler's `uri()` under `<PREFIX>_HTTP__GLOBAL_PREFIX`
  now carries the prefix (`/api/users/7`); `caller_path` read it already.
- A refusal of the table — a template outside the grammar, two shapes of one
  address, an address claimed by two registrants, one method registered twice,
  an undeclared path — names who registered what. The collision sentences the
  transport already said are unchanged.

### `HEAD`, path parameters and sub-paths, as the table answers them

- **`HEAD` on a `GET` route answers the `GET`'s header fields with no
  content**, `Content-Length` included when the content's size is known (RFC
  9110 §9.3.2), in process as on the wire.
- **A path parameter whose percent-decoded bytes are not UTF-8 answers `400`**,
  a problem document naming the parameter, never its value, before the
  handler runs. The router decodes a parameter once: `%2F` is a `/` inside the
  value.
- **GraphQL answers at its path alone**: `/graphql/anything` was served by the
  schema's endpoint, and is now `404`. MCP already answered at its path alone.
- **A self-mount's rate-limit bucket is its template** (`/files/{*path}`),
  never the path a caller addressed, so one budget covers the mount's whole
  subtree. Those buckets start afresh once.
- **A request the router fallback answers is named for its method alone**, as
  any request no route matched: the fallback holds no route template.
- **A malformed API-version header is filed at `debug`**, no longer `warn`:
  the line is the client's, and the access line already records its `400`.

### The extractors read `nest_rs::http::Request`: `Json`, `Path`, `Query`, `Form` and `Multipart` are nestrs's own

`Json`, `Path` and `Query` in a handler were poem's, and so was every
extractor's trait: a handler parameter could only be read off poem's request.
`nest_rs::http` now holds the extractors themselves, each implementing
`nest_rs::http::FromRequest`:

- **`Json<T>`** reads a body whose `Content-Type` is `application/json` or an
  `application/*+json` type, under the transport's cap, and answers `T` as
  `application/json; charset=utf-8`. A body of another type answers `415`, one
  past the cap `413`, one that does not decode `400` — said without the value
  it held. A value that does not serialize answers an opaque `500` and files
  the failure at `error`.
- **`Path<T>`** reads the route's parameters, each percent-decoded once by the
  router: one value, a tuple by position, a struct by name. A parameter whose
  text is not what its type reads answers `400` naming the parameter and what
  was expected (`path parameter `id` is not an integer`), never its value; a
  handler reading a parameter its route does not declare, or another number of
  them — a tuple shorter than the route included, which 6.x bound to the
  route's first parameters — is a signature mistake: it answers an opaque
  `500`, where 6.x answered `400`, and files the mistake at `error` on
  `nest_rs::http`, naming the parameter, never its value.
- **`Query<T>`** and **`Form<T>`** decode `application/x-www-form-urlencoded`,
  the query string or a posted form (on `GET`, the query), and refuse exactly
  as 6.x did — the same status and the same detail, said without the value.
  A posted form whose `Content-Type` is not a form's answers `415` without
  quoting the type the client sent, which 6.x quoted.
- **`Multipart`** reads a `multipart/form-data` body part by part; each
  **`Field`** has its `name`, `file_name`, `content_type`, and `bytes()`,
  `text()` or `into_byte_stream()` — the last replacing the `PartExt`
  extension on poem's field.

Two parameters of one handler reading the body are the other signature
mistake: the second read answers an opaque `500` and is filed at `error` on
`nest_rs::http` too.

`Ambient<T>`, `Scoped<T>`, `Header<T>`, `RawBody`, `ClientIp` and
`nest_rs_seaorm::Bind<A, S>` implement the vocabulary's `FromRequest`, and
`Header<T>` is a `nest_rs::pipes::Source`, as `Json`, `Query`, `Form` and `Path`
are, so a `Piped<P, E>` reads it: a handler written with `#[routes]` reads every
parameter through them (*A handler is written in nestrs's HTTP vocabulary*,
below).

### An `#[sse]` stream is framed by nestrs, and no line break in an event opens a field

`SseEvent` was poem's, and poem wrote each event as it found it: a lone `\r` in
the data, which an `EventSource` reads as a line break, passed through, so a
payload holding `"a\revent: admin"` reached the browser as an `admin` event;
a line break in an `id` or an event type did the same. `SseEvent` is now
nestrs's own and `text/event-stream` is written by nestrs (WHATWG HTML §9.2):

- every line of the data, split on `\r\n`, `\n` or `\r` as the browser splits
  them, goes on a `data:` line of its own, and a trailing line break reaches
  the client, which 6.x dropped;
- an `id` or an event type holds one line: a line break in it is dropped;
- `.retry(Duration)` sets the reconnection delay on an event, in whole
  milliseconds; an event with no data sets its `id` or its retry hint and
  dispatches nothing, as before;
- **`SseEvent::json(&payload)`** (new) writes a payload as the JSON a listener
  parses. A payload that does not serialize sends no event in its place: the
  failure is filed at `error` on `nest_rs::http` with its chain, `a JSON answer
  did not serialize`, as a `Json` answer's is, and the stream ends there with
  an error, so the browser reads no made-up state and its `EventSource`
  reconnects. The demo's transcode stream wrote `{"state":"error"}` in its
  place.

An ordinary stream is byte for byte what 6.x wrote, its keep-alive comment,
connection ceiling and headers included. poem's `sse` feature leaves the
workspace's pin.

**Breaking:** `SseEvent::retry(u64)`, poem's constructor of a retry-only
event, is gone, and `SseEvent` is a struct, no longer poem's enum: write
`SseEvent::message("").retry(Duration::from_millis(ms))`, and read nothing back
out of an event.

### A handler is written in nestrs's HTTP vocabulary

A `#[routes]` handler took poem's extractors, returned `poem::Result`, and its errors
implemented poem's `ResponseError`: the HTTP edge's most-written code named a private
dependency, so poem's next minor would have been a nestrs major.

- **`#[routes]` emits a `nest_rs::http::Endpoint`.** Every parameter is read through
  `nest_rs::http::FromRequest`, every answer goes through `nest_rs::http::IntoResponse`, and
  a handler returns `nest_rs::http::Result<T>` — `Result<T, HttpError>` — or an error of its
  own implementing `nest_rs::http::ResponseError`. A parameter that is not an extractor is
  refused at compile time naming `nest_rs_http::FromRequest`.
- **The extractors are nestrs's alone**: `Json`, `Path`, `Query`, `Form`, `Multipart`,
  `Header`, `RawBody`, `ClientIp`, `Scoped` and the rest, and the prelude's `Json`, `Path`
  and `Query` are these. A `Path<(A, B)>` binds by position, a `Path<S>` of a struct by
  name.
- **The framework's errors and replies answer through the same traits**: `AuthError`,
  `CredentialError`, `TokenError`, sea-orm's `ServiceError` and `#[crud]`'s errors implement
  `nest_rs::http::ResponseError`; `Opaque::opaque` on HTTP returns
  `Result<T, nest_rs::http::HttpError>`.
- **An exception filter catches a handler's error by its type** across the edge, as it did
  when the handler returned a poem error.
- **`AccessTokenResponse` answers itself.** It implements `IntoResponse`, stamping RFC 6749
  §5.1's `Cache-Control: no-store` and `Pragma: no-cache`, and is no longer `Serialize`:
  wrapping it in `Json`, which would drop both headers, is refused at compile time. A
  route returning it documents its body by its type, behind a `Result` or an alias, with
  no `#[api(response = AccessTokenResponse)]`.
- **A `5xx` token refusal files its cause.** `TokenError::Sign` (`500`) and
  `TokenError::Server` (`503`) answer the opaque `server_error`, and file the chain behind
  it once on `nest_rs::oauth::server` — at `error`, at `warn` behind the `503` — where they
  had filed nothing.
- **Breaking:** a handler on poem's types (`poem::web::Json`, `poem::Request`,
  `poem::web::Data`, `poem::Result`) no longer compiles; `nest_rs::http::PartExt` is gone,
  `TokenError` no longer converts into `poem::Error`, `AccessTokenResponse` no longer
  implements `Serialize`, and the poem twins of the extractors are gone.
- **Breaking:** the response-shaping seam and the response helpers move with the handler.
  `RouteResponseShaper::capture` reads a `nest_rs::http::Request`, `ResponseShaping::apply`
  wraps a `RouteFuture` that is now a future of `nest_rs::http::Result<Response>`, and
  `ShapedEndpoint` is a `nest_rs::http::Endpoint` over one; `set_created_location`,
  `set_next_link`, `caller_path` and `challenge::stamp_bearer_error` take nestrs's
  `Response` or `Request` in place of poem's.

### `#[crud]`'s update is `PUT /{id}`

`#[crud]` generated the update as `PATCH`, yet its body, `Update<E>`, carries every writable
field, each required: a client sending one field, as RFC 5789 allows, was refused `400`.

- **The update is `PUT /{id}`**, RFC 9110 §9.3.4's replacement of the writable state;
  `PATCH` at that address answers `405` with an `Allow` naming `PUT`. The OpenAPI document
  states the update as a `put` operation in place of a `patch`, with the same body; the
  GraphQL mutation is unchanged.
- **A `#[crud]` refusal answers its problem document**: a `503` for a statement the database
  cancelled at its bound carries `code` `UNAVAILABLE`, as every other `#[crud]` refusal
  carries its own.
- **Breaking:** a client calling `PATCH /<resource>/{id}` on a `#[crud]` controller calls
  `PUT`, and a client generated from the OpenAPI document regenerates.

### A create answers `Created<T>` and a page `Paginated<T>`, each documented off its type

A create handler erased its return to `Response` to stamp its `Location`: it took a
`&Request` only for `caller_path`, wrote `#[http_code(201)]` beside `set_created_location`,
which say one fact twice, and restated its payload in `#[api(response = T)]` — and its
document declared no `Location`, which `#[routes]` declared only for `#[crud]`'s own
marker. A paginated list was the same shape for its `Link`.

- **`Created::at(id, body)`** (new) answers `201` with `body` and
  `Location: <the path the caller addressed>/<id>`, whoever returns it — a route, a
  self-mount's entry, a route registered by hand — the global prefix and the version kept,
  the client's `Host` never named, as before. The id is now one percent-encoded path segment:
  an id holding a space, `?`, `#` or `/` wrote a `Location` that was no URI, or named another
  resource, and one that is empty, `.` or `..` writes none (logged at `warn`).
  **`Paginated::new(body, first, after)`** (new)
  answers its body, and `Link: <path?first=N&after=CURSOR>; rel="next"` is written, by
  whoever answers it, when `after` is `Some`.

  ```rust
  #[post("/")]
  async fn create(&self, Json(input): Json<CreatePost>) -> Result<Created<Json<Post>>> {
      let post = self.svc.create(input).await?;
      Ok(Created::at(post.id, Json(Post::from(&post))))
  }
  ```

- `#[routes]` documents the status, the `Location`, the `Link` and the body off the return
  type, a type alias or a renamed `Result` included: a `Json<T>` documents `T`, bare, inside
  either answer or inside a `Result`, and a `Json<T>` whose `T` has no schema is a compile
  error, as it was. A typed answer of another crate documents its body the same way (the
  token response does). `#[api(response = T)]` names the body of a handler building its
  own `Response`, and still wins where it is written. A `#[http_code]` or a `#[redirect]`
  still sets the status it names. A `Created` a handler turns into a `Response` keeps its
  `Location`.
- `#[crud]`'s create returns `Created<Json<T>>` and its cursor list `Paginated<…>`, so neither
  takes a `&Request`.
- **Breaking:** `nest_rs::http::{caller_path, set_created_location, set_next_link}` are gone
  from the public API: return `Created<Json<T>>` or `Paginated<Json<Vec<T>>>`. A
  `#[crud]` create needs its entity keyed by a `Uuid`, as its by-id operations already did.

### `#[crud]` takes the service alone, and every `#[expose]` names its wire type

`#[crud]` took the service's field and four types — `entity`, `output`, `create`, `update` —
which the service's `CrudService`, `Creatable` and `Updatable` impls and the entity's
`#[expose]` already declared, so a resource stated its five facts three times and a
mismatched `output =` still compiled. Its default generated `list`, `get` and `delete`, and
`create`/`update` only beside their input type, where the rule said all five.

- **`#[crud(service = UsersService)]`** names the service's type and reads every type off
  it: the entity off `CrudService::Entity`, the inputs off `Creatable::Create` and
  `Updatable::Update`, and the answer off the entity's wire type. Every operation reaches
  the service through the host's `#[inject]` field of that type, whatever it is called, so
  a public host may inject a crate-private service and the block may name its host through
  an alias, an import or another module. A host that injects no such service is refused
  once — "`NotesController` injects no `NotesService`", with the field to add — and the
  field's name, the 6.x spelling (`service = svc`), is refused naming the type to write.
- **`ops` is the one selector**, all five by default: an op whose trait the service does not
  implement is refused — "`NotesService` offers no create: implement `Creatable` for it, or
  drop `create` from `#[crud(ops = …)]`" — so a read-only resource lists its reads
  (`ops = [list, get]`).
- **`nest_rs::seaorm::Exposed`** (new): every `#[expose]` names its wire type as
  `<Entity as Exposed>::Wire`, on every build, `graphql` or not. Only the decorator implements
  it — a hand-written impl would put on the wire a type no `#[expose]` built — and
  `PkLoadable` and `RelatedTo` read the wire type off it.
- The GraphQL operation names and the default OpenAPI summaries are read off the host's name,
  its role suffix dropped and the rest made singular: `UsersResolver` generates `users`,
  `user`, `createUser`, `updateUser` and `deleteUser`, as `output = User` did, and
  `UsersController` documents `List User`.
- **Breaking:** the `entity`, `output`, `create` and `update` keys are refused (`unknown
  #[crud] argument`): drop them. A bare `#[crud(service = …)]` over a service without
  `Creatable`, `Updatable` or `Deletable` fails to compile: list its operations in `ops`. A
  block that left `create` or `update` out, over a service that implements `Creatable` or
  `Updatable`, now serves that write — `POST`/`PUT` or `createX`/`updateX`, behind the block's
  guards and grants — with no compile error: list `ops` to keep it closed.
  `PkLoadable::Wire` and `RelatedTo::Wire` are gone: `<E as Exposed>::Wire`. A resolver named
  apart from its wire type serves new field names (`AlbumsResolver` over `PagedAlbum` serves
  `albums`, where it served `pagedAlbums`), and so does a host named in the singular over a
  noun ending in `s`, which reads as its singular without the `s` (`StatusResolver` serves
  `statu` and `status`, where `output = Status` gave `status` and `statuss`): name it in the
  plural, as a module is (`StatusesResolver`).

### A problem document's `type` is `about:blank`

The framework's problem documents named an RFC 9110 section as `type`, a URI that defines a
status rather than a problem type.

- **Every status the framework answers carries `type: "about:blank"`** and the status's
  phrase as `title`, as RFC 9457 §4.2.1 prescribes — `413` is "Content Too Large" and `422`
  "Unprocessable Content", RFC 9110's names. A problem type of your own keeps its URI.
- **A refused media type never quotes the request's `Content-Type`**: a `Json`, `Form` or
  `Multipart` body under another type answers `415` naming the type it expected, on every
  handler, `#[crud]`'s included. A JSON body that does not decode answers `400` with
  "the JSON body does not decode", followed by where and what, never the value.
- **Breaking:** a client matching `type` on an `rfc-editor.org` URI matches `about:blank`
  and the status.

### The test kit drives an app through its own `TestClient`, and names no poem type

`nest_rs::testing` re-exported poem's test client whole, and `TestApp::http`
returned `TestClient<BoxEndpoint<'static, poem::Response>>`: every suite, and
any package's kit, was written against the server nestrs means to be able to
replace. The kit now owns the client, with poem's names and meaning:

- **`TestClient`** takes any `nest_rs::http::Endpoint`, and has no type
  parameter. `get`, `post`, `put`, `patch`, `delete`, `head`, `options` and
  `request(method, uri)` build a **`TestRequestBuilder`** — `header`,
  `content_type`, `query`, `body`, `body_json`, `form`, `multipart`, `data`,
  then `send`. A request runs through everything a socket's would — the edge,
  the router, the guards, the handler — and an error the endpoint answers is
  rendered as the server renders it. `query(name, &value)` and `form(&value)`
  write a value as poem's did, through its `Serialize`: a struct's fields in
  declaration order, a `None` left out, a float whole (`2.0`, `NaN`).
- **`TestResponse(pub Response)`** keeps `.0`, now `nest_rs::http::Response`,
  and the assertions: `assert_status`, `assert_status_is_ok`, `assert_header`,
  `assert_header_all`, `assert_header_exist`, `assert_header_is_not_exist`,
  `assert_content_type`, `assert_text`, `assert_bytes`, `assert_json`, and
  `json()`.
- **`TestJson`** walks the body as before — `value()`, then **`TestJsonValue`**,
  **`TestJsonObject`** and **`TestJsonArray`** (`object`, `array`, `string`,
  `i64`, `get`, `get_opt`, `len`, `iter`, `deserialize`, `assert_string`) —
  and serializes as the document it read.
- **`TestForm`** and **`TestFormField`** write a `multipart/form-data` body
  (`text`, `bytes`, `field`; a field's `name` and `filename`).
- **The helpers of `nest_rs::testing::mcp`** take `&TestClient`.

A failure now names what differed: the status, the header, or the JSON path
the walk stood at (`` `$.data.items[2]` has no key `id` ``). A malformed URI,
header name or header value panics naming it, where poem's panic named
nothing; a header value is never quoted, since it may be a credential. So
does a query parameter or a form field holding what a field cannot hold — a
sequence, a struct. A query parameter holding `None` or a non-finite float,
which poem's client panicked on, now writes nothing or the float, as a form
field does.

A request has no peer address, as poem's in-process requests had none, so
`ClientIp` reads `0.0.0.0` and no forwarding header is believed. **New:**
`TestRequestBuilder::peer_addr(addr)` sends a request as if from `addr`, which
is how a suite proves what a trusted proxy may forward
(`<PREFIX>_HTTP__TRUSTED_PROXIES`).

**Breaking:** `TestClient`, `TestRequestBuilder`, `TestResponse`, `TestJson`
and `TestForm` are nestrs's types; `TestJsonValue`, `TestJsonObject`,
`TestJsonArray` and `TestFormField` are exported beside them. `TestApp::http`
returns `&TestClient`. Only what suites call was kept: poem's
`default_header`, `typed_header`, `sse_stream`, `assert_header_csv`,
`assert_xml`, `assert_yaml`, the `f64`, `bool` and array readers and the
`assert_*` of the JSON values but `assert_string` are gone. `resp.0` is
nestrs's `Response`: its body reads with `into_bytes` and `into_string`
(poem's `into_vec` and `into_json` are not there: compare
`into_bytes().await?` or read `resp.json()`), and a header with
`headers().get(name)`.

### poem's `test` feature leaves the build, and with it the MPL-2.0 exception

No manifest asks for poem's `test` feature any more, so `sse-codec`, its
MPL-2.0 dependency, leaves the dependency graph, and `deny.toml` loses the one
licence exception it held for it.

### WebSockets open on nestrs's own upgrade, and `nest_rs::ws::CloseCode` is nestrs's

A gateway and the graphql-ws socket upgraded through poem's `WebSocket`
extractor, and `nest_rs::ws::CloseCode` was poem's enum, re-exported with the
whole of poem at `nest_rs::ws::poem`. Both sockets now open on the HTTP
transport's own handshake and speak the protocol through tokio-tungstenite
0.30, over the connection the transport's server hands across: the socket
keeps its place under `<PREFIX>_HTTP__MAX_CONCURRENT_CONNECTIONS` and its
`<PREFIX>_HTTP__SEND_TIMEOUT_SECS`, as before.

- **`nest_rs::ws::CloseCode` is a nestrs enum** with the variants 6.x matched
  — `Normal`, `Away`, `Protocol`, `Unsupported`, `Status`, `Abnormal`,
  `Invalid`, `Policy`, `Size`, `Extension`, `Error`, `Restart`, `Again`,
  `Reserved(u16)` — now `#[non_exhaustive]`, with `From<u16>` and
  `u16::from` as before; every code round-trips, an application's `4000`–`4999`
  through `Reserved`. `CloseCode::Away` still reads.
- **`nest_rs::ws::poem` is gone.** Nothing on a gateway's surface takes a poem
  type any more; `nest_rs::ws::nest_rs_http` stays.
- **A message past `<PREFIX>_WS__MAX_MESSAGE_BYTES` closes the socket
  `1009 Message Too Big`** (RFC 6455 §7.4.1), where it closed `1011`: the
  protocol layer's refusal now reaches the gateway by type. The `debug` line
  `websocket read error` names the size and carries `close_code = 1009`. A
  read that fails on broken framing still closes `1011`, and so does a connect
  hook that panics.
- **A handshake the server cannot accept answers a problem document** naming
  what it lacks, never what was sent: `400` for one sent as a `HEAD`, which
  the route table answers as its `GET` (RFC 6455 §4.1: a handshake is a
  `GET`), and for one without `Upgrade: websocket`, without `Connection:
  Upgrade`, or without one `Sec-WebSocket-Key` of 16 bytes in base64; `426`
  with `Sec-WebSocket-Version: 13` for another version (RFC 6455 §4.4). A
  gateway registers for `GET` alone, so any other method answers the route
  table's `405` with `Allow: GET, HEAD`, before the browser-origin check and
  the guards. A request over a connection that cannot switch protocols —
  HTTP/1.0, or the in-process test client — answers `426`, where poem
  answered `500`.
- **`Upgrade` is read as the list it is** (RFC 9110 §7.8): `Upgrade: h2c,
  websocket` asks for a WebSocket, where poem admitted only the whole value
  `websocket`, and the browser-origin check reads it the same way, so every
  upgrade a gateway admits is origin-checked first.

### The graphql-ws socket negotiates its subprotocol on the same upgrade, and its cap closes `1009`

The socket on the GraphQL path opens on the transport's handshake too. Of the
subprotocols a client offers in `Sec-WebSocket-Protocol`, the first one
async-graphql speaks is chosen — `graphql-transport-ws`, or the legacy
`graphql-ws` — and named back as the client spelled it; a handshake offering
neither is refused `400` with a problem document naming both. A frame past
`<PREFIX>_GRAPHQL__MAX_MESSAGE_BYTES` now closes the socket `1009 Message Too
Big`, as a gateway's does, where it closed `1011`; graphql-transport-ws's
`4408` and `4409` close as before.

The path's `POST` and `GET` are two entries of the route table now, so the
socket's handshake is refused as a gateway's is: a `HEAD` carrying it answers
`400`, where poem's router turned it into a `GET` and opened the socket, and
`Upgrade: h2c, websocket` selects the socket, origin-checked. Any other
method answers the table's `405` with `Allow: POST, GET, HEAD`, before the
guards, where poem's carried no `Allow`.

### `/graphql` is served on the transport's own types, and `nest_rs::graphql::async_graphql_poem` is gone

The GraphQL path's `POST` and `GET` were poem endpoints, the `POST` answer and
a multipart request read through async-graphql-poem, which
`nest_rs::graphql::async_graphql_poem` re-exported. Both are now endpoints of
the HTTP transport's own, and async-graphql-poem is no longer a dependency:
with it goes the last request for poem's WebSocket support, so a build that
serves GraphQL compiles one tokio-tungstenite, 0.30.

What a client reads is what 6.x answered:

- **An operation's answer is JSON, `200` whatever its `errors` hold**, sent as
  `application/json; charset=utf-8`; a batch answers its operations in order,
  and a batch longer than `<PREFIX>_GRAPHQL__MAX_BATCH_SIZE` is refused `413`,
  executing nothing.
- **The schema's cache hints become `Cache-Control`** when every operation in
  the answer succeeded, and **every header a resolver set** with
  `ctx.insert_http_header` or `ctx.append_http_header` follows, each value
  kept, copied as the header map async-graphql holds.
- **A GraphQL multipart request carries file uploads** as before, each file
  held to `<PREFIX>_HTTP__MAX_BODY_BYTES`; a body past it is answered `413`,
  and a malformed one `400` with the same sentences as 6.x.
- **What the endpoint refuses itself** — an oversized batch, a refused
  subscription handshake — reaches the app's exception filters before it is
  rendered, as a route's error does.

One answer changes: with the playground off, a plain `GET` on the GraphQL path
is still `405`, now with `Allow: POST` (RFC 9110 §15.5.6), where 6.x sent no
`Allow`; a GraphQL request travels as a `POST` there, and `GET` serves only the
subscription socket and the playground.

### MCP, OpenAPI, static files and health mount on nestrs's own HTTP seams

The last framework surfaces written on poem — the MCP endpoint, OpenAPI's document and UI,
the static files — now speak `nest_rs::http`'s vocabulary like every route. Nothing changes
for an app: the same paths answer the same bytes, the same statuses and the same headers.

- **`/mcp` hands rmcp an `http` request and reads its `http` response** through
  `StreamableHttpService::handle`: no tower bound, no adapter. The origin check, the request
  stage, the session cap, the transport refusals said by kind and
  the end of a standalone stream at the signal are unchanged.
- **OpenAPI's document, UI and assets** are `endpoint_fn`s over bytes read once at mount.
- **The static files** answer `404` and `405` with the router's own problem documents, and
  read the conditional fields — `If-Match`, `If-None-Match`, `If-Modified-Since`,
  `If-Unmodified-Since`, `If-Range` — through the
  [`headers`](https://crates.io/crates/headers) crate, in RFC 9110 §13.2.2's order.
- **poem's `tower-compat` feature, and with it `tower` 0.4 and `pin-project`, leave the
  framework's dependency tree**; `nest-rs-mcp`, `nest-rs-openapi`, `nest-rs-static-files`
  and `nest-rs-health` no longer depend on poem.

### A raw error lifted onto the problem document keeps its marks

The edge lifts a raw-text error onto the problem document (`normalize_error_response`),
rebuilding the response from its status and headers; it dropped the extensions the
original carried, so a `401` marked `nest_rs::http::challenge::NoBearerChallenge` —
`CredentialError`'s, an app's own — left the edge without its mark.

- **A lifted response keeps the extensions of the one it replaces.** A marked `401` still
  goes out with the bare `WWW-Authenticate: Bearer` RFC 9110 §11.6.1 asks of every `401`,
  and never with a protected resource server's RFC 9728 pointer.

### A route's record is read through accessors, and its method is the `http` crate's

`HttpRouteMeta` — what `#[routes]` records of each handler, which the
transport's route log and the OpenAPI document read — had 24 public fields and
called itself an internal ABI, so the next fact a document states about a
route would have been a break. It named its method with `HttpVerb`, a
five-variant enum of its own, where `nest_rs::http::Method` already names one,
and a method HTTP adds (`QUERY`) would have been a variant more.

- **`HttpRouteMeta` is read through one accessor per fact**, named as the
  field was: `method()`, `path()`, `handler()`, `versions()`, `summary()`,
  `description()`, `tags()`, `request_body()`, `response()`,
  `response_content_type()`, `error_responses()`, `masked()`, `path_params()`,
  `query_params()`, `header_params()`, `may_conflict()`, `sets_location()`, `sets_next_link()`, `response_headers()`,
  `success_status()`, `scoped_guarded()`, `public()`, `deprecation()`;
  `access_is_implicit` is unchanged. A fact a later release records is an
  accessor more, never a break.
- **`HttpControllerMeta` too**: `controller()`, `token()`, `path()`,
  `versions()`, `routes()`; `mounted_versions`, `effective_prefix`, `serves`
  and `mount` are unchanged.
- **A route answers a `nest_rs::http::Method`**: `route.method()` is
  `&Method::GET` where `route.verb` was `HttpVerb::Get`. The boot route log
  prints the same `GET`, `POST`, … tokens, and the OpenAPI document keys each
  operation as before — a regenerated document is unchanged.
- **`nest_rs::http::SchemaFn`**, the schema builder `response()`,
  `error_responses()`, `path_params()`, `query_params()`, `header_params()` and
  `RequestBodyMeta` hand out, is nameable at the root.
- **Only `#[routes]` builds either record, and mounts a controller.** A
  literal of `HttpRouteMeta` no longer compiles, `HttpControllerMeta::new` is
  no longer public, and the `Controller` trait is gone: a hand-written impl of
  it compiled and was never mounted. A route is a handler of a `#[routes]`
  block, which attaches its controller's record and the mount that registers
  its routes. A surface that registers entries of its own is a self-mount, an
  `HttpEndpointMeta`, whose closure registers them on `HttpRoutes`.

**Breaking:** `nest_rs::http::HttpVerb` and `nest_rs::http::Controller` are
removed; the fields of `HttpRouteMeta` and `HttpControllerMeta` are private;
`HttpControllerMeta::new` is removed from the public API.

### poem leaves the public surface and the dependency tree: the HTTP transport is nestrs's own

No public signature, re-export or decorator expansion names a poem type any
more, and poem is in no manifest and no lockfile — the framework's, the demo's,
the benchmark's or one `nestrs new` writes. The transport serves on hyper, routes
on its own router, answers CORS itself, decodes queries and forms with
serde_urlencoded and multipart bodies with multer, and encodes with
async-compression, each wrapped in one crate-private file of `nest-rs-http`, so
changing a library changes no application. Clippy's `disallowed-types` refuses
multer's and async-compression's types outside their file.

**Breaking:**

- `nest_rs::http::poem` is removed: an application writes its routes, layers
  and tests on `nest_rs::http`'s own types (`Request`, `Response`, `Body`,
  `HttpError`, `Endpoint`, the extractors) and the `http` crate's head types
  it re-exports.
- `ProblemDetails` no longer implements poem's `ResponseError` and
  `IntoResponse`, and `nest_rs::http::Response` no longer converts into poem's
  response: both answer through `nest_rs::http::{ResponseError, IntoResponse}`.
- The hidden migration seams are gone: `HttpRoutes::route_poem`,
  `HttpRoutes::route_any_poem` and `nest_rs::http::__private::poem_bridge`.

The route table compiles into a router of the transport's own, which reads
each template as nestrs's route grammar spells it: a literal segment before a
parameter before a catch-all, the next candidate tried when a deeper segment
does not match. It keeps every rule the conformance suites pin, and says two
edges the rule's way:

- **A catch-all never matches its bare prefix, at the root too**: a root
  catch-all (`/{*rest}`) no longer answers `/`, which reaches the root's own
  route, or the fallback's `404`. **Breaking in behaviour:** an app serving `/`
  through a root catch-all declares a route at `/` beside it.
- **A parameter matches no empty value**: `/a//b` is no longer `/a/{x}/b` with
  an empty `x`, as `/users/` never was `/users/{id}`. **Breaking in
  behaviour:** such a request is the fallback's `404`, where the handler read an
  empty parameter.

### CORS admits the methods your routes serve, never one a page cannot send, and says each refusal

- **A policy naming no method** (`<PREFIX>_HTTP__CORS_METHODS` unset, or
  `HttpCors::methods` empty) admits the methods the route table serves and the
  three CORS-safelisted ones (`GET`, `HEAD`, `POST`) — every method a page may
  send when an address answers any method itself, as `/mcp` does — where it
  listed every method the library knew, `CONNECT` and `TRACE` among them.
  **Breaking in behaviour:** a preflight asking for a method no route serves is
  refused `403`; list it in `methods` to admit it.
- **`*` in `methods` answers `Access-Control-Allow-Methods: *`**, as WHATWG
  Fetch spells the wildcard, and admits any method, an extension one included.
- **A method Fetch forbids a page to send — `CONNECT`, `TRACE`, `TRACK` —
  fails the boot** when `methods` lists it (**breaking**: a configuration that
  booted is refused), naming the method and the rule.
- **Each refusal files one `warn`** on `nest_rs::http`, `cross-origin request
  refused by the CORS policy`, with `reason` `origin_not_allowed`,
  `method_not_allowed` or `headers_not_allowed`, and nothing the request sent:
  a refused cross-origin request filed no line at all, its answer leaving
  before the edge that writes the access line.

### Three CORS answers poem got wrong

- **An answer under a CORS policy admitting any origin keeps every `Vary`
  value**: with compression on, `Vary: Accept-Encoding` was replaced by
  `Vary: Origin`, so a shared cache could hand a client a coding it never
  accepted. Both are said now.
- **A CORS policy without `max_age` sends no `Access-Control-Max-Age`**, as
  `HttpCors::max_age` documents: the library's own default, a day, was sent,
  so a browser kept a preflight that long after the policy changed. Set
  `max_age` (`<PREFIX>_HTTP__CORS_MAX_AGE_SECS`) to keep one longer than the
  browser's default of a few seconds.
- **An origin no header can carry fails the boot** naming the CORS allow-list,
  where the transport panicked building the policy.

### Shutdown ends within a bound, and every backend call the framework awaits has one of its own

A process asked to stop waited on whatever had not finished — an open stream, a
lifecycle hook, a lock — and a request waited on whatever backend it reached,
bounded at best by the HTTP edge's request timeout, which the other edges do not
have. Each such wait now carries a bound of its own: a net above the budget of the
adapter beneath it, so a healthy backend is never cut short, and a wait past it is
abandoned with a `warn` naming what it was waiting on.

- **HTTP waits for open connections no longer than
  `<PREFIX>_HTTP__SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_SECS`**, the transport's graceful shutdown: 20
  seconds by default, so the window, half a second for what it stopped to unwind,
  the shutdown hooks' 5 seconds, OpenTelemetry's 3-second flush and the console's
  1-second flush — 29.5 seconds, pinned by a test from the constants themselves —
  end inside the 30 a
  pod gets by default between `SIGTERM` and `SIGKILL`; from 1 to 3600, pinned
  through `HttpConfig`'s `shutdown_timeout` or read from the environment alike;
  `0` is refused rather than read as an off switch. A deployment that widens the
  window keeps `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` at least 9.5 seconds above it. At
  the bound a request still running is dropped unanswered, over HTTP/1.1 and
  HTTP/2 alike, and a download is cut, and one `warn` on `nest_rs::http` —
  `connections still open as the shutdown window closes are cut; …` — carries
  `cut`, `upgraded_open` and `shutdown_timeout_ms`. What has no end of its own
  ends at the signal instead, as *Long-lived connections end at the shutdown
  signal* below says; a socket a hand-built self-mount upgraded is never waited
  for: it ends with its handler or the process, and the line counts it as
  `upgraded_open`. 6.1 handed poem no
  window, so an `#[sse]` or MCP stream held a stopping replica until its own
  four-hour ceiling or the kubelet's `SIGKILL`, which left the shutdown hooks
  unrun. **Breaking** for an `HttpConfig` struct literal without
  `..Default::default()`.
- **The shutdown lifecycle hooks share one budget** —
  `nest_rs::core::SHUTDOWN_HOOKS_TIMEOUT`, 5 seconds across `OnModuleDestroy`,
  `BeforeApplicationShutdown` and `OnApplicationShutdown` together. Once it is
  spent every later hook is still started and polled once, so a hook that does
  not wait still runs, and a hook that waits is abandoned with a `warn`, `shutdown
  hook abandoned: …`, naming its module and the hook, with `waited_ms` and
  `budget_ms`.
- **A lifecycle hook that panics no longer unwinds out of `App::run`.** At
  shutdown it is logged at `error` on `nest_rs::lifecycle`, with the `panic`, and
  the hooks after it run; at init it is logged the same way and the boot fails
  with `lifecycle hook X::y (OnModuleInit) panicked`.
- **OpenTelemetry's final flush shuts the tracer, meter and logger providers down
  together**, bounded by `nest_rs::opentelemetry::FLUSH_TIMEOUT` — 3 seconds in
  all — so a collector that stopped answering never holds the exit; a provider
  still exporting at the bound is named on stderr.
- **An MCP operation ends with the transport that carried it.** One still running
  when the HTTP transport stops, or cancelled by its client
  (`notifications/cancelled`), is dropped where it waits rather than running on
  through the shutdown hooks. A self-mount that runs units of work off their
  connections declares a `nest_rs::http::DetachedWork`
  (`HttpEndpointMeta::runs_detached`), which the transport stops after its window,
  waiting at most `nest_rs::core::SHUTDOWN_SETTLE_TIMEOUT` (500 ms) for every
  mount together, with a `warn` counting what it stopped; `#[mcp]` declares one
  per endpoint.
- **A unit of work stopped before it settles still files its operation line.**
  An HTTP request dropped at the shutdown window, or by a client that reset its
  connection before the answer, files `http.request` with `outcome="cancelled"`,
  the time it ran, and no `status` or `bytes`; a handler that panics files
  `outcome="panic"`. An MCP operation dropped as above files `mcp.operation` with
  the new `nest_rs::core::operation_log::CANCELLED`. A queue attempt its worker
  stops — a drain closing its window on a running job, or a worker torn down —
  files `queue.job` with `outcome="cancelled"` in the job's trace; it comes from
  the port, so every driver files it. A scheduled tick the scheduler stops at its
  shutdown bound files `schedule.tick` with `outcome="cancelled"`. HTTP and the
  queue used to leave no line, and MCP filed `outcome="ok"` for an operation
  nobody answered. A stream ended at the signal or cut at the window still files
  its line when its body ends, `cancelled`, with the head's status and the bytes
  written. Every other edge files both ends too, as *Every edge files a unit
  cancelled and a unit that panicked* below says.
- **`AuthnGuard` waits `nest_rs::authn::AUTHENTICATE_TIMEOUT` — 18 seconds — for
  `Strategy::authenticate`**, on every edge that reaches it: a route, the GraphQL
  and MCP fallbacks, a gateway's upgrade. Past it the credential was never
  evaluated, which is where an unreachable identity store leaves it, so the answer
  is the same — `503 authentication unavailable`, `#[public]` routes included,
  never anonymous — and the cause goes on its own `warn`, `strategy did not answer
  within the guard's timeout; denying (fail-closed)`, with `strategy`, `transport`
  and `waited_ms`. The shipped JWT strategy answers on its first poll; a strategy
  calling introspection or an API-key store used to hold its request until the
  edge's request timeout, and for good where that was switched off.
- **The SeaORM pool waits 10 seconds for a connection by default**, where it waited
  sqlx's 30: at the guard's 18-second net when a strategy resolves an identity, and
  at the HTTP edge's 20-second request timeout in a handler, so a pool that ran dry
  was said as the net's timeout rather than its own. A deployment that set
  `<PREFIX>_SEAORM__CONNECT_TIMEOUT_SECS` keeps its value unless a net reaches it,
  as the next entry says. `EphemeralDatabase` opens its pool with the same 10
  seconds, so a test seeding it waits as the app's pool does.
- **Postgres cancels an app's statement after 15 seconds by default**
  (`statement_timeout`), set by `<PREFIX>_SEAORM__STATEMENT_TIMEOUT_SECS`, from
  1 to 3600: a statement waiting on a row lock, or on a plan gone wrong, held
  its connection and its locks for as long as it ran — past a request's
  deadline, which drops the future and leaves the statement running, and in a
  job until the job's own timeout — and a job context's `BEGIN`,
  `COMMIT` and `ROLLBACK` with it. It now ends as Postgres's own cancellation,
  and the boot holds the bound under each net reaching the pool, as it holds
  the acquire budget. The tools' `connect_from_env` (`migrate`, `seed`) opens
  without it: a migration takes as long as its data needs. Two budgets of one
  resource are now each held, where the boot merged them into the first.
- **A worker job ends within its `timeout`**, ten minutes by default
  (`nest_rs::core::JOB_TIMEOUT`, Google Cloud Tasks' dispatch deadline), set per
  method by `timeout = "30m"` on `#[process]`, `#[every]`, `#[cron]` and
  `#[after]`, from a millisecond to `"24h"`. No edge deadline sits above a job,
  so an attempt waiting on a call that never answers held its permit and renewed
  its lease, and a tick its schedule — every later occurrence skipped — for as
  long as the process lived. Past the timeout it is cut where it waits and fails
  retryably as `JobTimedOut`, naming the deadline and the key: a queue job is
  retried within its budget, then dead-lettered; a tick files
  `scheduled job failed` with `retryable = true`, and its next occurrence fires.
- **The boot holds every resource's budget under each net that reaches it.** A
  budget is a resource's own bound on one answer, a net the bound a port gives up
  at; a budget at or past a net cuts an answer short and puts a bare timeout in
  place of its cause, on every outage, so it is refused before the first one.
  `nest_rs::core` carries the order. The module opening a resource declares its
  `Budget`, and so does each binding handing it to a port or installing it around
  every unit of work, so a seeded resource is held too; whoever arms a net
  declares its `Net`: a binding over the resource it hands its port
  (`Net::over`), a guard around its strategy (`Net::around`, reaching what the
  strategy injects at any depth, optionally or not, through the bindings the app
  imports, and every ambient resource). A budget at or past a net reaching it
  fails the boot with `nest_rs::core::BudgetPastNetError`, naming the resource,
  the port, both durations and what lowers the budget — as soon as the resource
  exists, a seed before any factory runs, before a factory after it does I/O of
  its own. The Redis connection sits under the queue port's and the rate
  limiter's 18 seconds and the scheduler's 50, each declared by its binding,
  which checked on its own before; under the authentication guard's 18 when a
  strategy injects it; and the SeaORM pool under the guard's 18 when a strategy
  injects it, or wherever `SeaOrmDatabaseModule` binds `Repo`, which reaches it
  from any code. A health indicator's deadline is the orchestrator's probe, and
  the HTTP edge's request timeout the deployment's deadline: neither is a net.
- **The storage client bounds a call's answer apart from a transfer's stall**,
  with the AWS SDK's two timeouts. A call waits for S3's answer within
  `<PREFIX>_STORAGE__OPERATION_TIMEOUT_SECS` — 15 seconds by default, every retry
  included — and past it fails as its own error, `the call did not end within
  15s, every retry included — the budget <PREFIX>_STORAGE__OPERATION_TIMEOUT_SECS
  sets`, under the operation's
  `StorageError` variant. That error is for an S3 that never answers: retries
  stop once half the budget is spent and no backoff outlasts the rest, so
  a call whose attempts keep failing ends on S3's own error and its last cause,
  where it ended on the budget's sentence with the cause only in
  `object_store`'s `info` lines. The client's `Budget` is that timeout, so the boot holds
  it under the authentication guard's 18 seconds when a strategy injects
  `Storage`. An upload's body is part of its request: `put_bytes` moves within the
  budget and `put_stream` ships 5 MiB parts that each do. A download is never cut
  for its size nor for its reader's pauses: one that sends nothing for
  `<PREFIX>_STORAGE__READ_TIMEOUT_SECS` — 30 seconds by default — while it is
  read is cut and resumed by range, fenced on the object's `ETag` — sent as
  `If-Match` and checked on the answer — and fails
  naming the variable when the resumed body sends nothing within it; a body
  whose connection breaks is resumed the same way once `object_store`'s own
  resumption is spent, however long the download has run, and every resume is
  a `warn` naming the key and its cause. The bound
  is the client's own rather than reqwest's read timeout, which runs between two
  reads — a reader that paused, a slow client behind a streamed response, was
  cut — and through an upload's body, so an upload slower than it failed its
  every retry. 6.1 waited on
  `object_store`'s defaults, 30 seconds per attempt with the body inside it and
  retries for 3 minutes, so a silent S3 under a handler was the edge's bare `503`
  and a download longer than 30 seconds was cut and resumed until 3 minutes ran
  out. **Breaking** for a `StorageConfig` struct literal without
  `..Default::default()`.
- **`OAuthClient`'s HTTP client has a connect and a total timeout**, argued against
  the identity-provider calls it makes: the code exchange and the userinfo fetch,
  which `nest-rs-social`'s GitHub and Google providers go through too. It had none,
  and answered only to the edge's request timeout, which a deployment can switch
  off.
- **The queue port bounds every call it makes on a backend** — `JobProducer`'s
  `enqueue`, `remove` and `remove_unique`, `CheckpointStore`'s `load` and `save`,
  and every `JobConsumer` call — under a net above the Redis adapter's connection
  budget, an order a
  test pins. A push past it is an error to its caller; a checkpoint past it fails
  the attempt as retryable.
- The same rule gives `ThrottlerGuard` its `HIT_TIMEOUT` and the scheduler its
  bound on every lock call, both above; the queue worker's drain was already
  bounded, and is now 20 seconds by default and at most an hour.

### The way down is bounded to the exit

- **`#[nest_rs::main]` replaces `#[tokio::main]` on every binary.** Dropping a
  tokio runtime waits for every blocking task still running, so under
  `#[tokio::main]` a hook the shutdown budget abandoned while it waited on a
  `spawn_blocking`, or a request dropped at the window mid `tokio::fs` call, held
  the exit past every bound, after the last line. `#[nest_rs::main]` builds the
  same runtime — multi-threaded, every driver enabled, sized by
  `TOKIO_WORKER_THREADS` — and tears it down within what the shutdown hooks and
  the telemetry flush left of the hooks' budget; a `main` that ran no app, such as
  a migration or seed tool, gets the whole budget. What still runs at the bound is
  abandoned with one `warn` on `nest_rs::app`, `work still running as the runtime
  is torn down is abandoned: the exit no longer waits for it`. It takes no
  argument, refusing one with the reason, and its expansion is rooted at the
  umbrella, so an app's manifest needs no `tokio` line for it. **Breaking:**
  replace `#[tokio::main]` on every binary's `main`, and move a `tokio` line kept
  only for it to `[dev-dependencies]`. The scaffold, the demo and the benchmark
  already do, and the root `clippy.toml` refuses `#[tokio::main]` in every
  crate of both workspaces.
- **A second `SIGINT` or `SIGTERM` during the way down exits at once.** In 6.1 it
  was ignored, because the first had replaced the default handlers, so a stuck
  shutdown ended only with `SIGKILL`. The process now exits with 130 or 143 after
  one `error` on `nest_rs::app`, `shutdown signal received on the way down:
  exiting at once, abandoning what still runs`, naming the transports still
  stopping, the hook running (`phase`, `provider`, `method`), or the exit. Nothing
  runs after it, the telemetry flush included.
- **A scheduled tick still running at shutdown gets five seconds, then is
  stopped.** `nest_rs::schedule::Scheduler::SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT` is the shutdown
  hooks' budget, spent before the hooks so no tick runs through the cleanup it may
  depend on. A tick still running then is dropped where it waits, files
  `schedule.tick` with `outcome="cancelled"` in its own trace, and is named in one
  `warn`, `scheduled ticks still running at the shutdown bound are stopped; …`,
  with `jobs`, `running` and `shutdown_timeout_ms`; one that blocks its thread is
  named at `error` after the half-second settle. 6.1 waited for a running tick to
  end, so a tick that never returned held the whole way down. **Breaking** for a
  tick that relied on finishing during shutdown: push a queue job instead.
- **The queue worker drains within 20 seconds by default**, down from 30. With the
  hooks' 5 and the flush's 3, a 30-second drain overran Kubernetes' default
  30-second grace and took the hooks and the flush with it. Of the 20, running
  attempts get 10 on Redis by default: the drain keeps the backend's answer
  bound — one connection budget — back to hand the rest back. **Breaking** for
  a deployment relying on the old default: set
  `<PREFIX>_QUEUE__SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_SECS=30` and keep the grace period 9.5
  seconds above it.
- **The default way down is 29.5 seconds**: the longest transport's window plus
  half a second for what it stopped to unwind, then the hooks' 5, then
  OpenTelemetry's flush's 3, then the console's 1. A raised window keeps
  `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` 9.5 seconds above it.
  The settle bound is the kernel's, `nest_rs::core::SHUTDOWN_SETTLE_TIMEOUT` (500
  ms), shared by HTTP's detached work and the scheduler. A test in
  `nest-rs-testing` sums the `stop_bound()` of every transport the framework
  ships, at its defaults, and pins the 29.5.
- **Breaking for a hand-written transport: `Transport` gains a required
  `fn stop_bound(&self) -> Duration`**, the longest `serve` takes to return once
  told to stop, at the configuration `configure` left. `HttpTransport` answers
  its shutdown window plus the settle, `Scheduler` its five seconds plus the
  settle, and `QueueWorker` its drain window plus the settle — read at
  `configure`, so the bound is the deployment's own. With no default, a transport
  cannot be written without the bound it adds to the way down.
- **The boot files the way down it adds up to.** Once every transport is
  configured, one `way down bounded` line on `nest_rs::app` carries the longest
  stop bound among the transports the app mounted, settle included
  (`stop_bound_ms`), and the shutdown hooks' budget (`hooks_budget_ms`) — the demo
  api reads `stop_bound_ms=20500 hooks_budget_ms=5000`. Add OpenTelemetry's
  3-second flush when it is installed, which the kernel cannot see, and a
  deployment that raised a window reads off the line what its grace period has
  to hold.

### Long-lived connections end at the shutdown signal, the standard way

A stream with no end of its own — an `#[sse]` stream, an MCP session's `GET`
stream — held a stopping replica until something cut it, and a gateway's WebSocket
and a graphql-ws socket were never closed at all: poem stopped tracking a connection
at its upgrade, so they ran on under the shutdown hooks and left their clients a
`1006` they could not tell from a network fault. Now, the moment shutdown is asked
for, what has no end of its own ends as its protocol ends one, after whatever it is
answering:

- **An event stream ends cleanly**, its last chunk written, so an `EventSource`
  reconnects at once. A handler streaming events by hand opts in with
  `response.extensions_mut().insert(nest_rs::http::OpenEndedBody)`. A download
  keeps the window and is cut at its close.
- **A gateway's socket closes with RFC 6455's `1001 Going Away`**, after the
  message it is answering and after `on_disconnect`; a message still running when
  the window closes is dropped with its socket and files `cancelled`. A peer that
  stopped reading gets five seconds (`DetachedWork::CLOSE_GRACE`) to take its
  replies and the Close frame.
- **A graphql-ws socket answers each running subscription `complete`**, in the
  negotiated protocol's own wording, answers a query or a mutation still running
  over it, then closes `1001`; its lifetime ceiling now closes the same way, where
  it dropped the socket.
- **An MCP `subscriptions/listen` is answered its final result**, and a session's
  standalone `GET` stream ends like an event stream.

Each files its line `cancelled` — an `http.request` line keeps its `status` and
`bytes` beside it — except a gateway's socket closed idle, which ran no unit: its
`ws.disconnect` hook runs and files `ok`. **Breaking:** `SseSettings::respond`
returns the marked `Response`, and `gateway_endpoint` takes the gateway's
`DetachedWork`; both are emitted by the decorators. A socket a hand-built
self-mount upgraded is still the developer's own, and the drain line counts it as
`upgraded_open`.

Work a mount runs off the connection that asked for it — an MCP operation on
rmcp's task, a DataLoader batch on async-graphql's, a socket after its upgrade —
gets the rest of the window like a request, is stopped at its close, and is given
one half-second settle for every mount together; a stopped unit is never polled
again. A GraphQL batch in flight used to run on through the shutdown hooks.

### A contested declaration names both imports that made it

`ContestedDeclarationError` — two imports binding one port, two pins of one config
— named the type and the remedy, and the reader searched the tree for the pair. It
