now names both, each as the import and its position in the `imports` of the module
that lists it: `` contested declaration: `X` is declared twice — by
`A::for_root(..)` at `imports[0]` of `AppModule`, and by … ``. **Breaking for a
literal of the error**, which gains `first` and `second`.

### A composition missing a factory's output is a boot error, never a panic

- **`SeaOrmDatabaseModule` beside a seeded pool and no `SeaOrmModule`** panicked
  in its register phase, `DbContext.config: no provider registered for this
  dependency`. Its factory now waits for the pool and `SeaOrmConfig` both and
  fails the boot naming the one missing: `SeaOrmDatabaseModule: no
  `SeaOrmConfig` in the container — import `SeaOrmModule`, which resolves …`.
- **`App::new` refuses a queued factory before any module registers**, with
  `UnresolvedFactoryError`. A module whose register reads what its own collect
  queued — `SeaOrmDatabaseModule`, `GraphqlModule::for_root`,
  `OpenApiModule::for_root` — panicked on the synchronous path before the
  refusal was reached.
- **A config pinned on a `for_root` takes the place in the factory queue of the
  default it displaces.** It went to the back, so where a bare
  `X::for_root(None)` came first in `imports`, the factory reading the config —
  the Redis connection, the SeaORM pool, `JwtService`, `OAuthClient`, the rate
  limiter's guard — ran before it and panicked, `XConfig is resolved by
  ConfigModule::provide_feature`.
- **A register refuses the boot instead of panicking**, through
  `ContainerBuilder::refuse(error)`: the boot fails with that error once the
  register phase ends, as it fails with a factory's own error. A seeded
  unit-of-work binding skipped the factory checking `SeaOrmDatabaseModule`'s
  substrate, and its register panicked on the missing `SeaOrmConfig`; it now
  checks the substrate itself and refuses, and two providers of one module
  injecting each other with `ProviderCycleError`, where both panicked.
- **A factory queued during the register phase is `LateFactoryError`**, on
  either boot path. On `App::builder()` it was never drained, and the value it
  would have built was absent in silence. A declaration queued there is
  refused even where its type is already built, since that value is the
  default's.
- **A module's phases run through an import alone, `collect` first and each
  once** — **breaking** for a hand-written `impl Module` or `DynamicModule`.
  Their `collect` takes a `Collecting<Self>` and their `register` a
  `Registering<Self>`, which only the framework makes, so no module runs a
  phase by hand: passing its proof to another module, making one, or calling
  its own `collect` from its `register` does not compile. A module wires the
  modules it imports with `ContainerBuilder::import::<M>()` in both of its
  phases, which runs each of `M`'s once and its `collect` first;
  `ContainerBuilder::mark_collected` and `mark_registered` are gone, with the
  dedupe and the leading `Self::collect` every hand-written module carried. A
  hand-written importer that called a module's `register` alone left what its
  `collect` queues unbuilt, and the app booted — every job without its
  transaction, a port unbound; one importing a module in its `register` alone
  now has it collected there, and what it queues refused by name as late; one
  importing a module in its `collect` alone, whose providers then never
  registered, is refused naming it (`UnregisteredModuleError`).
  `ConfigSetup`, `McpSetup`, `OAuthResourceSetup` and
  `RedisQueueModule::for_root` import their module in both phases.
- **`#[module]` writes a dynamic import once.** Its expansion carried the
  import expression a second time, as a fallback no boot path reaches, since a
  module's `register` runs after its `collect`: a `for_root(..)` value that is
  not `Send` is now one compile error on the expression, where the whole
  attribute carried a second.
- **A seeded trait object wins over a factory binding it.**
  `App::builder().provide_dyn::<dyn UnitOfWork>(double)` beside
  `SeaOrmDatabaseModule` was replaced, in silence, by the binding's own: a
  factory bound through `provide_factory_dyn` ran because
  its concrete type was unseeded, and rebound the trait object. It now provides
  the concrete type alone and keeps the seed.
- **A trait object has one binding.** Two `provide_factory_dyn` of different
  types binding one `dyn D` both ran, and the first to run held it in silence;
  beside a `provide_declared_factory` of `Arc<dyn D>` the second to run was
  dropped, and beside a port's default the first queued won. The trait object a
  dyn factory binds is now a declaration: another implementation's binding of
  it fails the boot with `ContestedDeclarationError`, naming both imports and,
  when one side carries it, the port's own sentence; a port's default is
  superseded wherever it is queued; and a seeded concrete type, or one an
  import declared with `provide_declared_factory`, is what it binds, where the
  trait object was left unbound — beside a declared one, the whole factory was
  dropped, and a binding queued in `register` over it is now refused as late.
- **Two unit-of-work bindings fail the boot.** `SeaOrmDatabaseModule` binds
  `Arc<dyn UnitOfWork>` through `nest_rs::database::provide_unit_of_work`,
  carrying `nest_rs::database::BACKEND_REMEDY`, as the queue, schedule and
  throttler bindings declare theirs, so another database driver imported beside
  it fails the boot naming both, where one of the two ran every unit in silence.
- **A trait object bound twice fails the boot with `DuplicateProviderError`**, as
  a concrete type does. Two modules listing `X as dyn T` and `Y as dyn T`, or a
  seeded `provide_dyn::<dyn T>` beside a module binding it, kept whichever
  registered last and dropped the other in silence. A test replaces a binding
  with `override_dyn`, which stays the one replacement a trait object takes.

### A container built by hand returns what it refuses

`Container::builder()…build()` froze whatever registered and refused nothing: a type
registered twice kept its last value, a provider whose dependency was missing was never
built, a module whose `collect` queues a config registered without it — and the container
came back with no error saying why. A unit test found `None` where a provider should be.

- **`ContainerBuilder::build` returns `Result<Container, ContainerError>`.** The error is
  the first refusal the builder holds, read in the boot's order: a declaration contested,
  what a `register` filed with `refuse`, a factory nothing drains, a module imported in a
  `collect` alone, a type registered twice, then a cycle among request-scoped and transient
  providers, one among singletons of modules importing each other, a provider a module
  lists that was never built, one built before an optional dependency registered after it,
  and a resource's budget at or past the net of a port that waits on it.
- **`nest_rs::core::ContainerError`** has one variant per refusal the kernel names —
  `ContestedDeclaration`, `LateFactory`, `ProviderCycle`, `UnregisteredModule`,
  `DuplicateProvider`, `UnbuiltProvider`, `LateOptionalDependency`, `BudgetPastNet` — and
  `Refused` for a `register`'s own, as it filed it. The boot is unchanged: `App::new` and
  `App::builder().build()` refuse what they refused, and their error still downcasts to the
  concrete refusal.
- **A container built by hand is named as one.** A factory queued on it is refused with that
  builder's edit: "`C` is provided by an async factory, which a container built by hand never
  runs: provide `C` on the builder before what queues it … or boot through `App::builder()`,
  `TestApp` in a test, which runs it" — "and each trait object its factory binds off it
  (`provide_dyn`)" for a factory binding one. A declaration, a pinned `for_root` value among
  them, which no value provided on the builder stands in for, names the boot alone: "boot
  through `App::builder()`, `TestApp` in a test, which runs it". A type it registers twice is
  named "a call on a container built by hand (`Container::builder()…`)", where it read as a
  seed of `App::builder()`. A provider never built because the builder imported its module
  before providing what it needs gives `UnbuiltCause::ImportedFirst`: "provide it, or import
  what provides it, before `M`".
- **What `build` does not read:** the access graph and the wirings. The access graph holds a
  provider to what is global, a seed or a factory's output, and a builder made by hand cannot
  tell a seed from what its modules register; a wiring fills its registry from the reachable
  set the boot's seal hands the container. The boot reads both.

**Breaking:** every `build()` on a `ContainerBuilder` takes a `?` or an `.expect(..)`.

### An inert host is a warning only when it is this app's

A host discovered in the binary whose provider the app does not reach — a
`#[scheduled]` method, a `#[process]` method, an `#[on_event]` listener, a
lifecycle hook, a health indicator — was said at `warn` with one hint naming every
cause. In a workspace of several binaries one library crate holds the hosts every
binary imports some of, so each warned about the others' on every boot of a
correct deployment: the demo's worker filed four, telling it to import the api's
scheduled methods.

The boot now reads the cause, and says it at the level it earns. A library crate's
host this app neither imports the module of nor registers is another binary's,
said at `debug` (`skipped …: not this app's to run`, `cause="another_app"`); read
the edge's target at `debug` when a handler in a shared crate does not run. The
app's own code is a `warn` with a `cause` and a `hint` naming its remedy:
`module_not_imported` (naming the module), `not_listed`, `bound_under_another_key`
(naming the module and the key) and `registered_outside_modules`. A hint never
prescribes listing a provider a second time, which builds it twice. **Breaking for
a call to `report_inert_host!`** in an edge of your own, which takes the host's
type and the container; the inventory records it reads, `ProviderDescriptor` and
`LifecycleHook` — internal ABI the decorators emit — carry the host's type.

### A dependency's inert host is said at `debug` whatever its name

The boot decided that a host it skipped was the framework's by its crate's name:
any crate called `nest_rs_*` — a third-party plugin that took the prefix
included — was said at `debug` before the boot read the app's composition, under
the cause `framework`, while the same fact about a shared library crate was
`another_app`. The cause is now read off the composition alone: a host in any
crate your app depends on — the framework, a package, the workspace's
`features` crate — whose module this app neither imports nor registers is said
at `debug` with `cause="dependency"` and the module in the `hint`
(`a host of a dependency whose module `ReportsQueueModule` this app does not
import`). The order is unchanged, so your own code, and a host registered by
hand outside the imported modules, still warns.

**Breaking:** `nest_rs::core::is_framework_owned` is removed;
`InertHost::Framework` and `InertHost::AnotherApps` are one variant,
`InertHost::Dependency { module }`; the `cause` values `framework` and
`another_app` are `dependency` — update a log query or an alert filtering on
them. A container built by hand with `Container::builder()` carries no
composition, so a framework host inert there is now said at `warn` with cause
`unknown`: a test asserting on that line boots through `App::builder()`.

### A registry is filled before the first hook: an event an init hook emits reaches its listeners

Three framework registries were filled by an `OnApplicationBootstrap` lifecycle
hook — the event bus's listeners, the health service's indicators and the social
provider map — so a hook that ran first read them empty, and said nothing: an
event an `#[on_module_init]` hook emitted reached no listener, a readiness probe
it ran answered `up` without running an indicator, and `SocialRegistry::keys()`
was empty.

The kernel now runs a **wiring step** at the end of both boot paths
(`App::new`, `App::builder().build()`), after the container is sealed and before
any transport is built or any hook runs. A module attaches one with
`ContainerBuilder::provide_wiring(name, wire)` — `AppBuilder::provide_wiring`
at the app root — where `wire` is a synchronous `fn(&Container) ->
anyhow::Result<()>`, so no I/O slows the boot there. Wirings run once, in
registration order; the first that fails ends the boot with
`WiringFailedError`, naming the registry (`nest_rs::events::listeners`,
`nest_rs::health::indicators`, `nest_rs::social::providers`). Each attached
wiring is readable as a `WiringContribution` through `Discovery::meta`.
`EventsModule`, `HealthModule` and `SocialModule` fill their registries there.

An emit that still comes too early — from a task a factory or a constructor
spawned — reaches no listener, and is no longer silent: one `warn` on
`nest_rs::events`, `event emitted before the listeners were wired: dropped`,
names the event type. The no-bus report (`NO_BUS_REPORT`) is unchanged.

`App::init()` no longer wires anything; the boot did.

### `Foo as dyn Trait` is one singleton, held under its own type and each trait

**The hooks, scheduled methods, listeners, processors and indicators of a host
bound `as dyn Trait` now run.** The binding registered only `Arc<dyn Trait>`, so
the container held nothing under `Foo` and every provider-hosted decorator on it
was skipped at boot with the cause `bound_under_another_key`. It now builds one
`Foo` and holds that one allocation under `Foo` and under each trait it is
listed with: `providers = [Store as dyn Cache, Store as dyn Flush]` is one
`Store`, and the decorated methods run on the instance every `Arc<dyn Cache>`
consumer holds. A consumer elsewhere injecting `Arc<Store>` imports the module
that binds it, like any other provider.

- **Only a singleton can be bound.** A `scope = request` or `scope = transient`
  provider bound `as dyn Trait` was built once at boot, from whatever its
  dependencies were then — a boot panic when they were not there yet — and
  that one instance served every request; an edge host (`#[controller]`,
  `#[gateway]`, `#[resolver]`, `#[mcp]`) bound so was built a second time,
  beside the instance its edge mounts. Each is now a compile error on the
  provider path: "`as dyn Trait` binds one instance built at boot for the
  app's lifetime, so the provider must be a singleton the container holds.
  This one is not: an edge host (#[controller], #[gateway], #[resolver],
  #[mcp]) is built at mount, `scope = request` builds one per request, and
  `scope = transient` one per resolution. Bind a plain #[injectable]
  singleton; reach a scoped provider through a `Scoped<T>` parameter." A type
  no decorator records as a provider (an `#[interceptor]`, a plain struct)
  bound `as dyn` is refused by the residency trait's sentence, which now names
  both of its readers.
- **A provider is listed once.** `providers = [Foo, Foo as dyn Trait]` built two
  instances — the decorators fired on one while every `Arc<dyn Trait>`
  consumer held the other; it is now a compile error at the second entry,
  "`Foo` is listed beside `Foo as dyn Trait`, which already holds it under its
  own type — remove the bare entry". An entry written twice (`Foo, Foo`, or one
  binding twice) is refused the same way.
- **Across modules**, `Foo` in one module and `Foo as dyn Trait` in another —
  two silent instances — now fail the boot as a duplicate of `Foo`, naming
  both modules.

**Breaking:** `InertHost::BoundUnderAnotherKey`, added earlier in 7.0, is
removed with its cause `bound_under_another_key` (fold: drop it from *An inert
host is a warning only when it is this app's*), and `INERT_HOST_HINT` no longer
lists "bound only as `dyn Trait`". A host bound `as dyn` is reachable under its
own type, so it now sits in `ReachableProviders` and `ProviderOrder` beside its
trait objects.

### A type has one provider whatever its scope, and a duplicate names both registrations

Two registrations of one request-scoped or transient type warned
`provider override` and kept the last; a singleton and a transient of one type
warned `provider scope conflict` and left one unreachable; a singleton and a
request-scoped one said nothing. The error that did refuse, a second singleton
or trait object, named the type and not the registrations, so a driver author
could not tell which import collided.

Now any second registration of one type — in any two scopes — fails the boot
once the register phase ends, with `DuplicateProviderError` naming both:

```text
duplicate provider: `Shelf` is registered by `SingletonShelfModule` at
`imports[0]` of `AppModule` and again by `TransientShelfModule` at `imports[1]`
of `AppModule` (first as a singleton, then transient) — one type has one
provider: remove one registration; a test replaces a provider with
`override_value`, `override_arc` or `override_dyn`
```

A registration made inside a module is named by that module's import; a value
`App::builder()` seeded reads "a seed (`App::builder().provide…`)"; a factory's
output reads "a factory queued by" the import that queued it. The two warn
events are gone. A keyed provider still warns and keeps the last write, until
keyed injection itself is replaced.

**Breaking:** `DuplicateProviderError` is `#[non_exhaustive]` and gains
`first`, `second` and `scopes: Option<(ProviderScope, ProviderScope)>`, set
when the two scopes differ. `ProviderScope` (`Singleton`, `Request`,
`Transient`, `#[non_exhaustive]`) names the values of
`#[injectable(scope = …)]`. A composition that booted with one of the two warns
no longer boots. The *Boot sequence* page said a seed wins over a module-built
provider of its type; only a factory's is skipped — a seed of a type a module
lists was already, and stays, a duplicate.

### AppLifecycle: the app's state, its stop cause and its stop signal, injectable

The stop token was a local of `App::run`, so the first moment a provider learned the app
was stopping was its `#[on_module_destroy]` hook, after every transport had drained; and
the kernel told `SIGINT` from `SIGTERM` only in a log line. Every app now carries one
`nest_rs::core::AppLifecycle`, seeded before the first factory runs, which a factory, a
constructor or a hook injects as `Arc<AppLifecycle>`:

- **`state()`** is an `AppState`: `Starting` from the start of the boot, `Ready` once the
  transports serve, `Draining` once a stop is asked. **`ready().await`** resolves `true`
  at `Ready`, `false` once the app drains, whether or not it served first.
- **`cause()`** is `None` until the app drains, then a `StopCause`: `Interrupt`
  (`SIGINT`, Ctrl-C on Windows), `Terminate` (`SIGTERM`), or `TransportFailed` (a
  transport returned an error, panicked, or stopped serving on its own — an app whose
  transports all end, or that mounts none, stops for it). The first cause wins. It is
  what NestJS passes `onApplicationShutdown(signal)`.
- **`stopping()`** is a `CancellationToken` cancelled the instant the app turns
  `Draining`, before the transports drain: a loop a provider runs beside the app stops
  there and is joined in the provider's own shutdown hook.
- Only the kernel moves it. Each move is one `info` on `nest_rs::app`: `app ready`, with
  `transports`, and `app draining`, with `cause` and, for a signal, `signal` — which
  replaces the former `shutdown signal received` line.
- Every transport serves under a child of the app's stop token, so one transport
  cancelling its own token no longer stops the others; each transport that fails is
  named at `error` (`transport failed; shutting down` now carries `transport`), not
  only the first.
- `App::lifecycle()`, `TestApp::lifecycle()`, `HeadlessApp::lifecycle()` and
  `WsApp::lifecycle()` hand out the handle the providers inject; in-process a `TestApp`
  stays `Starting`, since no transport serves it.
- **Breaking: the init phases run once per app.** `App::init` and `App::run` shared no
  guard, so a `run` after an `init` ran every `#[on_module_init]` and
  `#[on_application_bootstrap]` again. Whichever comes second now skips them, and after
  a failed init both return `InitFailedError` without running a hook. **`TestApp::init`
  is removed**: `TestApp::builder().build()` already ran the init phases.
  `HeadlessApp::init` stays — `build_headless()` runs none — and runs them once.

### A signal during the boot stops it cleanly, naming the step

`SIGINT` and `SIGTERM` were handled only once the init hooks had run, so a signal during
the boot — a rollout replacing a pod still opening its pool — met the operating system's
default action: the process died with no line, no telemetry flush and no teardown.
`#[nest_rs::main]` now installs the handlers before it runs `main`.

- **A signal before any app begins its boot** exits at once with 130 (`SIGINT`) or 143
  (`SIGTERM`), after one `error` on `nest_rs::app`, `shutdown signal received before
  the app started: exiting at once`, with `signal`, `exit_code` and `step="main"` — on
  stderr, as `nestrs: shutdown signal received before the app started: …`, while no
  subscriber exists yet.
- **A signal during the boot** turns the app `Draining`; the boot stops at its next
  await — the factory, the transport's `configure` or the init hook it waits on is
  dropped there — files one `warn` on `nest_rs::app`, `shutdown signal received during
  the boot: stopping it`, with `signal` and `step`, and `build()`, `init()` or `run()`
  returns `BootInterruptedError { cause, step }`, so `main` exits 1 through `anyhow`
  after the telemetry flush. `BootStep` names the step: `Factory { type_name }`,
  `Configure { transport }`, `InitHook { phase, provider, method, origin }`.
- **A second signal** exits at once, as on the way down, and its `error` now names the
  boot step it abandons: `step="boot"` with the step's own fields (`type_name`,
  `transport`, or the hook's `phase`, `provider`, `method` and `origin`) — the escape
  from a step that blocks its thread.
- An app run outside `#[nest_rs::main]`, as a `#[tokio::test]` runs it, installs the
  handlers at the start of its `run` instead.

### The default request timeout fits the shutdown window

**Breaking (a default):** `<PREFIX>_HTTP__REQUEST_TIMEOUT_SECS` defaults to
**20** seconds, down from 30 — the shutdown window's own default, which keeps
its 20 s, so the 29.5-second way down does not move. With the old default a
request admitted just before a stop ran inside its own budget and was cut
unanswered when the window closed; it is now answered, at the latest with its
`503`.

- A deployment whose request timeout outlasts `<PREFIX>_HTTP__SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_SECS`,
  or is off, gets one `warn` on `nest_rs::http` at boot — `the request timeout
  outlasts the shutdown window: a request admitted just before a stop can be
  cut unanswered`, with `request_timeout_secs` (`0` when off) and
  `shutdown_timeout_secs`. It is not refused: a deployment serving long uploads
  with the budget off may accept the cut knowingly.
- **The framework's nets under a request wait 18 seconds, down from 20**:
  `nest_rs::authn::AUTHENTICATE_TIMEOUT`, `nest_rs::throttler::HIT_TIMEOUT` and
  `nest_rs::queue::BACKEND_TIMEOUT`. Each must sit below the request deadline,
  which starts first: at an equal 20 seconds the edge would always answer a hung
  strategy, store or backend with a bare `503`, and the net built to name it
  would never be heard on HTTP. They stay above every budget under them — the
  Redis command budget (10 s), the Postgres statement bound and the storage
  operation (15 s each), a social login's three calls (5 s each) — and the boot
  still refuses a budget at or past a net it reaches, now at 18 seconds.

### A module first imported in a `register` is refused before it registers, and no module panics on its config

- **A hand-written importer reaching `GraphqlModule::for_root` or
  `OpenApiModule::for_root` in its `register` alone panicked**, `GraphqlConfig
  is resolved by ConfigModule::provide_feature`, before the boot could say
  why. `ContainerBuilder::import` collects a module first imported in the
  register phase, as before; when that `collect` queued a factory nothing will
  drain, it now refuses the boot there with `LateFactoryError`, naming the
  factory's type, and never runs the module's `register` — nor does a later
  import of it. A module whose late `collect` queued nothing, or only a default
  whose type is already built, still registers, whatever another module left
  queued. When a late `collect` also declares a type a second time, the boot
  names that contested declaration first, as it does in the collect phase.
- **A container built by hand drains no factory, so it now refuses such a
  module too.** `Container::builder()` is in the register phase: in
  `Container::builder().import::<M>()`, a module whose `collect` queues a
  factory — a bare import's `#[config]` among them — is refused and left out,
  where its `register` used to run over the value nothing built.
  `ContainerBuilder::build` does not report that refusal (the boot reads it
  before building), so a unit test written this way finds the module's
  providers absent. Seed each config its `collect` queues first
  (`.provide(WsConfig::default())`), or boot the module through
  `App::builder()` or `TestApp`. A pinned `for_root` value is a declaration
  and never registers on a hand-built builder.
- **`ConfigModule::resolved::<C>(&container)` reads the config a module's
  `collect` queued**, or hands back `ConfigNotResolvedError` naming it: "`C` was
  read before any factory resolved it: the module owning it is not imported, or
  is imported in a `register` alone, or the factory reading it does not name it
  among its inputs". A `register` files it with `builder.refuse(..)`, a factory
  returns it with `?`, and the boot fails on it — where twelve first-party
  sites panicked or refused in words of their own: the GraphQL, OpenAPI and
  static-files registers, Server-Timing's (whose `MissingDependencyError` it
  replaces), the HTTP transport, and the factories of the Redis connection, the
  Redis queue consumer, the SeaORM pool, the rate limiter's policy and guard,
  the OAuth client, the resource server's metadata and `JwtService`.
- **Each of those factories names its config among its inputs**, so it runs
  once its config exists wherever its module falls in `imports = [..]`, where
  it relied on being queued after the config's factory in the same `collect`.
  The rate limiter's guard waits on its policy and config too, and refuses
  with `MissingDependencyError` where a missing policy panicked.

### A provider a module lists is built, or the boot refuses naming why

**If it boots, it's wired — now without exception.** When no provider of a
`#[module]` could progress, the register phase refused a cycle only if every
stalled provider waited on a sibling, and otherwise stopped with nothing
filed: the access graph then passed in two shapes, and the app booted with a
listed provider missing. Its first lazily built consumer panicked on the
absent dependency, and a decorated host on it went inert with the cause
`unknown`. The two shapes:

- **an import cycle a provider crosses** — `AModule` imports `BModule`,
  `BModule` imports `AModule`, and `BService` injects `AService`: one module
  registers before the other's providers exist;
- **a type a hand-written module or a `for_root` registers later in the boot**,
  injected by a provider of a module that does not import it.

Each now fails the boot once the access graph passes, with
`UnbuiltProviderError` naming the module, the provider, what it needed, and
the cause with its edit (`UnbuiltCause::ImportCycle` with the modules of the
cycle, or `UnbuiltCause::RegisteredLater`):

```text
module `UsersModule`: `UsersService` was never built — it needs
`PostsService`, which nothing had registered when `UsersModule`'s providers
were built; `UsersModule` and `PostsModule` import each other, so one
registers before the other's providers exist: move what both need into a
module both import
```

An import cycle no provider waits across still boots. An access violation in
the same app is refused first, since its remedy names the module to import. A
cycle among one module's providers is now refused also when another provider
of that module waits on something outside it, where the boot used to say
nothing, and so is a provider that waited forever on an optional dependency a
stalled sibling would have provided. A provider a test override replaces is no
longer missing, and is not refused.

**Breaking:** a composition that booted with a provider silently missing no
longer boots. `Discoverable` gains `optional_dependency_names`, the label of
each `optional_dependencies` entry, with an empty default; `#[injectable]` and
`#[interceptor]` emit it.

### Optional dependencies are inside the access and scope checks, and never depend on import order

An `#[inject] Option<Arc<T>>` field is how a provider degrades when a
capability is absent, and nothing checked it: it read whatever the container
held when its provider was built. Whether `T` was there could depend on the
order of `imports = [..]`, and the edge hosts (`#[controller]`, `#[gateway]`,
`#[resolver]`, `#[mcp]`) did not even record the field, so a `Net` around one
missed it too. Now the access graph holds it like any `#[inject]`:

- **`T` provided by a module the app reaches** must be in the consumer's
  import closure, or the boot fails with `AccessGraphError` naming the module
  to import. `T` that only another binary's module provides — linked, never
  imported here — is simply absent, as before.
- **A singleton's `Option<Arc<T>>` of a request-scoped or transient `T`** was
  `None` forever; it now fails the boot with `ScopeViolationError`.
- **A provider built before `T` arrives** — across an import cycle, or before
  a hand-written module or a `for_root` registers it — fails the boot with
  `LateOptionalDependencyError`, carrying `UnbuiltCause` and its edit, rather
  than running as if the capability were absent.

**Breaking:** a composition relying on one of these shapes no longer boots.
`Discoverable` gains `injected_optional_names`, the label of each
`injected_optional` entry, with an empty default; every decorator that builds
a provider emits it, and the four edge hosts now emit `injected_optional`.

### A request-scoped or transient cycle fails the boot, and every cycle sentence prescribes an edit that works

The boot refused a cycle among one module's singletons only. Request-scoped
and transient providers register no build-order dependencies, so a cycle among
them passed the boot and panicked inside the first request — or the first
`Container::get` — that resolved it. The seal now walks what every reachable
request-scoped and transient provider injects, optionally or not, and refuses
the first cycle with `ProviderCycleError`, a transient ↔ request-scoped one
included. So does a cycle among singletons of two modules importing each
other, optional edges included, which the register phase saw only as a
provider never built: it is named as the cycle it is, whichever module the
root imports first, rather than as an import cycle whose remedy — a module
both import — would only lead to it.

Every cycle sentence told the reader to break it "by injecting
`Arc<dyn Trait>`" — advice that cannot work here: an `Arc<dyn Trait>` field is
a dependency gated exactly like a concrete one, so the cycle stays. They now
say what does:

```text
module `PostsModule`: singleton provider cycle `PostsService` → `UsersService`
→ `PostsService` — break it by moving what both need into a third provider
both inject, by passing the value as a method argument, or by publishing an
event one side listens to
```

The resolution panics stay, with the same remedy, for factories registered by
hand with `provide_scoped` / `provide_transient`, which no module descriptor
shows the boot.

**Breaking:** `ProviderCycleError` is `#[non_exhaustive]`; `type_names` is
replaced by `scope: ProviderScope`, the scope of the chain's first provider,
and `chain`, the providers in the order each waits on the next, the first
repeated last (`["A", "B", "A"]`) — a singleton cycle's too, which used to list
its members. A request-scoped or transient cycle that booted and panicked at
first resolution now fails the boot.

### Lifecycle hooks run dependencies first, and in reverse at shutdown

Hooks ran in `(provider, method)` name order within each phase, and the shutdown phases
ran in that same order rather than in reverse: renaming a type reordered the boot, a
repository could flush after the pool it writes through had closed, and two hosts sharing
a short name ran in link order, which any code change reshuffles. The order is now the
dependency graph's, computed once at boot from the module descriptors:

- **Init hooks run dependencies first**: a host's hooks run after the hooks of every
  provider it injects, at any depth — `#[inject]`, optional or not, and a `Foo as dyn
  Trait` binding counts as `Foo`. **Shutdown hooks run in the exact reverse.**
- **Hosts the graph does not order follow the composition**: an imported module's hosts
  before its importer's, imports in their declared order, then each module's
  `providers = [...]` from left to right. A host no module lists (a seed, a factory's
  output, a hand-written registration) runs before every listed one at init and after
  them at shutdown; among themselves, such hosts run by type name, then module path.
- **A host's own hooks run in the order its `impl` declares them**, the same in every
  phase; spread over several `impl` blocks, by the module each block sits in first. One
  hook at a time, as before.
- The line a second signal files while a shutdown hook waits now carries the hook's
  `origin`, as the boot's steps already did, so two hosts with one name are told apart;
  so does the `debug` line `running lifecycle hook`, and the error a failing, panicking or
  interrupted init hook ends the boot with names the hook by its module path:
  `lifecycle hook api::migrations::MigrationGuard::check (OnModuleInit) failed`.

**Breaking:** no signature changes, but the hook order changes for every app whose
hosts were not already in dependency order by name. A hook that relied on name order
expresses the dependency by injecting the provider it needs.

### An extractor declares the provider it resolves, and the boot checks it

`#[inject]` fields and the `#[use_*]` layer lists were the whole access
contract; a provider a handler reached through a parameter — the edge's
`Scoped<T>`, `nest_rs_seaorm::Bind` — was resolved per request and never
checked, so a handler reaching a request-scoped provider its module does not
import, or one no module offers, booted and answered `500` at its first
request.

`nest_rs_core::ResolvesProvider` is the seam an extractor uses to say which
provider it resolves:

```rust
use std::any::TypeId;
use nest_rs::core::ResolvesProvider;

impl ResolvesProvider for CurrentTenant {
    fn provider() -> (TypeId, &'static str) {
        (TypeId::of::<Tenant>(), "Tenant")
    }
}
```

A host declares what its handlers resolve through the new default method
`Discoverable::injected_per_request`, which an edge's impl half fills by
asking, per parameter type, whether it implements the trait — read by its
type, never by its spelling, so an alias resolves the same; a parameter typed
with a generic parameter resolves none. The boot checks each one like an
`#[inject]` dependency: owned by the host's module, a module it imports, or
global infrastructure — else `AccessGraphError` naming the module to import,
or `MissingDependencyError` when no module offers it — but never for scope:
its consumer is a request, so a request-scoped provider is the point.

This release adds the seam and the check. The edges adopt it in their own
entries: the HTTP `Scoped<T>` and `Bind` implement the trait and `#[routes]`
probes its parameters; WebSocket, MCP and GraphQL take `Scoped<T>` as a
handler parameter. Until an edge does, its handlers' providers stay unchecked.

### A boot that fails after an init hook ran tears the app down

An init hook that failed returned the error at once: no shutdown hook ran, so the
leases, discovery registrations and connections the hooks before it took leaked until
they expired, and the transports were dropped unserved. A serve failure already ran the
teardown; a boot failure did not.

- **An init that fails, panics or is stopped by a signal runs the three shutdown phases**,
  in reverse, for every host the container holds — not only the hosts whose init ran,
  since every singleton was built — then closes the resources (below), within the
  shutdown hooks' one budget. One `warn` on `nest_rs::lifecycle` opens it, `the boot
  failed after init hooks ran: running the shutdown hooks`, with the failed hook's
  `phase`, `provider`, `method` and `origin`. A teardown failure is logged and never
  replaces the boot error: `init()` and `run()` still return the init's error
  (`BootInterruptedError` for a signal), and a later `init()` or `run()` returns
  `InitFailedError`.
- A boot that fails before any init hook ran — a factory, the seal, the wiring, a
  transport's `configure` — runs no hook, but still closes what the factories opened.
- The app turns `Draining` with the new **`StopCause::BootFailed`**, so a shutdown hook
  joining a loop on `stopping()` is not held to the budget.
- `TestAppBuilder::build()` and `build_ws()` tear down the same way before returning an
  error raised past the build; `build_ws()` runs the init before its transport serves,
  as `App::run` does, and `HeadlessApp::init()` stops the transports `spawn_transport`
  started before a failed init's teardown runs.
- The shutdown phases now file the `debug` line the init phases file per hook,
  `running lifecycle hook`, so an operator reads the reverse order from a log.

**Breaking:** behaviour only. A shutdown hook now also runs after a failed or interrupted
init, so it must tolerate a host whose init did not finish.

### Resources close at the end of the way down, and the container's drop is bounded

The way down promised to be bounded end to end, yet the container dropped after the last
bound, synchronously on the runtime thread, and no resource was ever closed explicitly:
the database pool's sockets fell when the process exited.

- **`ContainerBuilder::provide_close::<T>(|t| async { … })`** attaches a close for the
  `T` a module opened — the third kernel step beside `provide_wiring`, readable as a
  `CloseContribution`. The kernel runs the closes after every shutdown hook, the
  resource opened last closing first (a client a factory opened over a pool closes
  before the pool, whatever the import order), within what the hooks left of their
  budget, by the hooks' rules: a
  failure at `error`, a panic contained, one still waiting at the deadline abandoned at
  `warn` (`resource close abandoned: …`, with `close`), every later close still started.
  A `T` nothing provides, or one the app handed in (a seed, a test's override), is
  skipped: it is not the module's to close.
- **The SeaORM pool closes**: `SeaOrmModule` attaches `close_by_ref`, which sends each
  connection Postgres's `Terminate` and waits for checked-out ones. A connection your
  test seeds (`provide_arc(db.connection())`) is left to the test. Redis attaches none:
  a Valkey connection owes no goodbye (`QUIT` is deprecated since Redis 7.2). Storage
  attaches none: it is an HTTP client.
- **The container then drops on the blocking pool**, awaited until the same deadline: a
  provider whose `Drop` blocks is abandoned with one `warn` on `nest_rs::app`, `a
  provider was still dropping when the shutdown hooks' budget ran out: abandoned`,
  rather than holding the exit.
- The sum is unchanged: the closes and the drop sit inside the 5 s hooks budget, so the
  stated way down stays 29.5 s. A second signal during a close names it
  (`step="resource closes"`, `close`).

### App::close: the bounded teardown without serving

A migration, a one-shot command or a test booted the app without serving, and nothing
could run its shutdown phases: they were reachable from `App::run` alone.

- **`App::close(self)`** turns the app `Draining` with the new **`StopCause::Closed`**,
  runs the shutdown phases in reverse when its init ran, closes the resources and drops
  the container — the tail `run()` shares — within the shutdown hooks' budget, and
  returns `()`: what fails on the way down is logged, never returned. The one-shot
  shape is `build → init → container().get → work → close`.
- **The harness closes too**: `TestApp::close()`, `HeadlessApp::close()` — which first
  stops the transports `spawn_transport` started, now serving under the app's stop token,
  within the longest of their stop bounds — and `WsApp::close()`. Dropping a harness type
  without `close` still runs no teardown. `WsApp::shutdown()` keeps stopping the
  transport alone. A transport the harness spawned whose `serve` fails is filed at
  `error` on `nest_rs::app`, `a transport the harness spawned failed`, with its
  `transport` and the error's chain, where it was dropped unsaid.

### Transports listen before anything serves; the app says when it is ready

`App::run` spawned every transport's `serve` at once after the init hooks, and the HTTP
transport bound its port and checked its TLS material inside `serve`: a port already taken
or a certificate that could not serve surfaced only after every other transport had
started — the queue worker consuming jobs, then HTTP failing to bind and the app draining.
The kernel awaited each `serve` with no bound of its own, accepted `Duration::MAX` as a
stop bound, and dropped a `serve` that returned `Ok` on its own without a line.

- **`Transport` gains `listen`**, between the init hooks and `serve`: it takes what
  serving needs — binds a socket, checks TLS material, subscribes — and returns
  `Listening`, the addresses it bound. Its default listens on nothing, so a transport
  that needs no socket (the queue worker, the scheduler) writes nothing.
- **The boot order is configure, init hooks, listen, ready, serve.** Every transport's
  `listen` runs, raced against a stop like the other boot steps (`BootStep::Listen`, with
  the at-once exit naming the transport); only once every one has does the app turn
  `Ready` and the `serve`s start. A `listen` that fails fails the boot, its error
  carrying the step (``the `listen` of transport `HttpTransport`: the HTTP transport
  cannot listen on 0.0.0.0:3000: Address already in use``), with a failed boot's teardown:
  the shutdown hooks, since the init ran, then the closes, the app stopping for
  `BootFailed`. No transport has served anything. A transport that panics as it is
  built, configures or listens fails the boot the same way, where the panic unwound out
  of `run` past every hook and close: one `error` on `nest_rs::app` (`transport panicked
  during the boot; the boot is aborted`, with `transport` and `step`, the payload
  redacted), and the error ``transport `X` panicked``.
- **`app ready` carries the addresses**: one `info` on `nest_rs::app` with `transports`
  and `addresses`, comma-joined, the real port when the transport bound port `0`.
- **The HTTP transport binds in `listen`.** `serve` runs the accept loop on the socket
  `listen` bound, and is an error when `listen` never ran.
- **The kernel holds every stop bound.** `stop_bound` is read at the boot: one past
  `nest_rs::core::MAX_STOP_BOUND` (one day, above every first-party window) — or
  `Duration::MAX` — is refused with `UnboundedTransportError { transport, stop_bound_ms }`,
  "a transport's stop is bounded: declare its drain window", before any hook runs. On the
  way down `run` waits for the transports until the longest bound plus
  `SHUTDOWN_SETTLE_TIMEOUT` after the stop; one still serving then is aborted, named at
  `error` (`transport did not stop within its declared bound: abandoned`, with
  `transport` and `stop_bound_ms`), and is the app's error.
- **An early end is a failure.** A `serve` that returns before the app was asked to stop,
  `Ok` included, files `error` on `nest_rs::app` (`transport stopped serving before the
  app was asked to stop`, with `transport`), stops the app with
  `StopCause::TransportFailed`, and `run` returns the error.
- **The HTTP way down stays within its stop bound.** Past the window, the HTTP/2 streams
  it cut and the work its self-mounts ran off their connections now share one settle,
  until the window's end plus `SHUTDOWN_SETTLE_TIMEOUT`, where each waited a settle of
  its own and a handler blocking on drop beside stuck detached work ran half a second
  past the declared bound.
- **The test harness drives a transport as `App::run` does.**
  `HeadlessApp::spawn_transport` configures it, refuses an unbounded stop, has it listen,
  then serves it: once it returns, what the transport listens on accepts. `WsApp` binds
  the app's transport on loopback port `0` after the init phases and reads the address
  from `Listening`; the handshake is tried once, where it was retried for five seconds
  around a port reserved and released. `HeadlessApp::close` holds its transports to the
  longest bound plus the settle and names one it abandons at `error`, as the kernel does.
- **Breaking, for implementers of `Transport`:** `stop_bound` must be finite and at most a
  day; a `serve` returning `Ok` before the stop now fails the app; a transport that binds
  inside `serve` counts as ready before it really listens — move the bind into `listen`.
  `BootStep` gains `Listen`. A caller driving `HttpTransport` by hand calls `listen`
  before `serve`.

### A test override replaces the provider before anything is built from it

**`override_value`, `override_arc` and `override_dyn` now do what the testing
guide said they did.** An override was applied once the register phase had
built every eager provider, by swapping the map entry alone: a singleton
service built at boot kept the real `Arc`, so overriding the mailer a
`UsersService` injects changed nothing that service did — the shape where the
logic lives. The override is now installed before any module collects, and
whatever would register its type stands down for it:

- **a module's provider, of any scope, is never built.** Its constructor never
  runs and its own dependencies need not exist, so the access graph does not
  check them, and an override standing in for one member of a cycle breaks it.
  A request-scoped or transient provider becomes one double serving every
  request and resolution.
- **a factory output — a connection, a config — skips its factory**: no
  connection opens, no variable is read for it, and the module's close never
  runs on the double.
- **a seed of the same type is replaced**, whichever call comes first.

The double keeps its provider's module and scope: a consumer outside the
module's imports, a singleton injecting a request-scoped type through it, or a
second module registering the same type or binding the same trait object
(`DuplicateProviderError`) fails the boot in the test exactly as in
production. The double injects nothing, so its `on_module_init` runs before
those of the providers injecting it, even where the provider it replaced
closed a cycle with them. A provider bound
`Foo as dyn Trait` is overridden whole through either key; a consumer
injecting the key left absent fails the boot:

```text
unmet dependency: `Calendar` (in module `CalendarModule`) injects `Sundial`,
which the test removed with its provider — `Sundial` is overridden through
`Arc<dyn Clock>`: override the key this consumer injects
```

A host's `#[hooks]`, `#[scheduled]`, `#[listeners]`, `#[processor]` and
`#[indicators]` methods run on a double of the host's own type, as NestJS calls
the lifecycle methods of a `useValue` object; a host overridden through its
trait object leaves no instance for them, and the boot says so at `debug` with
the new cause `InertHost::Overridden` (`cause = "overridden"`).

An override nothing in the app's graph would register now fails the boot, where
it used to add a value nothing read:

```text
the test overrides `RecordingMailer`, which nothing in this app's graph
provides — no module lists it, and no factory or seed makes it — so the double
would never be read: override the type the app registers (for a
`Foo as dyn Trait` binding, `override_dyn::<dyn Trait>`)
```

An edge host (`#[controller]`, `#[gateway]`, `#[resolver]`, `#[mcp]`)
registers routes rather than a value, so overriding one is refused the same
way. The testing guide's mailer example overrode `RecordingMailer` itself, a
type the app never registered: it now overrides `dyn Mailer`.

**Breaking, in semantics (no signature changed):** an override reaches every
consumer, the provider it replaces is never built, and overriding a type the
graph does not provide fails the boot with `UnclaimedOverrideError`. A consumer
of a key an override removed fails with `OverriddenDependencyError`.
`InertHost` is `#[non_exhaustive]` and gains `Overridden`. `Discoverable` gains
a hidden default method the decorators emit; a hand-written `Discoverable`
provider is refused as unclaimed when a test overrides it.

### A handler's per-request provider is checked at boot

A handler parameter that resolves a provider per request — `Scoped<T>`, sea-orm's
`Bind<A, S>`, or a type of your own implementing `ResolvesProvider` — was resolved for the
first time on the first request, so a module the app forgot to import answered `500` there
instead of failing the boot.

- **`#[routes]` declares every parameter type that resolves a provider**, and the boot's
  access check reads them as it reads an `#[inject]` field: a provider no imported module
  offers fails the boot with `MissingDependencyError`, one whose module the controller's
  module does not import with the access-graph error, both naming the handler's host.

### A setup imported twice wires once, and an app runs one transport of each type

A feature module that needed HTTP could only write `HttpModule::for_root(None)` — the one
form there was — and in an app pinning its own, that attached a second `HttpTransport` on
the same port: the second failed to bind after the init hooks had run, naming neither
import. Two `GraphqlModule::for_root(None)` or `OpenApiModule::for_root(None)` mounted
their endpoint twice and the boot failed on the duplicate path. Two `McpModule::for_root`
naming a server were compared at the HTTP configure step and, when they differed, refused
with both identities printed, and two opening the endpoints with `Posture::Public` each
attached the opening and booted. Two `SeaOrmModule::for_root` closed the pool twice, and two
`ConfigModule::for_root()` failed the boot as a duplicate `Environment`.

- **What a setup wires per app runs in the module it imports by type.** `HttpModule`,
  `GraphqlModule` and `OpenApiModule` are modules a feature imports bare — a dependency
  declaration reading `<PREFIX>_HTTP__*`, `<PREFIX>_GRAPHQL__*`, `<PREFIX>_OPENAPI__*` —
  and their `for_root` returns `ConfigSetup<_, _>` behind the same alias (`HttpSetup`,
  `GraphqlSetup`, `OpenApiSetup`), so the transport attaches once and `/graphql`, `/api`
  and `/api-json` mount once however many modules import them. SeaORM's pool, its
  budgets and its close, and the active `Environment`, are registered once the same
  way.
- **An app runs one transport of each type.** `ContainerBuilder::provide_transport::<T>`
  records each type with the import that attached it; a second `T`, from any module,
  fails the boot with `ContestedDeclarationError` naming both imports and the remedy
  "An app runs one transport of each type: import the module that attaches it by type,
  bare beside the one `for_root` that configures it." A transport generic over a marker
  (`Consumer<Orders>`) is one type per marker.
- **The MCP server identity and the MCP opening are declarations.** An `McpIdentity`,
  and `McpAccess::Public`, given to `McpModule::for_root` are declared at their import site, so
  two `McpModule::for_root` naming a server, or two opening the endpoints — equal or not —
  fail the boot with `ContestedDeclarationError` naming both imports, before any factory
  runs; the refusal that printed both identities is gone.
- **Every first-party `for_root` is held to it**: `nest-rs-testing`'s suite imports each one
  twice — unpinned, pinned, and beside a bare import where the module takes one, in both
  orders — and checks it boots wired as if imported once, or refuses naming both imports.
- **Breaking:** two `McpModule::for_root` naming a server, or two opening the endpoints, fail
  the boot even when equal (one pin, one site); `HttpSetup`, `GraphqlSetup` and
  `OpenApiSetup` are type aliases of `ConfigSetup`, no longer structs; a module attaching a
  transport type another module already attached fails the boot.

### An edge adapter imports its transport, and a host left without one is named at run

An adapter the app imported did nothing when the app forgot its edge's transport
beside it, and nothing said so: the WebSocket adapters imported `WsModule`, but an
HTTP, GraphQL, queue or schedule adapter imported its port alone, so the app named
`HttpModule`, `GraphqlModule`, `QueueModule` or `ScheduleModule` a second time — and
without it a `#[scheduled]` method never ticked and a worker idled. No framework
module mounting on HTTP imported `HttpModule`, so the GraphQL quick start served
nothing to the `curl` that followed it. `nestrs g <edge>` wired the adapter, then
printed "Make sure the app imports … so the adapter is served". An app with no
transport at all logged `app ready transports=""`, then `app draining
cause=TransportFailed`.

- **Every edge adapter imports what serves its edge bare**: `HttpModule` for
  HTTP and WebSocket, plus `WsModule`, `GraphqlModule`, `McpModule`,
  `QueueModule` or `ScheduleModule`, so the edge's config is read wherever the
  adapter is imported. The import is idempotent and the app's pinned `for_root`
  supersedes it, so an app imports the adapters it serves and names a transport
  only to pin it. The queue's backend binding (`RedisQueueModule`) stays at the
  root, which chooses the backend; events' `EventsModule` stays at the port that
  emits. The demo's adapters and roots, and every page composing an app, follow.
- **`GraphqlModule`, `HealthModule`, `OpenApiModule`, `McpModule`,
  `StaticFilesModule` and `OAuthResourceModule` import `HttpModule` bare**, so each
  serves wherever it is imported. `WsModule`, the registry a port imports to push
  to clients, attaches no transport: a binary reaching that port opens no listener
  unless one of its adapters serves on HTTP.
- **`App::run` names a host left without its transport**: once the transports are
  attached, each host the app reaches whose edge's transport no module attaches —
  a controller, a gateway, a resolver, an `#[mcp]`, `#[process]` or `#[scheduled]`
  host — is one `warn` on `nest_rs::app`, `host not served: its edge's transport is
  not attached`, with `edge`, `host`, the `module` listing it and the `import` that
  serves it. Never at the seal: `TestApp` and `HeadlessApp` serve hosts on
  transports they build.
- **A run with no transport says so**: `app serves nothing: no transport is
  attached` at `warn`, with the remedy; its init hooks run, then it stops for the
  new `StopCause::NoTransport`, never turning `Ready`.
- **`nestrs g <edge>`** writes the import into the adapter's `module.rs` (one
  template, per edge), `g events` imports `EventsModule` into the port, and the
  "Make sure the app imports" step is gone; `g queue` names `RedisModule`
  and `RedisQueueModule` only when the app's root binds no queue backend. `nestrs
  new`'s root lists its adapter and no transport.
- **The no-bus line names the port**: a listener with no event bus asks for
  `EventsModule` in the module whose service emits its event.
- **Not a break**: an app root still importing a transport beside its adapters runs
  one transport of each type, as before.
- **Breaking:** a container built by hand importing `GraphqlModule`, `HealthModule`,
  `OpenApiModule`, `McpModule`, `StaticFilesModule` or `OAuthResourceModule` fails
  `build()` unless it seeds `HttpConfig` too, since each imports `HttpModule`; an app
  importing one of them without `HttpModule` now listens on `<PREFIX>_HTTP__PORT`; a
  run with no transport drains for `cause=NoTransport`, where it drained for
  `TransportFailed`.

### A container built by hand holds a seed to the checks a read one passes

A config seeded on `Container::builder()` beside the module that queues it skipped its
`Config::check`: the boot runs that check in its factory phase, where the seed makes the
factory skip, and a container built by hand drains no factory. A driver's own suite, which
builds by hand, passed a seed the app's boot then refused.

- **`ContainerBuilder::build` runs the check each queued factory declares on a seed of its
  output**, after a contested declaration and before what the register phase leaves, as the
  boot's order reads them, and refuses with **`ContainerError::SeedRefused`**, carrying the
  check's own error — a `ConfigError` naming the namespace for a config.

What fails: `build`, for a container built by hand seeding a value its module's check
refuses — which an app's boot already refused.

### A request-scoped or transient layer a job, a tick or a listener binds fails the boot naming its scope

An interceptor or exception filter bound with `#[use_*]` on a `#[process]` method, a
`#[scheduled]` trigger or an `#[on_event]` method, or on their block, passed the access
graph whatever its scope, while the site resolves its layers once, when it composes at boot.
A request-scoped one was refused there — at the wiring step for a listener, at the
transport's configure, after the boot, for a job and a tick — with "no imported module
provides it — import the module that provides it", which is false when a module provides
it. A transient one was built there once and shared by every run, as a singleton is.

- **The access graph refuses both with `nest_rs::core::ScopedLayerError`**, naming the
  layer, its host and the host's module, beside the scope violations: "a site resolves its
  layers once, when it composes at boot, so a request-scoped `PerAttempt` would never be
  built for it, and a transient one would be built once and shared by every run. Make
  `PerAttempt` a singleton."

**Breaking:** an app binding a transient layer on a job, a tick or a listener booted and
ran that layer as one instance shared by every run; its boot now fails, as a transient
layer on a controller, a gateway or a resolver already did.

What fails: the boot of an app binding a request-scoped or transient layer on a job, a tick
or a listener — a request-scoped one never ran, its app failing at the wiring step or at
configure; a transient one ran as one instance.

### A missing unit-of-work binding, port backend or relation loader is refused at boot with the line to add

Three wiring gaps booted, then failed every request that reached them. An app leaving
`SeaOrmDatabaseModule` out failed each `Repo` call with `no ambient database executor` at
`error` and an opaque `500`. A provider injecting `Arc<dyn JobProducer>` with no queue
backend was told to "add a provider for `dyn JobProducer` … or seed it at
`App::builder()`", while the port's own sentence, naming `RedisQueueModule`, was said only
for two backends. And a GraphQL relation whose target's module the serving app did not
import was counted at `warn` — a count, since a loader's registration carried no name —
then erred on every query selecting it.

- **A crate names the remedy for its own key**: `nest_rs::core::DependencyRemedy`, a
  link-time entry keyed by the key's `TypeId`, text only. `MissingDependencyError` gains
  `remedy: Option<&'static str>` (and `with_remedy`), and its sentence then names that
  line: "unmet dependency: `PostsService` (in module `PostsModule`) depends on `Jobs`,
  which no module this app imports provides. Import exactly one queue backend's binding
  — `nest_rs::redis::RedisQueueModule` binds the queue port over Redis." The queue port
  names its binding for `Jobs` (and the handle to inject for a backend's contract), the database port for the unit of
  work, `SeaOrmModule` for the pool and `RedisModule` for the default instance's
  connection (`RedisModule::<Cache>` for a named one); a key no crate names keeps the
  generic sentence.
- **A host whose generated code runs `Repo` needs a unit of work**: a `#[crud]` controller
  or resolver, a route or a field under `#[authorize(A, bind = S)]`, and the service a
  GraphQL-exposed entity names (`#[expose(service = S, graphql)]`). One the app reaches
  with no binding fails the boot naming `SeaOrmDatabaseModule`. A pool reached only by
  injection needs none, and boots without a word.
- **A served relation needs its loader's owner**: the schema build refuses an exposed
  relation the schema serves whose loader's owner the app does not reach, naming the
  field, the entity declaring it and the module to import — "`User.org` (declared on
  `…::user::Entity`) reads a loader of `…::OrgsService`: import `OrgsModule` into the
  module serving `User`". The count at `warn` stays for the loaders no served relation
  reads.

`SeaOrmDatabaseModule` stays the binding it was: it chooses every unit over SeaORM's
executor, the pool's ambient budget and the outbox relay, so folding it into
`SeaOrmModule::for_root` was refused (`.claude/decisions/declared-need-refused-at-boot.md`).
`nestrs g http` and `nestrs g graphql` over a resource port wire their `#[crud]` adapter
only into an app importing the binding, as `g resource` does, and otherwise name it in the
next step.

**Breaking:** an app reaching such a host with no unit-of-work binding, or serving such a
relation without its owner's module, no longer boots; each failed every request the gap
touched. The database port's `BACKEND_REMEDY` drops "with none, a unit runs bare", since
it is now said for none too.

What fails: the boot of an app with a `#[crud]` host, a bound route or field, or a
GraphQL-exposed entity's service and no `SeaOrmDatabaseModule`, naming the binding; the
GraphQL mount of an app serving a relation whose target's module it does not import,
naming the field and the module.

### A served field whose resolver or loader the app does not reach is refused at boot

An exposed relation was checked; a hand-written field was not. A `#[field_resolver]`'s
`&DataLoader<L>` parameter bound with `data_unchecked`, so an owner the app did not reach
panicked on every query selecting the field, while the docs taught a second spelling,
`ctx.data_unchecked`, in a snippet that would not compile. And `#[ComplexObject]` grafts a
field resolver onto its parent at compile time, so a served parent served the field whether
or not the app reached its resolver: a panic on what the resolver injects, answering the
whole request a `500`, or, with nothing injected, a body running outside every module the
app imports.

- **A `&DataLoader<L>` parameter declares the need**: the schema build refuses a served
  field whose loader's owner the app does not reach, as it does a relation's — "`Author.namesakes`
  (declared on `…::Author`) reads a loader of `…::PostsService`: import `PostsModule` into
  the module serving `Author`". The parameter is the one spelling taught, and a loader no
  `#[dataloader]` generated is a compile error saying so.
- **A `#[field_resolver]` declares its resolver**: "`User.avatarUrl` is resolved by
  `…::ProfilesResolver`: import `ProfilesGraphqlModule` into the module serving `User`".
- **Its absence fails a field, never panics**: a schema no `GraphqlModule` built, which
  reaches no check, answers the opaque error and files the field and the loader at `error`.
  A relation's loader answers the same, where it sent the client that sentence and its type.
- **What a schema serves is read by Rust type**, so an unrelated object of one GraphQL name,
  linked into the binary and served by another app, refuses nobody.

**Breaking:** an app serving such a field no longer boots, naming the import. Its queries
selecting the field failed; one whose resolver injected nothing answered them from outside
every module it imports. A `&DataLoader<L>` parameter over a `Loader` written by hand, which
read what a `GraphqlContextSeed` seeded, no longer compiles: `L` becomes a `#[dataloader]`
method, or the body reads a `Context` the boot does not check.

A `#[query]` or a `#[mutation]` takes no `&DataLoader<L>`, so one reading a loader through
`Context::data` answers its error at query time, and a `Context` read by hand declares
nothing the boot could read.

What fails: the GraphQL mount of an app serving such a field, naming the field, what it
needs and the module to import; the compile of a parameter over a hand-written `Loader`,
`E0277` saying so.

### A module is imported bare to read its environment, `for_root` only pins, and the root lists only what it serves

"Read my variables" had two spellings on half the modules owning a config —
`GraphqlModule` and `GraphqlModule::for_root(None)` queued the same factory — and only
`for_root(None)` on the other half: `SeaOrmModule`, `RedisModule`, `ThrottlerModule`,
`AuthnModule`, `OAuthResourceModule`, `OAuthClientModule` and `OAuthServerModule` had no
bare form, so a feature wrote `AuthnModule::for_root(None)` to declare a dependency, and
`None` was most of what a composition root said. A named instance was a value at its
opener, `RedisModule::for_root((Cache, None))`, and a type at every binding.
`ConfigModule::for_root()`, first in every demo root, registered an `Environment`
nothing injected and filed the undeclared-environment line — which an app `nestrs new`
scaffolded, writing no such import, never filed. And the roots listed modules every edge
they imported already brought.

- **Every module owning a config is imported bare to read its environment**: `SeaOrmModule`,
  `RedisModule`, `ThrottlerModule`, `AuthnModule`, `OAuthResourceModule`,
  `OAuthClientModule` and `OAuthServerModule::<P>` now implement `Module` by type, as
  `HttpModule` did, and their bare import does the whole wiring. A named instance is a
  type argument wherever it is imported bare, `RedisModule::<Cache>`, as every binding
  already was.
- **`for_root` takes only a value to pin**: `C`, or `(Marker, C)` for a module an app may
  open more than once, and never `None`. `ForInstance<I, C>` holds a pinned config only.
  Every setup that pinned and imported its module is a `ConfigSetup<M, C>` alias
  (`SeaOrmSetup`, `RedisSetup<I>`, `ThrottlerSetup`, `AuthnSetup`, `OAuthResourceSetup`,
  `OAuthClientSetup`, `OAuthServerSetup<P>`).
- **The primitive splits by what it queues**: `ConfigModule::provide_feature::<I, C>(builder)`
  queues what a bare import reads, `ConfigModule::provide_root(x, builder)` the declaration
  a `for_root` pins.
- **`ConfigModule::for_root()` and `ConfigRootSetup` are gone**: the first config a boot
  loads registers the active `Environment`, injectable as before, and files `no environment
  declared; running as production` once at `info` when the process declared none — in
  every app that reads a config, scaffolded ones included.
- **MCP's and static files' declarations fold into their setup**: `McpOptions` and
  `StaticFilesOptions` are gone. `McpModule::for_root` takes an `McpIdentity`, an
  `McpAccess`, an `McpConfig`, or the declarations before the config as a tuple —
  `(McpAccess::Public, identity, config)`; `StaticFilesModule::for_root` an
  `StaticFilesConfig`, an `Embedded`, or `(Embedded, StaticFilesConfig)`. The setup stays
  opaque, its conversions its only constructors.
- **The MCP opening is `McpAccess::Public`**, replacing `McpOptions::posture`,
  which took core's four-variant `Posture` and refused two at boot: a posture the endpoint
  cannot take no longer compiles, and `McpModule::for_root(McpAccess::Public)` is the one
  visible line that opens it. Gated has no spelling: it is what every import leaves, so
  no `for_root` repeats the bare import, and none reads as a closing that another
  import's opening would override.
- **The remedies name the new spellings**: a missing pool asks for
  `nest_rs::seaorm::SeaOrmModule`, a missing connection for `RedisModule` or
  `RedisModule::<Cache>`, an `OAuthResourceModule` with no verifier for
  `nest_rs::authn::AuthnModule`, and `#[mcp]`'s identity refusals for
  `McpModule::for_root(McpIdentity { … })`.
- **The root lists the edges it serves, plus what it pins**: the demo's roots drop
  `ConfigModule::for_root()`, every `for_root(None)`, and `AuthnModule`, `AuthzModule`,
  `AuthzGraphqlModule` and `SocialModule`, each reached through the adapters and
  providers that use them; `SignInModule` imports `nest_rs::authn::AuthnModule` bare, the
  token signer it injects. A gateway's adapter imports `AuthzModule` as an HTTP adapter
  does, so `AuthzWsModule`, a provider-less grouping, is gone from the demo, and `nestrs g
  ws` writes no `authz/ws/`. `nestrs g auth` wires `AuthnHttpModule` alone into the app,
  and `nestrs g <edge>` no longer lists the authz module the adapter already imports.
- **Breaking:** `X::for_root(None)`, `X::for_root(Some(cfg))`,
  `RedisModule::for_root((Cache, None))`, `OAuthServerModule::for_root::<P>(None)`,
  `ConfigModule::for_root()`, `ConfigRootSetup`, `McpOptions`,
  `ConfigModule::provide_feature(None::<C>, b)` and `ForInstance`'s `Default` and
  `Option` conversions no longer compile; `OAuthServerModule` takes its payload as a type
  parameter. Their migration is the upgrading guide's step 8.

What fails: the compile, at each spelling above. At boot, only an app that reads no config:
it gets no `Environment`, so a provider injecting it, or a test overriding it, is refused
naming the type; it reads `Environment::from_env()` instead. A test's override of
`Environment` in an app reading a config is claimed as before. An app root still listing
`AuthnModule` or `AuthzModule` beside its adapters boots as before.

### A suite boots the composition `main` runs: the app-wide layer pools are the root module's

An app's guard pool lived on the builder `main` called —
`App::builder().use_guards_global([guard::<AuthnGuard>(), guard::<AuthzGuard>()])` —
outside the module tree its suites boot, so every e2e suite copied it, and a guard
added to `main` and not to the copy went untested. Booting had two entry points, and
the four app-wide families registered three ways: interceptors and exception filters
through inherent builder methods, guards and pipes through extension traits imported
only to make a method resolve. Each suite also armed the app's outbox by hand through
a framework port, and built the unit of work a service test ran in by hand.

- **The app-wide pools are declarations the module the app boots lists**:
  `nest_rs::guards::GlobalGuards<(AuthnGuard, AuthzGuard)>`,
  `nest_rs::pipes::GlobalPipes<(…)>`, and core's `GlobalInterceptors<(…)>` and
  `GlobalExceptionFilters<(…)>`, in the root's `providers`. `main` is
  `App::builder().module::<AppModule>().build().await?.run().await` in every app, and
  `TestApp::builder().module::<AppModule>()` boots the same pool.
- **The pool is the root's**: listed by a module the app imports — a feature that would
  choose what covers every route — or registered by no module's providers, it fails the
  boot at the wiring step naming the declaration and the module; a second declaration of
  one family fails it naming both and the module listing each; what a layer injects that
  the declaring module does not reach fails the access graph. The pools are checked before
  every module's wiring.
- **A pool's layer is a singleton**: the pool resolves its layers once, at mount, so a
  request-scoped or transient one fails the boot with `ScopedLayerError`, naming the layer
  and the pool — as on a job, a tick or a listener. A transient global layer booted
  before, built once and shared by every request.
- **One boot**: `App::new` and `UnresolvedFactoryError` are gone — every module owning a
  config queues a factory, which the synchronous boot refused.
- **`TestApp` and `HeadlessApp` arm the outbox** of an app binding a store and a queue
  backend at their boot, through the relay's own arming (`queue` feature of
  `nest-rs-testing`): a push inside a unit waits in the test's database, and a test needing
  its job run spawns `OutboxRelay`. A store without its table refuses the boot naming the
  migration.
- **`TestAppBuilder::ephemeral_database::<Migrator>()`** (`orm`): the app boots on a
  database of its own, created, migrated and seeded as its pool, dropped after the app.
- **`TestApp::unit(ability, || svc.list())` and `system_unit(|| …)`** (`authz`,
  `database`; on `HeadlessApp` too): a service's body in one unit of the app's bound
  `UnitOfWork`, as a request granted the ability or as system work, committed on `Ok` and
  rolled back on `Err`. Its events name the unit `testing.unit`, on `nest_rs::testing`.
- **`TestApp::bearer(&claims)`** (`authn`): the `Authorization` value of a token over a
  claims value, signed with the key `ephemeral_signing_key()` has the app verify
  ([*A suite boots on its database and signs its tokens in one line*](#a-suite-boots-on-its-database-and-signs-its-tokens-in-one-line)).
- **An app loading no config is told what registers `Environment`**: a provider injecting
  it is refused naming the first config the boot loads as what registers it, or
  `Environment::from_env()` where the app loads none. The harness seeds no `Environment`,
  so a suite refuses what the binary refuses.
- **The demo** declares its pools in `ApiModule` and `LiveModule`; its suites no longer
  copy them, arm no outbox, and its service tests run through `unit` and `system_unit`.
- **Breaking:** `AppBuilder::{use_interceptors_global, use_exception_filters_global,
  provide_meta, provide_wiring}`, `TestAppBuilder`'s four `use_*_global`,
  `nest_rs::guards::{AppBuilderGuardsExt, GuardSpecs}`,
  `nest_rs::pipes::{AppBuilderPipesExt, PipeSpecs}`, `nest_rs::core::layer_chain::GlobalSpecs`,
  `App::new` and `UnresolvedFactoryError` are gone. Their migration is the upgrading
  guide's steps 7 and 9.

What fails: the compile, at each spelling above. At boot, a pool a feature's module
lists, two pools of one family, or a transient layer in a pool. In a suite, a push inside a unit is no longer filed
on Redis until a relay files it, and a test database lacking the outbox's table refuses
the boot.


### An app-wide interceptor at the handler stage is the root's, and a pool registers what it lists

The pools became the root's "so a feature never chooses what covers every route", yet
a feature's module listing an `#[interceptor]` did exactly that, at the same handler
stage, with no line of the app's: one thing to want, two declarations, opposite rules on
who may write them. And a pooled layer had to be a reached provider as well, so a root
owning one wrote its type twice in one list.

- **`GlobalInterceptors<(…)>` is the one spelling at the handler stage**: an app-wide
  interceptor after the guards and the class gate is listed by the module the app boots,
  on `#[injectable]`. **Breaking:** a bare `#[interceptor]` and `stage = handler` do not
  compile, and the refusal names the list. The handler stage runs the app's list, the
  host's, then the method's: a site composes no modules' bucket.
- **`#[interceptor(stage = unit)]` stays what a module's import brings**, around the
  guards: a capability (`Server-Timing`, the RFC 9728 challenge) adds no line to the root,
  and `stage = unit` is the visible opening.
- **A pool registers what it lists**, so `providers = [GlobalPipes<(TrimStrings,)>]` writes
  the type once, in all four families. Once every module has registered, each entry
  nothing else in the app registered — a module, a seed, a factory, a test's override,
  which it claims — is registered as the root's. What a layer a pool registers injects is
  held to the root's imports, a `#[config]` it injects loads itself, its hooks run after
  every listed provider's, and it stays a singleton or fails the boot
  (`ScopedLayerError`). A layer a module lists stays that module's, one it could not build
  stays that module's refusal, and listing it beside its pool at the root still boots.
- **Breaking: each entry of a pool is a provider**, a compile error naming it where the
  pool does (`` `X` is not a provider a module or an app-wide pool can register ``): a
  type registered by hand or by a factory takes `#[injectable]` or a `Discoverable` impl.
- **A layer a pool lists and no reached module does is registered, not refused**: the
  access graph refused it naming the module to import, and it now runs, built from what
  the root reaches.
- **A singleton provider of the root injecting a layer only a pool lists is refused**, told
  to list it (`UnbuiltCause::RegisteredByAPool`, new), since a pool registers once every module is
  built; a module injecting one is refused by the access graph naming the root; and a
  registration that leaves its layer absent fails the boot (`MissingDependencyError`), as a
  pool would otherwise run without it.

### A resource can be opened once per instance, each instance a type of its own

A provider is found by its type, so an app could not open two of one
resource — a queue's Valkey, which must never evict, beside a cache's, which
lives by evicting. An instance is now a marker type:

```rust
use nest_rs::core::instance;

#[instance]
pub struct Cache;

#[module(imports = [
    BucketModule,           // the default instance
    BucketModule::<Cache>,  // the `cache` instance, a type argument
])]
pub struct AppModule;
```

- **`#[instance]`** reads the marker's name off its ident in snake case
  (`ReadReplica` reads `read_replica`), never chosen, and refuses a struct with
  fields or generics, `Unnamed`, a name holding `__` or a character an
  environment variable cannot carry, and the layout's reserved words
  (`queue`, `http`, `service`, …).
- **The default instance is `Unnamed`**, which adds no segment: an app opening
  a resource once names no instance, and its variables and seeds stay as they
  were.
- **A named instance's variables insert its name after the vendor** —
  `<PREFIX>_<VENDOR>__CACHE__*`, and a binding of it
  `<PREFIX>_<VENDOR>__CACHE__<PORT>__*`. Its config is held as
  `Instanced<Cache, C>`, its resource as a type of its own, so its budget, its
  net, its injection and its pinned config are per instance: two pins of one
  instance contest, a pin per instance boots.
- **An instance whose namespace is taken fails the boot naming both**
  (`ConfigError::SharedInstance`): a linked `#[config]` declaring that
  namespace, or another marker reading the same name.
- **A marker exists only through `#[instance]`:** `Instance` is sealed, so
  no hand-written impl chooses a name or shares the default instance's key.
  A marker declared inside a function — a test, a doctest — is its own, never
  its twin.
- **The default instance still resolves through `Config::resolve`**, so an
  override of it keeps applying; a named instance takes the same steps under
  its own namespace, and `Default` is the base every instance overlays.
- **A module opened once per instance is written by hand**,
  `impl<I: Instance> Module for BucketModule<I>`, its `for_root` taking
