  `impl Into<ForInstance<I, C>>`; `nest_rs::config::ForInstance` shows one
  whole. `#[module]` on a generic struct is a compile error naming that form.

No first-party module takes an instance in this change (Redis's section says
its own). A database is never opened twice: `Repo` holds one connection per
binary.

**Breaking, for driver authors:** `ConfigModule::provide_feature` and
`ConfigModule::setup` take `impl Into<ForInstance<I, C>>`. `None`, a config and
an `Option<C>` value still convert, but a `for_root(config: impl
Into<Option<C>>)` hands `config.into()` to `setup`, and a turbofish names the
instance. `ConfigError::Validation`'s `namespace` is a `Cow<'static, str>`, the
namespace the config was read under, and `ConfigError::validation` takes
`impl Into<Cow<'static, str>>` (the variant is `#[non_exhaustive]`, built through it:
see *A compile error, a boot refusal and a boot line name what the developer wrote*).

### A second Valkey is a named instance of the Redis connection

The queue refuses `allkeys-*` eviction and a cache lives by it, so an app that
does both needs two Valkeys — and `RedisModule` opened one connection. It now
opens one per instance:

```rust
use nest_rs::core::{instance, module};
use nest_rs::redis::{RedisModule, RedisQueueModule, RedisScheduleModule};

#[instance]
pub struct Cache;

#[instance]
pub struct Locks;

#[module(imports = [
    RedisModule,           // <PREFIX>_REDIS__*
    RedisQueueModule,
    RedisModule::<Cache>,  // <PREFIX>_REDIS__CACHE__*
    RedisModule::<Locks>,  // <PREFIX>_REDIS__LOCKS__*
    RedisScheduleModule::<Locks>,
])]
pub struct AppModule;
// #[inject] cache: Arc<RedisConnection<Cache>>
```

- **The default instance needs no marker**: the bare `RedisModule`,
  `RedisModule::for_root(cfg)`, a bare `RedisQueueModule`, and
  `Arc<RedisConnection>` read `<PREFIX>_REDIS__*`.
- **A named instance reads the same keys under its name** —
  `<PREFIX>_REDIS__CACHE__URL`, `…__CONNECT_TIMEOUT_SECS`, `…__TLS_*` — on any
  topology, and a queue on it `<PREFIX>_REDIS__CACHE__QUEUE__LEASE_SECS`.
- **Every binding binds the instance its import names**:
  `RedisQueueModule::<Cache>`, `RedisThrottlerModule::<Cache>`,
  `RedisScheduleModule::<Cache>`, and `RedisQueueModule::for_root((Cache, cfg))`
  to pin a queue's config there. A port still takes one binding, whichever
  instance it runs on.
- **Each instance is held on its own**: its budget under the nets of the
  bindings over it, every refusal naming its own variables
  (`<PREFIX>_REDIS__CACHE__URL`, never the default's), every line on
  `nest_rs::redis` carrying `instance` (empty for the default).
- **`ConfigModule::resolved_for::<I, C>`** reads the config a factory resolved
  for an instance, the per-instance twin of `resolved::<C>`; its refusal,
  `ConfigNotResolvedError`, names the instance.

**Breaking:** `RedisError`'s variants carry the variable names their sentences
use (`url_var`, `connect_timeout_var`, `tls_ca_cert_var`, `tls_vars`, `lease_var`), so a pattern
binding every field changes; `RedisConnection`, `RedisSetup`,
`RedisQueueSetup`, the three binding modules and the three stores
(`RedisQueueProducer`, `RedisThrottler`, `RedisOccurrenceLock`) take a
defaulted instance parameter, so code naming them for the default instance
compiles unchanged — the rate limiter's `store` field still reads
`RedisThrottler`, and a named instance's `RedisThrottler<…::Cache>`;
`ConfigNotResolvedError` gains `instance`. `RedisConnection::connect` opens the default instance alone — a
named one is opened by its `for_root`.

### Two values of one type are two types: string-keyed injection is removed

`#[inject(key = "…")]` could not work as shipped. `App::new` sealed with an
empty keyed set, so any reachable keyed field failed the synchronous boot,
provider present or not; a keyed value a module installed in its `register` was
refused as soon as anything injected it, and no factory form installed one; and
a keyed field resolved through `.expect`, so a consumer whose key was missing
panicked before the typed error was raised — at boot for an `#[injectable]`, at
mount for a controller, gateway, resolver, MCP host or interceptor. Repaired, it
would still have been a second way to hold two values of one type, and the one
rustc cannot check. Instance markers are that way:

```rust
// 6.x
App::builder().provide_keyed("cache", RedisConnection::connect(&cfg).await?)
#[inject(key = "cache")] cache: Arc<RedisConnection>,

// 7.0
#[module(imports = [RedisModule::<Cache>])]
#[inject] cache: Arc<RedisConnection<Cache>>,
```

Two values of a type your app builds itself are two newtypes
(`pub struct UpstreamClient(reqwest::Client);`), or, where the value keeps its
own type, a seed under a marker —
`App::builder().provide(Instanced::<Upstream, _>::from(client))`, injected as
`Arc<Instanced<Upstream, reqwest::Client>>`.

`#[inject]` takes no argument. A `key` is refused naming what replaced it —
``#[inject] takes no `key`: string-keyed injection was removed in 7.0, …`` —
and any other argument reads ``unknown #[inject] argument `x`; expected no
arguments``. Every registration now goes through one duplicate check: a second
value under one type fails the boot naming both, where a second keyed value
under one key warned and replaced the first.

**Breaking:** removed `#[inject(key = "…")]`; `provide_keyed` and
`provide_keyed_arc` on `ContainerBuilder` and `AppBuilder`;
`Container::get_keyed` and `RequestScope::get_keyed`; `ProviderKey` (the
container keys on `TypeId`); `KeyedDependency`, `KeyedDependencyError` and
`Discoverable::injected_keyed`, which a hand-written `Discoverable` that
overrode it drops.

### Every variable can be given as a file, and a family's variables carry the family as a level

- **`<PREFIX>_<NS>__<KEY>_FILE` supplies any namespaced variable from the file it
  names** — the Docker and Kubernetes secrets convention, so a secret never has to
  sit in the process environment. The file is read once at boot, must be a regular
  file of at most a mebibyte, and is never quoted back: an unreadable one fails the
  boot naming the variable, not its value. Text loses its trailing line breaks, so
  a file holding nothing else is unset.
- **One key, one spelling per tier.** `KEY` and `KEY_FILE` both set in the process
  environment, or both in the `.env` cascade, fail the boot naming both. Between the
  tiers the deployment chooses: either spelling in the process environment — empty
  included — shadows both spellings in `.env`, as it shadows a `.env` value of its
  own name.
- **Breaking for every hand-written `from_env`: `ConfigService::get` and `list`
  return a `Result`**, since a file can fail to read, so a reader propagates it with
  `?`. `env.setting("KEY")?` keeps the value with the spelling that supplied it —
  `setting.refuse(reason)` names the variable the deployment actually set, and never
  quotes what a file held — `env.material("KEY")?` hands back the bytes of a
  certificate or a key with the file they came from, and `nest_rs_config::spellings`
  words a setting that was not read, both spellings at once.
- **Breaking for deployments: a family member's variables carry the family as a
  level.** `nest-rs-oauth-client` and `nest-rs-oauth-resource` read
  `<PREFIX>_OAUTH__CLIENT__*` and `<PREFIX>_OAUTH__RESOURCE__*` — the same string as the
  paths `nest_rs::oauth::client` and `nest_rs::oauth::resource`. These fifteen
  variables are renamed, and no other variable is:
  - `<PREFIX>_OAUTH_CLIENT__<KEY>` → `<PREFIX>_OAUTH__CLIENT__<KEY>` for `CLIENT_ID`,
    `CLIENT_SECRET`, `AUTH_URL`, `TOKEN_URL`, `REDIRECT_URL`, `USERINFO_URL` and
    `SCOPES`;
  - `<PREFIX>_OAUTH_RESOURCE__<KEY>` → `<PREFIX>_OAUTH__RESOURCE__<KEY>` for `RESOURCE`,
    `AUTHORIZATION_SERVERS`, `SCOPES_SUPPORTED`, `BEARER_METHODS_SUPPORTED`,
    `RESOURCE_NAME`, `RESOURCE_DOCUMENTATION`, `RESOURCE_POLICY_URI` and
    `RESOURCE_TOS_URI`.

  The old spellings are read by nothing; a boot that still carries them says so,
  as the next section shows.
- **A required value holding only whitespace is refused** like an empty one, in the
  OAuth, social and storage configs, and **a storage credential pair is replaced
  whole**: `ACCESS_KEY` set without `SECRET_KEY`, or the reverse, fails the boot
  rather than pairing it with a default or a value pinned in code.
- **A structured value is decoded by `ConfigService::json` and refused without its
  content.** `env.json::<T>("KEY")?` decodes a JSON value, inline or through its
  `_FILE` spelling, as any `Deserialize` type; one that does not decode fails the
  boot with `ConfigError::Decode`, worded by `nest_rs::core::DecodeError` — the
  variable, where the value failed, the kind of value found and the type
  expected, never the value. A `from_env` decoding a structured value through
  `serde_json` itself and handing the error to `Setting::refuse` or
  `ConfigError::parse` no longer quotes it back either, as the next entry says.
- **Security: a refused value never reaches a boot error, whatever format worded
  the refusal.** `ConfigError::Parse` is `#[non_exhaustive]` and built only
  through `ConfigError::parse`, which `Setting::refuse` and every duration's
  refusal go through. It drops a parser's source excerpt — the `1 | key = "…"`
  lines a TOML error opens with, which repeat the refused line verbatim — and
  says serde's quoting sentences without their value, so a secret in a mistyped
  value no longer lands in the boot error a deployment's logs keep. No framework
  config reads TOML; one of your own that does is covered the same way.
  **Breaking** for code building `ConfigError::Parse { var, message }` as a
  literal: call `ConfigError::parse(var, message)`.
- `OpenTelemetryConfig::from_env` returns a `Result`, and an unparseable
  `SAMPLE_RATIO` or `METRIC_INTERVAL_SECS` — `NaN` included — fails
  `OpenTelemetry::init` with `OpenTelemetryError::Config`, naming the variable,
  where it used to keep the default after a line on stderr.

### A variable nothing reads is reported at boot

A deployment that misspelled a variable, or kept a name 7.0 renamed, got the
default and no signal: nothing asked for the value, so nothing could say it was
ignored. `#[config]` now files its namespace with a link-time registry, and
`nest_rs_config::read` — the funnel every `from_env` passes through — checks the
environment and the `.env` cascade for three shapes, each reported once, at `warn` on
`nest_rs::config`, by name and never by value:

- `config variable read by no config` — a key under a namespace this binary read
  that nothing read, with the nearest key that was read as `suggestion`;
- `config variable under a misspelled namespace` — a variable spelling a linked
  namespace otherwise than the loader reads it: with other separators,
  `<PREFIX>_OAUTH_RESOURCE__*` for `oauth__resource`, or `<PREFIX>_SEAORM_URL`, the
  spelling every `DATABASE_URL` teaches, for `<PREFIX>_SEAORM__URL`; in another case,
  the prefix's included (`<PREFIX>_Seaorm__URL`, `nestrs_seaorm__url` under the
  default prefix), since the loader folds none; or one misspelled segment of six
  letters or more away, a family member's included (`<PREFIX>_SEAROM__URL`,
  `<PREFIX>_OAUTH__RESOURSE__…`). The `suggestion` is always a name the binary
  reads;
- `config variable from the .env cascade shadowed by a config pinned in code; …` —
  a key a cascade file sets in a namespace a pin in code cut off it
  ([its own section](#a-env-key-a-config-pinned-in-code-shadows-is-named-at-boot)).

Everything else stays silent on purpose: one `.env` serves several binaries, and
another binary's namespace is not a mistake. So a namespace near miss is held to
both halves of the variable. Its key must be one the near namespace reads, or one
edit from one — a misspelled namespace still carries the key the deployment meant,
while another binary's variable carries its own, so `<PREFIX>_OPENAI__API_KEY`
beside a linked `openapi` stays silent. And a misspelled segment must have six
letters or more, within a quarter of its length: a shorter one has no room for a
typo that is not also a word (`auth` and `authz` beside `authn`, `es` beside
`ws`), so there only separators and case count. The namespace half
runs at every read of its namespace, ended by an error or not, so a renamed
required variable is named ahead of the boot error its absence causes; the key
half runs per namespace, where that namespace's `from_env` ran, because a config's
keys are knowable only there — `HttpCors` reads five of its six keys only when
`CORS_ORIGINS` is set, so a process-wide dry run would report a correct
deployment. A read made before any subscriber listens waits for the first read
that has one, and a deployment that filters the target out pays nothing for the
scan. `nestrs doctor` stays out of it: it links no framework crate, so it cannot
know which namespaces a binary reads.

**Breaking: a namespace belongs to one type.** Two `#[config]` structs declaring
the same namespace fail the boot at the read of either, naming both
(`ConfigError::SharedNamespace`). 6.1 let both read it, and the report above
would file the keys only the second one reads as read by nothing, on a correct
deployment; from a variable, a reader now finds the one type that parses it.

### A `.env` key a config pinned in code shadows is named at boot

A config pinned in code (`Module::for_root(cfg)`) reads its namespace over the pin from the
real environment alone, so every key the `.env` cascade sets there lands nowhere — the field
the author chose and every field `..Default::default()` filled alike. Every demo app and every
`nestrs new` app pinned `HttpConfig { port, ..Default::default() }`, so a
`<PREFIX>_HTTP__TLS_CERT_FILE`, `CORS_ORIGINS` or `TRUSTED_PROXIES` written in `.env.production`
reached none of them, and nothing said so: the key counted as read, so the unclaimed-variable
report stayed silent.

- **The cut stays whole**: a committed file never undoes a pin, and Rust cannot tell a field
  set to its default from one the struct update filled, so a per-field pin would hand a
  deliberate value back to a file.
- **It is never silent**: each key a cascade file sets in a pinned namespace is filed once at
  `warn` on `nest_rs::config` —
  `nest_rs_config::unclaimed::SHADOWED_CONFIG_VARIABLE`, `config variable from the .env
  cascade shadowed by a config pinned in code; export it into the real environment, or set it
  in the pin` — with `variable`, `namespace` and `pinned_by`, the import that pinned it
  (`` `HttpModule::for_root(..)` at `imports[4]` of `ApiModule` ``), never the value.
- **Nothing pins by default**: the scaffold and the five demo apps import
  `HttpModule` bare and write the port in the app's own `.env` (below).

### An app's own directory holds a layer of the `.env` cascade

The cascade read the files of the directory a process starts in, and a workspace starts every
binary from its root, so one `.env` served every app: a value that identifies one binary — its
port, the audience it requires, the issuer's signing key — could not be written for it alone.

- **Under a declared `development` or `test` profile**, the app crate's own directory — the
  one `#[nest_rs::main]` captures, `apps/<app>/` in a workspace — holds the same four files,
  read whole over the root's, the order Nx gives a project's files over its workspace's:
  `apps/<app>/.env.<env>.local > apps/<app>/.env.local > apps/<app>/.env.<env> >
  apps/<app>/.env > .env.<env>.local > .env.local > .env.<env> > .env`. `.env.local` stays
  unread under `test`, and `<PREFIX>_ENV` and `NESTRS_ENV_PREFIX` are refused there as at the
  root. A deployed profile reads the root alone.
- **The test harness** reads the package under test as the app's layer, under its Cargo
  workspace's root (the package itself when it is its own), where the binary starts;
  **`nestrs doctor -p apps/<app>`** reports what that app reads.
- **`nestrs new`** writes `<PREFIX>_HTTP__PORT` into `apps/<app>/.env` and imports
  `HttpModule` bare; the next app's port is read off each app's `.env`, or a port still
  pinned in its `module.rs`.
- **The demo**: each app's port in its own file (the api's compression beside it), the api,
  the live app and `auth` requiring their own origin as `aud`, the signing key in
  `apps/auth/.env` alone, and `<PREFIX>_SIGN_IN__RESOURCES` and the demo client's
  `resources` naming the api, the assistant and the live app, the api first. Its chart takes
  an app's own `config` (`apps.api.config.HTTP__COMPRESSION`), laid over the shared one
  (chart 0.5.0).
- A binary that calls `load_cascade(dir, environment)` instead of running on
  `#[nest_rs::main]` reads `dir`'s files alone.

### Every duration has a floor and a ceiling, refused alike from the environment and from code

**Breaking for a value that booted before, and for a config of your own that read
a duration.** One reader, `nest_rs::config::DurationBounds`, holds every duration
the framework reads — its unit, a floor and a ceiling, each with its reason, bound
to the field it holds (below) — and refuses a value outside the range in one
sentence naming the variable, under its `_FILE` spelling when that supplied it,
and, for a value set in code, the field (`` `Type::field` set in code is … ``).
The ceiling is a required field, and never above what the library or the kernel
the value reaches accepts: a Redis budget past the kernel's 32 767-second
keepalive limit failed every dial with `EINVAL` against a Redis that answered, and
a SeaORM timeout past what a clock holds panicked the boot inside sqlx, naming
nothing.

- `<PREFIX>_REDIS__CONNECT_TIMEOUT_SECS` and `<PREFIX>_SEAORM__CONNECT_TIMEOUT_SECS`
  hold 1 second to an hour, `<PREFIX>_THROTTLER__WINDOW_SECS` 1 second to a day,
  `<PREFIX>_HEALTH__INDICATOR_TIMEOUT_MS` and `PROBE_DEADLINE_MS` 1 millisecond to a
  minute; `0` is refused, and so is a zero pinned in code (a sub-second Redis
  budget set in code is still taken). A zero Redis budget pinned in code booted
  into `could not reach Redis … within 0ns`, a zero SeaORM timeout failed every
  acquire and blamed the pool, and a zero window let every request through at any
  limit.
- `<PREFIX>_AUTHN__EXPIRES_IN_SECS` holds 1 second to thirty days and
  `<PREFIX>_AUTHN__LEEWAY_SECS` 0 to 300 seconds (RFC 7519 §4.1.4), and so does a
  `JwtOptions` built in code, checked by `JwtService::new`; an unbounded value
  overflowed every mint or verify.
- The HTTP shutdown window and the Redis worker's durations, above, are read the
  same way.
- **`0` is off only for an `Option<Duration>`.**
  `<PREFIX>_HTTP__REQUEST_TIMEOUT_SECS` holds 1 second to an hour, the connection ceilings
  `<PREFIX>_HTTP__SSE_MAX_CONNECTION_SECS`, `<PREFIX>_WS__MAX_CONNECTION_SECS` and
  `<PREFIX>_GRAPHQL__MAX_CONNECTION_SECS` 1 second to a day,
  `<PREFIX>_HTTP__SSE_KEEP_ALIVE_SECS`, `<PREFIX>_MCP__SSE_KEEP_ALIVE_SECS` and
  `<PREFIX>_MCP__SSE_RETRY_SECS` 1 second to an hour, and
  `<PREFIX>_HTTP__TLS_RELOAD_SECS` 1 second to a day — and for each, `0` from the
  environment, or `None` in code, is off, as before. A `Some(Duration::ZERO)`
  pinned in code is refused rather than read as a zero that would cut every
  connection: off in code is `None`.
- OpenTelemetry's metric period (`OTEL_METRIC_EXPORT_INTERVAL`, in milliseconds, or
  `OpenTelemetryConfig::metric_interval`) holds 1 second to an hour, and `0` is refused
  rather than read as "keep the default".
- **`ConfigService::seconds` is removed.** A config of your own reads a duration
  through the `DurationBounds` its field names, `#[config(bounds = …)]`, an
  `Option<Duration>` turning off at `0` — so its refusal is the framework's
  sentence; see [Configuration](https://nestrs.dev/configuration/#configservice-api).
- **Breaking: a duration's key and its unit cannot disagree.** `DurationBounds`'
  fields are private; it is built with `DurationBounds::secs(least, most)` or
  `DurationBounds::millis(…)`, whose unit puts its suffix, `_SECS` or `_MS`, after
  the field's name, so no key is written by hand. Read a declaration back with
  `unit()`, `least()` and `most()`.
- **Breaking: a duration is read through its bounds and nothing else.** Every
  `ConfigService` reader — `get`, `setting`, `material`, `parse`, `json`, `flag`,
  `count`, `list` — refuses a key ending in `_SECS` or `_MS` at boot, whatever the
  deployment set, with the new `ConfigError::UnboundedDuration`, which names
  `DurationBounds`.

### The `.env` cascade is published before the runtime starts; `Environment::init()` is gone

`Environment::init()` merged the cascade into the process environment with
`std::env::set_var`, sound only while no other thread reads the environment — and its
documented place, `main`'s first line, runs inside `#[nest_rs::main]`'s runtime, whose
worker threads already exist. `#[nest_rs::main]` now publishes the cascade itself, before
it builds the runtime, while the process has one thread.

- **Breaking: `Environment::init()` is removed.** Delete the line; `main` keeps
  `App::builder()…` alone. Every reader of `std::env` still sees `.env` on `main`'s first
  line — `<PREFIX>_LOG_FORMAT`, `<PREFIX>_LOG_SOURCE_LOCATION`, `OTEL_*`, `RUST_LOG`,
  and a `migrate` or `seed` binary under `#[nest_rs::main]`, which saw none of it
  before. The deployment's own value still wins.
- A binary that does not run on `#[nest_rs::main]` calls
  `nest_rs::config::load_cascade(dir, environment)` itself, first thing, as it could
  before. `Environment::from_env()` and `Environment::declared()` are unchanged.
- A cascade that names `NESTRS_ENV_PREFIX` or contradicts `<PREFIX>_ENV` still aborts the
  process; it now does so before any subscriber exists, so the hook's one redacted
  `nestrs: panicked at …` line on stderr is what says it.

### Every TLS client reads one vocabulary, `ClientTls`, under the same three keys

Redis, storage and the JWK Set client each read TLS through a type of their own
over one variable scheme, and each judged it its own way: storage and authn
read `TLS_CA_CERT` alone, so a client certificate a deployment set for either
was silently ignored while the peer refused every connection.

- **`nest_rs::config::ClientTls`** is what every TLS client the framework opens
  trusts and presents, read in its own namespace from `TLS_CA_CERT` — the
  authorities the peer's certificate must chain to, replacing the system's —
  and `TLS_CERT` / `TLS_KEY` — the certificate and key presented to a peer that
  requires one, both or neither — each with its `_FILE` spelling.
  `RedisConfig::tls`, `StorageConfig::tls`, `AuthnConfig::tls` and
  `JwtKey::Jwks`'s `tls` are a `ClientTls`; built in code, it is
  `ClientTls::new(Some(Material { bytes: pem, path: None }), None)`, the
  identity `TlsIdentity::new(cert, key)`. No field or method turns verification
  off.
- **The material is judged once, the same way for every client, before
  anything is dialled:** no CERTIFICATE block, an authority rustls cannot
  anchor trust in, a key the process's crypto provider cannot sign with, a
  certificate that is not the key's — each refused naming the namespace's own
  variable, and the `_FILE` one when a file was read, never quoting the PEM.
- **Every client trusts through one read of the system's store**:
  `ClientTls::authorities_pem` hands the system's authorities over when none is
  set, so storage now trusts what Redis and the JWK Set client trust, where
  object_store's own client read the store its own way.
- **Breaking: storage refuses `<PREFIX>_STORAGE__TLS_CERT` and `TLS_KEY`** at
  boot, naming object_store, which cannot present a client certificate; they
  were ignored.
- **The JWK Set client presents `<PREFIX>_AUTHN__TLS_CERT` and `TLS_KEY`** to
  an issuer that requires a client certificate — refused without
  `<PREFIX>_AUTHN__JWKS_URI`, like the authority. A fetch whose handshake
  failed still answers an opaque `503`; the `authentication unavailable` event
  it logs at `error` names the setting that fixes it in its `detail` field
  ("the JWK Set endpoint's certificate does not chain to an authority this app
  trusts — … set `<PREFIX>_AUTHN__TLS_CA_CERT` …"), rustls's reason after it.
- **Verification is held by a lint**: clippy refuses reqwest's
  `danger_accept_invalid_certs`, `danger_accept_invalid_hostnames` and their
  `tls_` forms, rustls's `dangerous()`, object_store's
  `with_allow_invalid_certificates`, and `AmazonS3Builder::from_env`, which
  reads `AWS_ALLOW_INVALID_CERTIFICATES` among every `AWS_*` variable.
- **One crypto provider, `nest_rs_config::crypto_provider()`**, aws-lc-rs
  installed when the app chose none, is the one Redis, the JWK Set client and
  the HTTP listener build from; the listener judges its certificate pair with
  the same check a client's identity passes.
- Two Redis handshake remedies no longer name Redis's own settings
  (`tls-protocols`, `tls-ciphers`, `tls-ciphersuites`): every client says "the
  protocol versions and the cipher suites" the peer allows.
- Postgres keeps libpq's URL parameters (`sslmode`, `sslrootcert`, `sslcert`,
  `sslkey`), the connection URI every Postgres tool shares; no
  `<PREFIX>_SEAORM__TLS_*` variable is added.

### A client certificate refused is named on the boot's first attempt, on every topology

- **A Redis refusing the client's certificate — none presented, or one it does
  not trust — fails the boot on its first attempt with `TlsRefused`, naming
  `<PREFIX>_REDIS__TLS_CERT` and `TLS_KEY`.** TLS 1.3 settles a client
  certificate after the client's side of the handshake is over, and `redis` 1.x
  hands a refusal landing before the connection's first command to that command
  as a closed connection. The boot read it as an outage and retried, 250 ms then
  doubling, until an attempt's first command went out in time — or the connect
  budget ran out and the boot said Redis was unreachable, never naming the
  certificate. The boot now asks a handshake of its own, which writes before it
  reads, whenever its first command meets a closed connection. The same
  handshake learns a client certificate refused after the boot — expired, or no
  longer from an authority Redis trusts — which the `redis refused a reopened
  tls connection` warning named only when a command happened to be in flight as
  the refusal landed.
- **A sentinel, the primary it names and a Cluster seed fail the boot at once
  the same way.** Each
  read its first answer, `HELLO`, where TLS 1.3 lands the refusal, and retried
  the closed connection `redis` hands back within the connect budget. Under
  Sentinel, a reconnection after the boot asks the same two steps, so its
  `warn` names the certificate too.
- **A Cluster connection opened after the boot hears the refusal too.** A node
  the Cluster connection reopened, a worker's blocked read on a slot's primary
  and an `ASK` sent no `HELLO` first, so a refused certificate reached the queue
  as a dropped connection with no line. Each now sends `HELLO` first and says
  the refusal once at `warn` on `nest_rs::redis`, naming
  `<PREFIX>_REDIS__TLS_CERT` and `<PREFIX>_REDIS__TLS_KEY`.

### A factory's inputs are one tuple, in one form per kind

`ContainerBuilder`'s factory forms were enumerated by hand, with holes: a
factory could wait on one other factory's output, and on no more. A driver
needing two or three had no form to write it in. There are now three kinds — a
default (`provide_factory`), a declaration (`provide_declared_factory`), a dyn
binding (`provide_factory_dyn`) — each with an `_after` twin whose inputs, the
factory outputs it reads from its snapshot, are written as one tuple of up to
four types, `FactoryInputs`:

```rust
builder
    .provide_factory_after::<RedisQueueProducer, (RedisConnection,), _, _>(producer)
    .provide_declared_factory_after::<BoundConsumer, (RedisConnection, RedisQueueConfig), _, _>(
        BACKEND_REMEDY,
        consumer,
    )
```

The boot runs each once every input is present, wherever the factories fall in
`imports = [..]`, as it did for the one-input forms. A bare type in the inputs
position is a compile error, "a factory's inputs are written as a tuple", whose
label writes the one-input spelling for you.

**Breaking, for driver authors:** every `_after` form takes a tuple, one input
included. An app that writes no factory by hand is unaffected.

### A backend that panics fails closed, as one that does not answer

Every port the framework awaits put a net under its backend's call, but only
the scheduler caught the call's panic. A rate-limit store or an authentication
strategy that panicked unwound through the guard into the HTTP edge — the
client's connection dropped, no `429` or `503`, no line from the guard; a
panicking producer unwound into the handler that pushed; a panicking indicator
took the probe down with it; and a queue backend panicking under the worker's
read stopped that method from receiving for the life of the process.

- **Every wait on a backend goes through one helper, `nest_rs::core::within`**,
  which answers the call's output, `Unanswered::Elapsed` past the wait, or
  `Unanswered::Panicked` with the payload. It polls the call once before arming
  a timer, so a store answering at once — the in-process rate limiter, the JWT
  strategy with its key held — pays no timer, and it contains the panic so the
  port files it once, at `error`, through `contained_panic!`.
- **Each port answers a panic as it answers silence past its net**: the rate
  limiter refuses the caller for the window (`429` with `Retry-After`) and logs
  `throttler store panicked; denying (fail-closed)`; the authentication guard
  denies with its `503`, `#[public]` routes included, and logs `strategy
  panicked; denying (fail-closed)`; a push, a cancel or a checkpoint call fails
  with the new **`QueueError::Panicked { queue, call }`** (the enum is
  `#[non_exhaustive]`); the queue worker files `queue backend panicked answering
  the worker; the call counts as unanswered` and makes the call again under its
  backoff; a health indicator reads `down` with `"check failed"` and the probe
  answers for the rest (`health indicator panicked`). Each line carries the
  `panic` and where it happened, never in an error a caller reads.
- **The queue refuses a backend whose own budget reaches its net.** A backend
  whose `prepare` answers `Prepared::answer_bound` at or past `BACKEND_TIMEOUT`
  (18 s) fails the worker's boot — ``the queue backend `<name>` answers within
  <bound>, at or past the port's 18s net: lower the backend's budget`` — since
  the net would cut every answer it was still bounding. The boot's budget check
  already held the Redis binding; this holds a third-party driver that declared
  no `Budget`.

### Every retry loop waits one jittered curve

The Redis boot and reconnect loops, the queue worker's loop over a failing
backend and `retry_on_conflict` doubled their waits with no jitter, so every
replica that lost Valkey together dialled it again together, and two
transactions that conflicted retried at the same instant into the same
conflict.

- **`nest_rs::core::Backoff`** is the framework's one retry policy:
  `min(ceiling, first · 2^(n − 1))` scaled into [0.8, 1.2] by a hash of a seed
  and `n` — gRPC's connection backoff, and the queue's job retries since 7.0,
  whose documented waits do not move. `Retries` counts one loop's failures and
  `process_seed()` is drawn once per process.
- Redis's boot attempts, its Sentinel reopen and its kept-connection proof wait
  250 ms doubling to 2 s, each from the process's own seed; the queue worker's
  calls 250 ms doubling to 5 s, seeded by the process and the queue;
  `retry_on_conflict` keeps its signature and defaults (5 ms, doubling, 30 s
  before jitter), each call drawing its own seed. Loops a library owns — redis's
  `ConnectionManager`, the Cluster client, object_store — stay the library's,
  which already jitter.

### A module's config is pinned as a struct literal; the `with_*` helpers are gone

Five `#[config]` types carried builder helpers beside their public fields, a
second spelling of the one value `for_root` takes. A `#[config]` struct is
exhaustive, so the literal over `..Default::default()` is the in-code path:

```rust
HttpModule::for_root(HttpConfig {
    global_prefix: Some("/api".into()),
    ..Default::default()
})
```

Removed: `HttpConfig::{with_global_prefix, with_max_body_bytes}`,
`WsConfig::with_max_connection`, `McpConfig::with_allowed_hosts`,
`HealthConfig::{with_indicator_timeout, with_probe_deadline}` (the fields are
`Duration`s: `indicator_timeout`, `probe_deadline`) and
`OAuthResourceConfig::{with_resource, with_authorization_servers,
with_scopes_supported}`.

### A config's overlay is read off its struct, and a duration or a count has one shape

Every `#[config]` wrote `Config::from_env` by hand, one line per field, each key a
string typed apart from its field: a forgotten line left a field settable from code
alone, and a retyped key renamed its variable without a word. `#[config]` now writes
it: each field reads `<PREFIX>_<NAMESPACE>__<FIELD>`, its name in SCREAMING_SNAKE,
through the reader its type chooses — a scalar, a `bool` in the flag vocabulary, an
`Option` that keeps its base when unset, a comma-separated `Vec`, a `Secret`, a
`ClientTls` under its `TLS_*` keys. A field says what its type cannot:
`#[config(bounds = RANGE)]` for a `Duration` or a bounded count, its key read off the
field with a duration's unit after it, `json`, `count` (`0` unlimited)
and `with = path` for a reader of your own. `from_env = "manual"` keeps a hand-written overlay for fields that
depend on each other. A type of your own read from one variable implements
`nest_rs::config::FromSetting`. A vocabulary's keys are its members' names after its
field's key, joined with `_` (`CORS_EXPOSED_HEADERS` for `cors.exposed_headers`), so
every variable is derived from the structs but `ClientTls`'s, which name the material
every TLS client shares (`TLS_CA_CERT`, `TLS_CERT`, `TLS_KEY`); the two vocabularies
that spelled theirs otherwise move (below).

- **Breaking: a `#[config]` struct writes no `impl Config`.** Delete yours, or say
  `from_env = "manual"`; a field named for a unit (`window_secs`), a `Duration` with
  no bounds and a tuple struct are compile errors naming the fix.
- **Breaking: a duration is a `Duration`, or an `Option<Duration>` where `None` turns
  it off and means nothing else**, its default in `Default`, no unit on the field:
  `SeaOrmConfig::{connect_timeout, statement_timeout}`,
  `AuthnConfig::{leeway, expires_in}`, `ThrottlerConfig::window` and
  `HealthConfig::{indicator_timeout, probe_deadline}` (their accessors go), and
  `HttpTls::with_reload_secs(u64)`, whose `0` stopped the watch, is
  `HttpTls::with_reload(Option<Duration>)`. The variables keep their names. Whether
  `0` is off is the field's type: an `Option<Duration>` reads it as `None`, a
  `Duration` holds it to the floor.
- **Breaking: a count follows.** `ThrottlerConfig::limit` is a `u32` (60),
  `SeaOrmConfig::{max_connections, min_connections}` are `u32`s (sqlx's 10 and 0,
  always handed to the pool), `HttpConfig::max_body_bytes` a `usize` (2 MiB),
  `AuthnConfig::explicit_typing` a `bool` (`true`). A `#[config(count)]` field, and
  `ConfigService::count`, refuse a pinned `Some(0)` with the variable unset — `None`
  is unlimited — as GraphQL's four counts do, and `<PREFIX>_GRAPHQL__MAX_DEPTH=0` or
  `MAX_COMPLEXITY=0` now turns the check off, where the environment could not
  before.
- **Breaking: `<PREFIX>_HTTP__CORS_MAX_AGE` is `<PREFIX>_HTTP__CORS_MAX_AGE_SECS`**,
  held to a second to a day, `0` for none: the one duration read with no bounds and
  no unit.
- **Breaking: `<PREFIX>_HTTP__CORS_EXPOSED` is `<PREFIX>_HTTP__CORS_EXPOSED_HEADERS`**,
  the name of the field it sets, `HttpCors::exposed_headers`.
- **Breaking: every security header's variable is its field's name under
  `SECURITY_HEADERS_`.** The switch `<PREFIX>_HTTP__SECURITY_HEADERS` is
  `<PREFIX>_HTTP__SECURITY_HEADERS_ENABLED`, and `CONTENT_TYPE_OPTIONS`,
  `FRAME_OPTIONS`, `HSTS`, `REFERRER_POLICY`, `CROSS_ORIGIN_OPENER_POLICY`,
  `CROSS_ORIGIN_RESOURCE_POLICY`, `CROSS_ORIGIN_EMBEDDER_POLICY`,
  `PERMISSIONS_POLICY` and `CONTENT_SECURITY_POLICY` each take `SECURITY_HEADERS_`
  before them: `<PREFIX>_HTTP__SECURITY_HEADERS_HSTS=off`.
- **Breaking: `ClientTls::from_env` is its `ConfigField::overlay`, read under a field
  named `tls` alone.** Every refusal of its material names `TLS_CA_CERT`, `TLS_CERT`
  and `TLS_KEY`, so a `ClientTls` under another field is refused at the read,
  `ConfigError::MisnamedField`, naming the variables it would have read and the
  field to name it; a config holding two TLS clients holds two resources, each
  its own namespace.
- The missing-namespace error no longer prescribes `namespace = "seaorm"`: it says the
  namespace is the stem of the file's path below `src/`, with the rule's examples. The
  warns of a surface open outside a development profile (GraphQL's playground and
  introspection, the OpenAPI documentation, Server-Timing) are filed by the module
  that mounts it.

What fails if you get it wrong: a hand-written `impl Config` beside `#[config]` is
`conflicting implementations` (E0119); a deployment still setting
`<PREFIX>_HTTP__CORS_MAX_AGE` sends no `Access-Control-Max-Age`, and one still
setting `CORS_EXPOSED` or the bare `SECURITY_HEADERS` has the default in its place,
every default header on. The boot says each once at `warn` as `variable read by
nothing since 7.0`, its `read_as` naming the key it became (`CORS_MAX_AGE_SECS`,
`CORS_EXPOSED_HEADERS`, `SECURITY_HEADERS_ENABLED`). A header's flat key (`HSTS`)
fails the boot — `<PREFIX>_HTTP__HSTS is read by nothing since 7.0 — set
<PREFIX>_HTTP__SECURITY_HEADERS_HSTS` — since a `Content-Security-Policy`,
`Permissions-Policy` or `Cross-Origin-Embedder-Policy` it set would go unsent. A
`ClientTls` field not named `tls` and a count pinned at `Some(0)` fail the boot.

### A bound names only its range, and a count has bounds of its own

**Breaking for a config of your own with a bounded field, and for a deployment
setting an MCP session cap past a million.** A `DurationBounds` carried its
variable's key and its field's path as two strings `#[config]` already knew and
only checked against the field: five lines a duration, the configuration page
opening with one. Its off switch was said twice, by `Option<Duration>` and by
`Floor::UnitsOrOff`, a mismatch waiting for the boot; and a count bounded at both
ends was a reader of its own, typing its field's path into its refusal by hand.

- **Breaking: a range names its ends alone.** `DurationBounds::secs(floor,
  ceiling)` and `DurationBounds::millis(floor, ceiling)`, each end with its reason.
  `#[config(bounds = RANGE)]` reads the key off the field — its name in
  SCREAMING_SNAKE, the unit's suffix after it — and binds the range to it as the
  associated constant named for the field, `PostsConfig::CACHE_TTL`, a
  `nest_rs::config::BoundedSetting` over the field's type: `key()`,
  `var(namespace)`, `var_or_field(namespace)`, `read(env, base)` and
  `check(namespace, value)`, so every sentence naming the variable reads it there.
  One range serves every field it fits, and a floor above its ceiling fails to
  compile in a `const`. A hand-written overlay binds its own,
  `BoundedSetting::new(RANGE, Field::new("KEY", "Config::field"))`.
  `DurationBounds::{key, field, read, read_optional, check}` and `BoundedDuration`
  go.
- **Breaking: off is the field's type.** `Floor::UnitsOrOff` merges into
  `Floor::Units`: an `Option<Duration>` reads `0` as `None` and refuses a pinned
  zero, a `Duration` holds `0` to the floor, so `ConfigError::MisshapenDuration`
  goes with the shape it refused.
- **Breaking: a count has bounds of its own.** `CountBounds::new(floor, ceiling)`,
  named on a `usize` field the same way. HTTP's connection cap and the outbox's
  batch read through it, in their ranges; `<PREFIX>_MCP__MAX_CONCURRENT_SESSIONS`
  gains a ceiling, 1 048 576 — an idle session holds about 110 KiB, so a million
  hold over 100 GiB — refused past it from the environment and from code.
- **Breaking: a reader of your own is handed its field.** `with = path` names a
  `fn(&ConfigService, Field, T) -> Result<T>`: `field.key()` is the key its name
  spells, `field.path()` the path a value set in code is refused under. The derive
  binds that `Field` to the struct as the associated constant named for the field,
  `HttpConfig::VERSION_HEADER`, so a check of a value built in code outside the
  reader names the same key and path.
- **Breaking: HTTP's shared ranges are one value each.**
  `nest_rs::http::{MAX_CONNECTION_FLOOR, MAX_CONNECTION_CEILING}`,
  `{SSE_KEEP_ALIVE_FLOOR, SSE_KEEP_ALIVE_CEILING}` and `{UNIT_TIMEOUT_FLOOR,
  UNIT_TIMEOUT_CEILING}` are `MAX_CONNECTION_BOUNDS`, `SSE_KEEP_ALIVE_BOUNDS` and
  `UNIT_TIMEOUT_BOUNDS`, named on a field as they are.
- A misuse is one compile error: a range on a type none holds is said at the
  field, naming the three that are, `Duration`, `Option<Duration>` and `usize`; a
  range of the other kind is said at its name in the attribute, `expected
  CountBounds, found DurationBounds`, and so is a misspelled one, with the range in
  scope as the suggestion.

**What fails:** `DurationBounds::secs("KEY_SECS", "Config::field", floor,
ceiling)` fails to compile — delete the two strings; `Floor::UnitsOrOff` names
nothing — write `Floor::Units`, the field's `Option` saying off; `BOUNDS.read(env,
base)` and `read_optional` — read `Config::FIELD.read(env, base)?`, or a binding of
your own; a `with` reader taking `key: &str` fails to compile — take `field: Field`
and read `field.key()`; a deployment setting `<PREFIX>_MCP__MAX_CONCURRENT_SESSIONS`
past 1048576 fails its boot naming the variable.

### A retired variable says it is retired, in one sentence everywhere

A variable 7.0 retired was said three ways: a security header's flat key was refused
as `invalid value for …`, though its key and not its value was refused; `CORS_EXPOSED`
fell to the unread-variable report as a near miss; OpenTelemetry's retired namespace
and the console's `<PREFIX>_LOG` each had a sentence of their own. One sentence now
serves all: `variable read by nothing since 7.0`,
at `warn` with `variable` and `read_as` — the variable that reads what it set — and,
where the boot refuses one, the new `ConfigError::Retired` in the same words: `<VAR>
is read by nothing since 7.0 — set <NEW>: <why>`.

- The nine security-header keys refuse the boot through `ConfigError::Retired`, as
  before but in these words.
- `<PREFIX>_HTTP__CORS_EXPOSED`, `CORS_MAX_AGE` and the bare `SECURITY_HEADERS` are
  said retired, `read_as` naming `CORS_EXPOSED_HEADERS`, `CORS_MAX_AGE_SECS` and
  `SECURITY_HEADERS_ENABLED` — the switch, where the report's guess gave none. A
  crate declares the keys it retired (`nest_rs::config`'s private registry); the
  report never reads one.
- A variable under `<PREFIX>_OPENTELEMETRY__` names the specification's variable
  that replaced it (`OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` for `OTLP_ENDPOINT`, `OTEL_*` for
  a key 6.x never had), and `<PREFIX>_LOG` names `RUST_LOG`.

**What fails:** a log filter or an assertion on `variable read by nothing: the
console's filter is RUST_LOG`, `variable read by nothing: OpenTelemetry reads the
OpenTelemetry specification's OTEL_* variables`, `invalid value for
<PREFIX>_HTTP__HSTS` or `config variable read by no config` for `CORS_EXPOSED`
matches nothing — match `variable read by nothing since 7.0`, or
`ConfigError::Retired`.

### A config has one base, and a development opening resolves where it is used

**Breaking for a pin of a development surface, for a config of your own with a profile
hook, for a program reading a log format back, and for a suite asserting a surface closed
under the test profile. What production serves and writes is unchanged.** "Default" had two meanings. `Default::default()` was the production-safe value
and `Config::defaults()` the profile's, so `GraphqlConfig { max_depth: Some(12),
..Default::default() }`, which the configuration page taught, closed the playground and
introspection and stopped the SDL emit under `development`, with no line to say so. The
base that kept them took `use nest_rs::config::Config`, and the console kept the opposite
rule: its `Default` read the profile, while `LogFormat`'s own default was `Text`.

- **Breaking: a profile-gated setting is a value, `nest_rs::config::Surface`.**
  `ByProfile`, its `Default`, opens under a declared development or test profile and stays
  closed in a deployment and in a process declaring none; `Open` and `Closed` hold in every
  profile, and `true`/`false` from a variable read as them. The consumer resolves it where
  it uses it, the framework's own at the boot, with `is_open()`, or `writes()` for an emit,
  whose `ByProfile` opens under development alone. `GraphqlConfig`'s `playground`, `introspection` and `emit`,
  `OpenApiConfig`'s `enabled` and `emit`, `ServerTimingConfig::enabled` and
  `StorageConfig::allow_http` are `Surface`s, and the variables that set them keep their
  names and their spellings. A deployment that opens a public surface is still said at
  `warn`.
- **Breaking: `Config::defaults()` and `#[config(defaults = …)]` are gone.** `Default` is the
  one base: a bare import, a named instance and a discovered provider overlay their
  environment on it. A pin over `..Default::default()` closes nothing it does not name, so
  it keeps development's surfaces, and opens nothing in a deployment. `<PREFIX>_STORAGE__ALLOW_HTTP`'s
  refusal now reads `is false, or unset outside a declared development or test profile`.
- **Breaking: `LogFormat` takes the same shape**: `ByProfile`, its `Default`, `Text` and
  `Json`. `LogFormat::by_profile()` is gone; the console resolves the format where it is
  built, and `LogFormat::writes_json()` says what it would write. `LogSettings::default()`
  and `OpenTelemetryConfig::default()` no longer read the process. A format read back from
  them, or from their `from_env()`, may be `ByProfile`, so the question to ask is
  `writes_json()`: `format == LogFormat::Json` compiles and takes the text branch in
  production.
- **A config of your own takes the shape with one field**: `pub synthetic_seed: Surface`,
  as the demo's `AudioConfig` does, and no function beside it. A value that differs by
  profile and opens nothing, a pool size or a level, goes in `.env.development` and
  `.env.test`.

**What fails.** `..T::defaults()` and `C::defaults()` no longer resolve, a config writing
`#[config(defaults = …)]` is told `unknown #[config] argument`, an `impl Config` writing
`fn defaults()` is told the method is no member of the trait, and a surface written as a
`bool` is a type mismatch: `Surface::Open` or `Surface::Closed`. A suite pinning a
config over `..Default::default()` and asserting a surface closed under the test profile
now finds it open, as development does: write `Surface::Closed`.

### A config a provider injects loads itself; `for_feature` is gone

`ConfigModule::for_feature::<C>()` repeated what `#[config]` on `C` and the
provider's `#[inject] Arc<C>` already said, and leaving it out only failed the boot.
`#[config]` now files each config, and once every module has collected, the boot
queues the factory of each one a provider of an imported module requires — unless a
`for_root` pinned it, which still supersedes it. A config only an unimported module
asks for is never read. A synchronous `App::new` injecting one is refused for the
factory it needs, and a seed of one is held to its checks in a container built by
hand too.

- **Breaking: `ConfigModule::for_feature` and `ConfigFeatureSetup` are gone.** Delete
  the import; a module reading its config only in a `register` of its own calls
  `ConfigModule::provide_feature(None::<C>, builder)` from its `collect`.

What fails if you get it wrong: `for_feature` left in an import list does not
compile; a config nothing injects is not loaded.

### One default for every long-lived connection's ceiling, keep-alive and deadline

The four-hour ceiling on an `#[sse]` stream, a WebSocket and a GraphQL subscription,
the 15-second keep-alive of a server-sent stream and the 20-second deadline of a unit
were restated in each edge's crate, kept equal by comments. `nest_rs::http` exports
`DEFAULT_MAX_CONNECTION`, `DEFAULT_SSE_KEEP_ALIVE` and `DEFAULT_UNIT_TIMEOUT` beside
their floors and ceilings, and every edge's `Default` reads them; each edge keeps its
own variable, and the HTTP configuration page names the three ceilings a deployment
tightening a revoked credential's reach sets.

### Every TLS client the framework opens is proved to verify

Each client now has a test dialling a server whose certificate a private authority signed
— one the system's store does not hold — and seeing it refused before any request, login
or credential crosses: the Postgres pool, `EphemeralDatabase`, Redis (on every topology
its e2e suite runs on), storage, the JWK Set, the OAuth client and the OTLP exporter. The
threat-model page lists them, what each trusts and the setting naming a private
authority, beside a table of every URL the framework dials and who decides plaintext.

- `sqlx::postgres::PgConnectOptions::ssl_mode` joins the methods the workspace's lints
  refuse in the framework's own code: the TLS mode is set once, by
  `SeaOrmConfig::connect_options`, which refuses a mode that encrypts without verifying.
- **Added: `nest_rs_testing::TestAuthority::client_tls()`** (the crate's `tls` feature):
  the `ClientTls` of a framework client trusting the test authority alone, as
  `TLS_CA_CERT` naming its `pem()` sets it.

### One boot proof for every resource the framework opens

Redis proved its connection at boot through a retry loop of its own, private to
the crate, so the next driver would write it again; Postgres was opened by one
`Database::connect`, which sqlx retried in silence for a refused connection
and a server starting or full until the budget ran out, the boot error naming
no cause, and not at all for a name that does not resolve yet.

- **`nest_rs::core::prove`** runs one attempt after another until the resource
  opens, an attempt meets what every attempt would (`Attempt::Refused`), or the
  budget is spent on failures that may clear (`Attempt::Failed`), each attempt
  handed the budget left and cut when it runs out, the wait between two a
  `Backoff` curve drawn from a seed. It ends in `Unproved::Refused` or
  `Unproved::Spent { attempts, last }`, and reports `Proving` events — a retry,
  a budget cut, the proof — for the resource's crate to say on its own target.
  A third-party driver proves its resource the same way.
- **Redis proves every topology through it**, its sentences unchanged.
- **The Postgres pool is proved at boot.** Refused credentials (SQLSTATE
  `28P01`, `28000`), a database the server does not have (`3D000`), TLS that
  fails and a configuration sqlx refuses fail the boot at once, naming
  `<PREFIX>_SEAORM__URL` and the SQLSTATE — never the URL, which carries the
  password, nor the server's own sentence. Anything else — a server not
  listening yet, starting up (`57P03`) or full (`53300`), **a host whose name
  does not resolve yet** — is retried within
  `<PREFIX>_SEAORM__CONNECT_TIMEOUT_SECS`, each attempt said at `warn` on
  `nest_rs::orm` by host and port ("database unreachable — retrying within the
  connect budget"), then refused as unreachable, naming what the last attempt
  met. Each attempt dials one connection before the pool opens, so no attempt
  waits out the budget inside the pool; the pool keeps the budget as its
  acquire timeout.
- **What is exempt says why**: the JWK Set (fetched on first use, so an issuer
  briefly down never stops a resource server), the OTLP collector (telemetry
  never stops the app it observes), the OAuth client's authorization server and
  every social provider (third parties, reached once per login).

### A queue driver reaches the kit and the test authority through the umbrella

The umbrella's `queue` feature now forwards `nest-rs-testing?/queue`, weakly,
and `testing` forwards `nest-rs-testing/tls`: a queue driver's suite reaches
`nest_rs::testing::queue_kit!`, `nest_rs::testing::queue::KitBackend` and
`nest_rs::testing::TestAuthority` with one dev-dependency,
`nest-rs = { …, features = ["queue", "testing"] }`, and a build without
`testing` compiles no harness. A port's conformance kit is named
`<subject>_kit!` after its port, never after a backend.

### One reconnect curve

`nest_rs::core::Backoff::RECONNECT` — a quarter of a second doubling to two,
±20 % jitter — is the wait between two attempts to open or reopen a resource:
Redis's links, the Postgres pool and the object store's proof read it, so every
resource comes back alike after an outage. `Backoff::first()` and `ceiling()`
read a curve's two ends.

### Every resource the framework opens answers the readiness probe under its own name

`/health/ready` listed only the database, while an app pushing to Redis and
storing to S3 depended on both — and the docs had you hand-write a Redis
`PING` and add `redis` to your manifest for it. A resource the framework
opens now ships its check in the crate that opens it, behind a `health`
feature the umbrella's `health` forwards, so the manifest still names the
umbrella alone — and the module that opens the resource attaches it, so an
app importing `HealthModule` checks every resource it or its features opened
and writes nothing else:

```rust
use nest_rs::core::module;
use nest_rs::health::HealthModule;
use nest_rs::redis::RedisModule;
use nest_rs::seaorm::{SeaOrmDatabaseModule, SeaOrmModule};
use nest_rs::storage::StorageModule;

#[module(imports = [
    SeaOrmModule,          // "seaorm"
    SeaOrmDatabaseModule,
    RedisModule,           // "redis"
    RedisModule::<Cache>,  // "redis:cache"
    StorageModule,         // "storage"
    HealthModule,
])]
pub struct ApiModule;
```

- **A key names the resource, never the probe**: the crate owning it, plus
  `:` and the instance for a named one — `seaorm`, `redis`, `redis:cache`,
  `storage`. Each runs on readiness and startup, never liveness. Your own
  indicator keeps its method name; one claiming a framework key fails the
  boot naming both.
- **A resource a feature opens is checked too**, and once however many
  modules open it: the demo's `api` imports no store and no `transcodes`
  Valkey of its own, and its readiness reads `storage` and
  `redis:transcodes`, which its `audio` feature opens.
- **Redis**: a `PING` on the instance's connection, within its budget — on a
  Cluster, every primary. Every binding's ACL rule already grants it.
- **Storage**: one attempt of the boot's proof per probe — a page of the
  bucket's listing under the URL's prefix, read as the proof reads it: a
  store reached over verified TLS reads up even when its policy grants no
  `s3:ListBucket`, as it boots, and the probe that first meets that refusal
  files the boot's `warn` naming the credential variables — credentials
  revoked under a running app are said once, never on every probe; a bucket
  gone, a redirect or no answer reads down. One request per probe, which a
  store billing per request bills. A test overriding `Storage` opens no store
  and probes none.
- **A package ships its own the same way**: `IndicatorEntry::new::<M>(name,
  kind, run)`, attached from the `register` of the module its `for_root`
  imports by type — so two features opening the resource attach it once —
  runs beside the `#[indicators]` methods and passes the same name check: the
  seam a resource opened once per instance needs, since a link-time registry
  holds no generic type.

**Breaking:** the pool answers `seaorm` on readiness and startup, where it
answered `db` and `db_ready` — a dashboard or an alert keyed on those
changes. `IndicatorReport::name` and the keys of `ProbeReport`'s `info`,
`error` and `details` are `Cow<'static, str>`, no longer `&'static str`.
`SeaOrmHealthModule` and `SeaOrmHealthIndicator` leave the public surface,
with no alias, and no `RedisHealthModule` or `StorageHealthModule` takes
their place: delete the `*HealthModule` imports — `HealthModule` checks every
opened resource. An import left behind fails to compile, naming the module.
An app importing `HealthModule` now leaves rotation while its database, a
Valkey instance or the store it or its features open is down, where 6.x
checked the pool only beside `SeaOrmHealthModule`, and nothing else.

### The queue port: a push names its job, a retry backs off, and a backend says what it honours

6.1's port was three methods over a string: `push_json(name, value)` for a backend
to implement, and `push_to::<Q>(job)` or `push(name, job)` to call. A job had no
identity the framework knew, a retry ran again at once, a delay or a unique job was
nowhere, and a backend could not say what it did not do. **Breaking, in every
producer and every driver** — the calls each producer changes are listed on the
upgrade page, and a driver is rewritten against
[Writing a driver](https://nestrs.dev/queue/writing-a-driver/).

```rust
// 6.1, on an injected `Arc<dyn JobProducer>`
queue.push_to::<AudioQueue>(command).await?;

// 7.0, on the injected `Arc<Jobs>`
let receipt = jobs.push(AudioQueue, command).await?;
```

- **A feature pushes through `Jobs`, the port's one handle.** The backend's binding
  registers `nest_rs::queue::Jobs` over its producer, and a feature injects
  `Arc<Jobs>`: `push(Q, job)`, `push_with(Q, job, options)`, `push_many(Q, jobs)`,
  `push_many_with(Q, jobs, options)`, `cancel(&receipt)` and `cancel_unique(Q, key)`
  are its inherent methods, so no extension trait is imported and no `None` passed.
  `JobProducer` stays the contract a backend implements, and a binding registers it
  as `Jobs::new(producer)`; no container holds it any more, so a provider still
  injecting `Arc<dyn JobProducer>` fails the boot naming `Arc<Jobs>`.
  `JobProducerExt` is gone.
- **Every push is typed.** A queue another deployment drains takes a marker of its
  own on the producing side, naming that queue and the payload both sides agree
  on, so the untyped `push_json(name, serde_json::Value)` hatch is gone.
- **A push answers with a receipt, and the job's id is the port's.** A push mints a
  `JobId` — a UUID v7 — and seals it in the envelope; every push answers with a
  `PushReceipt { queue, id }` per job. A delivery reads the id back out of the
  envelope, never from a backend's own task id, which rides beside it as
  `backend_id` — so `messaging.message.id`, the operation line's `job_id`, a cancel,
  a unique claim and a checkpoint name one value on every backend. `push_many`
  reaches the backend `ENQUEUE_BATCH` (100) jobs per call and is not atomic, which
  it says: a push that fails after the backend accepted some of its calls answers
  `QueueError::PartiallyQueued { receipts, source }`, the receipts of every job
  already queued beside the failure that stopped it, so a retry leaves them out.
  `push_to::<Q>` and the string-taking `push(name, job)` are gone. A job a 6.x producer sealed carries no id and still
  runs, under an id minted for each delivery until a retry seals one.
- **A queue's type is `Queue`, and `QueueName` is the checked name a backend
  receives.** `#[queue(name = "audio", job = TranscodeCommand)]` now implements
  `Queue` (its `NAME` and its `Job`) and `Destination`, what a push names:
  `<AudioQueue as QueueName>::NAME` becomes `<AudioQueue as Queue>::NAME`. A queue
  name is 1 to 128 of `[A-Za-z0-9_.-]` — no `:`, since a queue name is one level of
  a datastore key — refused at compile time for a `#[queue]` literal, at boot for a
  `#[process]` method's queue and at the push for a name a hand-written
  `Destination` gives. 6.1 checked none of them.
- **`PushOptions` carries what a push asks for**, given to `push_with`:
  `PushOptions::default().with_delay(..)` holds a job back and `.with_unique(key)`
  keeps at most one job per key pending or running — at most once over pushes, never
  a lock, which the method says. A second push under a held key is refused with
  `QueueError::UniqueKeyHeld`, naming the holder's `JobId`, and files nothing. A
  delay ending after 9999-12-31T23:59:59.999Z (`Delay::LATEST_DUE`) is refused at
  the push with `QueueError::InvalidOptions`, and `Delay::deadline` returns a
  `Result`.
- **A retry backs off, and the budget is the port's.** The envelope counts the
  attempt (absent reads as 1). A retryable failure with budget left waits
  `min(5 min, 1 s · 2^(attempt − 1))` before the next attempt, jittered into
  `[0.8, 1.2]` by FNV-1a over the job's id and the attempt number — derived rather
  than drawn, so the wait before any attempt is reproducible from the id, in a test
  or by an operator reading the job's lines. `#[process(retries = N)]` is the budget
  on every backend. A spent budget dead-letters the job once, and so, at once, does
  a failure no other attempt could clear — a payload that does not deserialize, a
  pipe rejection, a panic. A 6.1 retry ran again at once, in the worker.
- **Breaking: a job that takes its worker down every time is dead-lettered.**
  An attempt whose process died — an abort, an OOM kill, a pod killed mid-job —
  filed nothing, so 6.1 ran the job again as often as it crashed a replica. Each
  such delivery now costs the job a stall, counted by the backend's delivery
  count, and the third in a row dead-letters it without running (`STALL_LIMIT`);
  its retry budget stays for the attempts that answered.
- **An older worker hands a newer release's job back instead of failing it, for
  a day at most** — a newer envelope carrying keys this release never wrote
  included. During a rolling deploy, a job sealed with a wire-format version
  newer than the worker's runs nothing, spends no attempt and returns to its queue
  exactly as stored, due again after a minute (`NEWER_RELEASE_WAIT`), with
  one `warn`, `job sealed by a newer release handed back unread`, naming both
  versions, the job's id, how long it has waited unread (`waited_ms`) and for how
  long it may (`patience_ms`) — filed in the newer envelope's trace when it spells
  `traceparent` as this release does, never under its actor. 6.1 failed it as an
  error and spent its retries on it. A job still unread a day after it was first
  handed back (`NEWER_RELEASE_PATIENCE`) is a rollout that stopped — its
  producers rolled back, its consumers never coming — so it is dead-lettered once,
  at `error`, `job dead-lettered: a newer release sealed it, and none of its
  consumers ran it in time`, and its record stays in the dead set for a consumer
  of that release; a newer envelope naming no id this release reads cannot be
  followed from one delivery to the next, and is dead-lettered at once. Every line
  names the remedy that works: a consumer of that release kept running until the
  queue holds none of its jobs. A driver files the record of a
  `Disposition::Defer` back unchanged, and hands the port how long the job has
  waited unread from a record it keeps (`Delivery::with_deferred_for`); a
  backend keeping none leaves the port the push's age. The Redis driver keeps
  the first hand-back's instant on Redis's clock, so no two hosts' clocks are
  compared.
- **A retry keeps the trace it started in.** A job whose envelope carried no
  usable `traceparent` starts a trace on its first attempt; the record re-filed
  for the next one marks that trace as the consumer's (`"trace_minted": true`), so
  every attempt reports `continued_trace=false` and each later attempt is a child
  of the first. An envelope key a delivery cannot use is said once per job, not
  once per attempt.
- **`#[process(concurrency = N)]` is back**, reversing 6f787ce5 by owner decision:
  how many attempts of that method one replica runs at once, default 1 — per method
  and per replica, so another method's jobs never wait on this one's permits.
  Replicas are the horizontal bound. Not a capability: every backend owes it.
- **`#[process(throttle(limit = L, window = "1m"))]`** caps how many attempts of the
  method *start* per window across the deployment, and **a `Checkpoint<S>` parameter**
  keeps a job's progress across its retries and a replica that died holding it,
  cleared at the job's outcome; it requires `transactional = false`, so a retry never
  resumes past database work its failed attempt rolled back. A throttle window under
  a millisecond (`Throttle::MIN_WINDOW`), like a zero one, fails the boot: a
  millisecond is the finest a backend is handed.
- **`cancel(&receipt)` and `cancel_unique(Q, key)`** answer `Ok(true)` only for a
  job that had not started and now never will, and `Ok(false)` for one that started,
  finished or is unknown.
- **A backend declares what it honours.** A driver names a `QueueBackend` constant —
  its `messaging.system` and its `Capabilities`, over a non-exhaustive `Capability`:
  `DelayedPush`, `UniquePush`, `Cancellation`, `Throttle`, `Checkpoint`. The port
  refuses what a backend lacks at the earliest site that sees both facts, in one
  sentence naming the capability and the backend: the worker's boot for a
  `#[process]` key, the push for an option, the call for a cancel. The retry budget,
  a transaction per attempt and `concurrency` are owed by every backend and are not
  capabilities. The Redis backend declares all five.
- **A driver implements `enqueue` and a `JobConsumer`, and the port owns
  everything else.** `JobProducer` is `backend()`, `enqueue(queue, envelopes,
  options)` and — for a backend declaring cancellation — `remove` and
  `remove_unique`; the port checks the name and the options, mints the ids and
  seals the envelopes before any of it reaches the driver. On the consuming side
  a driver implements `JobConsumer` — `prepare`, `receive` leased `Delivery`s,
  `renew` their leases, `settle` each with a `Disposition` (`Complete`, `Retry`,
  `Defer`, `Requeue`, `DeadLetter`) fenced on its lease, and optionally
  `maintain`, `close` and `checkpoint` — and binds it as a `BoundConsumer`,
  declared like the producer, so two backends imported fail the boot naming
  both. The port's `QueueWorker` transport, attached by
  `QueueModule::for_root(QueueConfig)` (`<PREFIX>_QUEUE__SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_SECS`,
  20 s by default), runs the rest on every backend: discovery, which refuses a
  method whose keys the backend cannot honour and two methods draining one
  queue; each method's permits and one task per delivery; the attempt, its
  budget and its backoff; the lease's renewal, an attempt cut when its lease is
  lost; and the drain. The renewal goes out a third of the way into a lease, and
  `nest_rs_queue::lease_fits_renewal` tells a binding whether its lease leaves
  that renewal room to answer, so it refuses at boot one that does not, naming
  its own settings. `nest-rs-testing`'s new `queue` feature ships
  `queue_kit!`, the cases every backend owes the worker, which the in-memory
  test backend and the Redis backend both run — on paused time for a backend
  declaring `KitBackend::IN_PROCESS`, on the real clock a live service shares
  otherwise; the worker reads tokio's clock, so a paused one moves its leases
  too. What 6.1 exported for a driver to
  do those steps itself is gone: `envelope::seal` and `envelope::open` (the
  `envelope` module is private, its type is `nest_rs_queue::Envelope`),
  `check_duplicate_queue_claims`, and the `Processor` trait, which the framework
  never called — a consumer is a `#[processor]` host and its `#[process]`
  methods. `CheckpointStore::clear` is gone: the backend lets a job's checkpoint
  go in the settle that ends the job. The port's own attempt (`consume`) is no
  driver's seam and is hidden. The `tracing` re-export, which only the 6.1
  macro's expansion used, is gone too; a crate logging through it depends on
  `tracing` itself.
- **Two budgets, never one.** `retries` counts the attempts that answered; a
  delivery that ended without an answer — its worker killed, frozen, or cut off
  past its lease — is counted by the backend's delivery count, which every
  filing of the job's record starts over, and the third in a row
  (`STALL_LIMIT`) dead-letters the job without running it, as `job
  dead-lettered: its deliveries ended without an answer past the stall limit`.
  A crash no longer spends an attempt of the job.
- **`ProcessMethod` is read through accessors.** Its fields are private;
  `name()`, `queue()` and `options()` (a `ProcessOptions` holding `retries`,
  `concurrency`, `throttle` and whether the method checkpoints) replace them.
- **A `#[process]` method returns any `Result<(), E>`** whose error converts into
  `Box<dyn Error + Send + Sync>` — `anyhow::Result<()>` remains the usual spelling —
  and may be a plain `fn`.
- **Delivery is at least once, on every backend, and a handler is idempotent.** A
  storage that loses no job delivers some twice — a lease lapsed after a crash,
  an acknowledgement lost — and none promises more. The port's documentation,
  the rules and every page say so.

### The Redis queue runs on Redis Streams, every transition one fenced script

apalis is gone: apalis-redis 0.7.4 stops compiling on Rust 1.100, and its 1.0
line is still a release candidate. The Redis backend is written on the `redis`
client, as a `JobConsumer` under the port's worker.

- **Breaking: `RedisWorkerModule`, `RedisWorker`, `RedisWorkerConfig` and
  `RedisWorkerSetup` are gone.** `RedisQueueModule` binds the producer and the
  consumer, a bare import or `RedisQueueModule::for_root(RedisQueueConfig {
  lease })` (`<PREFIX>_REDIS__QUEUE__LEASE_SECS`, 30 s by default, 1 s to an
  hour); a worker app imports `QueueModule` beside it, which
  attaches the port's `QueueWorker`. The drain window moves to
  `<PREFIX>_QUEUE__SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_SECS`: 6.1's
  `<PREFIX>_REDIS__WORKER__SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_SECS` is read by nothing, and the
  boot does not say so, since a namespace no config claims may be another
  binary's.
- **Breaking: every queue lives under `nestrs:queue:{<queue>}:`.** One stream
  per queue, `…:jobs`, read through the consumer group `workers`; delayed jobs in
  `…:due` and `…:delayed`; dead letters in `…:dead`; a fact about one job a field
  of a per-queue hash. The braces are a hash tag, so a queue's keys share one
  slot. A KEDA trigger is `redis-streams` with `streamLength` on `…:jobs`, which
  holds a job until it ends, so it counts waiting and running jobs. 6.x kept jobs
  at the root of the keyspace under the queue's bare name, which 7.0 never
  reads: drain each queue with its 6.x workers before 7.0 workers take over, as
  the queue documentation's *Upgrading queues from 6.x* page lays out.
- **Every transition is one script, fenced on the pending entry.** An entry of
  `…:jobs` deleted by hand while delivered takes its job
  with it, and is said at `warn` naming it rather than lost in silence. A read hands
  a job over leased in one command (`XREADGROUP`); the worker renews the lease
  every third of it without counting a delivery, and a lease no renewal reached
  for a whole lease is taken over by the next worker asking for jobs with a
  permit free. An outcome, a renewal and a checkpoint write nothing unless the
  entry's owner and delivery count are still the worker's, so a worker whose
  lease lapsed under it writes nowhere, and an outcome acknowledges and deletes
  the entry in the script that files what comes next. The delivery guard, the
  settled mark, the startup sweep, the heartbeat and the lifted attempt cap are
  gone with the duplicates they filtered.
- **No record outlives its job.** The script that ends a job removes everything
  it held but its dead letter, so nothing needs an expiry. A dead letter keeps
  the job's id, its record as stored — its `actor_id` included, which the
  delivery page says a retention policy covers — the reason its line rendered
  and the unique key it held, on a stream kept a week — trimmed at every upkeep —
  and about 10,000 entries; the delivery page prints the one command that files
  it back, taking the key again or refusing while another job holds it, and the
  ACL rule of the operator who runs it, and a test runs both as printed. A
  held-back job whose record was deleted by hand is let go with what it held,
  and said, at the upkeep that finds it due.
- **Redis's clock decides when a job is due**, in milliseconds: a delay is
  measured by the push and anchored on `TIME` in the script, and every worker
  draining the queue files the jobs that fell due within a second.
- **A method asks for as many jobs as it has free permits, and waits for
  them.** A blocking read on a connection of its own replaces the poll, so a
  pushed job starts at once and an idle method costs one read a second, one
  upkeep script a second and one sweep of the group a minute. The scripts are
  loaded once, at the worker's boot, and sent as `EVALSHA`.
- **`Capability::UniquePush`.** A push under a held key is refused with
  `QueueError::UniqueKeyHeld`, naming the job that holds it, and files nothing;
  the key is claimed in the script that files the job, so racing pushes queue
  one job and no claim outlives a push that failed, and it is let go when the
  job completes, dead-letters or is cancelled.
- **`Capability::Cancellation`.** `cancel(&receipt)` and `cancel_unique(queue,
  key)` remove a job still waiting — on the stream, undelivered, or held back —
  and answer `true` only then. A job a delivery runs, one never known, or one
  long finished answers `false`.
- **`Capability::Throttle`.** `#[process(throttle(limit, window))]` is counted
  across every replica in one fixed window per queue, opened by its first start,
  before a read asks for jobs; a full window holds that replica's reads for the
  method until it ends, so the backlog waits on the stream.
- **`Capability::Checkpoint`.** A job's `Checkpoint<S>` outlives its retries and
  a replica that died holding it, is written only by the delivery holding the
  job, and goes with the job's outcome.
- **Each role's ACL rule is exact, and replaces what the user held.** Every
  rule starts with `reset`, so run on a user granted more by hand it leaves the
  rule alone — and Redis no longer refuses it on a user holding `~*`. The
  delivery page gives a producer's, a worker's and a KEDA scaler's rule — the
  last what KEDA's client, go-redis, sends: `PING`, `SELECT`, `CLIENT SETINFO`
  beside `XLEN`, replayed by the suite on every node; the suite runs a producer and a worker as
  users created from the page's lines through every path the binding has, and
  fails on a denial in `ACL LOG` or on a command the rule allows that `MONITOR`
  never saw the role send.
- **Breaking: the server is Valkey, in its latest release — 9.1.** Redis is no
  longer a supported server, nor is an older Valkey: the framework runs on open
  source end to end, and supports the newest release of the server it runs on.
  `nest-rs-redis` keeps its name, as it speaks the Redis protocol (`redis://`,
  `rediss://`), and its whole suite — the behaviour kit, the exact ACL rules,
  the fence, TLS — runs on Valkey 9.1.2, in CI and in the dev container.
  Standalone only for now: neither Valkey Cluster nor Sentinel yet — the keys
  are a cluster's already, the connection is a client of one server.

### The Redis connection is the connection, and a command Redis never answers fails within the budget

A Redis outage used to hold every caller — a rate-limited request, a push, the
queue's worker — for as long as the client kept reopening, and the client's own
reconnect backoff, a hundredfold from one second, put a minute between attempts
after two failures. **Breaking for code that reached the manager.**

- **`RedisConnection` implements `redis::aio::ConnectionLike` and `Clone`** over
  the one `ConnectionManager` every binding shares: the queue, the rate limiter
  and the occurrence lock send their commands on a clone, and a command of your
  own runs on one too. A worker's blocking read gets a connection of its own,
  opened from the same client.
  `manager()` is no longer public — there is nothing left to hand out.
- **Every command is bounded end to end by `<PREFIX>_REDIS__CONNECT_TIMEOUT_SECS`**,
  the wait for a reopened connection included, and fails as a timeout the caller
  reads with `is_timeout()`. A timeout says the answer did not come, never that the
  command did not run. The reconnect backoff doubles to a two-second ceiling.
- **The boot proves Redis and says what went wrong.**
  `RedisConnection::connect(&RedisConfig)` replaces `connect(url)` and
  `connect_within(url, budget)`: each attempt proves Redis with a `PING` on a
  connection opened once, closed before it opens the one the app keeps, so a Redis
  with one client slot left still boots. What every attempt would repeat fails at
  once — `RedisError::InvalidUrl` for a URL the client cannot parse,
  `RedisError::Refused` for an answer naming the deployment's own settings: refused
  credentials, an ACL denying the proof, a protocol it does not speak. Every other
  answer may clear — `LOADING`, `BUSY`, `MASTERDOWN`, `TRYAGAIN`, a code the client
  does not know — and is retried within the budget, which ends as
  `RedisError::Unready`, carrying Redis's last answer, or `RedisError::Unreachable`
  when Redis never answered. A budget built in code outside its range — zero, or
  past the hour the variable allows — is refused before anything is dialled
  (`RedisError::Budget`). `RedisError::Connect` is gone. The endpoint every error
  and line names is the address dialled, never the URL. A URL asking for RESP3
  (`protocol=resp3`) is refused at once too (`RedisError::UnsupportedProtocol`):
  every binding reads RESP2 replies, and redis-rs 1.x honours the flag, under
  which the worker's read failed on every receive.
- **A connection a failover left on the demoted primary is opened again.** A
  demoted primary keeps its clients' sockets open and answers every write
  `READONLY`, and the client reopens a connection only when its socket fails,
  so every write failed until the process restarted. The first `READONLY` a
  connection meets opens it again through the URL's name — once, for every
  clone — and says so at `warn`.
- **A database the server will not select fails the boot at once**, as
  `RedisError::DatabaseRefused`, naming the index the URL ends in: an index past
  the server's `databases` — a Cluster node's `cluster-databases` — or an ACL user
  without `+select`. The client reports every refused `SELECT` in one sentence that
  drops the server's code, so these were retried for the whole budget and reported as
  a Redis "not ready … if it clears on its own". The one refusal of `SELECT` that
  clears, a server busy running a script, is still retried.
- **Each Redis binding's page gives its ACL rule whole** — the queue under
  `~nestrs:queue:*`, the rate limiter under `~nestrs:throttler:*`, a schedule's
  claims under `~nestrs:schedule:*` — as one `ACL SETUSER` line listing the
  connection's `PING` and `SELECT`, `EVALSHA` and `SCRIPT LOAD` where the binding
  sends scripts, and every command it sends or its scripts call, and nothing
  more: Redis checks the commands inside a script against the caller's ACL too.
  An app running several bindings gives one user each rule. An e2e test creates
  a user from each page's line verbatim and runs the binding through it, reading
  Redis's `ACL LOG` for any denial; for the queue, `MONITOR` also proves the
  rule grants nothing its role leaves unused. No connection sends
  `CLIENT SETINFO`, so no rule needs it.
