### Valkey on Sentinel and Cluster, every binding on each, every connection on TLS

The connection reached one server — a primary, or a name the DNS moved at a
failover — so a Sentinel deployment was followed only as fast as its DNS, and a
Cluster not at all. Valkey runs in production three ways, and the queue, the rate
limiter and the schedule's lock now run on each, every connection verified TLS.

- **The scheme of `<PREFIX>_REDIS__URL` declares the topology**, in the grammar
  fred's `Config::from_url` documents: `redis://` / `rediss://` one server;
  `redis-sentinel://` / `rediss-sentinel://` the primary the sentinels name,
  each further sentinel a `node=` parameter and the service
  `sentinelServiceName=`, with `sentinelUsername=` / `sentinelPassword=` for
  sentinels holding users; `redis-cluster://` / `rediss-cluster://` a Cluster
  read from the seeds it lists. `RedisConfig` and the variables are unchanged.
  The database rides in the path on every topology — on a Cluster, Valkey 9's
  `cluster-databases`. A parameter the topology does not take fails the boot
  naming it, `node=` on a URL of one server included.
- **Under Sentinel, every reconnection asks the sentinels again**, as Valkey's
  Sentinel client spec requires: the primary is the address the first sentinel to
  answer names, kept once its `HELLO` says it is one, and a connection that drops,
  times out or answers `READONLY` is replaced by one to the primary named then —
  never reopened to the demoted one — commands meanwhile waiting within their
  budget, and the move said at `warn`.
- **On a Cluster**, each command goes to the node serving its keys' slot, a
  `MOVED` or `ASK` is followed and a failover read again within the budget. A
  script whose keys sit in two hash slots is refused before it is sent, on every
  topology, and a script a node has forgotten is loaded again on that node alone.
  No node is sent `CLIENT SETINFO`. A worker's blocked read holds a connection to
  its queue's primary alone, follows an `ASK` there while the slot moves key by
  key, and is opened afresh after a `MOVED`, a drop or a timeout. A slot moved
  whole by Valkey 9's `CLUSTER MIGRATESLOTS` keeps every push answering.
- **New boot refusals**: `RedisError::SentinelUnreachable` naming the sentinels,
  `RedisError::PrimaryUnknown` naming the service they do not know, and, at once,
  `RedisError::TopologyMismatch` naming the topology a URL declares and the one
  its server serves, as the server's `HELLO` says — a URL of one server at a
  Cluster node or a sentinel included. `RedisTopology` names the three.
- **The ACL each page prescribes holds on every node**, plus what the topology's
  connection sends: under Sentinel nothing more on the nodes, and a sentinels'
  user allowed `SENTINEL GET-MASTER-ADDR-BY-NAME`; `+cluster|slots +asking` on a
  Cluster. `HELLO`, which checks what a connection reached, needs no permission.
- **KEDA scales a worker on each topology**: the queue's scaling page gives the
  `redis-sentinel-streams` and `redis-cluster-streams` triggers, and the
  topologies page what KEDA's user adds — `+cluster|slots +command` on a
  Cluster's nodes, a rule of its own on the sentinels. KEDA's cluster scaler
  reads database 0 alone. The demo chart's triggers follow
  `keda.redis.topology`.
- **`redis` is pinned to 1.8** (`=1.8`): a Cluster's nodes are opened through a
  `#[doc(hidden)]` seam of its cluster client, which a minor may change.
- **nest-rs-redis's e2e runs on each topology** — Sentinel and a six-node
  Cluster, on TLS — a real failover under a running queue on each, and a slot
  moved under one on the Cluster. CI runs the one server alone; Sentinel and
  Cluster are dev container environments of their own, `nestrs — Valkey
  Sentinel` and `nestrs — Valkey Cluster`, opened with *Reopen in Container*.
  Their URLs sit commented in `test.just` and `demo/.env`: uncommenting one
  points the framework's `just test`, its failovers last, or the demo's apps at
  that topology. Every node is reached by its name, as the one server is —
  `valkey-sentinel-1`, `valkey-cluster-1` and their siblings, which the nodes
  announce and the certificate names. The dev container's server is
  `valkey-standalone`, where it was `redis`: a URL written for it reads
  `rediss://valkey-standalone:6379`. Its certificate now serves client
  authentication too, which a
  Cluster's nodes present to each other: a dev container's `certs` volume issued
  earlier is issued again at its next start.

### The rate limiter's keys carry their structure level, and a store that does not answer denies

- **Breaking for dashboards and ACLs:** the Redis store counts under
  `nestrs:throttler:buckets:<subject>`, not `nestrs:throttle:<subject>` — the concern
  read off `nest_rs_throttler::TARGET`, `buckets` off the port, so an operator
  scopes a `SCAN` to the structure. A rolling deploy starts every window over once,
  and while old and new replicas both serve, a subject can be let through up to
  twice its limit in that window.
- **`ThrottlerGuard` waits `nest_rs::throttler::HIT_TIMEOUT` — 18 seconds — for
  `ThrottlerStore::hit`**, on HTTP, GraphQL, MCP and WebSockets alike. A store still
  silent then is a store that cannot answer: the caller is denied for the window,
  fail closed — `429` with `Retry-After`, or the edge's own error frame — and the
  guard logs `throttler store did not answer within the guard's timeout; denying
  (fail-closed)` at `warn`, with `transport`, `store` and `waited_ms`. A custom
  store holding its call used to hold the request with it, and nothing timed out a
  socket's messages then. The bound is a net, never a store's budget: it sits above the
  Redis store's own (`<PREFIX>_REDIS__CONNECT_TIMEOUT_SECS`, 10 s by default, pinned
  below it by a test) and below the HTTP edge's 20-second request timeout, so a hung
  store reads as the same `429` on every edge.
- **`ThrottlerStore` gains a provided `name()`**, the implementor's type name by
  default, which the guard's line uses to say which store went quiet. No
  implementor has to change; a call written `store.name()` is ambiguous only where
  another trait in scope has a `name` method too.
- **Breaking: a throttle's window is never under a millisecond.** `Throttle`'s
  `limit` and `window` are private, read with `limit()` and `window()` as on
  `nest_rs_queue::Throttle`, and `Throttle::new` refuses a window under
  `Throttle::MIN_WINDOW` (1 ms): a compile error in a `const`, a failed boot at the
  route in `#[meta]`. `<PREFIX>_THROTTLER__WINDOW_SECS=0`, and a
  `ThrottlerConfig::window` of zero pinned in code, fail the boot naming
  the variable. A zero window reset every bucket on every hit, so every request
  was let through at any limit; and a window too long for Redis, up to
  `u64::MAX` milliseconds, is now kept for the longest Redis accepts, where it
  failed every hit, closed.
- **An IPv6 client is counted under its `/64`, an IPv4 client under its address.**
  A host picks the low 64 bits of its IPv6 address and rotates them (RFC 8981), so
  keyed on the whole address one client opened a fresh HTTP bucket per address and
  was never limited. An IPv4 client a dual-stack listener reports as
  `::ffff:a.b.c.d` is counted as IPv4. Every host on one IPv6 link now shares a
  bucket, as every host behind one IPv4 NAT already did; the rate-limiting page says
  so.
- **A hit in a window's last millisecond opens the next window on Redis**, as it
  does in process. Redis keeps a key through the millisecond it expires at and
  answers its TTL `0` there; the store counted that hit into the ending window and,
  over the limit, answered `Retry-After` for a whole window — an hour on an hourly
  limit. The script now deletes such a window (`PEXPIRE … 0`, already in the ACL
  line) before it counts.
- `BACKEND_REMEDY` moves beside the `ThrottlerStore` contract it belongs to; its
  path from the crate root is unchanged.

### A scheduled job can fire once across replicas

`#[every]` and `#[cron]` fire on every replica of an app. That is right for a
heartbeat and wrong for a tick that enqueues work: three replicas enqueue three
jobs per occurrence, and nothing said so.

- **`replicas = "one"`** on `#[every]` or `#[cron]` fires each occurrence on the
  one replica whose claim on it succeeds — at most once per occurrence. `"each"`,
  the default, keeps the previous behaviour. `#[after]` refuses the key at compile
  time, because each replica's boot is its own event. A claim holds the occurrence
  and nothing else: a run that outlasts its period leaves the next occurrence to
  another replica, and the two runs overlap. One replica never overlaps its own
  runs.
- **The claim goes through a port, `OccurrenceLock`**: `claim` takes an
  `Occurrence` — its token, the claim's hold and the trace id of the run it fires
  — and answers `Claimed` or `ClaimedElsewhere`; `claimed(token)` asks about an
  overrun occurrence. A backend binds it as one declared factory for
  `Arc<dyn OccurrenceLock>` carrying `nest_rs::schedule::BACKEND_REMEDY`. The boot
  fails when a reachable job declares `replicas = "one"` and no lock is bound,
  naming the job and that remedy; two lock bindings fail it too.
- **Redis binds it: `nest_rs::redis::RedisScheduleModule`**, behind the new
  umbrella feature `redis-schedule` (`cargo add nest-rs --features
  redis-schedule`). A bare import beside `ScheduleModule` and
  `RedisModule::for_root`, it claims each occurrence with one `SET … NX PX` on the
  shared connection, under
  `nestrs:schedule:claims:<crate>:<provider>:<method>:<instant_ms>`, whose value
  names the replica and the trace of the run it fired, and asks about an overrun
  occurrence with `EXISTS`. A claim whose answer is lost costs that occurrence and
  nothing more. Its ACL rule is `+set +exists` beside the connection's own, as the
  schedule page gives it whole.
- **A job is its crate, its type and its method.** An API and a worker that each
  declare their own `MaintenanceTasks::sweep` in their own crates never claim each
  other's occurrences through the Redis they share, a job declared in a crate both
  apps link is one job, and moving a job's module inside its crate keeps it.
  Renaming the type, the method or the crate starts a new job. **`key = "…"`**
  beside `replicas = "one"` pins the identity it had — the path the job's boot
  line names as `key`, e.g. `key = "features::NotificationsTasks::purge_expired"`.
  A key is one or more levels joined by `::`, each non-empty and free of `:`,
  whitespace and control characters, checked at compile time. It is refused at
  `#[after]`, at `#[process]` and beside a job firing on every replica, and two
  jobs firing once under one identity fail the boot, naming both.
- **The scheduler bounds every lock call itself.** A claim, or a question about
  an overrun occurrence, not answered by the time the occurrence goes stale — its
  hold less the ten seconds of clock skew — is abandoned: the occurrence is
  skipped with a `warn`, `occurrence skipped: its lock did not answer the claim
  before the occurrence went stale`, carrying `provider`, `method`, `occurrence`
  and `waited_ms`, and an abandoned question counts as `unanswered`. A lock that
  never answered used to hold its job's loop for good, with nothing said.
- **Shutdown never waits on a lock.** A claim still in flight when shutdown is asked
  for is abandoned at once and its occurrence skipped — `occurrence skipped:
  shutdown was asked for before its lock answered the claim`, at `warn`, with
  `provider`, `method`, `occurrence` and `waited_ms` — and questions about overrun
  occurrences still out are counted `unanswered` under their own `warn`, `occurrence
  lock had not answered whether an overrun occurrence was claimed when shutdown was
  asked for`, with `abandoned` and `waited_ms`. Without it a lock that stopped
  answering held the scheduler's stop until the claim went stale: about 50 seconds
  for an interval job, most of a day for a daily cron. Every job loop checks for
  shutdown before its timer, so a tick falling due as shutdown is asked for no
  longer fires; a claim answered before it still fires, and a run already started
  gets five seconds before it is stopped, as *The way down is bounded to the exit*
  below says.
- **An `#[every]` declared `replicas = "one"` ticks on multiples of its period
  since the Unix epoch**, so replicas booted at different moments reach the same
  instants. `replicas = "each"` still first fires one period after boot.
- **At most once per occurrence, never at least once.** A claim the lock cannot
  answer skips the occurrence with a `warn` on `nest_rs::schedule` carrying
  `provider`, `method`, `occurrence` and `error`, and so does a replica reaching
  an occurrence within ten seconds of its claim's hold ending, or whose claim is
  answered that late: its peer's claim may already be gone. A replica that
  crashes after claiming loses that occurrence and only that one; work that must
  not be lost belongs in a queue job the tick enqueues. Replica clocks must agree
  within ten seconds. A claim is made under `<crate>:<provider>:<method>:<instant>`,
  or a pinned key's levels, and lasts twice the gap to the following occurrence,
  at least a minute, so a replica that overran one occurrence can still ask who
  fired it.
- **Occurrences that fall due while the previous one is claimed or run are
  counted aloud**, whichever `replicas` a job declares: one `warn`,
  `occurrences skipped: they fell due while the previous one was claimed or run`,
  with `skipped`, the `occurrence` it started from and `overrun_ms`. A cron job,
  and an `#[every]` firing once, reach the latest occurrence due, late, rather
  than firing the stale one they slept for and then the latest; an `#[every]`
  firing on every replica fires the first tick it overran, late, and skips the
  rest. A job firing once asks the lock about the first hundred it overran, so the
  ones a peer fired are told apart from the ones nobody did (`claimed_elsewhere`,
  `unanswered`, `unchecked`).
- **Breaking:** `ScheduledMethod` and `CronJobMeta` gain `replicas` and `key`,
  and `CronJobMeta` gains `origin`, the `module_path!()` of the code declaring the
  job. The `scheduled job (…)` boot lines carry `replicas`, plus `key` on a job
  firing once; the `schedule.tick` line carries `replicas`, and a job firing once
  carries the `occurrence` it claimed.
- **Breaking:** two jobs sharing one `Provider::method` in one app fail the boot,
  naming both and where each was declared: their lines carry provider and method
  and could not be told apart. A job attached by hand whose origin, provider,
  method or key has an empty level, or a level carrying a `:`, whitespace or a
  control character, fails the boot too, since each is a level of its key.
- `#[every]` and `#[cron]` refuse `concurrency` with the sentence `a replica runs
  one occurrence of a scheduled job at a time — one falling due inside a run there
  is skipped and counted`.
- A job registered by hand with a zero interval fails the boot instead of
  panicking the scheduler, and so does one under a millisecond — finer than the
  duration grammar writes or the timer resolves — and a one-shot declaring one
  replica.
- A cron occurrence reached a moment early no longer fires twice: the next
  occurrence is computed from the one just fired.
- A panic outside a scheduled method — in the scheduler's own loop, or in a run
  function before it hands back its future — ended its job in silence while the
  process reported healthy. The first is named at `error` with the job it
  stopped; the second is caught like a panicking tick, and the job fires again.
- A failed tick's `error` names every cause beneath its error, not the wrapper
  alone.

### Every Redis binding is a feature, and `redis` is the connection alone

The umbrella's `redis` feature compiled the queue whatever the app used Redis for:
`nest-rs-redis` linked `nest-rs-queue` unconditionally, so an app wanting Redis for a
rate limit, a scheduled job's lock or a command of its own compiled the job queue, its
port and its worker. The rate limiter and the schedule were already features of their
own; the queue was the one binding that was not.

- **Breaking: the queue's Redis binding is the umbrella feature `redis-queue`.** It
  closes over `redis` and `queue`, as `redis-throttler` and `redis-schedule` close over
  `redis` and their port. An app importing `RedisQueueModule` replaces `redis` with
  `redis-queue` in its `nest-rs` line; one that never named the queue keeps `redis`,
  which is now `RedisModule` and `RedisConnection` alone and compiles no port.
- **`nest-rs-redis` has one feature per binding — `queue`, `throttler`, `schedule` —
  and none by default.** Each is read off the folder holding its binding, and the crate
  root holds the connection alone: the queue's backend declaration and key layout now
  sit under `queue/` with the rest of the binding. `RedisError::BudgetPastLease`, the
  queue's lease refusal, exists with the `queue` feature (the enum is
  `#[non_exhaustive]`, so no `match` changes).
- The set: `redis-<port>`, one umbrella feature per binding folder of `nest-rs-redis`,
  read off the folder; a feature named for a use (`redis-cache`) is refused. `cargo
  hack` builds each alone, and `nest-rs-macro-hygiene` compiles, under each
  `redis-<port>` alone, an app module importing that binding through `nest_rs::redis`.
- `nestrs g queue` writes `redis-queue` into the manifests it edits, where it wrote
  `redis` (*The install surface: 30 capabilities* counts them all).

### Each Redis binding's page states the eviction policy it survives

The only eviction guidance called a `volatile-*` policy safe because, of the queue's
keys, only the throttle window carries an expiry — true of the queue, and wrong for a
server the queue shares. The rate limiter's windows and the schedule's claims each carry
one, so under `volatile-*` at `maxmemory` an evicted denying window let its client
through, and an evicted claim let a second replica fire the same occurrence.

- **A server holding the rate limiter's windows or the schedule's claims runs
  `noeviction`**; the queue alone tolerates `volatile-*`; a cache belongs on another
  server. The topologies page states it once, in a table of what each binding's
  eviction costs, and the queue's, the rate limiter's and the schedule's pages each
  repeat their own line. The boot cannot check it — reading the policy takes
  `CONFIG GET`, which no prescribed ACL rule grants — so it is the deployment's.
- The delivery page no longer says the queue runs on a standalone Valkey only: every
  binding has run on Sentinel and Cluster since the topologies section.

### A rate-limit store says it could not count, and the guard alone decides

**Breaking for a store author:** `ThrottlerStore::hit` answers
`Result<Decision, ThrottlerStoreError>`. A store had no way to say it could not
count, so each picked its own answer — and one answering `allowed` on its
database's error failed **open**, an auth bypass the Redis store's own doc
warned of.

- **`nest_rs::throttler::ThrottlerStoreError`** wraps the store's own failure
  (`ThrottlerStoreError::new(error)`, an `anyhow::Error` keeping every link),
  the shape of `OccurrenceLockError`.
- **The guard is the one place a failure is decided**, on all four edges: an
  `Err`, a store silent past `HIT_TIMEOUT` and a panic each refuse the caller for
  the whole window — `429` with `Retry-After`, or the edge's error frame — with
  one line on `nest_rs::throttler` naming the `transport` and the `store`, never
  the key: `throttler store failed; denying (fail-closed)` at `warn` with the
  `error` chain for the first. A refusal for a store that could not count no
  longer also files `rate limit exceeded`: no limit was.
- **The Redis store answers Redis's error and logs nothing**; its `redis
  throttler unavailable; denying (fail-closed)` line, which carried the bucket's
  pseudonym as `key`, is gone — the guard's line says it once.
- `Decision` derives `Clone`, `Copy`, `Debug`, `PartialEq` and `Eq`.

### A job is filed once while its queue holds it

`JobProducer::enqueue` said nothing about an id it already held, and the Redis
push script filed whatever it was handed: one envelope sent twice became two
stream entries, and its job ran twice. Redis Cluster's client re-sends a script
after a connection error whose answer was lost, which is how a push could meet
it.

- **An envelope whose id the backend still holds is not filed again.** While its
  job waits, is held back by a delay or a retry, or runs, `enqueue` skips it and
  answers `Ok` for it, its options unread; once the job ended, the same envelope
  is filed as a new job. The contract is written on `JobProducer::enqueue`, and
  every queue driver owes it.
- **The Redis push script skips a job its queue still holds**, before its
  unique-key check and its filing, with the commands its documented ACL rules
  already allow.
- **`queue_kit!` holds every driver to it**: its new case enqueues one sealed
  envelope again while the job waits, while a delay holds it back (on a backend
  declaring delayed push), while a retry waits its backoff, while it runs, and
  once it ended; on a backend declaring unique push, a keyed envelope sent again
  is answered `Ok`, runs once, and its job keeps the key.
- **A job whose stream entry was deleted by hand stays held**, so its envelope
  sent again files nothing until `cancel` or `cancel_unique` lets it go; the
  worker's `warn` line now says so: `queue entries deleted while delivered;
  their jobs are gone but stay held, their ids and unique keys taken, until
  cancel or cancel_unique lets them go`.
- A caller retrying a `push` whose answer never came still enqueues twice: each
  push mints a new id. A unique key is still the way to keep one piece of work
  queued once.

`JobProducer` is new in 7.0 — a 6.x driver is rewritten against
[Writing a driver](https://nestrs.dev/queue/writing-a-driver/), which states the
contract — so no 6.x code meets this as a break.

### `nest-rs-worker` is gone: the job bound lives in the kernel

After the unit-of-work port took its seam, `nest-rs-worker` held one constant
and one error, which is no crate's subject. Both move to `nest-rs-core`, which
already owns every bound the framework waits under:
`nest_rs::core::JOB_TIMEOUT` and `nest_rs::core::JobTimedOut`. The umbrella's
`worker` feature goes with the crate (*The install surface: 30 capabilities*), `queue` no longer
forwards it, and `nest_rs::schedule` no longer re-exports the crate. A
`#[process]` or `#[scheduled]` method without a `timeout` key expands to the
kernel's `JOB_TIMEOUT`, so nothing changes for a decorated job.

### A job handler sees its id and attempt

A `#[process]` method may take an `Attempt` beside its job: the job's id — the
`JobId` its push's receipt returned, the same on every attempt and every
redelivery — its attempt number from 1, and its queue. Delivery is at least
once, so a handler whose effects leave the process keys them on
`attempt.job_id()`, the idempotency key the port already minted:

```rust
#[process(queue = NotifyQueue)]
async fn notify(&self, job: NotifyCommand, attempt: Attempt) -> anyhow::Result<()> {
    self.mailer.send(&job, attempt.job_id().to_string()).await
}
```

It is recognised by its type, like `Checkpoint<S>`, once each and in either
order; a second `Attempt` is a compile error naming the rule. The unit of work
an attempt opens names the same job and number (`UnitSpec::job`), so a driver
and the method agree on one identity. The parameter is optional: a method
without it is unchanged.

### Storage: one URL names the store, its scheme picks the backend, and the boot proves it

Storage said it drove S3, GCS, Azure, a filesystem and memory behind one client
while it opened AWS S3 alone; it named its target with a `BUCKET` beside an
`ENDPOINT`, built its client on the first call — so a wrong bucket shipped green
and failed in production — and no app could test it without a server.

- **`<PREFIX>_STORAGE__URL` names the store**, and its scheme picks
  object_store's backend: `s3://<bucket>[/<prefix>]` for AWS S3 and the servers
  speaking its API (`ENDPOINT` names one; unset is AWS), `memory:///` for a
  store in the process — development and tests, refused in a staging or
  production profile since a restart loses it. A path after the bucket confines
  every key under it; a prefix a URL would have to encode (a space, `%`, a
  letter beyond ASCII) fails the boot. `file`, `gs` and `az` fail the boot naming the scheme and
  what this release opens; the URL is never quoted. Every S3 variable set
  beside `memory:///` fails the boot naming it.
- **The boot proves the store** before any provider is built: an `s3://` bucket
  answers one page of its listing under the prefix within
  `<PREFIX>_STORAGE__OPERATION_TIMEOUT_SECS`. A bucket that does not exist fails
  the boot at once naming `<PREFIX>_STORAGE__URL`; a certificate the client does
  not trust fails it at once with the authority to install; a status every
  attempt would meet — a redirect, the sign of a wrong region, or a `400` —
  fails it at once naming the status, `REGION`, `ENDPOINT` and
  `FORCE_PATH_STYLE`; a store that refuses to list — its credentials, or a policy without `s3:ListBucket` —
  boots on with one `warn` on `nest_rs::storage` naming the credential
  variables; anything else — no answer, a `5xx`, a `408` or a `429` — is
  retried, each attempt a `warn`, then fails the boot as unreachable with its
  last cause. `memory:///` proves at once.
- **Test storage in process**: a `TestApp` over `memory:///` needs no server;
  presigning there answers `StorageError::Unsupported`. Beside a `.env` naming
  an S3 store, `.env.test` blanks each S3 key it sets, or the test pins
  `memory:///` through `StorageModule::for_root`. A test overriding `Storage`
  with a double opens no store and proves none.
- **Errors name the store, never a key**: each sentence names the operation
  and the store (its URL, and an S3 endpoint without credentials), and the
  chain says object_store's own links by their kind, without the key or the
  URL their sentences quote. object_store's error stays the source, boxed, so
  its releases are no longer nestrs's API.
- The dev container creates the `nestrs` bucket at each start, and CI before
  its tests, as each creates its database.

### A presigned upload binds its content type, and `head` reads it back

A presigned `PUT` signed nothing but the key, so the uploading client chose the
stored `Content-Type`: it could store `text/html` or `image/svg+xml` under a key
the app later served, a stored cross-site script one link away. And `head`
answered the size alone, so an app could not check what had landed.

- **`presign_put(key, &SignedPut::new(content_type), expires)`** folds the type
  into the SigV4 signature (`X-Amz-SignedHeaders=content-type;host`): the store
  refuses, with a `403`, an upload sending another type or none. The type is
  required — accepting any is `SignedPut::new("application/octet-stream")`, in
  sight — and a value no header carries as sent (a control character, a letter
  beyond ASCII, blank or padded) is refused at the call with
  `StorageError::Unsignable`, naming the argument, never the value.
- **Both presigns answer a `PresignedRequest`**: the `url`, its `method`
  (`http::Method`), the `headers` the uploader sends verbatim and `expires_at`,
  to the second and never later than the store's own lapse. Its `Debug`
  withholds the URL, a credential until it lapses.
- **`expires` is SigV4's, one second to seven days**: anything else is refused
  at the call with `StorageError::Unsignable`, rather than minting a URL the
  store refuses when it is used.
- **`head` reads the stored `content_type`, the `etag` and `last_modified`**
  beside `byte_size`, through one `HEAD`. `ObjectMetadata::byte_size` and
  `ObjectEntry::byte_size` are `u64`, as the store reports them.
- **A presigned `PUT` binds no size**: object_store refuses to sign
  `Content-Length`, so `SignedPut` carries none in 7.0; read `byte_size` back
  with `head` once the upload lands. The storage page says what binds where.
- **The demo's audio keys are minted, never named by the client**: an upload's
  key is a UUID, `file` is parsed as one at the edge, the transcode endpoint
  answers `202` without echoing it, and no log line or reply quotes a key, a
  file name, a chat author or a login's address the client sent.

### The tenant travels with every job, like the actor

`PrincipalIdentity` gains `tenant_id`, defaulted to `None`: a principal bound to
a tenant names it once, off its verified credential.

```rust
impl PrincipalIdentity for Claims {
    fn actor_id(&self) -> Option<String> { self.sub.map(|sub| sub.to_string()) }
    fn tenant_id(&self) -> Option<String> { Some(self.org_id.to_string()) }
}
```

- **The authentication guard makes it ambient** for the request and every
  continuation of it — a streaming body, a DataLoader batch, a socket's
  messages, an event's listeners, a task — read with
  `nest_rs::core::current_tenant_id()`. Unlike `current_actor_id()`, it is an
  authorization input: only a verified principal or a sealed job envelope sets
  it, never a header, and no log line carries it. An empty id names no tenant.
- **Every job a request pushes carries it**, in the envelope's new `tenant_id`
  key, and the worker restores it around the job's attempt:
  `current_tenant_id()` answers in a processor what it answered where the job
  was pushed — with or without a `traceparent` beside it — and every job the
  processor pushes carries it on. A job pushed from a scheduled tick, a boot
  task or `main` runs for no tenant.
- **A record whose `tenant_id` names no tenant** — empty, or not a string, which
  no push writes — is dead-lettered without running, non-retryably, naming the
  key and never its value: a job runs for the tenant it was pushed for or not at
  all.
- The demo's claims name their `org_id` as the tenant. *Multi-tenant SaaS in
  production* and *Authentication* say how a principal declares it and how a
  job reads by it.

### The envelope accepts and propagates W3C `baggage`

The 7.0 wire envelope (`v: 1`) reserves W3C Baggage's `baggage` key beside
`traceparent` and `tracestate`, so a later minor can add members without
bumping the wire format — a bump defers every job across a rolling deploy.

- **A job's `baggage` within W3C's limits** — 64 list-members, 8192 bytes — is
  kept for the attempt as it was written, and written into every job the
  attempt pushes: W3C has a platform propagate what it does not understand.
- **A malformed or oversized one is dropped, and the job still runs**: the
  delivery's one `warn`, `job envelope carries keys this consumer cannot use`,
  lists `baggage` among the keys and its `hint` says why, never quoting the
  value, and a retry's re-filed record leaves it out.
- **Nothing in 7.0 writes baggage from a request or reads a member.** A value
  the framework's posture depends on — the actor, the tenant — is a first-class
  key, never baggage: anyone writing to the queue can forge a member.

### A job failing with a client's error dead-letters at once

Every `Err` a `#[process]` method returned was retried until its budget was
spent, a `422` it answered and a `JobError::abort` it chose included. The
failure's disposition is now read once, in order: the problem an exception
filter answered; a `JobError`'s own; the method's error by its type — a
`ToProblem` error by the problem it says, any other by a `Problem` in its
chain. A `4xx` other than `408` and `429` is the client's error (RFC 9110
§15.5) and dead-letters the job on the attempt it happens, without spending
the budget; `408`, `429` and every other failure retry within the budget as
before. A `ServiceError::NotFound` or `Conflict` returned as such therefore
dead-letters at once.

### An event waits for the transaction it announces

**Breaking.** `EventBus::emit` inside a unit of work holding a transaction — a
mutating request, a GraphQL mutation, a WebSocket message, an MCP operation, a
queue attempt, a scheduled tick — no longer runs its listeners inline. They run
once the transaction commits, and never when it rolls back, fails to commit or the
boundary fails. 6.1 ran them inside the emitter's transaction, so a listener that
pushed a job or notified subscribers did it before the commit, about a write that
might never land and that a worker could not see yet, and a listener's failed
statement poisoned the emitter's writes.

- Listeners run outside that transaction, on the pool, under the emitter's scope
  and ability.
- With nothing to wait for — a safe request, `transactional = false`, no database
  — dispatch is unchanged: before `emit` returns.
- Inside a transaction you open yourself, emit after your own commit: the
  framework cannot see that commit.
- A `push`, a WebSocket broadcast and a storage write still happen where they are
  written, since each answers its caller and that answer exists only once the
  backend is asked. To push after a commit, emit an event and push from its
  listener, as the demo's publish does.
- **Added `nest_rs::database::after_commit` and `Executor::after_commit`**, the
  seam the bus waits through. A database driver whose boundaries settle a
  transaction overrides `after_commit`; the default runs the work at once. In
  `nest-rs-seaorm`, `LazyTransaction::finalize` — the one place every edge settles
  through — runs the held work after a commit, or after a boundary that succeeded
  without opening one, and drops it otherwise with one `debug` line; a panic in
  held work is contained at `error`.

### A unique violation is a 409, read once for every edge

`ServiceError` took a `DbErr` whole, so `?` on a unique violation answered `500`
where the HTTP `#[crud]` answered `409` for the same insert — the demo's concurrent
publish of one post among them. `nest_rs::seaorm::DbOutcome` (new) classifies a
`DbErr` once, off the error alone — `Conflict` for a unique violation, `NotFound` for
a row an update or delete found gone, `Internal` for everything else — and
`From<DbErr> for ServiceError` reads it: `Conflict` becomes `ServiceError::Conflict`
(`409`, `CONFLICT`), `NotFound` becomes `ServiceError::NotFound` (`404`,
`NOT_FOUND`), `Internal` stays `ServiceError::Db` (`500`, opaque). `RecordNotInserted`
stays `Internal`: sea-orm also raises it for an `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` that inserted
nothing, so only `#[crud]`, whose create raises it for its scope re-check alone,
answers it `403`. HTTP and GraphQL `#[crud]` share the one mapping.

**Breaking:** a `?` from a `DbErr` into `ServiceError` answers `409` for a unique
violation and `404` for `RecordNotFound` / `RecordNotUpdated`, where it answered `500`.
`ServiceError::from(err)` classifies; `ServiceError::Db(err)` still builds the opaque
variant directly.

### A savepoint is one call: `nest_rs::database::atomic`

A statement that fails inside a transaction aborts the whole transaction, and the one
savepoint the framework had was the SeaORM driver's own, reachable from `Creatable`'s
create alone. A service that expected a failure — a unique key a concurrent request may
just have taken — and swallowed it on a mutating request poisoned that request into a
`500`; the demo's social login did, on every login racing another for the same email.

`nest_rs::database::atomic(work)` (new) runs `work` inside a savepoint of the ambient
transaction, or in a transaction of its own on the pool, with the block installed as the
ambient executor under the same scope. `Ok` keeps its writes and hands what it emitted
to the enclosing unit, which still commits or rolls back as a whole; `Err`, a panic, or
a statement that failed inside rolls the block back alone and drops what it emitted. A
block in a block is a savepoint of its own. Nothing is sent before the block's first
statement, so a block that never queries costs no round trip.

```rust
let linked = atomic(async {
    Ok::<_, ServiceError>(Repo::<UserIdentities>::insert_unscoped(identity).await?)
})
.await;
match linked {
    Ok(_) | Err(ServiceError::Conflict(_)) => Ok(()),
    Err(e) => Err(e),
}
```

- **A unit of work the framework opened waits for an open block**: its own statements
  queue until the block settles, since a statement issued beside it would land inside the
  savepoint and roll back with it. A statement issued from inside a block on an enclosing
  unit's executor, taken before the block, is refused — whether or not the block has
  opened yet — rather than left to wait for itself. A transaction the caller opened and
  installed itself (`SeaOrmExecutor::Txn`) is not held back: a statement the caller issues on it
  beside an open block lands inside the block and rolls back with it, so the caller
  awaits the block before writing on.
- **A block dropped before it settles** — cancelled by a timeout or a `select!` — leaves
  the unit unable to commit; so does a `RELEASE` or `ROLLBACK TO` the database refuses
  (`AtomicError::Database`, also answered when a block that is a transaction of its own
  fails its `COMMIT`; a refused `SAVEPOINT` fails the block's first statement instead). A handle that outlives its block writes nothing and the
  block answers `AtomicError::Escaped`; a block that returns `Ok` over a failed statement
  answers `AtomicError::Poisoned`, and nothing it wrote stands.
- `AtomicError` (`Unsupported`, `Database`, `Poisoned`, `Escaped`) is
  `#[non_exhaustive]`; `ServiceError` takes one, a database refusal by its `DbOutcome`,
  the rest opaque.
- `Creatable::create_from_active` runs its insert and scope re-check in a block, so its
  rollback is the block's, and a failed undo is filed on `nest_rs::orm` where it was
  `rollback of the create SAVEPOINT/transaction failed`: inside a request's or a job's
  transaction (a mutating request's create) as `an atomic block's savepoint could not be
  settled; its unit cannot commit` with the unit's `transport` and
  `outcome = "rollback_and_fail"`; on the pool or inside a caller's own transaction as
  `transaction rollback failed` with `transport = "atomic"`. Neither names the entity:
  the request's or job's trace locates it.
- A driver offers blocks through the new `Executor::atomic`, returning an `AtomicBlock`;
  the default offers none, and `atomic` then answers `AtomicError::Unsupported` without
  running the work. `nest_rs::seaorm::SeaOrmExecutor` gains `Savepoint`, the block a lazy
  transaction or another block holds.

### Every edge runs its unit of work through one port, `nest_rs::database::UnitOfWork`

Each edge used to reach the data layer through a seam of its own: a SeaORM-only HTTP
interceptor, a worker `JobContext`, and a WebSocket and an MCP data context an app had to
list in its authz modules. Two units committed on the wrong signal — an MCP tool that
reported its own error in band (`CallToolResult { isError: true }`, how the MCP
specification reports a failed tool) committed what it wrote — WebSocket connection hooks
ran with no unit of work at all, and a gateway or a tool host whose module forgot the
data context ran every `Repo` call bare.

`nest-rs-database` now holds the port. An edge declares each unit it runs as a
`UnitSpec` — its operation-log unit, its scope, its `TransactionPolicy` — and runs it
through `run_unit`, which asks the bound `dyn UnitOfWork` to open it, runs the body with
the unit's executor installed, and settles it on the edge's own outcome; with no binding
the body runs bare. Each edge resolves the binding once, at mount or boot.

| Unit | Commits when |
|---|---|
| HTTP request (mutating method; a safe one runs on the pool) | the handler's own success, answered `2xx` or `3xx` — a failure an exception filter answers `2xx`, or a `404` a handler answers on purpose, rolls back |
| GraphQL operation | its answer reports data; each mutation root field settles its own savepoint |
| WebSocket message | the reply is not an error frame |
| WebSocket connect / disconnect hook | the hook ran to its end |
| MCP request operation | `Ok`, and no `isError: true` in a tool's result |
| Queue attempt, scheduled tick | `Ok` (the pool on `transactional = false`) |

- **An app lists no data context.** A gateway's adapter imports `AuthzModule` and
  `WsModule`; `AuthzMcpModule` provides its operation guard alone. The WS
  ability the upgrade's guards attach crosses the connection into every message and
  hook; the MCP operation's is what its request's guards attached.
- **A read is a touch**: a message, a hook or a tool that only reads still opens its
  transaction on the first query, and commits it.
- **A driver implements one trait**, `UnitOfWork` (`open(&UnitSpec) ->
  Result<Box<dyn OpenUnit>, Unhonoured>`), whose `OpenUnit` hands out the executor and
  settles once the body and its executor are dropped (`Settlement::Settled`, or
  `Settlement::Unhonoured` for a success it could not keep, which the edge then answers
  as its failure). It binds `Arc<dyn UnitOfWork>` as a declaration carrying
  `nest_rs::database::BACKEND_REMEDY`: two bindings refuse the boot. A streaming body, a
  DataLoader batch and after-commit work run on a `TransactionPolicy::Pool` unit of the
  binding that opened their unit (`current_unit_of_work()` answers it), never on its
  transaction; a graphql-ws socket, which outlives its upgrade's unit, runs as
  a `Pool` unit of the binding the mount resolved. `nest_rs_database::Executor::__pool`
  is gone.
- SeaORM's driver is `nest_rs::seaorm::SeaOrmUnitOfWork`, which `SeaOrmDatabaseModule`
  binds. Its commit-failure event is one for every edge: `transaction commit failed` on
  `nest_rs::orm` with `transport` (the edge: `http`, `ws`, `mcp`, `queue`, `schedule`),
  `error` and `retryable` — or, on a request with `observe_serialization_conflicts`, the
  `warn` `serialization conflict at commit`. On HTTP the edge runs a request's unit
  itself (*HTTP runs on the one pipeline*); `DbContext` is gone.
- `nest-rs-worker` is gone too: `JOB_TIMEOUT` and `JobTimedOut` are the kernel's
  (*`nest-rs-worker` is gone: the job bound lives in the kernel*).

### A soft-deleted row is hidden from every Repo read

`#[expose(..., soft_delete)]` stamped `deleted_at` only when the entity's service also overrode
`CrudService::soft_delete_column`, and only `CrudService`'s own `list`, `page` and `access` hid a
stamped row: a custom service read through `Repo` — `all`, `find_by_id`, `scoped`, `unscoped`,
`page` — returned a deleted user or a deleted org, data a deletion promised to hide, unless the
developer remembered to AND `live_condition` by hand.

- **Breaking: the entity is the one declaration.** `#[expose(..., soft_delete)]` registers the
  entity, with or without a `service`, and every `Repo` read and update of it ANDs
  `deleted_at IS NULL`, table-qualified, after the ability's condition: `all`, `find_by_id`,
  `scoped`, `page`, the relation batches, `update` (a deleted row is `RecordNotUpdated`) and
  `can` (`false`). `Repo::unscoped` and `unscoped_by_id` hide deleted rows too: they drop the
  ability, not the deletion, so a deleted account cannot sign in. `CrudService::access`, and so
  `Bind`, answers `Missing` for a deleted row whatever the action. `Deletable::delete` stamps the
  row when the entity is declared `soft_delete`, and erases it otherwise.
- **`Delete` reaches a deleted row.** `Repo::delete` stays the hard purge, `Repo::soft_delete`
  stays idempotent, and `Repo::can(Delete, …)` answers for a tombstone, so a retention job selects
  through `Repo::scoped(Action::Delete)` as before. A create checks its fresh row against the
  ability alone, so a row inserted already deleted (an archive import) is no denial.
- **New: `Repo::with_tombstones(action)`**, the ability-scoped read that keeps deleted rows — the
  one way to read one, for an administrator's view or a retention job. In a job it reads every
  row, as `scoped` does. It reads only: 7.0 has no `Repo` write that brings a deleted row back
  (`Repo::update` hides it too); `Repo::restore` is planned for 7.x.
- **Breaking: removed** `CrudService::soft_delete_column`, `CrudService::live_read_filter`, the
  boot audit `audit_soft_delete_bindings` (a half-wired soft delete can no longer be written), and
  the public `SoftDeleteRegistration` and `inventory` re-exports, which only the decorator's
  expansion used. `live_condition::<E>()` stays, the condition a query built outside `Repo` ANDs.
  `resolve_unique_slug` takes any entity: its probe reads through `unscoped`, so a deleted row
  frees its slug as before.
- **Breaking: `SoftDeletable` is sealed.** Only `#[expose(..., soft_delete)]` implements it, beside
  the registration `Repo` reads: a hand-written impl, which registered nothing and left `Repo`
  showing tombstones and `Deletable::delete` erasing rows, no longer compiles.
- **A value unique among live rows takes a partial index.** With deleted rows hidden, a full
  unique index refuses, with a `409`, a value every read calls free: index it
  `WHERE deleted_at IS NULL`. The CRUD page says so; the demo's user email and org name moved to
  partial indexes.

### `#[expose]` names an entity's columns as SeaORM does

`#[expose]` spelled a field's `Column` variant with its own case conversion and ignored
`#[sea_orm(enum_name = …)]`, where SeaORM's derive converts with heck and honours `enum_name`: an
entity with a field such as `ownerID`, or a renamed variant, failed to compile (loudly) as soon as
`#[expose]` named its column — in the response mask's defaults, a relation loader or the
soft-delete column. Every variant is now the one SeaORM derives.

### A timeout cuts the work, never its commit

A deadline used to wrap a whole unit of work, so it could land during `COMMIT`
or the after-commit work behind it: a queue attempt whose write had landed was
retried as a timeout and its events emitted twice, a request whose write had
landed was answered `503` with `Retry-After`, and a client gone or an MCP
operation stopped could drop a `COMMIT` mid-flight.

- **Every deadline cuts the body alone.** A request's timeout, a WebSocket
  message's, an MCP operation's, a job's and a tick's `timeout` cut the
  handler's body inside its unit of work: a cut body rolls back and is answered
  as a timeout — `503` with `Retry-After`, an `UNAVAILABLE` frame or error with
  `reason: "timeout"`, a retryable `JobTimedOut` — honest now, since nothing it
  wrote committed. A cut unit is answered at its deadline even while its
  rollback waits on a statement Postgres still holds.
- **A commit always reaches its answer.** SeaORM settles a transaction on a
  task of its own, which a dropped edge future never cuts and which the binding
  drains before the pool closes; its events keep the unit's trace.
- **A commit that does not answer is in doubt, never retried.** SeaORM waits for
  it at most its statement budget plus a second (`UnitOfWork::settle_net`);
  past it the unit fails with `Unhonoured::IN_DOUBT` — a request `500` without
  `Retry-After`, a job dead-lettered (`job dead-lettered: its commit did not
  answer in time, and what it wrote may have landed`, `outcome="in_doubt"`), a
  tick reported `retryable=false` — and the driver files `the unit's commit did
  not answer within its net; its writes may have landed` on `nest_rs::orm`.
- **A slow listener never fails a committed unit.** The answer waits for the
  work held for the commit until the unit's deadline, then the work runs on
  (`after-commit work outlived the unit's deadline`, `debug`).
- **An atomic block on the pool commits on the driver's task too.** A block
  of its own — in a non-transactional job or tick, or on a safe route — that
  the cut catches mid-`COMMIT` finishes on SeaORM's task, and its unit fails
  in doubt rather than as a retryable timeout (`the unit was cut while an
  atomic block's commit ran; its writes may have landed`, `error`).
- **A job's checkpoint is read under its deadline.** A `Checkpoint<S>` store
  that does not answer within the job's `timeout` fails the attempt as a
  retryable `JobTimedOut`, never as an in-doubt commit.
- **HTTP hands its deadline down.** The edge hands the deadline down in every
  request's extensions when a request timeout is set; the unit layer takes it
  over, and the edge's own timer then waits the driver's settle net past the
  deadline.

`UnitOfWork` gains a required `settle_net`, and `OpenUnit::settle` owes the
contract above: a driver written against 7.0's port adds the one method. A
failed unit's settle answering `Unhonoured::IN_DOUBT` now replaces the failure
(`run_unit`); any other answer to a failure still leaves it standing.

### A statement Postgres cancelled is a timeout, not an internal error

A statement Postgres cancels past `<PREFIX>_SEAORM__STATEMENT_TIMEOUT_SECS`
(SQLSTATE `57014`) surfaced as an opaque `500`, filed at `error` on every edge.
`DbOutcome::Unavailable` now classifies it: `ServiceError` and `#[crud]` answer
`503` / `UNAVAILABLE` on HTTP, GraphQL, WebSocket and MCP, and a job's attempt
fails retryably — inside a transaction nothing it wrote stands, so a statement
the job swallowed poisons the attempt as retryable too. Every edge files the
chain behind a `503` answer at `warn` (`request unavailable`, `graphql
operation unavailable`, `mcp operation unavailable`, the WebSocket handler's
line, SeaORM's `service error surfaced as 503` and `crud operation
unavailable`): the server could not serve it in time, which is no failure of
its own. A `500` still files at `error`.

### An event listener runs in its own transaction and may return a Result

A listener could only return `()`: its failures were a log line it wrote
itself, and the dispatch filed `ok` whatever it met. It ran on the pool, so a
listener writing two rows could half-write. Each listener call is now a unit of
work of its own, run through the same port as a request or a job, under the
scope and the ability its emitter carried:

- **One transaction per call.** It commits when the listener answers `Ok` and
  rolls back when it answers `Err`. `#[on_event(transactional = false)]` runs
  it on the pool instead, where each statement commits on its own. An emit
  outside every unit of work — an app with no database, a task the app
  spawned — has no binding to open one, so its listeners run bare, on whatever
  the emitter installed.
- **A listener may answer a `Result`.** The method returns `()` or
  `Result<(), E>` with any `E` converting into `Box<dyn Error + Send + Sync>`
  (`anyhow::Result<()>`, a crate's own error), read by its type: an alias or a
  renamed `Result` behaves as the literal, and any other answer is a compile
  error at the return type naming the two. An `Err` rolls the call back, files
  the `events.dispatch` line as `error`, and logs `event listener failed —
  dispatch continues with the next listener` at `error` on `nest_rs::events`
  with `event`, `listener` and the whole `error` chain, each link kept as the
  type it is (an `anyhow::Error` unwrapped), so a driver's `SafeLink` still says
  its own error without the value it quotes. The next listener runs either way,
  and the emitter's outcome stands.
- **An event a listener emits waits for that listener's commit**, and is
  dropped when it rolls back (it dispatched at once, on the pool).
- **A listener is cut at its timeout**, ten minutes unless
  `#[on_event(timeout = "30s")]` declares one (the job family's key and range,
  at most `"24h"`, never off), counted from the call's own start: its body is
  cut inside its unit, which rolls back, and the failure reads `the listener
  did not end within its 30s timeout, and was cut — #[on_event(timeout = …)]
  sets it`. A commit is never cut: a call whose commit does not answer within
  the driver's settle net past the deadline fails as
  `Unhonoured::IN_DOUBT`, its writes possibly landed.
- **A caller's own transaction is not joined.** A listener reached while a
  caller's own `SeaOrmExecutor::Txn` is installed, inside a unit of work, runs in a
  transaction of its own beside it — never as a passenger whose `Err` could not
  roll back what it wrote. On another connection, it does not see the caller's
  uncommitted rows, waits on a row the caller locked until its statement budget
  or its timeout cuts it, and commits even if the caller rolls back afterwards:
  emit after your own `commit`. Outside every unit of work it still runs inside
  the caller's transaction.

`#[on_event]` takes `transactional` and `timeout` and refuses any other key
listing the two (`unknown #[on_event] argument `retries`; expected
`transactional` or `timeout``). The job family's ceiling refusal now reads
`work running past a day is a process of its own` at every member. The CLI's
listener scaffold answers `anyhow::Result<()>`, and the demo's notifications
listener returns the push's `QueueError` rather than logging it.

### A GraphQL operation is a unit of work, and each mutation root field a savepoint

A GraphQL request rode its HTTP request's unit of work: a batch holding a mutation ran in
the request's transaction, which committed on any `2xx` — and a GraphQL response is always `200`.
A mutation field that failed after a write committed that write and dispatched the events it
emitted; a statement the database refused left the request's transaction unable to commit, so the
whole response became an HTTP `500` with no GraphQL body, where REST answers `409`.

Each operation of a GraphQL document is now a unit of work of its own (`graphql.operation`): a
query runs on the pool, a mutation in one transaction opened on its first statement. Its root
fields resolve one after the other, each in a savepoint of that transaction, settled on the
answer that field gives (*A GraphQL mutation keeps exactly the work its answer reports*, below):
a failed nullable root field answers `null` and rolls back alone, its events dropped. The field's
error reaches the client as the resolver wrote it, a unique violation as its `CONFLICT`. A field
that never touches the database sends no `SAVEPOINT`. An operation whose commit cannot be honoured
answers `data: null` and one opaque `INTERNAL` error, so a response never reports a write that did
not land.

- **The request runs on the pool.** A request addressed to a self-mount whose request carries
  units of its own — `/graphql`, `/mcp` — runs on the pool whatever its method: the guards read
  there, and each operation settles its own work.
- **A batch holding a mutation runs its operations one at a time**, in document order, so its
  units never hold several pooled connections at once; a batch of queries still runs them at once.
- **graphql-ws:** each query and mutation sent over the socket is a unit of its own, opened inside
  the socket's pool unit; a mutation there now runs in a transaction. A subscription keeps the
  connection.
- **graphql-ws: a named subscription sent without `operationName` is completed at the socket's
  end** like any other. The socket read a lone named operation selected without its name as a
  query, so at the connection ceiling or the shutdown signal it neither stopped nor completed it,
  and the socket stayed open until the close grace or the shutdown window dropped it; it now gets
  its `complete`, and the socket closes `1001`.
- **Under a request timeout**, a request holding a mutation takes the deadline over: an operation
  still running at it is cut inside its unit, rolled back and answered `UNAVAILABLE` in band —
  said at `warn` under that operation's ids, naming it and the request's `timeout` — and one
  already committing is waited for. A request of queries alone is cut by the edge, `503`, as
  before.
- **One `graphql.operation` line per operation** on `nest_rs::operation`, with `operation_type`
  and `operation_name` (read off the document, never the request's free text), `error` when its
  response carries an error; the root fields' `graphql.field` lines nest under it. A DataLoader
  batch keeps its request's identity and span.
- **A driver offering no atomic block** now fails every GraphQL mutation closed (`INTERNAL`)
  rather than commit a failed field's writes.

A relation a mutation's response resolves through a DataLoader still reads the pool, so it does
not see the rows the operation wrote.

**Breaking, in behaviour:** a mutation root field that answers `null` for an error no longer
commits its writes nor dispatches its events; a batch's operations no longer share one
transaction; a graphql-ws mutation runs in a transaction; `/graphql` and `/mcp` no longer open an
HTTP transaction of their own, so a guard's read there runs on the pool.

### A GraphQL field that errs answers `null`, and a Non-Null one nulls its parent

A field whose resolver failed was left out of `data`: async-graphql 7.2.1 adds the error and
inserts no key for the field, and answers `null` for an object whose every field failed. A
Non-Null field's error nulled nothing above it. Apollo Client, Relay and generated types read a
missing key as a malformed response.

- **A field that errs is present as `null`** beside its error, whose `path` names it — a query's
  field, a mutation's root field (its savepoint rolled back, as before) and a nested one alike, as
  GraphQL (October 2021) §6.4.4 handles a field error.
- **A Non-Null field's `null` climbs to its nearest nullable parent**: an object, a list item, a
  list — and `data` itself from a Non-Null root field. A `Result<T>` return is `T!`; a
  `Result<Option<T>>` is nullable and keeps its siblings' answers when it fails.
- **Everywhere `/graphql` answers**: `POST`, each operation of a batch, and every graphql-ws
  `next` — a query's, a mutation's and each subscription item's.
- **A placed `null` answers where the selection names its key** (§7.2.2, *Serialized Map
  Ordering*): a mutation's failed first root field reads first, never after the fields that
  answered.
- **An answer without a field error pays nothing**: the nulls are placed only when an error names
  a path, read off the schema's SDL, parsed once at mount, and the operation's selections. Each
  error then costs a walk down its own path, however many errors the request raised.
- **Two selections keep async-graphql 7.2.1's answer.** A field resolved through an interface
  keeps its key absent, its error filed without a `path`: select it inside
  `... on <ConcreteType>` and its `null` lands. An object selected twice at one place (two
  fragments selecting it) keeps whichever copy finished first; when that copy's every field
  failed, the object answers `null`, or its nearest nullable parent does, never an object missing
  what the other copy answered.

### A GraphQL mutation keeps exactly the work its answer reports

A mutation's root field rolled its writes back whenever its answer carried an error, and the
operation then committed whatever its root fields had kept. Three answers disagreed with the
database: a root field whose nullable sub-field failed answered its value while its writes were
rolled back; a Non-Null root field that failed nulled `data` (GraphQL §6.4.4) while the root
fields before and after it committed, so the client read nothing of work that stood, and a retry
applied it twice; and a root field selected twice ran twice, async-graphql 7.2.1 running one
execution per selection, its two answers merged into one.

- **A root field settles on the answer it gives.** Its savepoint rolls back when the field answers
  `null` for an error — its own, or a Non-Null sub-field's whose `null` climbs to it — and is
  released when the `null` stays below it, a nullable sub-field's or a Non-Null one's held by a
  nullable parent: the root field answers its value, that sub-field `null`, and its writes and
  events stand.
- **An answer with no data rolls the whole operation back.** When a Non-Null root field's `null`
  reaches `data`, the root fields that answered before it roll back too, their events dropped, and
  no root field after it runs: its guards, its handler and its writes never start, and it adds no
  error. On `POST`, for each operation of a batch, and over graphql-ws; an app with no database
  skips the later root fields all the same.
- **An operation's unit commits only when its answer reports data**, so the operation line, the
  answer and the database agree.
- **A mutation selects each root field once.** A document whose mutation names one response key
  twice — two selections under one key, two fragments that each select it, one fragment spread
  twice, a copy `@skip` leaves out included — is refused before any root field runs: `data: null`
  and one `INVALID_ARGUMENT` error pointing at the second selection. On `POST`, for each operation
  of a batch, and over graphql-ws. A query naming a key twice runs as before.
- **An interface field's error keeps its root field's work.** async-graphql 7.2.1 files it without
  a `path`, so no `null` lands for it and the root field's answer stands: select the field inside
  `... on <ConcreteType>` for its `null`, and its rollback, to land.

**Breaking, in behaviour:** a mutation document whose Non-Null root field fails commits none of
its root fields and runs none after the failed one; a root field whose nullable sub-field fails
keeps its writes; a mutation document naming one root field twice is refused.

### Lifecycle hooks are units of work with a data context

A lifecycle hook ran bare: no trace of its own, one `debug` line as it started,
and no executor, so a hook could not reach `Repo` — `no ambient database
executor` — and a shutdown hook kept an injected `Arc<DatabaseConnection>` as
the documented escape. Each hook, init and shutdown alike, now runs as one
`lifecycle.hook` unit of work, the kernel's own unit on `nest_rs::lifecycle`:

- **A trace and a line of its own.** A hook has no caller, so it mints its
  trace; its span carries `phase`, `provider`, `method` and `origin`, and one
  line on `nest_rs::operation` closes it with `outcome` (`ok`, `error`,
  `panic`) and `duration_ms`. A shutdown hook abandoned at the hooks' budget,
  or an init hook a boot signal stopped, files `cancelled`. The `running
  lifecycle hook` `debug` line is gone; the failure lines (`lifecycle hook
  failed`, `shutdown hook panicked, …`, `shutdown hook abandoned: …`) stay,
  now in the hook's trace.
- **The data context a tick gets.** With a database binding imported, a hook
  runs in one transaction, opened on its first data-layer touch, committed on
  `Ok` and rolled back on an error, a panic or the budget. It is system work —
  the Job scope, no ability — so `Repo` reads unscoped, as a job does. A commit
  that fails is the hook's error, and at init it aborts the boot. An event a
  hook emits waits for its commit, and each listener runs in a transaction of
  its own. Shutdown hooks run before the pool closes, so they have it too. With
  no binding imported a hook runs bare, as before.
- **A driver binds both seams through one helper.**
  `nest_rs::database::provide_unit_of_work::<Inputs, _, _>(builder, factory)`
  binds a driver's unit of work as the app's one `Arc<dyn UnitOfWork>` — still a
  declaration carrying `BACKEND_REMEDY`, so a second driver fails the boot — and
  as the unit of work every lifecycle hook runs in. `SeaOrmDatabaseModule`
  calls it, and so does a driver of one's own: a binding that declares
  `Arc<dyn UnitOfWork>` by hand still serves every edge but leaves every hook
  bare.

The `#[hooks]` grammar is unchanged.

### The pool closes once every unit settled, whatever a connection still waits on

SeaORM's pool close waited for every connection to come back before it said
goodbye to any, so a connection no unit held kept it until the hooks' budget
ran out — `resource close abandoned close="DatabaseConnection"` after five
seconds, then `a provider was still dropping …`. Two shapes did it: a request
cut at its deadline while Postgres still ran its statement (behind a table
lock), whose connection stays checked out until the statement ends; and a
connection whose release failed, which sqlx 0.9 then never gives back, its
flush of a quiet TLS socket waiting for the server to send. The close now takes
each connection back as it lands, half a second at most
(`SHUTDOWN_SETTLE_TIMEOUT`) — every unit has settled by then, the binding's own
close drains them first — and says goodbye to each; what is still checked out
closes when it returns, and one `warn` on `nest_rs::orm` counts it (`pool closed
with connections still checked out: each closes as it comes back`,
`checked_out`, `waited_ms`).

### A cancelled statement says how long to wait, and a service's error its code

A statement Postgres cancelled at its `statement_timeout` answered `503 UNAVAILABLE` with no
wait on any edge, while the HTTP edge's own request timeout answers its `503` with the budget
that ran out.

- **A cancelled statement names the statement bound of the pool its unit ran on as its
  wait**, in whole seconds rounded up — `Retry-After` on HTTP, `retryAfterSeconds` on
  GraphQL, WebSocket and MCP — for a `#[crud]` operation and a `ServiceError` alike.
  Outside a unit, or on a pool opened without a bound, none is invented.
- **A `ServiceError` answers its `code` on HTTP**, as a `#[crud]` operation's document for
  the same class and every other edge already did: `409` reads `code: CONFLICT`, a
  validation failure `INVALID_ARGUMENT` beside its `errors`. Its `503` carries its code and
  wait and, as every `5xx` problem, no `detail`; an opaque failure (`Db`, `Masking`,
  `Internal`) answers the `500` a `#[crud]` operation does, `code: INTERNAL` and no
  `detail`.
- **Breaking:** a `ServiceError`'s HTTP `503` and `500` no longer carry `detail` (the `500`'s
  was the constant `"internal error"`); a client reads their `code`.

### A job pushed inside a transaction is filed after its commit, never lost and never orphaned

A push called the backend where it was written. Inside a transaction, a
rollback left the job behind; the prescribed workaround, an event whose
listener pushes, lost the job when the process died between the `COMMIT` and
the push, and turned a refused push into a log line after the client held its
`2xx`.

With the `seaorm` and `queue` features, a push inside a unit's transaction — a
mutating request, a GraphQL mutation, a WebSocket message, an MCP operation, a
job attempt, a tick, a listener's call, a lifecycle hook — is now written into
that transaction, as a row of the framework's table `nestrs_outbox`:

```rust
// inside the request's transaction: kept by it, filed once it commits
self.jobs.push(NotifyQueue, NotifyCommand { org_id, message }).await?;
```

- **The outbox relay files what committed.** `OutboxRelay`, the queue port's
  transport, claims committed records with `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` (replicas
  never share one), hands them to the backend — one call per queue and delay,
  a keyed record alone — and deletes what was filed. Nothing is listed:
  `SeaOrmDatabaseModule` keeps the store and imports the relay's
  `OutboxModule`.
- **The receipt is honest.** It names a job that exists exactly when the
  caller's writes do, and a `push_many` there is one write, landing whole or
  not at all. `cancel` and `cancel_unique` delete a record no relay filed yet
  — waiting a moment for a relay filing it at that instant, so a job filed a
  moment later is never answered "too late" — then ask the backend; on a safe
  request too, and an atomic block there keeps its pushes until it commits.
- **Delivery stays at least once.** A relay dying between its call and its
  delete sends the same job again, which the backend files once while it holds
  it (`JobProducer::enqueue`'s contract); a handler keys its idempotency on
  `Attempt::job_id`.
- **A backend that refuses delays the job, never loses it.** The relay retries
  each record on the job retry backoff, files `outbox records not filed; each is
  due again after its wait` at `warn` per pass and queue, and `outbox records
  have waited past the stall warning unfiled; …` at `error`, once per outage.
- **A unique key is checked once the transaction committed.** A record whose key
  another job holds is dropped, in one `info` line naming both jobs; the push
  itself answered `Ok`.
- **Settings**, under the port's namespace, pinned through
  `OutboxModule::for_root(OutboxConfig { .. })`:
  `<PREFIX>_QUEUE__OUTBOX__SWEEP_INTERVAL_MS` (1000, 100 to 60000),
  `<PREFIX>_QUEUE__OUTBOX__BATCH_SIZE` (100, 1 to 100) and
  `<PREFIX>_QUEUE__OUTBOX__STALL_WARNING_SECS` (300, 1 to 86400).
- **The way down cuts no push.** The relay starts none once the app stops; the
  one in flight answers within the port's 18 s net, so the relay stops within
  18.5 s, under the worker's drain window.
- **The boot proves the table.** The relay arms the store through the boot's
  proof: a missing table refuses at once, naming the remedy, and a database
  failure that may clear is retried within the store's net, each said at
  `warn` (`outbox store failed arming; retrying within its budget`).
- **A data driver keeps the outbox through the database port**:
  `Executor::write_outbox` (and `remove_outbox`, `remove_outbox_unique`) on the
  handles its units settle, an `OutboxStore` / `OutboxClaim` it binds, and the
  relay's `OutboxModule` it imports. `write_outbox` reads its records from an
  iterator that seals each job as it is read, so a handle filing at once pays
  for none. A driver that does not files a push at once, as before. A queue
  backend owes nothing new.

A pool — a safe request, a `transactional = false` job — and a transaction you
opened yourself file a push at once, as before, and so does an app binding no
database.

### `nestrs g migration nestrs_outbox` registers the framework's migration

The outbox's table, `nestrs_outbox`, is created by a migration the framework
ships, `nest_rs::seaorm::queue::OutboxMigration`, and an app binding SeaORM
beside a queue backend refuses to boot without it. The CLI now writes that
registration for you:

```bash
nestrs g migration nestrs_outbox
```

- **One `migrator.rs` entry, no file.** The command adds
  `Box::new(nest_rs::seaorm::queue::OutboxMigration)` to `migrations()` at its
  date's place, among your own, and turns on the `seaorm` and `queue` features
  of the workspace's `nest-rs`, which compile it. On a workspace without the
  migrations crate it writes the crate with that entry. Run again, it says the
  entry is there and writes nothing. `nestrs run db up` then creates the table.
- **`nestrs g queue` registers it too**, wherever the workspace keeps a
  migrations crate: the queue it enables keeps a job pushed inside a
  transaction in that table until the commit.
- **Every later `g migration` keeps the entry.** `migrator.rs` is still
  rewritten from the `mod` list in `lib.rs` on each generated migration, and now
  keeps every framework migration it lists — boxed by path or imported.
- **The `nestrs_` prefix is the framework's.** `g migration` refuses a name
  taking it that the framework does not ship (`nestrs_foo`), and a migration
  whose table would take it (`create_nestrs_foo`), naming the one it accepts:
  `` `nestrs_foo` claims the `nestrs_` prefix, which names the framework's own
  tables: `nestrs g migration` registers under it only a migration the framework
  ships — `nestrs_outbox` ``. `g resource` and `g entity` refuse a table under
  the prefix with the same sentence (`g resource nestrs_widget`,
  `g entity shelf/nestrs_crate`), before they write anything.

### A page cursor that does not parse is refused, never read as the first page

`after` was read with `Uuid::parse_str(..).ok()` at every edge reading it, so a truncated
or mangled cursor answered `200` with the first page: a sync loop following `next`
re-read, and re-applied, the whole collection without a sign. It is now parsed at the
edge into a type, and one no page issued is the edge's client error:

- a `#[crud]` list over HTTP answers `400`, the problem document a pipe refusal is, its
  `detail` `after is not a cursor this endpoint issued` — the parameter, never the value;
- a `#[crud]` list over GraphQL, and every auto-resolved `has_many` relation, answer a
  field error with the same sentence, its `extensions.code` `INVALID_ARGUMENT` — the code
  a malformed `id` answers there;
- an empty `after` (`?after=`) is still the first page, so a client templating the query
  on its first call keeps working. A valid cursor's text is unchanged: the key's own,
  hyphenated, and any spelling of a UUID reads back the same.

The OpenAPI document now declares the `400` on every paginated `#[crud]` list.

`nest_rs::seaorm::PageCursor` (new) is the cursor: `From<Uuid>`, `Display` writing the
key's text, `FromStr` refusing with a `PipeError`, `Serialize` as its text. It is `Clone`,
not `Copy`, so a later key type's cursor joins it additively. It joins the
keyset vocabulary: `Page::next_cursor` is `Option<PageCursor>`, and `Repo::page`,
`Repo::relation_pages`, `CrudService::page` and `RelationKey::after` take
`Option<PageCursor>`; `RelationKey` is therefore `Clone`, no longer `Copy`. `PageParams::cursor()` replaces `PageParams::after_uuid()`: `None`
when `after` is absent or empty, the refusal when it is not a cursor.
`nest_rs::http::pipe_error` (new) is the HTTP twin of `nest_rs::graphql::pipe_error`,
`nest_rs::mcp::pipe_error` and `WsReply::pipe_error`: it renders a `PipeError` as the
`400` a pipe refusal answers, so a hand-written list refuses a bad cursor the way the
generated one does:

```rust
let after = params.cursor().map_err(|refused| pipe_error(&refused))?;
let page = CrudService::page(&*self.svc, params.limit(), after).await?;
```

**Breaking**, on the wire: a client sending a cursor no page issued now gets `400` (or a
GraphQL field error) where it got the first page; that client was re-reading the
collection. In the API: `after_uuid()` is gone, the cursor types moved from `Uuid` to
`PageCursor`, and `RelationKey` lost `Copy`.

### A create fills what the caller's grants pin, and an insert seeds the key, a default and its creation time

A tenant-scoped entity could not keep `#[crud]`'s create. Nothing filled `org_id`, so
`users` overrode the generated create on HTTP and GraphQL, and `posts` on HTTP, each
restating the `201`, the `Location`, the posture and the validation to stamp the tenant (and
the author) from the token — and losing the `409` and `Location` the generated operation
documents. The fact was already written twice: the `Create` grant pins `org_id =
actor.org_id`, and `Creatable` re-checks the fresh row against it. The `v7` key was seeded by
`CreateModel` alone, so every row built for `Repo::insert_unscoped` minted its own, and
`timestamps` demanded an `updated_at`, so an append-only table wrote its creation time and
an empty `ActiveModelBehavior` by hand.

- `Creatable::create` and `create_from_active` fill, before the insert and its re-check,
  each column the caller's `Create` grants (`Manage` included) pin by equality and the input
  left unset — CASL's `rulesToFields`, read by `Ability::pinned_values::<E>(action)` (new).
  A column is filled only when every grant constraining it pins it to one value; grants
  pinning it apart, or one constraining it any other way (`is_in`, a comparison, `any`,
  `not`), leave it unset and the re-check refuses the row. A value the input carries is
  never overwritten. A pinned value its column cannot hold is left unset, with one `warn` on
  `nest_rs::orm` naming the column.

  ```rust
  if let Some(author) = actor.sub.user_id() {
      ab.can(Action::Create, post::Entity).when(|p| {
          p.all([
              p.eq(post::Column::OrgId, actor.org_id),
              p.eq(post::Column::AuthorId, author),
          ])
      });
  }
  ```

- `#[expose]` registers each exposed entity's column defaults, which every insert through
  `Repo` — `Creatable`'s and `Repo::insert_unscoped` — fills where the row left them unset,
  after the grants' pins: a `Uuid` key with a v7 UUID, and a column's
  `#[expose(default = expr)]` (new), a server-side constant on a column the create input does
  not carry. The registration is `soft_delete`'s shape, so the entity's
  `ActiveModelBehavior` stays its own, hooks included. `timestamps` takes an entity with
  `created_at` alone, and stamps `updated_at` only when the entity has one. `default` on a
  primary key, a relation, a `create` input or a column `timestamps` stamps is a compile
  error.

The demo's `users` and `posts` creates are `#[crud]`'s again, on every edge they serve; the
author is a `Create` grant of its own, declared for a user subject only, so the admins'
org-wide grant on posts is `Update` and `Delete`. A table no transport exposes takes
`DEFAULT uuidv7()` on its key in its migration.

### A `#[crud]` write a unique key refuses names the input's field it holds

A unique violation on a `#[crud]` create or update answered a bare `409`: the operator's
line named the constraint, the client nothing it could put beside a field, so telling a
taken email from any other conflict meant leaving the generated create. The `409` now
carries the input's fields the violated key holds, under the `errors` member every edge
already ships, in a validation failure's shape — never the value, the index or the table:

```json
{ "type": "about:blank", "title": "Conflict", "status": 409, "code": "CONFLICT",
  "errors": { "email": [{ "code": "unique" }] } }
```

on HTTP, and under `extensions.errors` on GraphQL.

- **`SeaOrmDatabaseModule` reads the keys once, at boot**, from `pg_index`, for every
  `#[expose]` entity's table and, through `pg_inherits`, its partitions: a primary key, a
  unique constraint, a unique index, partial ones included, `INCLUDE` columns left out. The
  pool bounds the wait for a connection and Postgres the statement, and a net above both
  fails the boot on a server that stopped answering, naming the read. A conflict costs the
  request no statement, and a binary with neither `http` nor `graphql` sends no catalog
  query.
- **A key's columns the input does not carry are not named**: a tenant a grant pins beside
  the slug the client sent names `slug`; a key over none of them, over an expression
  (`lower(email)`), on another table, or created after the boot names nothing.
- **`CreateModel::field_for` and `UpdateModel::field_for` (new)** name the field carrying a
  column, by the name serde reads it under, which validation's `errors` keys share, and
  GraphQL answers [as its schema spells it](#a-graphql-error-names-a-field-as-the-schema-spells-it).
  `#[expose]` writes them; a hand-written input answers `None` until it does, and compiles
  unchanged.
- **`nest_rs::database::UnitOfWork` is `Any`**, so a driver reads what its binding learned
  at boot back off `current_unit_of_work`; every implementor is `'static`, so none changes.

A hand-written service's `ServiceError::from(DbErr)` keeps the bare `409`.

### A GraphQL error names a field as the schema spells it

`extensions.errors` named a two-word field by its Rust name, `org_id`, where the input a
client sent spells it `orgId`, so the client could not match the key to its field. A
validation failure, a `#[crud]` conflict and a `Problem`'s field map shared that convention.

Every field's answer now names each key as the schema does, at every depth and whatever
built the map, under the rename rule the input declares: an input with
`rename_fields = "snake_case"` keeps `org_id`. A key is matched against the names the schema's
inputs and arguments serve, by what is left once case and underscores are set aside, never
