re-cased, and the index is read by the first field error that needs it. HTTP, WebSocket and
MCP keep the name serde reads, which is what their clients send.

A key no input serves stays as it was said: `__all__`, a field `#[graphql(name = "…")]`
renamed to another word, which the registry cannot tell, and a variable pipe's, answered
before the schema runs. Inputs of one schema spelling one word differently are told apart
only when the key is itself one of those spellings.

**Breaking:** a client reading `extensions.errors.org_id` reads `orgId`.

### An insert that may meet a held key returns the row holding it: `Repo::insert_unscoped_or_existing`

To absorb a unique collision a concurrent caller may cause, a service ran the insert in an
`atomic` savepoint and matched `ServiceError::Conflict`, two blocks deep with `Ok::<_,
ServiceError>` hints in the demo's social login, because a violation aborts the enclosing
transaction. `Repo::insert_unscoped_or_existing(active, key)` (new), the on-conflict form
of `insert_unscoped` under the same bar, sends `INSERT … ON CONFLICT (key) DO NOTHING
RETURNING`, then reads the live row holding the key, so nothing fails and the unit carries
on with no savepoint:

```rust
let linked = Repo::<UserIdentities>::insert_unscoped_or_existing(
    link,
    [user_identity::Column::Provider, user_identity::Column::Subject],
)
.await?;
let fresh = matches!(linked, Insertion::New(_));
```

`nest_rs::seaorm::Insertion` (new) is `New` or `Existing`. The key needs a unique index
over exactly its columns; on an entity declared `soft_delete` a partial index over its
live rows is inferred too, and a tombstone is never returned. A key column the row leaves
unset, or its `before_save` unsets, is refused before anything is sent, and the row holding
the key is read by what that hook returned; a key only a tombstone holds meets its violation
inside a savepoint. The demo's `users` drops its nested blocks; no savepoint wraps every
write by default.

### A subscription reads a domain event off the bus: `EventBus::stream`

A GraphQL subscription over a domain event took a `tokio::broadcast` hub of the developer's,
a listener host feeding it and an events adapter imported by hand, with nothing reporting a
forgotten import. The service already emitted the fact, the bus held it for the commit and
the mask ran per item.

`EventBus::stream::<E>()` (new) is the bus's read side, the events of `E` emitted from now
on, each once its emitter's transaction committed and never one that rolled back:

```rust
#[subscription]
#[authorize(Read, PostEntity)]
async fn post_published(&self) -> impl Stream<Item = Post> {
    self.bus
        .stream::<PostPublishedEvent>()
        .map(|event| event.post)
}
```

- **A reader goes ahead of the listeners**, so a slow one holds nothing back, and an event
  no open stream or listener reads costs its emitter no held work.
- **A reader more than 64 events behind the newest loses the oldest**, and one `warn` on
  `nest_rs::events` says how many. The stream ends with the app.
- **It reaches this process**: a reader on another replica reads nothing, so telling every
  replica's takes a backend of its own.

The demo's `posts` drops `PostFeed`, `PostsListener`, `PostsEventsModule` and the
`async_graphql::Error` wrapper, and its `PostPublishedEvent` the two ids only that listener
read. The demo holds no `#[listeners]` host now: [Events](https://nestrs.dev/events/) keeps
teaching one, its counterpart the events suite.

### An HMAC secret is held to its hash's size, a key is judged once, and the config is `AuthnConfig`

`JwtService::new` is the one place a secret's length, an EdDSA key's PEM and the
pairing of two keys are judged — reached by a config-driven boot and by a
`JwtOptions` built in code alike — so `AuthnConfig::into_options` only decides
which key the settings make.

- **Breaking: `nest_rs::authn::JwtConfig` is `AuthnConfig`**, with the same
  fields. A `#[config]`'s type is named for the stem its namespace and its path
  read, and `nest-rs-authn`'s reads `<PREFIX>_AUTHN__*`; the variables are
  unchanged. A refusal naming the type says `an AuthnConfig or JwtOptions built in
  code`.
- **An HMAC secret is held to its algorithm's hash size** (RFC 7518 §3.2): 32 bytes
  for HS256, 48 for HS384, 64 for HS512. 6.1 held every algorithm to 32, so a
  `JwtOptions` choosing HS384 or HS512 over a shorter secret now fails to build.
  `<PREFIX>_AUTHN__SECRET` is HS256, as before.
- **An EdDSA pair is proved one pair** by signing and verifying a probe, an
  algorithm that does not fit its key is refused naming the fitting ones, and a
  secret set beside an EdDSA key fails the boot rather than one of them winning.
- Every refusal names the variable's two spellings, and the guard's logged errors
  render their chain.

### An identity store or provider that does not answer is a 503, with its Retry-After

**Breaking for code that matched `AuthError::Unavailable(detail)`.** An OAuth
provider that timed out, refused the connection or answered `5xx` failed as
`AuthError::Failed` — a `401` telling the person signing in that their sign-in was
wrong — and an unreachable identity store was a `500` blaming this server's code.
The caller did nothing wrong in either case (RFC 9110 §15.6.4).

- `AuthError::Unavailable { detail, retry_after }` renders `503`, with a
  `Retry-After` when the failing party gave one in delay-seconds and none invented
  when it did not; it is logged at `error` and is never a challenge.
- The OAuth client reports a transport failure, a bound it ran past, and a `5xx`
  or `429` answer from the provider — on the code exchange and on a read alike —
  as `Unavailable`, carrying the provider's own `Retry-After`. A provider that
  answers and refuses is still `Failed`, a `401`.
- `Denial::Unavailable` (`Denial::unavailable(retry_after_secs, reason)`) carries
  it through `AuthnGuard` to every edge: `503` and `Retry-After` on HTTP,
  `extensions.code = "UNAVAILABLE"` with `retryAfterSeconds` on GraphQL, `reason:
  "unavailable"` with `retryAfterSeconds` on MCP and WebSocket. A strategy that
  outlives `AUTHENTICATE_TIMEOUT` gets the same `503`.
- `TokenError::Server` — the identity store a grant needed did not answer — is a
  `503` with the opaque `server_error` code, where it was a `500`.

### A resource server verifies an external issuer's tokens against its JWK Set

A verifier held a shared secret or one static EdDSA public key, so an API whose
users sign in with better-auth, Keycloak, Auth0, Cognito, Entra ID, Google,
Firebase, Clerk or Zitadel could not accept their tokens: those issuers sign
with RSA, EC or EdDSA keys they publish, and rotate, in a JWK Set (RFC 7517).
`<PREFIX>_AUTHN__JWKS_URI` names it, and `JwtService` verifies RS256, RS384,
RS512, PS256, PS384, PS512, ES256, ES384 and EdDSA tokens against it.

- **What a key verifies is the verifier's, never the token's** (RFC 8725 §3.1):
  each key only an algorithm of its type, and only the `alg` it names when it
  names one. A key whose `use` is not `sig`, or whose `key_ops` leaves out
  `verify`, is ignored, and so is a symmetric one: no `HS*` or `none` token is
  accepted against a JWK Set, and such a token is refused before the set is
  fetched. The token's `kid` picks the key; a token naming none is checked only
  when exactly one key fits its algorithm.
- **`<PREFIX>_AUTHN__ALGORITHMS` narrows what is accepted**: every asymmetric
  algorithm a key fits by default, `EdDSA` or `RS256` once set, which binds a key
  published without `alg` to that one. Beside a secret or a PEM key it names the
  algorithm the key signs and verifies with, so HS384 and HS512 are settable from
  the environment too. An unknown name, `none` included, fails the boot.
- **Fetched on first use, never at boot**, so an issuer briefly down does not
  stop the API from starting. One fetch at a time, which tokens arriving
  meanwhile wait for; at most one per `JWKS_REFRESH_FLOOR` (30 seconds), whatever
  asks — a `kid` the set lacks earns one, so a rotation is picked up at once and
  a burst of forged `kid`s costs the issuer one request. The set is fresh for its
  answer's `Cache-Control: max-age`, net of `Age`, from the floor to an hour (ten
  minutes when unstated), and served stale while it refreshes.
- **An issuer that cannot be had is a `503`, never a `401`**: a set never
  fetched, a refresh failing while the key a token names is looked for, or a set
  an hour past its freshness answers `AuthError::Unavailable` with `Retry-After`,
  on a `#[public]` route too. A failed refresh otherwise keeps the last good
  set, said once at `warn` with its cause. A fetch connects within 3 seconds,
  ends within 5, follows no redirect, reads 128 KiB at most, and reports a
  document that is not a JWK Set without quoting it. The bounds are
  `nest_rs::authn::JWKS_*` constants.
- **The endpoint is `https` and verified** against the system's authorities, or
  those `<PREFIX>_AUTHN__TLS_CA_CERT` (`AuthnConfig::tls`, `AuthnTls`) names,
  as Redis and storage name theirs. A plain-`http` URI, a URI beside a secret or
  a PEM key, and an authority without a URI fail the boot.
- `AuthError::UnknownKey` (`reason = "unknown_key"`) tells a token signed by a
  key the set lacks from any other invalid token: a URI naming another tenant's
  set shows as that at once.
- A claim that does not decode is logged at `debug` without the value it held.
- **Breaking: `JwtService::verify` is `async`.** `verify_handshake` stays
  synchronous: a handshake verifies only under the static key that signed it, and
  a JWK Set-backed service refuses one as it refuses to sign.
  `JwtOptions` is `#[non_exhaustive]` and its `algorithm` is
  `algorithms: Vec<Algorithm>`, assigned after a constructor rather than written
  in a literal; `JwtKey` is `#[non_exhaustive]` and gains
  `Jwks { uri, tls }` (`JwtOptions::jwks(uri)`); `AuthnConfig` gains
  `jwks_uri`, `algorithms` and `tls`.

### A stored hash the hasher cannot run is reported, never read as a wrong password

**Fixed, and breaking for code matching `PasswordError`.** `verify_password`
answered `Ok(false)` for every verification error, so a stored PHC string that
parses but names an algorithm, a version or parameters Argon2id refuses — an
scrypt hash, a bogus memory cost — read as a wrong password: its owner was
locked out, and a login service logged the one reason that was false. Only a
password that does not match is `Ok(false)` now; anything else is
`Err(PasswordError::InvalidHash(cause))`, which a caller already logs as an
unusable record.

- `PasswordError::HashFailed` and `PasswordError::InvalidHash` carry the hasher's
  error as their `#[source]`, so `HashFailed` says whether the RNG or the
  allocator failed, and `InvalidHash` which part of the hash was wrong; the
  source is an `Arc<dyn Error + Send + Sync>`, never `password_hash`'s own type
  (*No third-party error or trait in the API of `authn`, `core` or `config`*).

### A token response says `Cache-Control: no-store`, and no `Debug` prints the token

RFC 6749 §5.1 requires `Cache-Control: no-store` and `Pragma: no-cache` on any
response carrying a token. A token endpoint's refusals (`TokenError`) sent both; its
success did not: handlers returned `Json<AccessTokenResponse>`, and a shared cache or
a browser's back-forward cache could keep the bearer token — the social login
callback is a browser `GET`.

- **`nest_rs::oauth::server::AccessTokenResponse` renders itself.** A handler returns
  it bare, and the answer is its JSON with both directives, its body documented by its
  type:

  ```rust
  #[post("/login")]
  #[public]
  async fn login(&self, Json(body): Json<LoginDto>) -> Result<AccessTokenResponse> {
      Ok(self.svc.grant_password(&body.email, &body.password).await?)
  }
  ```

  `Json<AccessTokenResponse>` still compiles — `Json<T>` is generic — and still sends
  neither directive; no lint can refuse the spelling, so the docs and the demo stop
  showing it. The type keeps `Serialize` and `JsonSchema` for an MCP tool or a
  GraphQL field.
- **`AccessTokenResponse`'s `Debug` prints `access_token: "<redacted>"`**, the
  spelling every redacting `Debug` in the framework uses; it derived `Debug` over the
  token, one `?field` away from a log line.
- **The OpenAPI component of `AccessTokenResponse` and `AccessTokenRequest` is one
  sentence**, §5.1's or §4.4.2's: schemars publishes a type's whole rustdoc as its
  description, so the example and the reasoning written for a Rust reader reached
  every app's document.
- The demo's `/token`, social callback and `/login` return the type bare, and the
  demo's `LoginDto`, OAuth callback query and `PresignedUrlDto` no longer derive
  `Debug` over the password, the authorization code and the presigned URL.
- `nestrs g auth`'s development token route returns it too, the token a `Secret`
  — `AccessTokenResponse::bearer(self.jwt.sign(&claims).opaque()?, self.jwt.ttl_secs())` —
  and `g auth` enables `oauth-server`; its `DevTokenResponseDto`, the `"Bearer"`
  literal and its two `#[response_header]` lines are gone.

### `nestrs g auth` draws its signing secrets at random and commits none

`nestrs g auth` appended `<PREFIX>_AUTHN__SECRET=dev-only-insecure-secret-change-me-32b`
to the committed `.env`, which the cascade reads in every environment, production
included. The value was the same in every project ever generated, and an HS256 key
mints tokens as well as verifying them: a deployment that forgot to override it
accepted tokens anyone could forge from a public repository.

- **Two independent 256-bit secrets, drawn from the OS's random number generator**,
  each 64 hexadecimal characters (RFC 7518 §3.2): one into `.env.local`, git-ignored,
  for your development runs; one into `.env.test`, committed and read only under
  `<PREFIX>_ENV=test`, so a teammate and CI run the suites with no copy step and a
  token signed with it passes nowhere else.
- **The committed `.env` holds no secret**: a comment says where the two live, how to
  draw another (`openssl rand -hex 32`) and that every deployed environment sets its
  own `<PREFIX>_AUTHN__SECRET` (or `_FILE`), or switches to EdDSA. `.env.example`
  gains the commented variable a teammate's `.env.local` needs.
- **No secret lands beside your own key material.** A secret file is left out when
  the runs that read it already read a `<PREFIX>_AUTHN__` setting — in that file, in
  `.env`, or in its environment's own files (`.env.development`,
  `.env.development.local`; `.env.test.local`) — since a secret beside an EdDSA key
  or a JWK Set URI fails the boot, and beside another secret shadows it. The run
  names the file that kept each one out.
- The run's printed diff shows an added secret as `<PREFIX>_AUTHN__SECRET=<redacted>`,
  never its value, so neither a terminal nor a CI log holds it.
- `nestrs g resource` and `nestrs g graphql` over a CRUD port, which scaffold the auth
  adapter on the way, write the same files and print the same lines about them.
- New dependency of the CLI: `getrandom` 0.4, rust-random's OS-RNG crate (already in
  the tree).

### The response mask decides only what the row it rebuilt shows

The mask rebuilds each row from the wire value, so a relation, a column `#[expose]` keeps off
the wire, and an exposed column the value's own type leaves out are not in it. It decided a rule
on them anyway: a relation as matched, a hidden column on the placeholder it had invented, a
left-out column on the `None` serde gave it. A rule the mask cannot decide is now decided by
where the row came from. A reply an `#[authorize]` operation read through `Repo` trusts that
read, whose SQL applied every rule; a pushed value — a GraphQL subscription item, a
`masked_reply` value — went through no read, and is refused.

- **Breaking: a pushed row that only a relation, a hidden column or a column it left out admits
  no longer ships.** A subscription under `can(Read, Post).when(|p| p.related(…))` streamed every
  post to every subscriber, a grant on a hidden `deleted` flag admitted every pushed row on its
  placeholder, and a denial on an exposed `archived_at` an item's type omitted never matched.
  Each refuses the item now, and a denial the mask cannot decide refuses it too. The first
  refusal per entity and action that undecidability alone made logs one `warn` on
  `nest_rs::authz`, `reason = "undecidable_on_push"`, naming the entity and the action, never a
  value. A rule on a column the item carries is decided as before: a feed scoped by an exposed
  `org_id` is unchanged.
- **Fixed: a reply under a relational denial keeps the rows its read admitted**, where every row
  used to be dropped; and a relational grant no longer widens the fields of a row a narrower
  grant admitted. A row only relational grants admit keeps the fields they all permit. This holds
  for the decorated masks and for `Ability::mask_many`, `mask` and `permitted_fields`, which
  judge rows read through `Repo` for the action they are asked about.
- **Fixed: the in-memory check reads a row as the SQL it renders would.** `eq` and `ne` against
  `None` render `IS NULL` and `IS NOT NULL`, which the row decides; any other comparison with
  `NULL` is SQL's `UNKNOWN`: `tag <> 'x'`, or `NOT (tag < 'm')`, on a `NULL` tag matched in
  memory where SQL's filter admits neither. A comparison across two value types (an `i32` column
  against an `i64` bound) was guessed unequal, which under `NOT` admitted; it is now the
  database's to decide.
- **Breaking: `Ability::can` refuses on a denial the model cannot decide.** `can` checks a model
  its caller holds, perhaps read for another action, so no read is known to have applied this
  action's denials: one reading a negated relation or comparing two value types now refuses, and
  a comparison with `NULL` no longer matches. A grant's relation is still taken to hold, as the
  query that read the model is trusted to have checked it; a negated one still grants nothing.
- **Fixed: a masked value ships only the keys it carried.** An exposed `Option` column the
  value's type left out came back as `null`, a value the sender never stated.
- **Breaking: `masked_reply` judges its value as a push, and a lone object it refuses comes back
  `null`**, the way a list drops the row. It masks for the ability ambient where it runs —
  inside a handler, the sender's — so a push to other connections (`WsServer::emit`,
  `broadcast`) is not masked per recipient.
- **Breaking: `Predicate::matches` answers whether SQL certainly admits the row** — `false` for
  a relation, which answered `true`, and for a `NULL` comparison under `NOT`.
- **`#[expose]` names the columns its rebuild invents and the key each exposed column
  serializes under**, from the walk over its fields, so no rule is decided on either; no
  hand-written impl remains (see
  [A `Model` serializes nowhere](#a-model-serializes-nowhere-expose-writes-the-masks-round-trip-and-a-hidden-column-it-cannot-rebuild-does-not-compile)).
- **Fixed: `#[expose]` keys a raw identifier the way serde writes it.** An exposed `r#type`
  column was strained off the wire as `"r#type"`, and a hidden one failed its rebuild.

### `Ability::can` grants nothing on a relation, and `Repo::can` decides it in SQL

`Ability::can` checks a model its caller holds, perhaps read for another action. It took a
grant's relation to hold, as if the read had checked it, which is true only of a read scoped to
the same action: `can(Update, &post)` on a post read for `Read` granted `Update` to a user a
relation-gated `Update` grant names nowhere, as soon as their `Read` rules let the post through.

- **Breaking: `Ability::can` grants nothing on a rule the model cannot show.** A grant resting
  on a relation (`p.related(…)`) grants nothing, as one comparing two value types only the
  database compares already did, and a denial resting on one still denies. A grant whose other
  branch the model decides is decided as before: `org_id = 7 OR related(…)` grants on a row of
  org 7. `can` stays synchronous, with no query. The masks
  (`mask`, `mask_many`, `permitted_fields`, and every decorated reply) are unchanged: they judge
  rows read through `Repo` for the action they are asked about.
- **New: `Repo::can(action, &model)`, the instance check SQL decides.** One query on the model's
  primary key under the action's own condition, on the ambient executor and ability like every
  `Repo` read: `true` when the row is one the caller may `action`, `false` when a rule refuses
  it, when it is gone, or when the scope denies every row. A route that bound its row through
  `Bind<Update, S>` was already authorized in SQL and needs neither check.

### A protected resource's URIs carry no userinfo, and plain `http` on loopback serves development and test alone

`OAuthResourceModule` accepted `https://user:secret@api.example.com/` as the
resource or an issuer, and its trailing-slash `warn` then logged the whole URI,
credential included. It also accepted `http://localhost` in every profile,
production too, and `https:///api`, a URI naming no host, on which the
challenge's metadata URL was then built.

- **Userinfo in `<PREFIX>_OAUTH__RESOURCE__RESOURCE`, in any
  `AUTHORIZATION_SERVERS` entry, or in `RESOURCE_DOCUMENTATION`,
  `RESOURCE_POLICY_URI` or `RESOURCE_TOS_URI` fails the boot** (RFC 9110
  §4.2.4: an `https` URI a sender generates carries none, and the document
  publishes every one of them), naming the setting and never the value.
- **A resource or issuer URI naming no host fails the boot** (RFC 9110
  §4.2.2), as one naming no scheme did.
- **Plain `http` on `localhost`, `127.0.0.1` or `[::1]` is accepted only in a
  declared development or test profile** (`<PREFIX>_ENV`); staging and
  production speak `https` on loopback too, and the refusal names the profile it
  read. An undeclared profile is production.
- **A line naming the resource shows it through `endpoint_shown`** — the
  trailing-slash `warn`, the boot's `debug` and the serialization `error` — so
  no userinfo, query or fragment reaches it. The audience check, which compares
  the two exact strings, names `<PREFIX>_AUTHN__AUDIENCE` and
  `<PREFIX>_OAUTH__RESOURCE__RESOURCE` in its `warn` (`audience_variable`,
  `resource_variable`) and echoes neither value.

### The OAuth client dials https alone, and reads a provider's answer to 64 KiB

- **The OAuth client's HTTP backend speaks `https` alone.** Every call it makes — the
  code exchange, `userinfo`, `fetch` — is refused by the client itself when its URL is
  plain `http`, before a connection opens: a provider package that builds a URL at run
  time cannot send a code, a client secret or a bearer token in clear even past the
  configuration's checks. The JWK Set client already did.
- **Added: `OAuthClient::ANSWER_MAX_BYTES` (64 KiB).** A provider answer longer than that
  — the token response, the userinfo document, a `fetch` — is refused on its
  `Content-Length`, or as soon as the streamed body passes the cap, and is never buffered
  whole. The call fails as `AuthError::Unavailable` (a `503`): "the OAuth provider's
  userinfo endpoint (https://…/userinfo) answered more than 65536 bytes, the most an
  OAuth answer is read to". A token response or a userinfo document is a few KiB, an
  id_token included.
- **A provider whose certificate the client `OAuthClientModule` builds refused, or which
  refused its handshake, says which setting fixes it**, as Redis and the JWK Set do:
  `<PREFIX>_OAUTH__CLIENT__TLS_CA_CERT` for an authority this app does not trust,
  `TLS_CERT` and `TLS_KEY` for a provider requiring a client certificate, `TOKEN_URL` or
  `USERINFO_URL` for a certificate naming another host. rustls's own reason follows in
  parentheses. A client built with `OAuthClient::new` over a config its caller composed —
  the GitHub and Google sign-in providers', whose URLs are constants and whose trust no
  variable sets — names none of these variables, which it never reads, and keeps
  rustls's reason alone, as `fetch` does on every client.

### The OAuth transaction cookie is the framework's

The cookie carrying an OAuth flow's signed transaction across the provider's redirect
had no name or attributes of the framework's: every app wrote it by hand and parsed it
back, and the docs and the demo disagreed on both. `OAuthClient` now owns it.

- **Breaking: the cookie is `__Host-oauth_tx`** (`OAuthClient::TRANSACTION_COOKIE`). The
  `__Host-` prefix makes a browser refuse it unless it is `Secure`, on `Path=/` and
  without `Domain`, so a sibling subdomain can neither plant nor overwrite it. A flow in
  flight across the deploy that renames it fails once, inside its ten-minute window, and
  the user starts again.
- **Added: `AuthorizationRedirect::set_cookie()`**, the whole `Set-Cookie` value:
  `__Host-oauth_tx=<token>; Path=/; Max-Age=600; HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Lax`.
  `SameSite=Lax` lets the provider's top-level redirect carry it back; `Max-Age` is the
  signed token's own lifetime, `OAuthClient::TRANSACTION_TTL_SECS`, whatever the
  `JwtService`'s `expires_in`.
- **Added: `OAuthClient::transaction_from(cookie_fields)`**, which reads the transaction
  back from every `Cookie` field of the callback (HTTP/2 may split them), by its exact
  name — never a prefix — and answers `None` when it is absent or empty.
- The OAuth2 page no longer says the cookie is scoped to the callback path, nor that the
  transaction inherits the `JwtService`'s expiry: neither was true. The threat model
  records why there is no CSRF middleware — no nestrs credential rides a cookie, and the
  one cookie is read on a `GET` callback held by `state`, PKCE and its provider binding —
  and that origin-based CSRF ships with the first credential that does.

### `nest_rs::authn::Algorithm` is nestrs's own enum, spelled as RFC 7518 spells `alg`

`nest_rs::authn::Algorithm` was the JWT library's enum, re-exported, and
`AuthnConfig::algorithms` and `JwtOptions::algorithms` held it, so each of that
library's majors — it shipped three in two years — would have been a nestrs major, and a
token-verification fix landing only in its next one would have waited for nestrs 8.

- **Breaking:** `Algorithm` is now nestrs's own `#[non_exhaustive]` enum, at the same
  path and with the same twelve variants — `HS256`, `HS384`, `HS512`, `RS256`, `RS384`,
  `RS512`, `PS256`, `PS384`, `PS512`, `ES256`, `ES384`, `EdDSA` — so
  `options.algorithms = vec![Algorithm::RS256]` keeps compiling. Code passing a
  `jsonwebtoken::Algorithm` it obtained elsewhere names `nest_rs::authn::Algorithm`.
  What the library's enum carried beyond its variants is gone: `Default` — an implicit
  `HS256` is no default a verifier should offer, so an algorithm is always named —
  `Serialize`/`Deserialize`, so a serde struct holding one stores its `as_str()` and
  reads it back with `parse()`, and `family()`.
- `Algorithm::as_str` and `Display` give the RFC's spelling; `FromStr` reads it
  case-sensitively and refuses `none` with the new `UnknownAlgorithmError`, which never
  repeats the name it was given. `<PREFIX>_AUTHN__ALGORITHMS` accepts and refuses exactly
  what it did, with the same sentences.

### A social provider is opened by importing its module

A social provider used to come up in every app that imported `SocialModule` and held
its credentials, and the social-login page said a third-party provider needed only its
dependency line. Neither held. A crate no code names is not linked at all, so its
registry entry never existed: an operator who added a provider crate and its
credentials got a `:provider` route answering `404`, and no boot line said why. And a
provider linked by any other code activated in every app importing `SocialModule`,
with no line in the composition root naming it.

- **Breaking: each provider ships a module, and an app imports it beside
  `SocialModule`** — `GithubSocialModule`, `GoogleSocialModule`:
  `#[module(imports = [SocialModule, GithubSocialModule, OAuthHttpModule])]`. A
  provider whose module the app does not import stays closed whatever its credentials
  say, and is said once at `debug` on `nest_rs::social` with `provider`. Every provider
  an app can log in with is now a line in its composition root.
- **Breaking: `SocialProviderEntry` gains `module: fn() -> TypeId`**, the provider's
  module; the registry keeps an entry only in an app whose composition reaches it. A
  provider of your own (or a provider package) adds `module.rs` —
  `#[module(imports = [SocialModule])] pub struct AcmeSocialModule;` — and
  `module: || std::any::TypeId::of::<AcmeSocialModule>()` in its entry. The app's
  import names the crate, which is what links it.
- The three outcomes per provider are unchanged for an imported one: complete
  credentials activate it, none leave it inert with one boot `warn`, a partial set
  fails the boot naming it.

### A scope refusal names the narrowest grant's scopes

When several grants would have allowed an operation, the scopes a refusal named were their
union, sending a client to ask for more than it needed.

- **The list is one grant's**: the action's own before `Action::Manage`'s, the first declared
  among them. `Ability::missing_scopes`, the HTTP challenge and GraphQL's `requiredScopes`
  name the same list on every edge.
- **An HTTP scope refusal names its scopes** in RFC 6750 §3's `scope` attribute
  (`Bearer error="insufficient_scope", scope="posts:write"`) without a protected resource
  server too; a scope outside RFC 6749 §3.3's grammar leaves the attribute out, and files a
  `warn` on `nest_rs::http` naming the rule's scopes.

### An authentication strategy reads nestrs's request

`Strategy::authenticate` took poem's request, so the authentication guard converted the
request its view holds into poem's and back on every authenticated request, and the
credential readers took poem's request to read one header.

- **A strategy takes `&mut nest_rs::http::Request`**, the request the edge holds for the
  unit — its route's parameters (`path_param`), its headers, its body, which a strategy
  verifying a signed webhook reads and sets back for the handler. The guard lends it
  without a conversion.
- **`bearer_token` and `basic_credentials` read a `HeaderMap`**:
  `bearer_token(req.headers())`.
- **Breaking:** a `Strategy` implemented on `poem::Request` no longer compiles;
  `bearer_token(req)` and `basic_credentials(req)` take `req.headers()`.

### A step-up challenge says only scopes a client can request

A protected resource server rebuilt a `403`'s `insufficient_scope` challenge from the scopes
the refusal required, joined unchecked: a scope holding a space read as two scopes, one
holding a quote ended the attribute early.

- **The challenge names the scopes only when each is an RFC 6749 §3.3 `scope-token`**, as
  the HTTP edge's own challenge does; otherwise it goes out with its `error` and
  `resource_metadata` and without `scope`, and one `warn` names the rule's scopes — on
  `nest_rs::http` where the edge stamped a guard's step-up, on `nest_rs::oauth::resource`
  for a `403` built by hand.
- **No line carries the challenge**: the two `error` lines for a challenge that is not a
  header value — unreachable after the boot's checks — logged it, and its pointer holds
  the resource's URI with its query.

### An authenticated client names its client id, and is its own audit actor

`nest_rs::oauth::server::AuthenticatedClient`, the principal the token endpoint's client
authentication attaches, carried the registered client's payload and scopes but not which
client it was. It gains `client_id` (RFC 6749 §2.2), filled by `authenticate_against_registry`,
and `actor_id()` returns it: a client acting on its own behalf is its own subject (RFC 9068
§2.2), so the audit of what it does — the token request's lines, `current_actor_id()` — names
it, as the authentication pages already ask of every principal. The demo's refused-scope line
names the client through that `actor_id` in place of its organisation, restating it in no field
of its own.

**Breaking:** `AuthenticatedClient::actor_id()` returns `Some(client_id)` where it returned
`None`; an MCP endpoint a client-credentials guard gates now opens the client a session bound
to its id.

### Authentication and service failures carry their cause

**A masking failure no longer logs the row's values.** When a relation loader
`#[expose]` emits could not reconcile a loaded row into its wire type,
`ServiceError::Masking` held serde_json's rendered error — `invalid type: string
"…", expected u64` — and HTTP filed it whole at `error` on `nest_rs::orm`, quoting the
column's value. It now holds the failure as its `nest_rs::core::DecodeError` report, its
source: the kind found, the type expected and the position, never the value.

**`ServiceError::Internal` keeps what failed.** An internal failure that is no database
error is built by `ServiceError::internal(detail)` or, with its cause,
`ServiceError::internal_because(detail, source)`; the detail, then the cause, are the
error's `std::error::Error::source` chain, shared so the error stays `Clone` for
dataloaders. Every
edge files the failure's line with `error_message` of the error — the detail, then the
cause's chain, a decode failure or a driver's error in it said without its value — where
the detail had to carry a `format!`ted cause, and where GraphQL, WebSocket and MCP filed
the bare `"internal error"` and lost the detail altogether. An atomic block's failure
that is no database refusal keeps its `AtomicError` as that cause.

**The wire is the edge's constant.** `ServiceError::Internal` still displays `"internal
error"`; the detail and the cause sit beneath it, in its source, so a feature error that
renders a wrapped `ServiceError` by its `Display` answers no detail. No edge answers
anything that cause carries: wrapped in `anyhow` or an error of your own, an
`internal_because` whose cause is a decode failure answers GraphQL and WebSocket clients
`"internal error"` (`INTERNAL`), never `400 INVALID_ARGUMENT` with the upstream's decode
report. HTTP answers every opaque variant (`Db`, `Masking`, `Internal`) with the
constant `"internal error"`, as GraphQL, WebSocket and MCP already did — `Db` and
`Masking` answered `"database error"` and `"response masking failed"` there.

**Breaking:** `ServiceError::Internal(String)` and `ServiceError::Masking(String)` are
`#[non_exhaustive]` struct variants, built only through their constructors and matched as
`Internal { .. }` / `Masking { .. }`; `Masking` is built by the relation loaders alone.
`ServiceError::internal` takes `impl Into<Cow<'static, str>>`: a `String` or a `&'static
str` passes as before, a borrowed string that is not `'static` is passed owned. The detail
is read through `std::error::Error::source`, no longer off the variant. An HTTP `500` from
a `ServiceError` is the one opaque problem document, `code: "INTERNAL"` and no `detail`
(*Every opaque server failure on HTTP is one problem document*), and its line carries the
chain — `database error: …` for `Db`, `response masking failed: …` for `Masking`, and
`internal error: <detail>: <cause>` for `Internal` — filed by the edge (*A `5xx` a
`ResponseError` answers is filed once, by the HTTP edge*).

**`AuthError::Failed` and `AuthError::Unavailable` keep what failed.** A strategy, a social
provider or an OAuth client builds them with `AuthError::failed(detail)` and
`AuthError::unavailable(detail, retry_after)` or, with the error that caused them,
`AuthError::failed_because(detail, source)` and
`AuthError::unavailable_because(detail, retry_after, source)`. The detail, then the cause's
chain, are the error's `std::error::Error::source`, and the line the guard or the error's own
response files carries them whole as its `error` field, through `error_message` — where
`Failed(String)` and `Unavailable { detail, .. }` held a cause flattened into the detail
with `format!`, and the line filed a `detail` field. The framework's own sites keep their
cause: the JWK Set's and the OAuth client's failed calls (reqwest's error without its URL; a
timeout says the bound it hit, and nothing beneath it), an answer that does not decode — a
JWK Set, a profile, a token answer — as its `DecodeError`, never a byte of the answer, in the
line or in the error's `Debug`, a provider's refusal of the exchange, a key that is no EdDSA
key in PEM form, a configuration refusal and its `ConfigError`.

**The wire is the constant.** `AuthError::Failed` displays `"authentication failed"` and
`AuthError::Unavailable` `"authentication unavailable"`, the bodies they answer; the detail
and the cause sit beneath, so an app error that renders a wrapped `AuthError` by its
`Display` answers no detail, and no edge answers anything the cause carries — a decode
failure beneath it is never read as the client's bad input.

**A provider's zero wait is answered as one second.** An identity provider or a JWK Set
endpoint that answered `Retry-After: 0` was relayed as `Retry-After: 0`, which RFC 9110
§10.2.3 reads as "retry immediately"; `AuthError::retry_after_secs` now rounds up and holds
the floor of one second every `Retry-After` the framework sends holds.

**Breaking:** `AuthError::Failed(String)` and `AuthError::Unavailable { detail, retry_after }`
are `#[non_exhaustive]` struct variants, built only through their four constructors and
matched as `Failed { .. }` and `Unavailable { retry_after, .. }`. Their `Display` is the
constant alone; the detail is read as `err.source()`'s `Display`, and the whole chain with
`nest_rs::core::error_message(&err)`. A `From<CredentialError>` folds into `Failed` with the
detail `"invalid credentials"` and no cause. The authentication lines on `nest_rs::authn`
file `error` (the chain) where they filed `detail`.

### A refused OAuth client is challenged with the scheme it used

**`TokenError::InvalidClient` says how the client tried to authenticate**, as a
`nest_rs::oauth::server::TokenEndpointAuthMethod` — RFC 7591's
`token_endpoint_auth_method` values `None`, `ClientSecretPost` and `ClientSecretBasic` — and
answers by it. A client refused over HTTP Basic gets the `401` RFC 6749 §5.2 requires, with
`WWW-Authenticate: Basic realm="authorization server"`, the scheme it used; the `401` went out
with no challenge at all, which RFC 9110 §15.5.2 forbids. A client that sent its credentials
in the body, or none, gets §5.2's `400 invalid_client`, no challenge: a `Basic` challenge to a
request that never used HTTP authentication would have a browser carrying a public client
prompt for a password. No `401` the token endpoint answers leaves without a challenge.

**The demo's password login answers `invalid_grant`.** A wrong email or password at the
`auth` app's `/login` answered `401 invalid_client`, a client-authentication failure no client
had attempted; it now answers RFC 6749 §5.2's `400 invalid_grant`, as the password page
already prescribed for rejected resource-owner credentials.

**Breaking:** `TokenError::InvalidClient` is a tuple variant carrying the
`TokenEndpointAuthMethod` the request attempted, and a `ClientSecretPost` or `None` refusal
is a `400` where every `invalid_client` was a `401`.

### A token endpoint refuses its client and its request as RFC 6749 §5.2 answers

A token endpoint authenticating its clients through `AuthnGuard<S>` refused them as a resource
server refuses a caller: a wrong secret over HTTP Basic got `401`, an `application/problem+json`
document with `code` `UNAUTHENTICATED` and `WWW-Authenticate: Bearer error="invalid_token"`; a
client with no `Authorization` header got the same `401` with a bare `Bearer` challenge; and in an
app that is a resource server too, the RFC 9728 pointer joined it. The token request's own refusals
were the edge's problem documents: no `grant_type` a `400` `INVALID_ARGUMENT`, a JSON body a `415`.
A conforming client parses §5.2's JSON alone, so `nest-rs-oauth-client` — the framework's own —
reported each as a body that does not parse.

- **`ClientAuthenticationGuard<S>`** (`nest_rs::oauth::server`) authenticates the client at a token
  endpoint (§2.3), bound with `#[use_guards]` over the app's own authn `Strategy`, which it runs as
  `AuthnGuard` does — the same bound, net and contained panic, the client attached as the request's
  principal and named as its `actor_id`. It refuses through `TokenError`'s one rendering: a client
  refused over the `Authorization` header, whatever its scheme, `401` with
  `WWW-Authenticate: Basic realm="authorization server"` and `{"error":"invalid_client"}`; one that
  sent no header `400 invalid_client`, no challenge; a strategy that could not evaluate it `503
  server_error`, with the store's `Retry-After`. It reads no body — `client_secret_post` is not
  served — refuses on `#[public]` routes too, answers an unknown client and a wrong secret the
  same bytes, and files one `warn` per refusal on `nest_rs::oauth::server`,
  `client authentication failed`, with the `method` and the `reason`, never the client.
- **Breaking: `AccessTokenRequest` is a handler parameter, and the request's only reader**, reading
  §4.4.2's form and documented as one. It no longer derives `Deserialize`, so
  `Form<AccessTokenRequest>` and `Json<AccessTokenRequest>`, which skipped every refusal below, no
  longer compile. A request that does not parse is `400 invalid_request` with an
  `error_description` saying where, never the value: ``form field `grant_type`: missing`` for no
  `grant_type` or `grant_type=`,
  ``form field `grant_type`: duplicate`` for two, ``the request's content type is not
  `application/x-www-form-urlencoded` `` for a JSON body, and a client credential in the body
  beside the `Authorization` header — more than one mechanism. `scope=` is no scope. A body past
  the cap keeps the transport's `413`.
- **The umbrella's `oauth-server` feature enables `authn`**: the guard runs an authn `Strategy`,
  which the app writes with `AuthError` and `basic_credentials`.
- **The HTTP guard stage serves the error a refusing guard writes into its view**
  (`HttpView::respond`) when it is a `4xx` or `5xx`, and renders the denial otherwise; a guard
  that writes nothing renders as before.
- **Not covered**, each a limit: the client authentication methods `none`, `client_secret_jwt`,
  `private_key_jwt`, `tls_client_auth` and `self_signed_tls_client_auth`; RFC 7009 revocation and
  RFC 7662 introspection endpoints; `/login`-style JSON sign-ins, which are no §4.3 token request
  and keep the edge's problem documents; and the OpenAPI document, which lists the guard's
  `400`, `401` and `503` as problem documents, the `401` with a `Bearer` challenge.

### An OAuth client's redirect is `https` in a deployment

**Plain `http` on loopback is a development convenience now, and only that.**
`OAuthClient::new`, `OAuthClientModule` and `nest_rs::oauth::client::refuse_plaintext_redirect`
accepted an `http` redirect on `localhost`, `127.0.0.1` or `[::1]` in every profile, staging
and production included, where the authorization code then returned in clear. They accept it
in a declared development or test profile alone (`<PREFIX>_ENV`, read by the process, as the
protected-resource identity already does); a staging or production process — and one that
declares no profile, which runs as production — refuses it at boot, naming the variable, the
RFC and the profile. The demo's social providers carry their loopback redirect in
`.env.development` and `.env.test`, no longer in `.env`.

**A client you compose names no variable it never read.** `OAuthClient::new` over an
`OAuthClientConfig` built in code — a social provider's — refuses an invalid field, a
plaintext endpoint and unusable TLS material by the field (`OAuthClientConfig::token_url`,
the authorities, certificate or key of `OAuthClientConfig::tls`, the validation under
`'OAuthClientConfig'`), where it named
`<PREFIX>_OAUTH__CLIENT__*`, which such a deployment never set; `OAuthClientModule`'s client,
read under those variables, names them as before.

**Breaking:** a deployment running a staging or production profile — or none — with an
`http://localhost…` redirect for the OAuth client or a social provider fails its boot.

### Every token `JwtService` signs carries `iat`, `exp` and a `jti` of its own

RFC 9068 §2.2 requires `iat`, `exp` and `jti` in a JWT access token, and `JwtService::sign`
stamped none of them: an app had to remember each in its claims — asking the signer for its own
lifetime (`JwtService::expiry()`) only to hand it back — or mint access tokens a conformant
verifier, a revocation list or a replay check reads nothing from. `sign` and `sign_for` now
write them beside the configured `aud` and `iss`: `iat` the time the token is signed, `exp`
the service's `expires_in` after it, `jti` a random v4 UUID drawn for that token alone. A
claim the claims type carries itself is kept as written — a test's expired `exp`, say; one it
serializes as `null` counts as absent, for the five alike. `sign_handshake` stamps the same but
`exp`, which a handshake's claims name with `expiry_in(secs)` — the OAuth transaction's ten
minutes: claims carrying none are refused, never given the access-token lifetime. What only
the app can name stays in an access token's claims: `sub` — the user, or the client acting on
its own behalf — and `client_id`; the demo's and `nestrs g auth`'s `Claims` carry no `exp`.

**Breaking:** `JwtService::expiry()` is gone: drop the `exp` field from a claims type, or name
another lifetime with `expiry_in(secs)`.

### A scope is declared once, as a `Scope` a rule requires and a resource server advertises

The scopes an app's rules gate on were plain strings typed four times — the rules' constants,
`<PREFIX>_OAUTH__RESOURCE__SCOPES_SUPPORTED`, each registered client, the tests — and
`requires_scope` took any string, so a rule could require a scope nothing declared, and a drift
between the rules and the advertised list surfaced only as a `warn` on a refused request.

- **`nest_rs::guards::Scope`** (new) is RFC 6749 §3.3's scope token as a type, declared as a
  constant — `pub const POSTS_READ: Scope = Scope::new("posts:read");` — and refused outside the
  grammar, at compile time in a `const`. The umbrella's `authz` feature brings `guards`.
- **Breaking: `RuleSpec::requires_scope` takes a `Scope`**, so a rule cannot require one no
  declaration holds.
- **`OAuthResourceModule::for_root` takes the declared set** — `[Scope; N]`, the config, or
  both as `([Scope; N], config)`: the metadata document and the `WWW-Authenticate` challenge
  advertise it when the deployment names no `SCOPES_SUPPORTED`, which, set, replaces the list.
  A second declared set fails the boot naming both imports. `OAuthResourceSetup` is a setup
  of its own rather than a `ConfigSetup` alias.
- A registered client's scopes stay strings — deployment data — and a product checks them
  against its set in its own module-init hook, as the demo does. The demo's `.env` and chart
  no longer repeat the list.

### A capability no table holds is a subject, gated by `#[authorize]` on every edge

A rule named an entity alone — `AbilityBuilder::can` took an `EntityTrait`, and
`Action` is closed — so a decision about a capability rather than rows had no
posture: "may transcode" was written as `Manage` on an unrelated entity and checked by
a hand-written guard re-implementing the class gate, a grep for `#[authorize]` missed
it, and the `#[sse]` refusal told developers to write exactly that guard.

`#[subject]` (new, `nest_rs::authz::subject`) declares a unit struct a subject no
table holds — `#[subject] pub struct Transcoding;`. `AbilityBuilder::can` takes any
`Subject`, so `ab.can(Action::Manage, Transcoding).requires_scope(AUDIO_TRANSCODE)`
grants it whole; `when`, `fields` and `cannot` stay an entity's, by type, since a
capability has no rows to narrow and a grant not written is its denial.
`#[authorize(Manage, Transcoding)]` is a posture on every edge — a route, an `#[sse]`
stream, a GraphQL field, a WebSocket message, an MCP tool: the same class gate, with
its refusals and RFC 6750's `insufficient_scope` challenge, and no mask, since the
subject has no model; an HTTP route it gates still withholds the ability from its
reads. `#[authorize]` naming an entity on an `#[sse]` route is now refused by the
subject's type, its message naming this posture. A posture naming a type that is
neither an entity nor a `#[subject]` is refused with a message naming both. A route's
guard stage documents what its class gates refuse, so a route gated this way keeps its
`403` in OpenAPI.

`RouteResponseShaper::SHAPES` (new, provided, `true`) lets a type that only declares
`GATE` leave the route unshaped. `nestrs g graphql` and `g mcp` over a port with no
entity write the port's `#[subject]` in `<port>/subject.rs` and gate the generated
operation on it, where they wrote `#[public]`; `subject` joins the reserved vocabulary,
so `nestrs g` refuses a module of that name, as it refuses `event`. Their next step
spells the grant by the path `authz/ability.rs` resolves, a `#[crud]` resolver's for
every action at once. `nestrs g entity` refuses a port whose `subject.rs` declares the
entity's name, which the subject's re-export would silently shadow, and lists the
re-gating. `nestrs lint` reaches a folder's singular, so `cities/subject.rs` holding
`City` passes. Nothing breaks: an entity's rules and postures read as before.

### A token endpoint reads its grant as a `GrantType`, grants scope strictly, and holds its client registry

An authorization server built on `nest-rs-oauth-server` wrote by hand what RFC 6749 fixes:
it compared `grant_type` to a literal, narrowed the requested scope to the client's
registration and logged each refusal itself, kept the registered clients under a config of
its own, and authenticated their HTTP Basic pair in a strategy it wrote. The framework's own
guard example taught the string compare.

- **Breaking: `AccessTokenRequest::grant_type` is a `GrantType`** (new), read off the IANA
  OAuth grant-type registry (`GrantType::ClientCredentials`, §4.4). A request naming a grant
  the endpoint does not serve — `password`, a URN, `Client_Credentials` — is §5.2's
  `400 unsupported_grant_type`, answered by the request before the handler runs, so a
  handler compares nothing. The `String` had been kept so the issuer could say that
  refusal; the request has been its own extractor since this release, so it says it itself.
- **Breaking: `AccessTokenRequest::scope` is §3.3's list**, a `Vec<String>` read off the
  space-delimited parameter.
- **`AuthenticatedClient::scope_for(&request.scope)`** (new) grants §3.3 strictly: none
  asked is the client's whole registration, a scope it is not registered for refuses the
  request whole as `invalid_scope` — never a quietly narrower token — and a repeat is
  granted once. It, `audience_for` and the request's `unsupported_grant_type` each file one
  `warn`, `token request refused` on `nest_rs::oauth::server`, its `reason` the wire code,
  the client the line's `actor_id`, never what it asked for.
- **`OAuthServerConfig<P>`** (new) is the authorization server's registry under
  `<PREFIX>_OAUTH__SERVER__CLIENTS` (a JSON array, or `_FILE`), registered by
  `OAuthServerModule::<P>`, generic over the payload `P` each `RegisteredClient<P>` carries
  for the app. The boot refuses a registry that serves no token or the wrong one — none, a
  client with no resource, a duplicate or empty id, an empty secret, which an empty Basic
  password would match — naming each client by its id, never its secret.
  **`ClientSecretBasicStrategy<P>`** (new) checks §2.3.1's Basic pair against it, in constant
  time, behind **`ClientGuard<P>`** (new), `ClientAuthenticationGuard<ClientSecretBasicStrategy<P>>`:
  the module provides both, so a token endpoint binds `#[use_guards(ClientGuard<P>)]` and its
  app lists neither — one listing either registers it twice, which fails the boot. What a
  product checks over the registry beyond it (a scope outside its rules, a client id its
  subject scheme reads as a user's, RFC 9068 §5) is its own module-init hook, refusing in the
  registry's words (`OAuthServerConfig::<P>::NAMESPACE`).
- The demo's token endpoint is now `#[use_guards(ClientGuard<ClientPayload>)]` and two calls,
  `scope_for` and `audience_for`; its `scope.rs`, its two strategies and its client-guard
  alias are gone.
- **Not covered:** the grants the endpoint does not serve (`authorization_code` at this
  server, `refresh_token`, the device and JWT-bearer URNs), which `GrantType` gains as each
  is built; a partial scope grant, which is policy — a product wanting one reads
  `request.scope` itself.

### The social callback is the framework's strategy, and carries the resource the sign-in asked for

Every app on `nest-rs-social` parsed RFC 6749 §4.1.2's authorization response by hand in a
strategy of its own — the `{provider}` path segment, `code`, `state`, the transaction
cookie — and none read §4.1.2.1's `error`, so a provider's `access_denied` was reported as
"missing code". The sign-in minted for the first resource the app serves, since nothing
carried the one the authorize leg asked for across the provider.

- **`SocialStrategy<S>`** (new, `nest_rs::social`) is the redirect URI's strategy, bound as
  `AuthnGuard<SocialStrategy<S>>`: it reads the provider the route names, matches the response
  to the transaction cookie, exchanges the code, fetches the profile, and hands it to the
  product's **`SocialSignIn`** (new) — `async fn sign_in(&self, profile, resource) ->
  Result<Self::Principal, AuthError>` — which says who that profile is here. A forged,
  expired or foreign transaction, or another `state`, is a `401` before anything else in the
  response is read; a matched §4.1.2.1 refusal is a `401` (`server_error` and
  `temporarily_unavailable` a `503`), the edge's `guard denied the request` on
  `nest_rs::authn` naming its registered code in its `error` chain.
- **Breaking: `OAuthClient::callback`** (new) reads the redirect URI's query — §4.1.2's
  response or §4.1.2.1's error response — against its transaction, before anything else in
  it: verified, bound to the provider, `state` compared in constant time; then the provider's
  refusal, or the code, into an `AuthorizationResponse`. A parameter sent twice (§3.1) is
  refused naming it. **`OAuthClient::exchange` trades only that response**:
  `exchange(&jwt, provider, transaction, state, code)` is
  `callback(&jwt, provider, transaction, query)?` then `exchange(response)`.
  `SocialProvider::exchange` takes the `AuthorizationResponse` too, so an override never
  checks `state` again.
- **Breaking: `OAuthClient::authorize` and `SocialProvider::authorize` take
  `resource: Option<&str>`**, the RFC 8707 resource the sign-in asks a token for, signed into
  the transaction; `AuthorizationResponse::resource` reads it back at the callback. The
  demo's `/social/{provider}/authorize?resource=` checks it before the redirect and mints for
  it at the callback.
- **`TokenSet::new(access_token)`** (new), with `with_id_token` and `with_refresh_token`: a
  provider overriding `exchange` outside the client crate can build its answer.
- **`SocialRegistry::authorize(provider, &jwt, resource)`** (new) answers the authorization
  request (§4.1.1) as a **`SocialRedirect`** (new) a handler returns: a `302` to the provider
  with the `__Host-oauth_tx` transaction cookie and `Cache-Control: no-store`, so no cache
  keeps the response that set it, or, for a provider no imported module registers, the `404`
  problem a route that does not exist answers. The route documents its `302` and `Location`
  by the type. Which resources a sign-in may name stays the product's check before it asks;
  the demo's handler is one line.
- The GitHub and Google providers' profile answers type the email, name and login
  `Secret<String>`, so a `Debug` of one prints none of them.

### A development-only route is one guard: `DevelopmentOnlyGuard`

`nestrs g auth` wrote, in every app that took it, a guard refusing outside `development` and
`test` and a provider of its own refusing the boot — about seventy lines keyed only on the
declared environment, which every consumer would write the same way.
`nest_rs::authn::DevelopmentOnlyGuard` (new) admits a request only in a process declaring
`development` or `test` (`<PREFIX>_ENV`), answers `403` otherwise — the edge's `guard denied
the request` on `nest_rs::authn`, `reason = "outside_development"` — and, listed in a module's
`providers`, refuses at its module-init hook the boot of any other process —
`DevelopmentOnlyGuard guards a route only development and test serve, and <PREFIX>_ENV
declares production`, naming the environment declared, never the raw value. An undeclared
environment is production, so a process nobody told refuses. `nestrs g auth`'s
`authn/http/` is now the controller and its module: the controller binds the guard and
builds the app's own `Claims`, and `audit.rs` and `guard.rs` are no longer written.

### A stored password hash comes back a `Secret`

**Breaking: `hash_password` returns `Result<Secret<String>, PasswordError>`**: the PHC string
a password column keeps, which a `Debug` or a log line prints as redacted. Store it as is,
and read it with `.expose_secret()` for `verify_password`. `verify_password`'s,
`authenticate_against_registry`'s and `ThrottlerGuard::new`'s parameters stay borrowed
`&str`: a value in flight, read once where it arrives.

### A claims type derives its principal: `#[derive(PrincipalIdentity)]`

A claims type wrote two impls by hand in every app: an empty `impl AmbientValue`, so the
authentication guard could attach it, and an `impl PrincipalIdentity` whose methods each
returned one field — the same dozen lines in the demo, in what `nestrs g auth` writes and on
every page that showed a principal.

`#[derive(PrincipalIdentity)]` (new, `nest_rs::authn::PrincipalIdentity`, beside the trait;
the new `nest-rs-authn-macros` holds it) writes both. `#[principal(actor)]` marks the subject
`actor_id` returns, and is required; `#[principal(tenant)]` and `#[principal(scopes)]` mark
what `tenant_id` and `scopes` return, and a role left unmarked answers the trait's default.
An actor or a tenant is read by its type, never its spelling: the text its value prints, or
`None` from an `Option` holding none. The scopes are a list: an `Option` is refused at
compile time, since it would read a token sent without its `scope` claim as one no scoped rule
narrows. The `AmbientValue` impl keeps the default carry, so the claims never ride a job's
envelope. Claims marking no actor, a role marked twice or unknown, a generic struct, an enum
and a union are compile errors naming what the derive takes. The demo's `Claims` and its
sign-in `Caller`, the claims `nestrs g auth` writes, and the authentication pages use it.
Nothing breaks: hand-written impls compile as before, and a principal generic over a type, or
narrowing what it carries, keeps them.

### `OAuthResourceModule` imports the verifier it binds

`OAuthResourceModule` binds the token verifier `AuthnModule` builds to the
resource it advertises, yet asked the app to import `AuthnModule` beside it,
refusing the boot with "import `nest_rs::authn::AuthnModule` alongside it". It
now imports it itself, bare. A resource server with no key to verify with is
refused at boot by the verifier, naming `<PREFIX>_AUTHN__SECRET`,
`<PREFIX>_AUTHN__PUBLIC_KEY` and `<PREFIX>_AUTHN__JWKS_URI` — never one
accepting nothing, nor anything. A root still importing `AuthnModule` beside it
boots as before, and `AuthnModule::for_root(cfg)` still pins a configuration in
code.

### Every edge files a unit cancelled and a unit that panicked

A unit stopped before it settled, or one that unwound, filed nothing at several
edges. Every edge now files both:

- **An MCP tool that panics answers its client a JSON-RPC internal error**, where
  6.1 left the client waiting out its own timeout, and files `mcp.operation`
  `panic`, the panic's text logged at `error` on `nest_rs::mcp`.
- **A WebSocket handler that panics answers an error frame and its socket goes
  on**, where 6.1 lost the socket. A connect hook that panics closes the socket
  `1011`, and a graphql-ws subscription that panics does the same; a disconnect
  hook that panics files `panic` and the close completes.
- **A GraphQL field dropped with its request files `graphql.operation`
  `cancelled`**; a field that panics files `panic`, and a sibling field torn down
  by that panic files `cancelled`, since one panic is one `panic` line.
- **An event listener dropped with its emitter files `events.dispatch`
  `cancelled`.**
- A query that reads only `ok` lines of `graphql.subscription` now also meets the
  sockets a shutdown ended, as `cancelled`.

Each edge's own suite drives a unit to both ends and asserts the line it files.

### A unit's span says how it ended, and a request cut before it answers keeps its route

Every operation span now carries OpenTelemetry's `error.type` and an `Error` status
(`otel.status_code`) when its unit did not end `ok`, with the same word as the
line's `outcome`: `cancelled`, `panic` or `error`. This covers HTTP, GraphQL
operations and subscriptions, MCP, queue attempts, WebSocket messages and hooks,
events and schedule ticks. An HTTP response answered 5xx carries its status code
there instead, and a 4xx carries nothing, as the HTTP conventions ask. Before, a
backend showed a request cut at the shutdown window, or a job that panicked, as a
span that succeeded. A request dropped before it answered also exported as an
anonymous `http.request` with no `http.route`. It is now named `{method} {route}`
for the template the router matched, because every endpoint the framework mounts
notes it (`nest_rs::http::matched` wraps a self-mount's endpoint the same way). A
dashboard keyed on span status starts counting these as failures.

What a client's close files is now stated. An ordinary close (FIN) is a legal
HTTP/1.1 half-close, so hyper runs the handler to its end and the line carries the
answer's `status`. Only a reset — an HTTP/2 stream cancel, `SO_LINGER` of zero, a
crashed client — drops the request and files `outcome="cancelled"`.

### A unit of work is a typed `Unit`, and its span and its line read every slot off it

**Breaking for code reading a unit's constant as a string, or calling
`operation_span!`.** A unit was a `&str` constant beside a separate kind and
target, so a span could be opened under one name and its line filed under
another. Each `<edge>::unit::*`
constant — `nest_rs_http::unit::REQUEST` and its siblings — is now a
`nest_rs::core::operation_log::Unit`, carrying its name, its target and its
`Kind`; `.name()` gives the string.

- **A unit is declared with `nest_rs_core::unit!`** —
  `unit!("http.request", target: crate::target::HTTP, kind: Server)` — evaluated
  in a `const`, so a name off the `<edge>.<unit>` grammar, an edge outside
  `operation_log::EDGES`, a target other than `nest_rs::<edge>` and a declaring
  crate other than `nest-rs-<edge>` are compile errors.
- **`operation_span!` takes the unit as a path** —
  `operation_span!(nest_rs_http::unit::REQUEST, &correlation, …)` — and reads its
  target, name and span kind off it; a literal does not match, and a unit another
  crate declared is refused at compile time.
  `operation_log::kind::{SERVER, CONSUMER, INTERNAL}` is the enum
  `operation_log::Kind`, declared with the unit and never at the span site.
- **`operation_line!` files a unit's operation line** — its target, name,
  message, `outcome` and `duration_ms`, written once — and records the outcome on
  the unit's span, so the line and the span cannot say two things. Every edge
  files its line through it. **Field order changes:** `outcome` and
  `duration_ms` now come before the edge's own fields.
- **`contained_panic!` logs a contained panic at `error` under `panic::FIELD`.**
  Every seam that contains one — lifecycle hooks, the event bus, a GraphQL
  subscription, an MCP operation, a queue attempt, a Redis delivery, the
  scheduler, a WebSocket gateway, SeaORM's after-commit work — logs through it, so
  the field is spelled once. Those lines now put the message first, then
  `panic`, then the seam's fields.

### A logged error names every cause beneath it, and a value in a text line cannot forge another

- **`nest_rs::core::error_message(&e)`** renders an error and every cause beneath it
  as one sentence, saying a cause its parent already inlined once. Every event the
  framework files with an `error` field goes through it — the kernel, the guards,
  the ORM, authz, health, OpenTelemetry and every edge — so a wrapper such as
  `the queue backend failed` no longer logs without the cause that explains it. Use
  it for your own `error` fields: `error = %error_message(&e)`.
- **Breaking: `.opaque()` takes an owned error that converts into
  `Box<dyn Error + Send + Sync>`**, on HTTP, GraphQL, WebSockets and MCP alike,
  where it took anything `Display`, so the line it files carries the whole chain.
  An `anyhow::Error` is boxed with every link kept (`nest_rs::core::boxed_error`),
  so a decode failure inside it is said without its value. `anyhow`, a `DbErr`, a
  `String` and every `thiserror` type qualify. A type that is only `Display`
  implements `std::error::Error` first, and a `&str` borrowed from a local becomes
  a `String`. `JobError::retry` / `abort`, `WsReply::from_handler_error` and
  `OccurrenceLockError::new` take the same.
- **A payload that does not decode is reported without its value, at every site
  the framework decodes at and in every text it renders one in.** serde's
  sentences quote what they refuse: a value, a variant, a key. The queue's
  dead-letter line and record, the WebSocket payload error, the GraphQL
  variable-pipe error, the OAuth client's read failures and authz's mask failures
  all carried it. Each now says where the payload failed, what kind of value was
  found and what type was expected, through `nest_rs::core::DecodeError`. It keeps
  a missing or duplicate field's name, answers an unknown field with the fields
  its type expects (the key is the sender's, and is dropped), and bounds what it
  keeps. `error_message` reads every link of a chain that way, and also serde's own
  quoting sentences in any text, so a cause behind `#[error(transparent)]`, a `{0}`
  wrapper or anyhow's box is covered too, and every answer says where, in the
  type's own names (*A value that does not decode is answered naming where it
  failed*, below). **Breaking:** `WsReply::payload_error` takes any
  `Into<DecodeError>` — a `serde_json::Error` among them — and
  `MaskReplyError::Irreconcilable` carries a `DecodeError`.
- **An HTTP request that does not decode is refused without the value it sent.**
  A `Json`, `Form` or `Query` that fails answers `400` with a
  `detail` naming where and what kind, e.g. `parse error: invalid type: an
  integer, expected a string at line 1 column 25`. poem's own sentence quoted the
  value, and a `400` is logged by proxies and kept by caches. The same holds for a
  handler's own decode returned as an `Err`, on every rendering path, with or
  without a wrap registered. `Header<T>` answers a type's own refusal in its own
  sentence naming the header. **Breaking:** `ProblemDetails::from_error(status,
  title, &err)` takes the error rather than any `Display`, and says its decode
  failure without the value; a sentence of your own is `.with_detail(…)`.
- **MCP arguments that do not decode are refused without the value.**
  `nest_rs::mcp::Parameters` is the framework's own, with rmcp's shape and schema.
  A call whose arguments do not decode is still a tool error the model can correct
  (`failed to deserialize parameters: invalid type: a string, expected f64`), but
  the value no longer lands in the model's transcript. **Breaking:** `Parameters`
  is no longer rmcp's type.
- **Every GraphQL error message says a decode failure without its value**, on
  `POST` and over the socket alike. That covers a resolver's `?` on serde or on an
  anyhow chain holding its error, and async-graphql's own `Json<T>` scalar
  coercing an argument. A JSON body that is not a GraphQL request — `"variables"`
  as a string — was answered `400` with nothing in it; following GraphQL-over-HTTP
  it is now answered `400` with one `errors` entry saying where and of what kind
  the decode failed, never the value sent, and no `data` member.
- **A WebSocket handler's error frame says a decode failure without its value**,
  in every tier (`Send + Sync`, local, `Display`-only), and a `WsError` returned
  through `anyhow` is sent whole instead of losing its details.
- **A Redis dead-letter record is the line's rendering**: every cause, each decode
  failure said without its value. It was the error's source verbatim.
- **Breaking: `panic_message` returns a `String`**, with any decode failure in the
  payload said without its value: `.unwrap()` on a failed decode formats serde's
  error into the panic.
- **The text console escapes every field value it writes**, the message included —
  controls, bidirectional overrides and invisible formatting characters as Rust's
  debug escapes — so a client-chosen string carrying a line break can never forge a
  second log line (CWE-117). JSON was escaped by construction and is unchanged.
- **Breaking: a trace is `minted`, `continued` or `inherited`.**
  `Correlation::mint()` becomes `Correlation::minted(actor)` — `None` wherever a
  guard fills the actor, `Some` where it cannot be re-derived: a queue job whose
  envelope names who pushed it but carries no usable `traceparent` now starts a
  trace for that actor rather than running anonymous.
- **`CapturedEvent` carries `trace_id`, `span_id` and `actor_id`**, read off the
  correlation the event was filed under, so a suite asserts the trace a line
  belongs to without parsing it.

### A value that does not decode is answered naming where it failed

A value its type refused was answered without saying where. `GET /audio/download?file=not-a-uuid`
answered `a value its type does not accept`, naming neither the parameter nor what its type
expected; a form missing a field answered ``url decode: missing field `grant_type` ``; a JSON body
named a line and a column, never the field; a WebSocket message that is no envelope answered
`invalid envelope`, and a WebSocket payload, an MCP tool's arguments, a job's payload and a
structured config value said what failed and nothing of where.

- **Where is said in the names the type declares, never in the payload's.** A field by the name
  its struct gives it, a variant by its enum's, an element by its index — `author.name`,
  `items[2]`, `payment.Card.number`. **A map's entry is said as `[…]`** (`limits[…]`): its key is
  the sender's, and is never quoted, nor is a key the struct does not declare.
- **Each edge says it in its own words**: ``query parameter `file`: …``, ``form field
  `grant_type`: missing``, ``JSON body at `author.name`: invalid type: an integer, expected a
  string``; on WebSockets ``invalid envelope at `event`: missing`` and ``invalid payload for
  `send` at `text`: missing``; on MCP ``failed to
  deserialize parameters: argument `latitude`: invalid type: a string, expected f64``; a job's
  dead letter ``failed to deserialize job for queue `audio` at `file`: …``; a GraphQL request body
  ``the body is not a GraphQL request: the JSON body at `variables`: …``; a structured config
  value ``invalid value for <PREFIX>_OAUTH__SERVER__CLIENTS at `[0].scopes`: …``. A payload refused as a whole says so
  (`query string: …`, `form: …`, `JSON body: …`).
- **A field missing or given twice is placed at the field**: ``form field `grant_type`:
  missing``, ``JSON body at `amount`: duplicate``, where serde said ``missing field `grant_type` ``
  with no place.
- **A type's refusal in its own words says what that type expected**: ``query parameter `file`:
  a value its type does not accept, expected a formatted UUID string``, `… expected u32`, `… expected
  a boolean` — the description of the type that refused, as serde's `expected …` tails already
  were, and none when the type refused a value it had read (a `try_from` newtype, inside an
  `Option` or not); the value is still never said.
- **A body that is not JSON keeps its line and column**, and a located failure drops them: the
  path says where.
- **poem's `url decode:` prefix leaves a form's refusal**: the sentence says `form field`.
- **A boot refusal no longer quotes what a deployment wrote**: the `.env` cascade's refusal of
  `<PREFIX>_ENV` says the process `carries another value` or `does not carry it`, its refusal of
  `NESTRS_ENV_PREFIX` names the prefix already resolved and not the file's, and a Redis URL naming
  a parameter its topology does not take names the parameter's place in the query (`parameter 2
  of its query`), never its text.
- **What it costs**: the place is found by running a failed decode once more under a tracker, so a
  payload decoded from its text — a query, a form, a JSON body, a GraphQL request body, a config
  value, a WebSocket envelope — pays nothing when it decodes. A WebSocket message's `data`, an MCP
  call's arguments and a job's payload are already a `serde_json::Value`, which must outlive its
  first decode to be read again: they are now decoded by reference instead of moved, so every
  string they hold is copied on every decode — about 90 ns on an eight-string payload, a fifth of
  its parse and decode (release build).
- **Added**: `DecodeError::located`, which places a report by running the failed decode again
  over the same payload, and `DecodeError::path`; `WsReply::payload_error` takes any
  `Into<DecodeError>`, a located report or the `serde_json::Error` it took before.
- **Not covered**, each a limit: a multipart GraphQL request's `operations` and `map` parts are
  said without a place (async-graphql hands back its error, not the part); a queue record that is
  no JSON at all keeps its line and column; a graphql-ws frame that is no protocol message still
  closes the socket with `not a graphql-ws message` (async-graphql's message type is an internally
  tagged enum, which the tracker cannot see into, and a Close frame's reason holds 123 bytes); what serde decodes from a buffer of its own is placed
  at the struct or enum that holds it — an internally tagged or untagged enum's content, a
  `#[serde(flatten)]` field — and a struct with a flattened field says its own fields as entries,
  `[…]`, since serde then reads it as a map; GraphQL's arguments and variables are named by
  async-graphql, as before.

### A GraphQL root field files `graphql.field`; a field resolver files nothing

Every dispatched GraphQL field filed a `graphql.operation` line and opened a span
of that name, `#[field_resolver]` calls included — one per parent row. A query
listing 1,000 rows with one resolved field filed 1,001 lines, a volume the client
chose, and the name `graphql.operation` was about to name the document's
operation as well.

- **A root field is the unit `graphql.field`** — a `#[query]`, `#[mutation]` or
  `#[entity]` body, on `nest_rs::graphql::unit::FIELD` (new). Its line and span
  keep their fields (`role`, `operation`, `graphql.field.name`).
- **A `#[field_resolver]` call is no unit**: it runs its guards and its body
  under the request's trace context, with no line, span or correlation of its
  own. A failure reaches the client as a field error, as before.
- **`nest_rs::graphql::unit::OPERATION` names the document's operation** —
  its `query`, `mutation` or `subscription` — and nothing files it yet.
  `graphql.subscription` is unchanged.

**Breaking:** a log filter or a dashboard keyed on `graphql.operation` finds
nothing; the per-field line is `graphql.field`, and field resolvers file none.

### A unit's exported span carries the ids its log lines carry

`nest-rs-opentelemetry` adopts the framework's trace and span ids so that an
exported trace and the log lines name one unit of work by one value. Since
`tracing-opentelemetry` 0.32 builds the SDK's span only when its context is
first read, the SDK's id generator ran after the framework had stopped
publishing its ids, and every exported span — every edge's — took random ones,
which no log line carried. The span is now linked while the ids are published,
so the export and the lines agree again; nothing to change in your code.

### Metrics are recorded through `Metrics`, with instruments named by the OpenTelemetry Metrics API

`OpenTelemetryModule` provided `OpenTelemetryMeter`, the `opentelemetry` crate's `Meter`
behind a `Deref`, and apps imported `opentelemetry::KeyValue` themselves. That put a
`0.x` crate in every app recording a metric: each of its releases was a nestrs major, and
an app on another `opentelemetry` version than the framework's did not compile.

- **Breaking: `OpenTelemetryMeter` is removed.** `OpenTelemetryModule` provides
  `Arc<Metrics>` instead, under the same `otlp` feature, over the same global meter,
  imported by the module whose provider injects them.
  `metrics.counter(name)`, `up_down_counter`, `histogram` and `gauge` hand back the
  instrument of that name — `Counter` (`add(u64, …)`), `UpDownCounter` (`add(i64, …)`),
  `Histogram` (`record(f64, …)`), `Gauge` (`record(f64, …)`) — made on the first call and
  the same one after it: asking at the call site costs a lookup, never a rebuild, and
  each handle is a cheap `Clone` a service may keep in a field.
- A measurement is recorded under `Attribute`s — `Attribute::new("source", "api")`, a
  value a `bool`, an `i64`, an `f64` or a string (`AttributeValue`); up to eight cost no
  allocation of their own.
- What an instrument declares — its unit, its description, a histogram's bucket
  boundaries — is declared once, by the `_with` call that makes it:
  `metrics.histogram_with("posts.publish.latency", Describe::default().with_unit("ms"),
  &[5.0, 25.0, 100.0])`, and `counter_with`, `up_down_counter_with`, `gauge_with` alike.
- No public signature of `nest_rs::opentelemetry` names an `opentelemetry` type any more,
  so the framework takes OpenTelemetry's releases in 7.x minors.

### A binary with no `App` installs the console its log settings describe

The binaries that run no `App` — the CLI's generated `migrate`, the demo's `migrate` and
`seed` — read `LogSettings` and refused a bad value, then wrote their own subscriber:
text whatever `<PREFIX>_LOG_FORMAT` said, and never the source location. A setting read
and ignored.

- **`nest_rs::core::logging::install_console(&settings)`** installs the kernel's console
  subscriber — the one an `App` installs when `main` installed none — from a
  `LogSettings`: its filter, its format, its source location, through the kernel's
  console writer. It answers `false` when the process already has a global subscriber,
  which it keeps. `log` records reach the console too; a `log` logger set before keeps
  them, and the console, installed all the same, says so once at `warn`.
- **Fixed:** `OpenTelemetry::init` returned `OpenTelemetryError::Init` when a `log` logger
  was already set, though it had installed its subscriber. It now succeeds, the `log`
  records kept by that logger and said once at `warn`; `Init` means a global subscriber
  was already installed, and is kept.
- `nestrs new` and `nestrs g migration` generate `migrate` calling
  `install_console(&LogSettings::from_env()?)`; the generated `migrations` crate and the
  workspace manifest no longer depend on `tracing-subscriber`.

### The access line names the route, and the boot lines the path served
