- **`http.request` carries `route`, the template the router matched** (`/posts/{id}`), in
  place of `path`, the path the client sent: a line no longer repeats a client's value, and
  groups as a dashboard does. A request no route matched carries no `route`.
- **Under a global prefix, `mounted route`, `mounted endpoint` and the unguarded-route
  warning name the path served** (`/api/posts`), not the path declared.
- **No HTTP line names the path a client sent**: the versioning selector's two `debug`
  refusals name the strategy alone, as the access line records the `404`.
- **Breaking:** a log query filtering `http.request` on `path` filters on `route`.

### A request the router's fallback answers is named for its mount

A request the static files answered was named for its method alone — `GET` — on its span's
`otel.name` and its access line, with no `http.route`.

- **It is named for the mount's catch-all**: `GET /{*path}` at the root, `GET
  /assets/{*path}` under `/assets`, on the span's `http.route` and the access line's
  `route`, so a tracing backend groups them as one.

### A log setting that does not parse stops the boot, naming the variable and not its value

The three console variables were read twice — by the kernel's fallback subscriber and
again by OpenTelemetry's — and neither refused alike: a mistyped
`<PREFIX>_LOG_FORMAT=jsno` or `<PREFIX>_LOG_SOURCE_LOCATION=ture` quietly took the
profile's default under the kernel, and was printed raw on stderr by OpenTelemetry, which
then kept the default too. A filter that did not parse was refused by both, but in a
sentence that quoted the directive.

- **`nest_rs::core::logging::LogSettings` is the one reader** of `RUST_LOG`,
  `<PREFIX>_LOG_FORMAT` and `<PREFIX>_LOG_SOURCE_LOCATION`, for every
  subscriber the framework ships and any a crate adds: `LogSettings::from_env()` reads
  and checks all three, `LogSettings::check_filter(directive, setting)` checks a
  directive set in code. Unset or blank is the default (`info`, the declared profile's
  format, `false`); a value that does not parse is a `LogSettingsError` naming the
  variable — `Filter`, `Format` or `SourceLocation` — whatever the profile. A value
  that is not UTF-8 is set, and refused the same way: 6.x read it as unset.
- **A filter's refusal says what is wrong by kind** — a malformed directive, a level
  that is not one of `off`, `error`, `warn`, `info`, `debug`, `trace`, or a field
  filter that does not parse — and never repeats `tracing_subscriber`'s sentence, which
  quotes the level or the field pattern it could not read.
- **Breaking: `OpenTelemetryError::InvalidLogFilter { value, source }` is replaced by
  `OpenTelemetryError::LogSettings(LogSettingsError)`**, which names the variable, or
  `OpenTelemetryConfig::log_filter` for a filter pinned in code, and
  carries no `tracing_subscriber` type. `OpenTelemetryConfig::from_env` now returns
  `Result<Self, OpenTelemetryError>` (it returned `ConfigError`, which cannot carry a
  console setting); its own variables' refusals are the same `ConfigError`, inside
  `OpenTelemetryError::Config`.
- **Breaking: `LogFormat::resolve` is removed**: `LogSettings::from_env()` reads the
  variable, and `LogFormat::parse` stays.
- OpenTelemetry reads the three from the process environment, as the kernel always
  did — the `.env` cascade reaches it there, published by `#[nest_rs::main]`. A binary
  that runs neither `#[nest_rs::main]` nor `load_cascade` no longer has its `.env`'s
  console settings read by `OpenTelemetryConfig::from_env` alone.

### The console log never stalls the app: a stalled stdout drops lines and says how many

Both subscribers the framework installs wrote each line to stdout on the thread that
filed it. When stdout stopped taking lines — a full pipe, a container log driver that
stalled — every tokio worker that logged blocked inside `write(2)`, and the app stopped
answering, its way down included, since the signal path, the hooks and the drain all log.

- **Under `#[nest_rs::main]` the console is a queue**: one thread, `nestrs-log`, writes
  stdout from a queue of 16 384 lines (`nest_rs::core::logging::CONSOLE_QUEUE_LINES`),
  and a line that finds the queue full is dropped and counted, never waited for. Once a
  second, while lines were dropped, one `warn` on `nest_rs::app`, `log lines dropped
  while the console was stalled`, says how many since the process started (`dropped`);
  if it is dropped too, the next one carries the larger count. A line stdout refuses
  outright — its reader gone — is dropped and counted too, and the first refusal is
  said on stderr.
- **Every exit `#[nest_rs::main]` controls waits until each line queued before it is
  written**, within `nest_rs::core::logging::CONSOLE_FLUSH_TIMEOUT` (one second):
  `main` returning, a panic leaving it, a signal exiting at once. Past the bound the
  rest is lost, and one line on stderr says so. It is the last step of the way down,
  which now sums to 29.5 seconds by default: keep a pod's grace period 9.5 seconds
  above the longest window you raise, where it was 8.5.
- **Outside `#[nest_rs::main]`** — a test binary, a process that runs an `App` without
  it — stdout is written directly, as before, so a failing test keeps its last lines.
- `nest_rs::core::logging::ConsoleWriter` is that writer, shared by the kernel's
  fallback subscriber and OpenTelemetry's console layer; a subscriber of your own writes
  through it with `.with_writer(ConsoleWriter::get())`. A line `println!` writes is not
  queued, so it can appear before log lines filed earlier.
- **It writes in batches**: a line that wakes the writer waits up to 2 ms for the lines
  filed after it, and they leave in one write. A busy app pays one wake and one
  `write(2)` a batch, where it paid a `write(2)` a line on the thread that filed it; a
  console line reaches stdout up to 2 ms after it is filed.
- The queue and its thread are the kernel's own: no dependency is added.
- The `migrate` binary `nestrs new` scaffolds reads its filter through `LogSettings` and
  writes through `ConsoleWriter`, where it took a filter that did not parse for `info`;
  a project scaffolded before can do the same in its own `migrate` and `seed`.

### The log filter is narrowed by six library clamps, and a directive naming one opens it

Every subscriber the framework ships narrows the filter it is given by `rmcp=off`,
`h2=error`, `rustls=error`, `sqlx_postgres=error`, `tungstenite=info` and
`opentelemetry-otlp=info`: an event is written when the filter writes it and the clamps let
it through, so the process's level stays the filter's. `EnvFilter` keeps the later of two
directives for one target, and the filter's directives come after the clamps, so a
directive naming the library — or one of its modules — replaces its clamp:
`RUST_LOG=info,rmcp=debug` writes rmcp's debug lines, and with them what they quote.
The framework's targets and the app's are never clamped. `sqlx::query`, the statement log
`<PREFIX>_SEAORM__SQLX_LOGGING` turns on, is not clamped: it writes each statement's SQL
with its values bound apart. As with any `EnvFilter` directive, `rustls=error` also covers
rustls-platform-verifier (`rustls_platform_verifier=warn` opens it alone), and a span
directive opens what runs inside the span it names, a clamped library's events included.

### The request lines' fields, and what the request span names

- The `http.request` line has no `user_agent`, and its `method` reads `_OTHER` for a method
  outside the conventions' known set that no route registers (a mount answering `PROPFIND`
  keeps `PROPFIND`). The request span's `http.request.method` reads `_OTHER`
  there too, with `http.request.method_original` beside it, and its `otel.name` reads `HTTP`
  (`HTTP /posts/{id}`), as the conventions spell it.
- The browser-origin refusal line files `reason`, `scheme`, `trusted_proxy` and `hint` in place
  of `origin` and `expected`. `OriginRefused` keeps no string of the request: `origin()` and
  `expected()` are replaced by `reason()` (an `OriginRefusedReason`), `scheme()` (an
  `OriginScheme`), `trusted_proxy()` and `hint()`.
- The `ws.message` line's `event` and the span's `ws.event` read `_OTHER` for an event no
  handler serves. `WsReply::unknown()` takes no argument, and its frame's text is `unknown
  event`.
- The `graphql.operation` line has no `operation_name`.
- The `mcp.operation` line's `method` reads `_OTHER` for a custom method, and its `operation`
  names a tool only by the name a host declares (`_OTHER` otherwise), and no prompt, resource
  or task.
- `nest_rs::core::operation_log::OTHER` (`"_OTHER"`) is the word every edge gives a name the
  server does not declare.
- An MCP refusal's `message` reads by kind: `prompt not found` for an undeclared prompt, the
  JSON-RPC code's words (`Method not found`, `Header mismatch`, …) for a JSON-RPC error rmcp
  answers outside `200`, and the status's problem document for a transport refusal rmcp wrote
  as text. A GraphQL request whose `operationName` names no operation reads `Unknown operation
  named by operationName`.

### A prompt reaches the host serving it, and the request span records `url.scheme`

- On a path several `#[mcp]` hosts share, a `prompts/get` whose host answered an error in a
  code that also means *not found* (`-32002`, `-32601`) was answered `prompt not found`,
  that host's error filed nowhere and its prompt missing from `available_prompts`. A prompt
  is now answered by the host declaring it, whatever it answers; a host writing rmcp's
  handler by hand is asked through its own `prompts/list`; a prompt no host declares is
  refused with every host's prompts listed, as `prompts/list` lists them.
- Two hosts declaring one prompt name on a path now fail the boot, naming both, as two
  declaring one tool name do: the second was unreachable.
- On a shared path a `tasks/get`, `tasks/update` or `tasks/cancel` for a task the second
  host held was answered by the first host's `unknown task`; the route now passes a host
  that does not hold the task.
- On a shared path, a request every host refused — a `resources/read`, a
  `resources/subscribe`, a task, a custom method, a `logging/setLevel` — could be answered
  `Method not found` by a host that does not serve the method at all, over the refusal of
  the host that does, and a `completion/complete` could be answered so over that host's
  empty completion, depending on the order the hosts registered in. The first host serving
  the method now answers.
- A `#[tools]` host serving prompts alone is no longer reported at boot as a host whose
  tools the duplicate check cannot read.
- The HTTP request span records `url.scheme`, which OpenTelemetry's HTTP server conventions
  require: `https` or `http`, the client's own when a proxy listed in
  `<PREFIX>_HTTP__TRUSTED_PROXIES` forwards it (`Forwarded` `proto=`, else
  `X-Forwarded-Proto`), otherwise the scheme the request arrived over.

### The console no longer formats span fields it never prints

A console line carries the trace context and no span state, yet `tracing-subscriber`'s `fmt`
layer, which the console writes through, formatted every field of every span as the span opened
and again at each `record`. Under JSON, the release default, each `record` parsed the span's
whole stored object back and wrote it anew. An HTTP request records four fields after it opens,
so each of its attributes was formatted five times per request, into a string nothing read — and
an attribute 7.0 adds to it, `url.scheme`, cost a measurable share of throughput on its own.

The console's layer is now told of events alone, under every subscriber the framework installs:
the kernel's fallback, `install_console`, and `OpenTelemetry::init`. A
span's fields still reach the OTLP export, and never a formatter, so an attribute on any edge's
span — the request's, a WebSocket message's, an MCP operation's, a job's, a tick's — costs the
console nothing. The lines are unchanged, byte for byte.

### HTTP's "a unit settled without a response" line names its site

The `error` line on `nest_rs::http` a request files when its pipeline settled and no layer left a
response — a wiring defect the client reads as the opaque `500` — carried no field, where every
line carries a constant message and at least one field. It now names the site, as GraphQL's,
MCP's and WebSocket's lines of the same kind name theirs:

```text frame="terminal"
ERROR nest_rs::http: a unit settled without a response; answered 500 site="/{id}/publish" trace_id=… span_id=…
```

`site` is the address the site declares, never the path as addressed: a route's template as its
method attribute writes it, without its controller's path or the app's prefix
(`#[post("/{id}/publish")]` under `#[controller(path = "/posts")]` is `/{id}/publish`), a
self-mount's path, `*` for what the router answers itself. The request's operation line, under
the same `trace_id`, names the whole template as `route`.

### Every refused unit says so on its line, once

What the edges told an operator about a refused unit disagreed with what the unit did. A
`tools/call` answered with its error in band rolled its unit of work back, and its line said
`outcome="ok"`; a WebSocket frame that is no envelope was answered and filed nothing at all; a
WebSocket payload that does not decode filed a `warn` beside its line, two lines at the level kept
for denials for an error a client can make on every frame.

- **An MCP tool's error in band is a failed operation on its line and its span.** Arguments that do
  not decode, which rmcp answers as an `isError: true` tool result, and a tool's own
  `CallToolResult::error` file `mcp.operation … outcome="error"`, and the span records
  `error.type="tool_error"`, the class OpenTelemetry's MCP semantic conventions give such a result,
  with an `Error` status. The line reads the verdict that settles the unit, so a unit that rolls
  back never files `ok`. The client's answer is unchanged.
- **A WebSocket frame the gateway cannot read is a message on its line.** A text frame that is no
  envelope — no `event`, a non-string `event`, a non-object, a cut frame — a binary frame, and a
  frame past the in-band size cap each file `ws.message event="_OTHER" outcome="error"` with its
  span, the line naming only an event a handler declares. The error frame the client reads is
  unchanged.
- **What the WebSocket gateway refuses of what a client sent is reported at `debug`**: a payload
  that does not deserialize (still saying where, never the value), a pipe's rejection, an unknown
  event and a frame that is no envelope, on `nest_rs::ws`, beside the message's line — as HTTP's
  `400` and `404`, MCP's argument refusal and GraphQL's refusal of a body that is no GraphQL
  request already said one client error once. A guard's denial stays at `warn`, and a handler's own
  answered `Err` too.
- **A page cursor no page issued is refused naming where it was read**, as every other value its
  type refuses: ``query parameter `after`: not a cursor this endpoint issued`` on a `#[crud]` list
  and from `PageParams::cursor()`, `Invalid value for argument "after", not a cursor this endpoint
  issued` on a GraphQL list or relation, where `after is not a cursor this endpoint issued` was
  answered on both. `PageCursor`'s `FromStr` says the reason alone, `not a cursor this endpoint
  issued`, for its reader to say where.
- **Not covered**, each a limit: an MCP operation answered with a JSON-RPC error records
  `error.type="error"`, where the MCP conventions ask the error code's string; a WebSocket
  handler's own `Err` answered as a `4xx` is still filed at `warn` with its chain, where HTTP,
  GraphQL and MCP file their line alone.

### A client's error is one line on every edge, and the MCP span says its code

- **The WebSocket envelope is a JSON object, and only an object.** `WsEnvelope` derived serde's
  `Deserialize`, which reads a struct from an array by position too: the frame `["message", {…}]`
  was served as a `message`, and `["<word>"]` answered as an unknown event named `<word>`. A frame
  that is not an object — an array, a string, a number, `null` — is now refused unread as no
  envelope, ``invalid envelope: invalid type: a sequence, expected an object with `event` and
  `data` `` under `INVALID_ARGUMENT`, its `ws.message` line `event="_OTHER" outcome="error"`; the
  refusal no longer names a Rust type (``expected struct WsEnvelope``). An object keeps saying
  where it failed: ``invalid envelope at `event`: missing``, ``… at `event`: duplicate``.
  `serde_json::from_str::<WsEnvelope>` refuses an array the same way.
- **A WebSocket handler's refusal files its message's line alone.** A handler's own `Err` answered
  as a refusal — its `WsError`, a `4xx` `Problem`, a pipe's rejection, a decode failure, bare or in
  its chain — filed a `warn` with its chain on `nest_rs::ws` (`subscribe_message handler returned
  Err`), while the same failure answered `4xx` by an exception filter filed nothing. It is filed by
  the status its frame answers, whoever answered it, as on HTTP, GraphQL and MCP: the `ws.message`
  line `outcome="error"`, nothing beside it. The chain behind a `503` stays at `warn`, now worded
  `websocket message unavailable` as the other edges word theirs; the chain a `5xx` withholds
  stays at `error` (`websocket message failed`); a bare `Problem` withholds nothing and files
  nothing beside its line.
- **An MCP operation answered with a JSON-RPC error records its code on its span**, as
  OpenTelemetry's MCP semantic conventions ask: `rpc.response.status_code` with the code's string
  (`"-32603"`), and `error.type` with the same string and an `Error` status when the code is the
  server's failure, where `error.type="error"` was recorded for every one. A code the conventions
  read as the caller's own — `-32700`, `-32600` (a refusal among them), `-32601`, `-32602` (a tool
  or prompt no host declares among them), `-32002` — fails nothing on the server's span, as an
  HTTP `4xx` does not: it records `rpc.response.status_code` alone. A tool's error in band keeps
  `tool_error`; a tool that panics keeps `error.type="panic"` and records the `-32603` its client
  is answered with. The line keeps `outcome="error"` for every one: the unit rolled back.
- **Not covered**, each upstream's: async-graphql's `Request` derives `Deserialize`, so a GraphQL
  body `["{ x }"]` is read as the request `{"query":"{ x }"}`; its `ClientMessage`, every
  graphql-ws frame the subscription socket reads, takes an array's first element for its `type`,
  so `["subscribe", "1", {"query": "…"}]` is read as a `subscribe` and `["complete", "1"]` as a
  `complete`; rmcp's `JsonRpcResponse` and `JsonRpcError`, what a client answers to a request the
  server sent, read an array by position too. `#[expose(input(...))]`'s `Create<E>` and
  `Update<E>` keep serde's derive, as a developer's own DTO does.

### The app is named after its binary, and telemetry reads the OpenTelemetry specification's variables

Three surfaces name the app, and each got its name and version a different way:
OpenAPI invented them (the demo's 7.0.0 api published `"nestrs API"` `0.1.0`), MCP
refused a name with no version and had the app write `env!("CARGO_PKG_VERSION")`,
and OpenTelemetry took the service name as a literal (`OpenTelemetry::init("auth")`)
with no version at all. Telemetry also read its own `<PREFIX>_OPENTELEMETRY__*`
copies of what the OpenTelemetry specification names: `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT`
never took effect, and the `OTEL_SERVICE_NAME` the SDK had read was overwritten, so
an operator — or the OpenTelemetry Operator — setting the standard variables got no
export and a name the code chose.

- **`#[nest_rs::main]` captures the binary's `AppIdentity`** — its package's name,
  version and description, read off the manifest where the macro expands — before
  the runtime exists: `nest_rs::core::AppIdentity::current()`. The test harness
  publishes the tested package's the same way, from what `cargo test` and `cargo
  nextest` hand a test process, so a suite boots its app under the name and
  version its binary reports.
- **OpenAPI's `info.title`, `info.version` and `info.description` default to it**;
  `<PREFIX>_OPENAPI__TITLE`, `VERSION` and `DESCRIPTION`, or a pinned field, still
  override. The demo's committed `openapi.json` says `"api"` `7.0.0`.
- **MCP's `serverInfo` name, version and description default to it**, under the
  app's `McpModule::for_root(McpIdentity { … })` and a host's `#[mcp(name, title)]`;
  its `name` and `version` fields stay the override, and an app adding its
  branding sets `title` and `instructions` in a literal over `Default`. An endpoint
  nobody named reports the binary's — a host naming itself in its own `get_info`
  keeps its name — and the boot's refusal of a name with no version behind it,
  or of any other field with no name behind it, and its `warn` on rmcp's default
  name, are left to a process no `#[nest_rs::main]` started.
- **OpenTelemetry's `service.name` and `service.version` default to it**, and the
  exporter reads the specification's variables: the SDK reads
  `OTEL_SERVICE_NAME`, `OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES` and `OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER` (with
  `_ARG`) and nothing overrides what it read, `OTEL_SERVICE_NAME` winning over a
  `service.name` in the attributes as the specification orders them; the crate
  resolves the endpoints
  (`OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` with each signal's path, or
  `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_TRACES_ENDPOINT`, `_METRICS_`, `_LOGS_` as given — a signal
  exports only when its endpoint is set), the export timeouts as before,
  `OTEL_METRIC_EXPORT_INTERVAL` in milliseconds (a second to an hour), and the
  certificates — the files `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_CERTIFICATE`,
  `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_CLIENT_CERTIFICATE` and `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_CLIENT_KEY` name,
  each with its per-signal spelling — in the specification's order, over what
  `OpenTelemetryConfig` pins, so a value is refused naming its variable and never
  quoted. A protocol variable naming another than `http/protobuf`, the one this
  exporter speaks, fails the boot. `OTEL_*` keeps its name, as `RUST_LOG` does: no
  prefix renames it.
- **`OpenTelemetry::init()` takes no argument**, and its guard is `#[must_use]`:
  `let _ = OpenTelemetry::init()?`, which drops it on the spot, is refused where
  `clippy::let_underscore_must_use` is on — in every workspace `nestrs new`
  writes, whose root denies it and whose members inherit it. `init` stays a line
  of `main`, its whole wiring — not a `#[nest_rs::main]` key, which could not
  take a pinned config, and never an install keyed on the Cargo feature, which
  unifies across a workspace build.
- **`OpenTelemetryConfig` is set one way**: its fields, over
  `OpenTelemetryConfig::default()` or `from_env()` (which reads the console's
  `RUST_LOG`, `<PREFIX>_LOG_FORMAT` and `<PREFIX>_LOG_SOURCE_LOCATION`). `service_name` and `service_version` are `Option`, the
  binary's when `None`; a pinned `trace_sample_ratio` outside `[0, 1]` fails
  `init_with` naming the field.
- **`nest_rs::config::TlsNames`** names a `ClientTls` refusal by the variables
  that read it — a namespace's `TLS_*`, through `ConfigService`, or a
  specification's own — with `ClientTls::overlaid` and `Material::from_file`
  for material read that way.
- **The demo's five apps export**: `api`, `live`, `worker` and `assistant` now call
  `OpenTelemetry::init()`, as `auth` did; the assistant declares only its title and
  instructions, and reports itself as `assistant` at the package's version.

**Breaking:** `OpenTelemetry::init(name)` is `OpenTelemetry::init()`;
`OpenTelemetryConfig::new(name)` and its `with_*` setters are gone (a literal over
`OpenTelemetryConfig::default()` or `from_env()`, its `service_name` and
`service_version` now `Option<String>`); `OpenTelemetryConfig::from_env` takes no
argument; `<PREFIX>_OPENTELEMETRY__SERVICE_NAME`, `SERVICE_VERSION`,
`SERVICE_ENVIRONMENT`, `SERVICE_INSTANCE_ID`, `OTLP_ENDPOINT`, `SAMPLE_RATIO`,
`METRIC_INTERVAL_SECS`, `TLS_CA_CERT`, `TLS_CERT` and `TLS_KEY` are read by nothing
(`OTEL_SERVICE_NAME`, `OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES`, `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT`,
`OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER` with `OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER_ARG`, `OTEL_METRIC_EXPORT_INTERVAL`
in milliseconds, and the certificate files take their place); OpenAPI's default
`info` and MCP's default `serverInfo` are the binary's.
What fails if you get it wrong: a deployment still setting
`<PREFIX>_OPENTELEMETRY__OTLP_ENDPOINT` exports nothing — `init` says so at `warn` on
`nest_rs::opentelemetry`, once per variable under the retired namespace, naming it and
never its value — and a client generated from an OpenAPI document reads the crate's
version in `info.version` now, not `0.1.0`.

### OpenTelemetry's exporter switches are honoured, and the console's variables overlay a pinned config

Telemetry read the specification's endpoints but not its switches: a signal exported
whenever an endpoint named it, so a deployment setting `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` for
its metrics and `OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=none` still shipped its traces. And
`OpenTelemetry::init_with(config)` laid `OTEL_*` over a pinned config but not the
console's variables, so an app that pinned its log filter could not have its deployment
raise it.

- **`OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER`, `OTEL_METRICS_EXPORTER` and `OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER` are read**:
  `otlp`, the default, or `none`, in any case, or a comma-separated list of `otlp`. `none`
  switches the signal off whatever endpoint names it.
- **`OTEL_SDK_DISABLED=true` switches every signal off**, as the specification orders;
  any other value leaves the SDK on, and one neither `true` nor `false` is said at `warn`
  on `nest_rs::opentelemetry`, naming the variable.
- **What this crate does not build is refused at boot**: an exporter the specification
  knows (`console`, `zipkin`, `prometheus`, `otlp/stdout`), a misspelling, `none` beside
  `otlp`, and `otlp` named for a signal no endpoint reaches each fail `init` with
  `OpenTelemetryError::Config`, naming the variable and what it accepts, never the value.
  A binary built without the `otlp` feature reads the selectors too, and refuses any value
  but `none`, `otlp` included. The specification would warn and ignore an exporter it does
  not recognise; a deployment believing a signal ships where nothing ships is what the
  boot refuses instead.
- **The boot line says why a signal exports nothing**: `traces`, `metrics` and `logs` on
  `OpenTelemetry initialised` hold the endpoint, `off (OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=none)`,
  `off (OTEL_SDK_DISABLED=true)` or `off (no endpoint)`, where `<none>` stood for all
  three. The `warn` for a variable passed over is one line for every kind — `an
  OpenTelemetry variable is passed over` — with the `variable` and the `reason`.
- **`init_with` lays `RUST_LOG`, `<PREFIX>_LOG_FORMAT` and
  `<PREFIX>_LOG_SOURCE_LOCATION` over a pinned config, field by field**, through the
  kernel's new `LogSettings::overlaid_by_env`, which reads and checks each as `from_env`
  does, and checks the filter set in code whatever the variables set, naming
  `LogSettings::filter`; `LogSettings` gains `Default`. A pinned filter that does not
  parse is still refused naming `OpenTelemetryConfig::log_filter`. Telemetry starts before any module,
  so the `.env` cascade overrides a pin here as the deployment does.

**Breaking:** an `OTEL_*_EXPORTER` naming anything but `otlp` or `none` (anything but
`none` in a binary built without the `otlp` feature), `none` beside `otlp`, and `otlp`
named for a signal with no endpoint fail the boot; a signal `none` or
`OTEL_SDK_DISABLED=true` switches off exports nothing; a log setting pinned through
`init_with` gives way to the variable that sets it; the boot line's `<none>` reads
`off (no endpoint)`, and the passed-over `warn` has one message.
What fails if you get it wrong: a deployment carrying `OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=zipkin` (or
`console`) for another service's sake fails `OpenTelemetry::init` with `invalid value for
OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER: must name otlp, …`; unset it, or set `none`.

### Every OpenTelemetry variable for what nestrs builds is honoured or refused at boot

The specification's variables this crate did not serve were passed over: a `gzip`
compression the SDK warned about and dropped, a misspelled sampler or temporality it
warned about and replaced by its default, an `OTEL_PROPAGATORS` and an
`OTEL_CONFIG_FILE` nothing read, an `OTEL_METRIC_EXPORT_TIMEOUT` nothing applied, and an
endpoint a binary built without the `otlp` feature ignored while the deployment believed
it exported. A collector's credential, in `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS` or in the
endpoint's query, crossed the network in clear beside an `http://` endpoint, and an
export to a collector on loopback went to a proxy the environment named.

- **`OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_COMPRESSION` (and per signal) is honoured**: `gzip`, the one
  compression the OTLP specification names, compresses every export (`content-encoding:
  gzip`); `none` leaves it plain, a signal's own variable over the general one.
  `opentelemetry-otlp` builds with `gzip-http`, over the flate2 the workspace already
  locks.
- **`OTEL_METRIC_EXPORT_TIMEOUT` bounds a metric export**, in milliseconds, 30 s by
  default; the exporter's own timeout bounds the same export, and the earlier cuts it.
  Each exporter is handed its resolved timeout and compression, so the SDK reads neither
  again.
- **`OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER`, `OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER_ARG` and
  `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_METRICS_TEMPORALITY_PREFERENCE` are resolved here**, as the SDK
  reads them, and handed to the builders; an argument beside a sampler that takes none is
  passed over and said at `warn`.
- **`OTEL_PROPAGATORS` may only name `tracecontext` and `baggage`**, its default: the
  framework propagates W3C Trace Context at every edge, and W3C Baggage on the queue alone,
  where a job carries its `baggage` onto every push it makes. The SDK's global propagator
  holds both formats, the specification's default, where it held Trace Context alone.
- **A collector on this host's loopback is dialled directly**, past any proxy
  `HTTP_PROXY`, `HTTPS_PROXY` or `ALL_PROXY` names, which reached its own host's
  loopback and carried the export, its headers included, off the machine.
- **The boot line's states read whole to a logfmt reader**: `traces`, `metrics`, `logs`
  and `service` on `OpenTelemetry initialised` are strings, quoted on a text console —
  `traces="off (no endpoint)"`.

**Breaking:** each of these fails `OpenTelemetry::init` with `OpenTelemetryError::Config`,
naming the variable or the field and never its value —

- `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_COMPRESSION` (or a signal's own) naming anything but `gzip` or
  `none`, `zstd` included;
- `OTEL_PROPAGATORS` naming another format (`b3`, `jaeger`, `xray`), leaving `tracecontext`
  or `baggage` out, or saying `none`, which cannot turn the framework's correlation off;
- `OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER` naming a sampler the SDK does not build (`jaeger_remote`, `xray`,
  a misspelling, a name not in lowercase), and `OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER_ARG` that is not a
  number between 0 and 1 beside a ratio sampler — each of which the SDK replaced by its
  default, sampling every trace;
- an `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_METRICS_TEMPORALITY_PREFERENCE` other than `cumulative`, `delta`
  or `lowmemory`, which the SDK replaced by its default, cumulative temporality;
- `OTEL_CONFIG_FILE` set: the Rust SDK has no file configuration;
- `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS` (or a signal's own) beside an `http://` endpoint whose host
  is not loopback, and such an endpoint carrying userinfo or a query: a collector's
  credential would cross the network in clear (RFC 6750 §5.3). `localhost`,
  `127.0.0.0/8` and `[::1]` keep receiving both;
- in a binary built without the `otlp` feature, an endpoint — `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT`,
  a signal's own, or a pinned `OpenTelemetryConfig::otlp_endpoint` — for a signal neither
  its selector's `none` nor `OTEL_SDK_DISABLED=true` switches off.

What fails if you get it wrong: a deployment carrying `OTEL_PROPAGATORS=b3` or
`OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_COMPRESSION=zstd` for another service's sake fails the boot naming
it; unset it, or set what the refusal accepts.

### No OpenTelemetry variable, endpoint or header is passed over in silence, and a piped console is not coloured

What the previous entry left still slipped through: a batch processor's number or a span
limit the SDK could not parse was replaced by its default with no word, and the
specification's attribute, log record, exemplar, histogram and log-level variables were
read by nothing; a signal's headers variable set empty sent that signal no header, where
the specification reads it as unset; a credential in an endpoint's userinfo was dropped
by the HTTP client, and the collector answered 401; a query on the base endpoint swallowed
the signal's path, and the export went to the collector's root; a member of
`OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES` with no `=` was dropped, and a percent-encoded value exported as
written; and a text console wrote colour escapes into a pipe, around every key a logfmt
reader looked for.

- **The span limits are read here and handed to the tracer**: `OTEL_SPAN_ATTRIBUTE_COUNT_LIMIT`,
  `OTEL_SPAN_EVENT_COUNT_LIMIT`, `OTEL_SPAN_LINK_COUNT_LIMIT`, and the two the SDK read
  nowhere, `OTEL_EVENT_ATTRIBUTE_COUNT_LIMIT` and `OTEL_LINK_ATTRIBUTE_COUNT_LIMIT`; the
  three attribute counts fall back on `OTEL_ATTRIBUTE_COUNT_LIMIT`, as the specification
  orders a model's limit before the general one.
- **`OTEL_BSP_EXPORT_TIMEOUT` and `OTEL_BLRP_EXPORT_TIMEOUT` bound a span and a log
  export**, 30 s by default, beside the exporter's own timeout, the earlier cutting it, as
  `OTEL_METRIC_EXPORT_TIMEOUT` bounds a metric export: the batch processors the SDK builds
  read neither.
- **The headers are read here and handed to each exporter**: a signal's own
  `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_*_HEADERS` set empty reads as unset, as the specification orders, so
  the general `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS` reach that signal too, where the SDK sent it none.
- **`OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES` is read here, as the resource specification writes it**:
  each key and value is percent-decoded — a comma or an equals sign in one is written `%2C`
  or `%3D` — where the SDK exported an escape as written.
- **A specification variable this build serves nowhere is said once at `warn`** on
  `nest_rs::opentelemetry`, naming it and why, never its value:
  `OTEL_ATTRIBUTE_VALUE_LENGTH_LIMIT`, `OTEL_SPAN_ATTRIBUTE_VALUE_LENGTH_LIMIT`,
  `OTEL_LOGRECORD_ATTRIBUTE_VALUE_LENGTH_LIMIT`, `OTEL_LOGRECORD_ATTRIBUTE_COUNT_LIMIT` (and
  `OTEL_ATTRIBUTE_COUNT_LIMIT` while the logs export), `OTEL_METRICS_EXEMPLAR_FILTER`,
  `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_METRICS_DEFAULT_HISTOGRAM_AGGREGATION`, the SDK's own
  `OTEL_BSP_MAX_CONCURRENT_EXPORTS` and `OTEL_LOG_LEVEL`. A value naming what the build does
  says nothing (`always_off`, `explicit_bucket_histogram`, `1`). The passed-over line reads
  `an OpenTelemetry variable is passed over`, with the `variable` and a `reason`, where it
  read `… does not hold what it must; passed over, as the specification orders` with an
  `expected`.
- **The kernel's text console is coloured on a terminal alone**: piped to a reader or a
  file, its lines carry no escape code, and `NO_COLOR` (no-color.org) turns colour off on
  a terminal too. JSON is unchanged.

**Breaking:** each of these fails `OpenTelemetry::init` with `OpenTelemetryError::Config`,
naming the variable or the field and never its value —

- a number the SDK reads that is not a whole number its type holds (the refusal says the
  bound): `OTEL_BSP_SCHEDULE_DELAY`,
  `OTEL_BSP_MAX_QUEUE_SIZE`, `OTEL_BSP_MAX_EXPORT_BATCH_SIZE`, `OTEL_BLRP_SCHEDULE_DELAY`,
  `OTEL_BLRP_MAX_QUEUE_SIZE`, `OTEL_BLRP_MAX_EXPORT_BATCH_SIZE` (matched as the SDK matches
  them, digits alone), a span limit or `OTEL_ATTRIBUTE_COUNT_LIMIT`,
  `OTEL_BSP_EXPORT_TIMEOUT` or `OTEL_BLRP_EXPORT_TIMEOUT`, and an
  `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_TIMEOUT` (or a signal's own), `OTEL_METRIC_EXPORT_TIMEOUT` or
  `OTEL_METRIC_EXPORT_INTERVAL`, which the previous entry passed over and said at `warn`;
- a headers pair the SDK would drop: no `=`, an empty key or value, a name no header may
  carry, a value no header may hold;
- a member of `OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES` the SDK dropped or kept under no name — no `=`, an
  empty key — or an escape in it that does not decode to UTF-8, where the specification
  discards the whole variable;
- userinfo in any endpoint — `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT`, a signal's own, or a pinned
  `OpenTelemetryConfig::otlp_endpoint` — over `https://` and to loopback too, pointing to
  `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS`, where the credential travels;
- a query on the base endpoint, `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` or the pinned one: the
  specification sends each signal to a path relative to the base, which leaves the query
  no place. A signal's own endpoint keeps its query, used as given.

What fails if you get it wrong: a deployment carrying `OTEL_BSP_SCHEDULE_DELAY=5s` or
`https://key@collector:4318` fails the boot naming the variable; write milliseconds, and
move the credential to `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS`. A `%` meant literally in a resource
attribute is decoded now: write it `%25`. A logfmt pipeline that stripped the console's
escape codes reads none to strip.

### No OpenTelemetry value leaves an app exporting nothing, or under a name it did not write, without a word

What the previous entry still handed over as read: a zero `OTEL_BSP_MAX_QUEUE_SIZE` or
`OTEL_BLRP_MAX_QUEUE_SIZE`, with which the SDK's batch processor takes no span or log
record and drops every one; a batch size above its queue, which the SDK lowered to the
queue in silence; a zero export timeout, which the specification reads as no limit and
which cut every export before it was sent; and a blank `OTEL_SERVICE_NAME`, which nestrs
read as unset while the SDK took it as the name, so the app exported under a blank one.

- **A zero timeout is no limit, as the specification reads it**: under a zero
  `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_TIMEOUT` (or a signal's own) the processor's bound cuts the export —
  `OTEL_BSP_EXPORT_TIMEOUT`, `OTEL_METRIC_EXPORT_TIMEOUT` or `OTEL_BLRP_EXPORT_TIMEOUT`,
  30 s by default — and under a zero processor's bound the exporter's.
- **A zero span, event, link or attribute count limit is honoured**: the span keeps none,
  each dropped and counted.
- **An empty `OTEL_SERVICE_NAME` is unset**, as the specification reads an empty variable:
  the binary names the service.

**Breaking:** each of these fails `OpenTelemetry::init` with `OpenTelemetryError::Config`,
naming the variable or the field and never its value —

- a queue or batch size of zero, `OTEL_BSP_MAX_QUEUE_SIZE`, `OTEL_BSP_MAX_EXPORT_BATCH_SIZE`,
  `OTEL_BLRP_MAX_QUEUE_SIZE` or `OTEL_BLRP_MAX_EXPORT_BATCH_SIZE`: the specification's
  sizes are positive, as the reference SDK's autoconfiguration (Java) holds them;
- a batch size written above its queue, the queue's variable or 2048 when unset: the
  specification has the batch at most the queue;
- an `OTEL_BSP_SCHEDULE_DELAY` or `OTEL_BLRP_SCHEDULE_DELAY` under 1 ms, on which the
  SDK's worker polls its channel without pause, a core per processor, idle or not; and an
  `OTEL_BSP_MAX_QUEUE_SIZE` or `OTEL_BLRP_MAX_QUEUE_SIZE` above 1,048,576, every slot of
  which the SDK allocates when the processor starts, beside a batch buffer that may reach
  the queue — the sentence says what the ceiling costs, up to 720 MiB of spans under
  opentelemetry_sdk 0.33 — where a size past what the process holds aborted the boot
  naming nothing. The metric period was already held to a second to an hour; one rule now
  holds the family: a number the SDK turns into a spin or into an allocation no process
  holds is refused, whatever the specification's type admits;
- an export's two timeouts both at zero — the exporter's and its processor's — which
  leave nothing bounding it, where every wait the framework owns is bounded;
- a service name that names nothing: a blank `OTEL_SERVICE_NAME`, an empty or blank
  `service.name` in `OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES`, or an empty or blank pinned
  `OpenTelemetryConfig::service_name`.

What fails if you get it wrong: a deployment carrying `OTEL_BSP_MAX_QUEUE_SIZE=0`, a batch
above its queue, `OTEL_BSP_SCHEDULE_DELAY=0`, a queue above 1,048,576,
`OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_TIMEOUT=0` beside `OTEL_BSP_EXPORT_TIMEOUT=0`, or
`OTEL_SERVICE_NAME=" "` fails the boot naming the variable; write a positive size, a batch
at most the queue, a delay of 1 ms or more, a queue within the ceiling, a bound in
milliseconds on one of the two, and a name or nothing. One
that wrote a zero export timeout and saw nothing exported now exports.

### The console's filter is `RUST_LOG`

`<PREFIX>_LOG` was a second name for the ecosystem's `RUST_LOG`, read before it, and the
`.env` cascade publishes a committed file's keys into the process environment: a
`.env.development` carrying `NESTRS_LOG=debug` outranked an operator's real `RUST_LOG`,
the one place the real environment lost to a file, with no word said. The run retired
`<PREFIX>_OPENTELEMETRY__*` for the same reason — two names for one setting, one of them
invented — and 7.0 is the major.

- **Every subscriber the framework ships reads its filter from `RUST_LOG` alone** — the
  boot's fallback, `install_console`, `OpenTelemetry::init` and `init_with`, over a pinned
  `log_filter` as before. `<PREFIX>_LOG_FORMAT` and `<PREFIX>_LOG_SOURCE_LOCATION` stay:
  the ecosystem has no name for either.
- **A set `<PREFIX>_LOG` is said once at `warn`** on `nest_rs::app` — `variable read by
  nothing since 7.0`, its `variable` field naming it and `read_as` naming `RUST_LOG` — through
  the subscriber the framework installs, or the app's own at its boot. It refuses
  nothing, whatever it holds.
- `nestrs new` writes `RUST_LOG=debug` in `.env.development`, as the demo does; the
  demo's chart sets the filter, unprefixed, through `extraEnv`, and the bench's quiet
  run is `RUST_LOG=warn`.

**Breaking:** `nest_rs::core::logging::var::FILTER` is gone, and `<PREFIX>_LOG` filters
nothing: rename it `RUST_LOG` in every `.env` file and deployment.
What fails if you get it wrong: a deployment or a `.env` still setting `<PREFIX>_LOG`
logs at `info`, or at what `RUST_LOG` says, and the boot says once at `warn` that the
variable is read by nothing.

### OpenTelemetry is one line in `main`, and `App::run` refuses an endpoint nothing exports to

Every demo app wrote telemetry three times: `OpenTelemetry::init()` in `main`,
`OpenTelemetryModule` in its root, and `.with_test_telemetry()` in every suite, since a
suite boots the root and never runs `main`. The module refused the boot when `init` had
not run and provided `Metrics`, which nothing in the demo injected, so its import only
checked that the other line was written — and an app with neither line, which is what
`nestrs new` wrote, exported nothing in silence while its deployment named a collector.

- **`let _otel = OpenTelemetry::init()?;` in `main` is the whole wiring**: no module to
  import, nothing for a suite to call. `nestrs new` writes it, with the umbrella's
  `opentelemetry` feature on the app's own `nest-rs` dependency; the demo's five roots
  drop the import and its ten suite calls go.
- **`OpenTelemetryModule` provides `Metrics` and refuses nothing.** It is imported by the
  module whose provider injects `Arc<Metrics>`, under the `otlp` feature, where `Metrics`
  is. It installs them in its own `register`, as `EventsModule` installs `EventBus`, which
  the access graph does not gate: an app in which nothing imports it is refused naming the
  module, and a provider whose module skips the import reaches `Metrics` when a module
  importing it registered first and fails the boot when it registers before. A suite boots
  it without `init`, and its `Metrics` record on a meter nothing exports.
- **`App::run` refuses an OTLP endpoint while the kernel's console is all the process logs
  through** (`UnservedExportError`): `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT`, or a signal's own,
  set for a signal `OTEL_SDK_DISABLED=true` or its selector's `none` does not switch off,
  before any transport is built, naming the variable and the line to add. The check is the
  kernel's, so it holds in a binary that links no telemetry. An app installing a subscriber
  of its own is its own to judge, and `App::builder().build()`, which `TestApp` and
  `HeadlessApp` boot through, never refuses.

**Breaking:** `OpenTelemetryError::InitMissing`, `TestAppBuilder::with_test_telemetry`
and `nest-rs-testing`'s `opentelemetry` feature are gone; `OpenTelemetryModule` exists
under the `otlp` feature alone; and a binary run with an OTLP endpoint set and no
`OpenTelemetry::init()` now fails `App::run`, where it booted and exported nothing.
What fails if you get it wrong: a platform injecting `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` into
every pod stops a nestrs binary whose `main` installs no exporter, naming the variable —
add the `init` line, or set `OTEL_SDK_DISABLED=true` where that binary exports nothing; a
root still importing `OpenTelemetryModule` without `init` now boots, and a suite still
calling `.with_test_telemetry()` does not compile.

### Every impl-half decorator reads a method with one grammar

The decorators hosted on an `#[injectable]` — `#[processor]`, `#[listeners]`,
`#[scheduled]`, `#[hooks]`, `#[indicators]` — and the edge collectors
(`#[routes]`, `#[messages]`, `#[operations]`, `#[tools]`) each judged a method's
shape their own way, and several deferred the judgement to rustc, which reported a
borrow error, `E0283` or a type mismatch inside code the developer never wrote.
They now share one grammar in `nest_rs_codegen`, each refusal worded once with a
trybuild snapshot:

- **A method compiled out takes its registration with it.** Attributes are read
  through `#[cfg]` and `#[cfg_attr]`, so a method behind a false `#[cfg]` takes its
  handler, its inventory entry, its schedule entry and its dataloader with it, and
  two methods claiming one key under true `#[cfg]`s — one route, one WS event, one
  GraphQL field, one MCP tool or prompt name — are refused naming both.
- **The receiver is judged by the rule.** `self: &Self`, an alias of the host and
  `self: &Arc<Self>` are `&self`; `&mut self`, a borrow of another pointer or a
  `'static` one is refused saying the container holds the one instance in an
  `Arc`. A generic method is refused, a role written twice is refused counting the
  copies, `-> ()` is the unit return and a raw identifier is a name.
- **A plain `fn` is accepted** for a lifecycle hook, a health indicator, an event
  listener, a scheduled trigger, a `#[process]` method, a GraphQL subscription and
  an `#[entity]` resolver, and is called without an `.await`.
- **Five grammars are worded once for every decorator that takes them**: a
  duration (`"500ms"`, `"30s"`, `"5m"`, `"1h"`), a queue name, `replicas`, a
  `#[routes]` path read with the route grammar, and dispatch keys. A key a sibling
  decorator takes and this one cannot — `retries` or `concurrency` on an
  `#[every]`, `tz` on a `#[process]` — is refused with the fact that makes it
  meaningless there, never as a misspelling. The job family's keys are one table,
  in `nest-rs-codegen`'s `job.rs`: every member (`JobDecorator` — `#[process]`, `#[every]`,
  `#[cron]`, `#[after]`) crossed with every key (`JobKey`) is a cell of one
  exhaustive `match`, so a key or a member added without an answer at every
  crossing does not build, and each parser reads its keys from it.
- **`#[hooks]` calls the method by its path**, so a trait on `Arc` sharing its name
  no longer runs instead of the hook. A trigger's refusals in one `#[scheduled]`
  host arrive together, and a refusal keeps the impl block, so what rustc reports
  beside it is only what is wrong in the developer's own code.

### Every value refusal opens with the decorator and the key

A refusal of a value read without its source frame — a problems list, a CI summary —
said which key and not whose, and `transactional` is a key of four decorators. Every
value refusal now opens with its site, worded once: a value of the wrong kind reads
``#[process] `retries` takes a whole number``, a value that breaks a grammar names
itself (``#[controller] `version`: "1/2" is not a path segment — …``), and a value
outside a closed set keeps ``unknown #[attr] … `x`; expected …``. The family is
closed across the macro crates — a route's path and `#[version]`, every `#[api]`
value, `#[http_code]`, `#[response_header]`, `#[redirect]`,
`#[interceptor(priority)]`, every `#[use_*]` entry, `#[expose]`'s values,
`#[queue]`, `#[process(queue)]`, `#[cron]`'s expression and `tz`, `#[gateway]`,
`#[subscribe_message]`, GraphQL's `#[authorize(bind, id_arg)]` and `#[module]`'s
lists included — and no decorator leaves a value of the wrong kind to syn's own
sentence, which names neither: `#[crud]`'s values were the last that did.
`#[api]` joins the argument family too: an unknown key reads
``unknown #[api] argument `x`; expected …`` and a key written bare
``#[api] `summary` needs a value — write `summary = ...` ``, where its own
sentence and syn's `expected =` stood. **Breaking for anything matching on a
diagnostic's text.**

Three values no longer reach a panic or a type mismatch inside an expansion.
`#[expose(name = "…")]`, and a `#[sea_orm(from = "…")]` that `#[expose]` reads,
holding something that is not an identifier are compile errors naming the key,
where they were `proc macro panicked`, and a `from` naming no column of the entity
is refused on the literal. `#[cron(3600)]` — a literal that is not a string — is
refused at the attribute. `#[http_code(201u8)]` and `#[redirect]` accept a
suffixed status and emit the validated one unsuffixed, where `201u8` passed
validation and then failed as a type mismatch.

A string literal forwarded through a `macro_rules!` as `$x:expr` is read through the
invisible group it arrives in, where it was refused as not a string literal at
`#[controller(path)]`, `#[gateway(path)]`, `#[mcp(path)]`, `#[cron(tz)]`,
`#[config(namespace)]`, `#[api(summary)]` and the version list — depending on the
argument's position — and a cron expression forwarded that way is validated at
compile time like a literal written in place. A whole `version = [...]` list
forwarded that way is read too, where it was refused.

A decorator read once refuses a second copy by name — `#[public]` at every edge,
every flag, `#[api]` and `#[inject]` — where rustc said `cannot find attribute`.
**Every `key = value` grammar refuses a repeat of every key**, through one reader in
`nest_rs_codegen`, `Grammar`, which refuses an unknown key, a repeated one and one
written bare before the decorator sees a key, and underlines a repeat at the key —
eighteen decorators, both halves of `#[expose]` among them; a repeated
`via` on the `#[expose]` written on a column used to load the rows the second
foreign key links. `#[crud(create = T, ops = [list, get])]` is refused, where the
input type of an op `ops` leaves out was dropped in silence, and so is a
`paginate` beside an `ops` that leaves out `list` — `#[crud(ops = [get], paginate
= none)]` dropped the documented opt-out in silence, though `paginate` configures
`list` alone. `#[interceptor]` names a misspelled key rather than reporting a
repeated `priority`, and a refused `#[mcp(...)]` argument no longer blames the
`#[tools]` impl beside it. `#[api("…")]` — an argument that is not a key — is
refused with the shared unknown-argument sentence naming it as written, and
`#[mcp]`'s refusal of a server-identity field points at the key rather than at
the whole argument.

**Breaking for decorator authors** building on `nest_rs_codegen`: the worker-job
helpers take the member of the family they word rather than its name —
`transactional_value(expr)` is `transactional_value(JobDecorator::Process, expr)`,
`job_argument_needs_a_value(attr, name)` is `job_argument_needs_a_value(member,
name)` — and `job_key(member, &arg)` reads the `Arg` that
`JobDecorator::grammar()` hands over against the table above. A decorator reads
its `key = value` arguments through `Grammar` — declared as a `const` with
`Grammar::new(attr, keys)`, read with `parse`, `parse2` or `parse_attr`, and
`take_all` for a list mixing positionals and keys — and the closure it hands over
receives only keys of its own grammar, each the first time it is written.
`WrittenKeys`, 6.1's `unmatched_meta` and `reject_duplicate_argument` are gone with
the per-decorator loops they served, and the root `clippy.toml` refuses
`syn::meta::parser` and `syn::Attribute::parse_nested_meta` in the repository's
own crates. `DecoratorPair` can no longer be constructed outside
`nest-rs-codegen`: the nine pairs are `nest_rs_codegen::pair::{HTTP, GRAPHQL, WS,
MCP, HOOKS, PROCESSOR, INDICATORS, SCHEDULED, LISTENERS}`, listed by `pair::ALL`,
where a macro crate reads its own. `takes_value` and `site` word the value
family's sentences, `must_be_async` is gone with the rule it worded (a method may
be synchronous; `await_if_async` emits the call), `CrudConfig` is
`CrudDeclaration`, and the grammars above are public — `cfg_attrs`,
`DispatchKeys`, `Grammar` and its `Arg`, `duration_millis`, `replicas_value` and
its `Replicas`, `key_value`, `is_valid_job_key`, `RoutePath`, `HostBorrow`,
`ungrouped_expr`, `JobDecorator`, `JobKey`, `job_keys`, `job_returns_a_result`,
`unread_job_key` and `versioning::parse_version_args` among them.

### Macro plumbing lives at `<crate>::__private`, and nothing outside it is hidden

The framework's extension surface was drawn by `#[doc(hidden)]` alone: 154 items
sat hidden at public paths — a struct a decorator builds, a slot a sibling crate
seeds, a constant only a test reads — and nothing told a seam from plumbing. Every
crate an app links now has three tiers:

- **Everything public outside `__private` is API**, documented and covered by
  semver.
- **`<crate>::__private`** — one `#[doc(hidden)]` module per crate — holds what
  decorator expansions and sibling framework crates call. It is not API and may
  change in any release; the framework's crates require each other at the exact
  version, so their expansions always meet the plumbing they were built against.
  Where Rust cannot place an item in a module, it keeps a `__` prefix instead:
  `Namespaced::__DECLARATION`, the exported macro `__shaper_of!`, a hidden
  error field (`__cause`), and the marker `#[expose]` emits beside an entity
  for each `belongs_to` a parent names through `via`, now `__By<Column>`
  (`__ByAuthorId` for `author_id`; `By<Column>` in 6.x).
- **The rest is `pub(crate)`.**

Nothing an application writes through a decorator changes: the expansions name
the new paths. Hidden methods on public types are gone from those types —
`EventBus::subscribe` and `subscribe_named`, `McpIdentity::declared`,
`OpenTelemetry::init_for_tests`, `ProcessMethod::new`,
`ProcessMethod::required_capabilities`, `JobError::undecodable`,
`QueueName::is_valid`, `Checkpoint::open`, `Correlation::set_sampled` and
`ContainerBuilder`'s import bookkeeping — and two that only their own crate
called are crate-private now: `GraphqlOperationContext::federation` and
`PushOptions::required_capabilities`. `TransportContribution` moved like the rest;
the sealed `ContainerBuilder::provide_transport` that replaces it is described
with the transport contract.

Two hidden re-exports were the opposite of plumbing: `nest_rs::ws::serde_json`,
which a gateway, a guard and a broadcast name `Value` through, and
`nest_rs::ws::tracing`, the gateway's logger. Both are documented API now.

**Breaking:** a path that reached a moved item no longer compiles. All but three
were hidden, never API; the three were public and documented, yet only decorator
expansions call them: `nest_rs::pipes::{ValidateProbe, MaybeValidateFallback}`,
graphql's re-export of that pair, and `nest_rs::ws::resolve_ws_data_pipe`.

### Tier 1 holds what a developer writes, each item at one path

Everything public outside `__private` is what 7.x promises to keep, so a seam
only a decorator's expansion or a sibling framework crate calls would have been
frozen until 8.0 and listed in the index a developer reads: the WebSocket
edge's reply and error probes, which the GraphQL and MCP edges already kept in
`__private`, and the HTTP edge's path and version helpers. And one item had up
to seven paths: `async_trait` was reachable from `core`, `guards`, the guards
prelude, `graphql`, `http`, `queue` and `ws`, `#[input]` from `core`, `http`,
`ws`, `mcp` and `queue`, so two files of one feature imported the same
attribute from two crates.

- **What only expansions and sibling crates call is `__private`**: the
  WebSocket edge's `ReplyValue`, `ReplyValueFallback`, `ReplyOutcome`,
  `ErrorReport`, `ErrorReportChain`, `ErrorReportFallback`, `ErrorReportLocal`
  and `HandlerFailed`, its `target` module and its `nest_rs_http` re-export;
  the HTTP edge's `version_path`, `versions_declare`, `join_path`,
  `normalize_mount_path`, `literal_mount_path` and `declared_versions`; the
  global pools' `GuardSpecs` and `PipeSpecs`, which `use_guards_global` and
  `use_pipes_global` seeded, and `nest_rs::core::layer_chain::GlobalSpecs`, the
  trait every global pool implemented — all three since gone with those seams,
  each pool now a declaration its root module lists (*A suite boots the
  composition `main` runs*); MCP's `hosts_on`; and the scheduler's
  `nest_rs_database` re-export. MCP's `current_container`, read by its own
  crate alone, is crate-private: an operation reaches a provider through
  `#[inject]`, never a locator.
- **`async_trait` and `#[input]` have one path**, `nest_rs::core`, and
  `nest_rs::prelude` carries both. `nest_rs::guards::prelude` is gone: a guard
  imports its contract from `nest_rs::guards` and the kernel's vocabulary from
  `nest_rs::core`. `nest_rs::http::{current_request_scope,
  with_request_scope}`, a second path to core's, are gone too, and so is
  `nest_rs::opentelemetry::LogFormat`, a second path to
  `nest_rs::core::logging::LogFormat`.
- **The layer specs a decorator builds are `__private`**: `nest_rs::guards::{guard,
  GuardSpec}`, `nest_rs::pipes::{pipe, PipeSpec}`, and core's `InterceptorSpec`,
  `ExceptionFilterSpec` and the functions `interceptor()` and `exception_filter()` —
  what `#[use_guards]`, `#[use_pipes]`, `#[use_interceptors]` and
  `#[use_exception_filters]` expand to, and what the four app-wide declarations
  build. Since the pools became declarations no app names them; the attribute
  `#[interceptor]` keeps its path, and `nest_rs::core::LayerSpec` stays API.
- **`nest_rs::authz::http::Scope<E, A>` is gone.** It handed a route the
  caller's row filter as a `Condition` for a query of its own, which a handler
  can no longer run since a service holds no connection: `Repo::scoped` filters
  the query the service runs.
- **The `schedule` feature turns `database` on**, so a scheduled method's
  `TransactionPolicy` is read at `nest_rs::database`, its one path; the crate
  was compiled already.
- **What the docs, the CLI or a package hand out stays API**, whoever else
  calls it: `class_gate`, `masked_reply`,
  `current_body_limit`, `RouteResponseShaper` and `ResponseShaping`,
  `normalize_error_response`, `endpoint_identity`, `declare_endpoint` and
  `HttpEndpointMeta`, `SchemaFn`, `ConfigModule::provide_feature` and
  `provide_root`, `ForInstance`, `McpAccess`, `McpSetup` and
  `StaticFilesSetup`.

**Breaking:** a path that reached a moved item no longer compiles. Import
`async_trait` and `input` from `nest_rs::core` or through `nest_rs::prelude::*`;
the upgrading guide lists the rest, each with what replaces it.

### A concept has one type, and what a developer names is re-exported where they look

A feature with HTTP, WebSocket and MCP adapters imported three `Scoped` types that
differed by nothing, and the prelude's was HTTP's, which a resolver's parameter
refused. A custom validation rule took a `validator` line pinned to the framework's
`0.x` minor, against the project's no on a decorator forcing a manifest line. A JWT-only
app turned on the ORM to derive a role. And `nest_rs::mcp` offered rmcp's router macros
as a second way to write tools, beside transport types no app mounts, each frozen until
8.0.

- **`Scoped<T>` is `nest_rs::core::Scoped`**, one parameter on every edge, as
  `Ambient<T>` is, and `nest_rs::prelude` names it. `nest_rs::http::Scoped`,
  `nest_rs::ws::Scoped`, `nest_rs::graphql::Scoped` and `nest_rs::mcp::Scoped` are gone.
  What a miss answers, on each edge, is unchanged. `Opaque` stays per edge, since its
  output is the edge's own error type.
- **`#[wire_enum]` is `nest_rs::core::wire_enum`**, beside `#[input]` and `#[output]`:
  it derives nothing from SeaORM, so `nest_rs::seaorm::wire_enum` is gone, and `nestrs g
  auth`, and `g graphql` and `g mcp` over a port with no table, add no `seaorm` to a
  manifest: a JWT-only tree that takes it off the root, where `nestrs new` still writes it,
  keeps it off. `#[wire_enum(graphql)]`
  reaches `nest_rs::graphql` by its key alone, and a build without the feature says so in
  one sentence naming it. The prelude still carries `wire_enum`.
- **`nest_rs::pipes::validator` is validator**, at the release the framework derives
  with, so `custom(function = …)` on a `#[input]`, an `#[expose]`d column or a `#[config]`
  names `nest_rs::pipes::validator::ValidationError` and the manifest gains no line. The
  umbrella's `seaorm` and `config` features now enable `pipes`, which is what lets either
  alone reach it.
- **`nest_rs::mcp` keeps `rmcp` whole and what a page or an expansion names beside it**:
  `model`, `service`, `McpError`, `ServerHandler`, `ServerConfig`, `Parameters` and `Json`.
  rmcp's `tool`, `tool_router`, `tool_handler`, `prompt`, `prompt_router`, `prompt_handler`
  and `schemars`, `ToolRouter`, `PromptRouter`, `LocalSessionManager`,
  `StreamableHttpServerConfig`, `StreamableHttpService`, the `handler` and `transport`
  modules, and the root `CallToolResult`, `ContentBlock`, `ServerCapabilities` and
  `ProtocolVersion` are gone from the root, reached through `nest_rs::mcp::rmcp` or
  `nest_rs::mcp::model`.
- **`McpIdentity` is set one way, its public fields over `Default`**, as
  `OpenTelemetryConfig` is, so a later field is a minor. `new(name, version)`, `title`,
  `description`, `website_url`, `icons` and `instructions` are gone:
  `McpIdentity { title: Some("Acme assistant".into()), ..Default::default() }`. In a process
  no `#[nest_rs::main]` started, a version, title, description, website or icons with no
  name behind them, which went unread, now refuse the boot as a name with no version does,
  and that refusal says the app stated the name when no host did.
- **What only expansions and sibling crates call left tier 1**: `nest_rs::http::RejectedCredential`,
  `nest_rs::http::RequestDeadline` and `nest_rs::authz::masked_output_ambient` moved to
  their crates' `__private`, and `nest_rs::authz::graphql::masked_value_for`,
  `nest_rs::authz::mcp::masked_value_for` and `nest_rs::authz::ws::masked_reply_for` are
  private to their crate. A body masking by hand reads `Ability::mask` for a row and
  `nest_rs::authz::masked_reply` for a value it built. `nest_rs::core::RETIRED_VARIABLE`,
  added earlier in 7.0's cycle, moved too: the pages quote its sentence. So did
  `nest_rs::core::trace_context::field`, which `operation_span!` and authn's guard read.
  `McpIdentity::is_empty` is private to its crate.
- **An item has one path, modules included.** `nest_rs::core` re-exported at its root what
  its public modules also held, so `nest_rs::core::container::Container` and
  `nest_rs::core::Container` froze one type twice, and so did about ninety others. `access`,
  `app`, `container`, `discoverable`, `discovery`, `env_flag`, `env_prefix`, `error`,
  `error_message`, `layer`, `layer_chain`, `lifecycle`, `module`, `problem`,
  `request_scope`, `trace_context` and `transport` are private, each item at the root alone;
  `nest_rs::core::problem::code` is `nest_rs::core::code`, beside `target` and `unit`;
  `Edge` is `nest_rs::core::Edge` and `panic_message` `nest_rs::core::panic::panic_message`,
  neither any longer at a second path. `nest_rs::social::providers` is private, its modules
  and configs at `nest_rs::social`. `nest_rs::authz::ws` and `nest_rs::authz::mcp`, which
  held nothing public, are private too.

**Breaking:** a path above no longer compiles, with no alias; the upgrading guide lists
each with what replaces it.

### `#[expose]` is SeaORM's: `nest_rs::seaorm::expose`

`#[expose]` turns a SeaORM entity into its wire DTO, its inputs and its
GraphQL object, yet it lived in `nest-rs-resource`, a crate named for a capability —
and `nest_rs::resource` beside `nest_rs::oauth::resource` gave one word two meanings. An
entity is SeaORM's in every line, so its decorator now lives with the ORM.

- **Breaking: `nest_rs::resource` is gone.** `#[expose]` is `nest_rs::seaorm::expose`, and
  `#[wire_enum]`, which derives nothing from SeaORM, `nest_rs::core::wire_enum`;
  `nest_rs::prelude::{expose, wire_enum}` is unchanged. The relation bridges an `#[expose(…, graphql)]` entity implements —
  `PkLoadable`, `RelatedTo`, `RelationKey`, `RelationPage`, `SoleForeignKey` — are
  `nest_rs::seaorm::graphql::…`. The umbrella's features keep their names, and `seaorm`
  still carries everything `#[expose]` emits.
- **Breaking: the masking contract `#[expose]` implements is `nest-rs-authz`'s.** Response
  masking retains only the keys it names, so the crate that masks owns it, and
  `nest-rs-authz` no longer links an exposure crate on any edge. It is now sealed: see
  [A `Model` serializes nowhere](#a-model-serializes-nowhere-expose-writes-the-masks-round-trip-and-a-hidden-column-it-cannot-rebuild-does-not-compile).
- **Breaking: a build without `seaorm` no longer turns on async-graphql's `uuid` and
  `chrono` scalars, nor chrono's `serde`.** They reached `graphql`, `http`, `ws` and
  `mcp` builds through `authz`, which linked the exposure crate; now `seaorm` alone turns
  them on, for its entities. An app on `graphql` without `seaorm` whose own resolver,
  object or input carries a `Uuid` or a chrono `DateTime` adds `uuid` or `chrono` to the
  features of its own `async-graphql` line, declaring one if it has none; an app without
  `seaorm` that serializes a chrono type adds `serde` to its own `chrono` line. Every app
  `nestrs new` scaffolds carries `seaorm` and is unaffected.
- **Breaking: `nest_rs_seaorm::TARGET` is `nest_rs_seaorm::target::ORM`**, beside
  `target::LOADER`, the relation dataloaders' target, which was `nest_rs::resource::TARGET`.
  The strings are unchanged — `nest_rs::orm` and `nest_rs::loader` — so a log filter
  keeps working; the loaders' target is now named on the logs and traces pages.
- The crates: `nest-rs-resource` is folded into `nest-rs-seaorm`, and
  `nest-rs-resource-macros` is `nest-rs-seaorm-macros`, the companion of its surface
  crate. The macro-only re-exports the expansions name (`serde`, `schemars`,
  `validator`, `chrono`, `uuid`, `serde_json`, `async_trait`, `tracing`, async-graphql
  and the dataloader decorator) sit in `nest_rs_seaorm`'s hidden tier, not at a public
  path.
- `nestrs g entity`, `g resource` and `g auth` write `use nest_rs::seaorm::…`; the demo's
  entities import from there too, and the exemplar's `users::Model` prints
  `password_hash` as `"<redacted>"` in its `Debug`, which a new entity copies.

### The data layer's enums and outcomes can grow within 7.x

`nest_rs::database::ExecutorScope` and `TransactionPolicy`, and
`nest_rs::seaorm::SeaOrmExecutor`, `FinalizeOutcome` and `DbOutcome` are
`#[non_exhaustive]`, as are the `nest_rs::database::Unhonoured` and
`nest_rs::core::JobTimedOut` structs, whose fields stay readable and which are
built through `Unhonoured::new(reason, retryable)`, `::retryable`,
`::deterministic` and `JobTimedOut::new(timeout)`. A variant added in a 7.x
minor — a transaction policy, a scope, an executor — is then no break.
`Access<M>` stays exhaustive: found, denied or missing is the whole answer to a
load by id.

A unit of work may refuse to open, answering `Unhonoured` without running its
body, and the edge reports that unit as the failure it describes; the SeaORM
binding refuses a transaction policy it does not know that way. The SeaORM
crate's own documentation says PostgreSQL, the one engine CI runs, where it
named MySQL and SQLite.

**Breaking:** a `match` on those enums outside their crate needs a `_` arm — one
that fails closed — and an `Unhonoured { .. }` or `JobTimedOut { .. }` literal
outside its crate is a constructor call.

### A decorator's paths never take a community crate for the framework

A decorator's expansion re-rooted any `::nest_rs_<x>::` path at the umbrella,
so a third-party crate named `nest_rs_kafka` was rewritten to
`::nest_rs::kafka::`, which does not exist, and the missing-dependency message
derived its feature from the crate's name. One table of the crates the umbrella
re-exports now decides both: a crate outside it is left alone, an OAuth role
roots at `::nest_rs::oauth::client`, `::server` or `::resource`, and the message
names the feature each crate ships under.

### A package declares its own units of work

A package that attaches a transport — a Kafka consumer, a gRPC server — now
declares its units like an edge does, under its own crate's name, and gets the
same span, the same ids and the same operation line:

```rust
// nestrs-kafka/src/unit.rs
pub const MESSAGE: Unit = nest_rs::core::unit!(
    "nestrs_kafka.message",
    target: crate::TARGET,
    kind: Consumer,
);
```

`operation_span!(MESSAGE, correlation)` opens it on the crate's own target
(`nestrs_kafka` or `nestrs_kafka::<concern>`), and `operation_line!` files its
line on `nest_rs::operation` beside every edge's, so
`nest_rs::operation=off` silences it too. The edge words (`http`, `queue`, …)
and `lifecycle`, the kernel's, stay the framework's; `unit!` refuses, each in
its own sentence, a unit named after another crate, a target outside the
crate's, and a crate that takes the framework's `nest-rs-` prefix, spelled
`nest-rs-` or `nest_rs_`. The crate
segment of a unit name may carry digits (`nestrs_s3.upload`).

### A community package has one naming set

`nest-rs-*` is the framework's. A package you publish is `nestrs-<subject>` —
NestJS's split between `@nestjs/*` and `nestjs-*` — and every name it owns is
rooted at its crate: paths `nestrs_<subject>::…`, span targets
`nestrs_<subject>[::<concern>]`, units `nestrs_<subject>.<unit>`, configuration
`<PREFIX>_<SUBJECT>__*`, datastore keys `nestrs_<subject>:…`, and the crates.io
keyword `nestrs`. The two spellings part at their fifth character, so
`nest_rs=off` never silences a package and `SCAN nestrs:*` never matches its
keys. The driver pages, the queue crate's extension contract and
[Writing a package](/semver/#naming-a-package) now say so; a third-party crate
that followed the old driver pages renames itself before it publishes.

### No third-party error or trait in the API of `authn`, `core` or `config`

A library a developer never writes in their own code is wrapped: its next major is a
nestrs minor.

- **Breaking: `PasswordError`'s variants carry the hasher's error as
  `Arc<dyn Error + Send + Sync>`**, no longer `argon2::password_hash::Error`.
  `PasswordError` is `#[non_exhaustive]` and loses `Copy`, `PartialEq` and `Eq`; match it
  with a pattern, never `==`. The chain reads as before, and `source()` still downcasts
  to the hasher's error.
- **Breaking: `nest_rs::core::logging::{TextFormat, JsonFormat, ConsoleWriter}` leave the
  API** — they implement `tracing_subscriber`'s traits. A binary that wrote its own
  subscriber with `ConsoleWriter` calls `install_console` (below).
- **Breaking: `nest_rs::config::crypto_provider` leaves the API**: the process's rustls
  provider is the framework's own plumbing between its crates.

### The constructors a decorator emits are hidden

Every `#[injectable]`, `#[controller]`, `#[gateway]`, `#[resolver]`, `#[mcp]`
and `#[interceptor]` emitted a public `from_container(&Container) -> Self`
(and, for `scope = request` or `transient`, `from_scope(&RequestScope)`),
documented "called by the register phase, not by hand", which panicked on a
missing dependency and froze its signature into the API. They are now
`#[doc(hidden)] pub fn __nestrs_from_container` and `__nestrs_from_scope`, the
macro ABI beside the `__nestrs_injected*` helpers, and every expansion calls
the new names. The hand-written constructors of the same shape are private to
their crate: `AuthnGuard::from_container`, `Storage::from_container` and
`McpMount::from_container`.

**Breaking:** code calling `T::from_container(&container)` or
`T::from_scope(&scope)` on a decorated type, or one of the three hand-written
constructors, no longer compiles. The container builds the type: resolve it
with `container.get::<T>()` from a booted app (`TestApp`, `App`), or register
it into a container in a test with `<T as Discoverable>::register(builder)`.
`AuthnGuard::new(strategy)` and `Storage::new(config)` build one by hand; an
MCP mount is built by the boot.

### A headless app links no HTTP stack it does not serve

A worker or a scheduler built without `http` used to compile the HTTP edge and
poem anyway, through two crates that never needed them at run time.

- **Fixed: a worker importing SeaORM without HTTP no longer links poem.**
  `nest-rs-seaorm`'s HTTP binding — `Bind`, `#[crud]`'s error mapping — was
  already compiled only under its `http` feature, but the crate depended on
  `nest-rs-http`, the 6.x interceptor crate and poem unconditionally.
  They now come with `http` alone, which the umbrella's `http` turns on as
  before: `nest-rs = { …, default-features = false, features = ["queue",
  "redis", "seaorm"] }` builds without poem.
- **Breaking: the HTTP half of the test harness is `nest-rs-testing`'s `http`
  feature**, which the umbrella's `http` turns on (`graphql`, `ws`, `mcp` and
  every capability serving over HTTP imply it). `TestApp`, `TestAppBuilder::{build,
  build_ws, use_*_global}`, `nest_rs::testing::mcp` and the re-exported
  `TestClient`, `TestJson`, `TestForm`, `TestRequestBuilder` and `TestResponse`
  exist only there. A headless suite starts from `HeadlessApp::builder()`, which
  compiles with `features = ["queue", "testing"]` and no `http`:

  ```rust
  let app = HeadlessApp::builder().module::<WorkerModule>().build_headless().await?;
  ```

  `TestApp::builder()` keeps working wherever `http` is on, so an app on the
  default features sees no change. A queue driver's `KitBackend::app` returns
  `HeadlessApp::builder()`, and `queue_kit!` runs in a driver crate that links
  no HTTP.
- **Fixed: a transport the harness abandons is aborted.** `HeadlessApp::close`
  held a spawned transport to its stop bound plus the settle and named it at
  `error`, but left its task running into the shutdown hooks; it now aborts
  it, as `App::run` does.
- **Fixed: two spawned transports of one type are each held to their own stop
  bound.** The first to return removed the other's entry by its type name, so
  `HeadlessApp::close` could abandon the one still stopping at the wrong bound.
- **Fixed: a spawned transport that panics is contained.** Its panic is filed
  once at `error` on the app's target (`a transport the harness spawned
  panicked`, with the panic and the transport), and its `TransportHandle`
  answers `transport `…` panicked`, as `App::run` contains a transport.
- `EphemeralDatabase` bounds its statements with SeaORM's own default rather
  than a copy of it.

### GraphQL stops compiling sea-orm; a decorator attribute names the feature it needs

- **Breaking: the umbrella's `graphql` no longer turns `authz` on.** A GraphQL
  gateway or BFF with no database builds without `nest-rs-authz` and sea-orm. A
  resolver gating with `#[authorize]` names its feature, as WebSocket and MCP
  apps already do — or `seaorm`, which implies it:

  ```toml
  nest-rs = { version = "7.0", features = ["graphql", "authz"] }
  ```

- **Added: an attribute whose expansion needs a feature the build lacks says
  which one.** `#[authorize]` without `authz`, or `#[crud]` and
  `#[authorize(…, bind = …)]` without `seaorm`, used to fail as rustc's
  `E0433` inside the expansion; on every edge it is now a compile error naming
  the attribute and the `nest-rs` line to add, before rustc's own errors:

  ```text
  error: `#[authorize]` expands into `nest_rs::authz`, which this build does not enable. Add the feature to Cargo.toml:

             nest-rs = { version = "7.0", features = ["authz"] }
  ```

  A method compiled out by a `#[cfg]` takes the check with it, as it takes its
  wrapper.

### A module attaches a transport with `provide_transport`

A module gained a transport by hand-building a struct the crate marked "do not
hand-construct", `nest_rs::core::__private::TransportContribution { name, build }`, through
the untyped `provide_meta`: its own doctest built it, a package writing a transport had no
public path to one, and the name the boot lines carried was a string the author typed, free
to drift from the type it named.

- **`ContainerBuilder::provide_transport::<T>(build)`** attaches one: `App::run` builds it
  from the assembled container, configures it, has it listen and serves it, as before. Its
  name is read off `T` — its short type name, so the boot lines still say `HttpTransport`,
  `QueueWorker` and `Scheduler`. `build` is a function, `fn(&Container) ->
  anyhow::Result<T>`: a non-capturing closure reads as before, and a closure holding a value
  is refused at compile time, since what a transport needs is read from the container, where
  a `for_root` value has landed.
- **`nest_rs::core::TransportContribution`** is the record the kernel reads, public and
  read-only beside `WiringContribution` and `CloseContribution`: `name()` through
  `Discovery::meta`, no public field, no constructor.
- **A third-party transport is supported, and adds no edge.** Core's crate documentation
  and the extending page state what one does: serve infrastructure no caller identifies,
  carry system work into an edge that exists under the actor `Correlation::continued`
  carries, or ride HTTP as a self-mount whose guards decide when it is request-shaped and
  must authenticate.
- **A `configure` that fails carries its step**, as a `listen` that fails does: the boot
  error downcasts to `BootStep::Configure { transport }` and reads ``the `configure` of
  transport `X`: …``, a panic in `configure` included.
- **The test harness names a transport as the kernel does**, by its short type name, in
  the lines `HeadlessApp` files and in `UnboundedTransportError`, whose `transport` is now
  an owned `String`.
- **Breaking:** `nest_rs::core::__private::TransportContribution` is gone, and
  `UnboundedTransportError::transport` is a `String`.

### A package mounts a surface of its own through the seam the framework uses

A self-mount — a surface beyond the route table, answering under a path it owns — declares a
`static` `Handler` whose posture says how the guard pool meets it. Only
`nest_rs::core::__private::declare_handler` built one, so a package mounting its own surface
had to reach a hidden item, while OpenAPI's document, `/mcp` and `/graphql` reached it there.

- **`nest_rs::http::declare_endpoint::<M>(name, address, posture)`** is a `const fn` building
  that site from what a mount varies: the mounting type and its name, the mount path, the
