  `Posture`. Its unit is `http.request`; it names no method and declares no metadata.

  ```rust
  use nest_rs::core::{Handler, Posture};
  use nest_rs::http::declare_endpoint;

  pub struct StatusPageModule;

  static PAGE: Handler =
      declare_endpoint::<StatusPageModule>("StatusPageModule", "/status", Posture::Public);
  ```

  `HttpEndpointMeta::new(&PAGE, "/status", "status", |_, routes| { … })` then mounts it;
  OpenAPI's document and every `#[mcp]` endpoint build their sites the same way.

### `nest-rs-codegen`'s root is a kit for third-party decorators; the rest is `__private`

`nest-rs-codegen` is published, so every name its root re-exported — a
hundred-odd helpers and three public modules — was semver-public, while its
manifest called it internal, its README invited third-party macro crates, and
the upgrading guide taught decorator authors the job table and the pair
table. Its status is now one, on every surface:

- **The crate's root is the kit** a third-party `proc-macro` crate builds a
  decorator with, covered by semver, each item with a runnable example:
  `Grammar` and `Arg` read a decorator's keys and word its refusals as every
  framework decorator does; `Refusals` (new) keeps every refusal of an item and
  reports them at once; `duration_millis` reads a duration; `pascal_case` and
  `snake_case` derive a name; `take_flag_attr`, `take_single_attr`,
  `take_path_list` and `repeated_attribute` take a marker attribute off an
  item; `reroot` roots the finished expansion.
- **Two rules for a decorator of your own**, in the crate's documentation: it
  emits a framework path as `::nest_rs_<concern>::<documented item>` and
  passes its expansion through `reroot`, never a hidden path — a package's
  macro source is written against one 7.x and runs in apps on later ones,
  where hidden items differ — and it emits its own items as
  `::<its crate>::…`.
- **Depend on `nest-rs-codegen = "7.N"` from a `proc-macro` crate only.** The
  kit names `syn` 3 and `proc-macro2` 1 types, so their next majors are a
  NestRS major.
- **The repository's `clippy.toml` refuses `syn`'s meta parsers** —
  `syn::meta::parser`, `syn::Attribute::parse_nested_meta` and
  `syn::MetaList::parse_nested_meta` — in favour of `Grammar`, as the guide
  already said it did.

**Breaking:** every other item moved to `nest_rs_codegen::__private` and is not
API: the job table (`JobDecorator`, `job_key`, `transactional_value`), the pair
table (`pair::HTTP`, `DecoratorPair`), `CrudDeclaration`, `RoutePath`,
`MountPath`, `reject_path`, `VersionedEdge`, `take_meta`, `PostureRules`, the
`#[injectable]` body builders and the shared refusal sentences. A third-party
decorator that used one keeps its own copy, or puts the framework's decorator
on the item (`#[injectable]` on the struct).

### The install surface: 30 capabilities

`nest-rs` installs as one dependency whose features are the capabilities, and
7.0 counts 30 of them where 6.1 counted 31: three arrive, four leave, and four
mean less than they did.

- **Added:** `redis-queue`, the job queue on Redis Streams, which `redis`
  carried in 6.x; `redis-schedule`, the occurrence lock a `replicas = "one"`
  job claims through; `static-files`, a built front end served as the router's
  fallback.
- **Breaking — removed:** `interceptors`, `filters` and `exception-filters`:
  `Interceptor`, `ExceptionFilter` and `#[interceptor]` are `nest_rs::core`'s
  and take no feature, and an exception filter over `dyn Error + Send + Sync`
  replaces `Filter`. `worker`: `queue` and `schedule` carry what a job needs,
  and its job-context seam (`JobContext`, `JobTransaction`,
  `run_in_job_context`) is gone with the unit-of-work port
  (`nest_rs::database::{UnitOfWork, run_unit}`). The deadline every
  `#[process]` and `#[scheduled]` method defaults to,
  `nest_rs::core::JOB_TIMEOUT`, is new in 7.0 and takes no feature.
- **Breaking — narrowed:** `redis` is the connection alone (`RedisModule`,
  `RedisConnection`), each binding a feature of its own, and no longer turns
  `queue` on; `queue` no longer turns `worker` on; `graphql` no longer turns
  `authz` on, so a resolver gating with `#[authorize]` names `authz`, or
  `seaorm`, which implies it; `testing` alone is the headless harness
  (`HeadlessApp`), and `TestApp` comes with `http`.
- Every other feature keeps its name and its meaning. `default` is still
  `http` and `config`, and `full` every capability but `testing`.

A manifest naming a removed feature fails to resolve: Cargo reports that
`nest-rs` does not have that feature. CI builds the umbrella and the decorator
witness under each feature alone, which is what keeps every row above true.

- **Fixed: `nestrs g entity` and `nestrs g migration` no longer turn `http`
  on.** Both enabled `seaorm` and `http` on the `nest-rs` line they edited, so
  a headless project (`default-features = false`) that generated an entity,
  or the migrations crate, compiled the HTTP stack again. They enable `seaorm`
  alone; `g resource` and the authz adapters still enable `http`, through
  `authz`, since what they generate serves a controller.
- **Fixed: `nestrs g http` turns `http` on.** It enabled no feature, so on a
  headless project the controller it wrote imported `nest_rs::http`, which
  the umbrella gates on `http`, and did not compile. Every edge generator now
  enables its own edge's feature.

### Each crate says what it holds

The description crates.io shows and the documentation's first paragraph are
rewritten to the code:

- The decorator crates list the decorators they export: `nest-rs-graphql-macros`
  named a `#[graphql]` that does not exist and left out `#[operations]`,
  `#[crud]` and `#[dataloader]`; `nest-rs-http-macros` claimed `#[interceptor]`,
  which is `nest-rs-core-macros`'; `nest-rs-mcp-macros` left out `#[tools]`.
- `nest-rs-ws` names its pair, `#[gateway]` and `#[messages]`;
  `nest-rs-authz` lists its `ws` binding; `nest-rs-redis` opens one connection
  per instance; `nest-rs-core` names the pipeline its interceptors and
  exception filters run on.
- `nest-rs-oauth-client`'s documentation sends client authentication at a
  token endpoint to `nest-rs-oauth-server`, where it lives.
- No published description spells a variable `NESTRS_…`: `nest-rs-seaorm`'s
  reads `<PREFIX>_SEAORM__*`, the name a deployment that renamed its prefix
  actually sets.

### Public types leave room for what 7.x adds

A 7.x release adds a variant or a field only where the type says it may — `#[non_exhaustive]`,
or a private field — and adding either after 7.0 would itself break programs. Every public
enum and struct now says it where a later release will grow it: the next versioning strategy,
OpenTelemetry's other span kinds, MCP's resources, a new boot-error detail, a field a page or a
token response gains (`docs/semver`, *Match and build the rest the way it grows*).

- **Enums are `#[non_exhaustive]`**, a `match` outside the framework ending in `_`:
  `nest_rs::http::{ApiVersioning, ClientOrigin, RequestBodyMeta}`, `nest_rs::authz::{Action,
  FieldSet, Predicate, MaskReplyError}`, `nest_rs::core::operation_log::Kind`,
  `nest_rs::core::logging::LogFormat`, `nest_rs::config::{DurationUnit, Floor}`,
  `nest_rs::graphql::SeedLifetime`, `nest_rs::guards::GuardPhase`,
  `nest_rs::health::IndicatorStatus`, `nest_rs::mcp::McpOperationKind`,
  `nest_rs::redis::RedisTopology`, `nest_rs::schedule::{OccurrenceClaim, Replicas, Trigger}`,
  `nest_rs::ws::WsReply`, and the testing drivers' `GraphqlEvent`, `GraphqlRefusal`, `WsFrame`,
  `WsRead`. Kept exhaustive, because a standard or their own definition closes them and a
  caller gains from naming each arm: `ProbeKind` (Kubernetes' three probes), `Delay`,
  `Attempt` and `Unproved` (a proof's attempt and its end), `Access` (found, denied, missing)
  and `ReplyOutcome`.
- **Errors and outcomes are `#[non_exhaustive]`**, their fields still readable: the kernel's
  boot refusals (`AccessGraphError`, `MissingDependencyError`, `ScopeViolationError`,
  `ContestedDeclarationError`, `UnresolvedFactoryError`, `FactoryCycleError`,
  `FactoryFailedError`, `BootInterruptedError`, `UnboundedTransportError`, `InitFailedError`,
  `UnregisteredModuleError`, `BudgetPastNetError`, `WiringFailedError`,
  `UnresolvedLayerError`), `Discovered`, `MalformedRuleError`, `JobError`,
  `Bound`, `Material`, `IndicatorReport`, `ProbeReport`, `PrincipalClaim`, `ClientIp`,
  `DeprecationMeta`, `AuthorizationRedirect`, `AccessTokenRequest`, `AccessTokenResponse`,
  `RegisteredClient`, `AuthenticatedClient`, `Occurrence`, `Page`, `PageParams`,
  `RelationKey`, `RelationPage`, `CapturedEvent`, `CapturedSpan`, `TestCertificate`,
  `WsEnvelope`, `WsError` and `OpenApiLicense`. What a program builds by hand has a
  constructor: `MissingDependencyError::new`, `Bound::new`, `Material::inline`,
  `Occurrence::new`, `Page::new`, `PageParams::new`,
  `RegisteredClient::new`, `AuthenticatedClient::new`, `OpenApiLicense::new` with
  `with_identifier` / `with_url`.
- **A seam you fill by hand is built through its constructor**:
  `SocialProviderEntry::new::<Provider, Module>(key, config_namespace, build)`, which names the
  module gating the provider as a type; `GraphqlContextSeed::new(owner, lifetime, seed)`, its
  owner an `Option<fn() -> TypeId>`; `CronJobMeta::new(origin, provider, method, trigger, run)`
  at the defaults a trigger without keys takes, and `with_timeout`, `with_transaction`,
  `with_replicas`, `with_key` for what a key would set. Each is a `const fn`, so it sits in
  `inventory::submit!`.
- **A registry entry a decorator submits is read through accessors**: `ScheduledMethod`,
  `ListenerMethod` and `HealthIndicator` — like `ProcessMethod` — expose `origin()`,
  `provider_type_id()` (what a reader gates on `ReachableProviders`) and what was declared
  (`provider()`, `method()`, `trigger()`, `replicas()`, `name()`, `kind()`, …); only the
  decorator builds one.
- `ClientOrigin::ip()` is the address a request is attributed to, `None` with no peer at all.
- Kept as they are, because no growth reaches them or the way they are built is how they grow:
  a newtype over its one value (`Json(value)`), a shape a standard closes (`TraceParent`,
  Server-Timing's `Entry`), and the configurations — a `#[config]`, a `Default` struct nested in
  one, and `OpenTelemetryConfig`, which now grows as a `#[config]` does: a later 7.x may add a
  field, so start from `new` or `from_env` and set what you change.

**Breaking**, each a compile error with its fix beside it: an exhaustive `match` on an enum
above needs a `_` arm; a struct literal of a type above is its constructor; a field read on
`ScheduledMethod`, `ListenerMethod`, `HealthIndicator`, `CronJobMeta` or
`SocialProviderEntry` is the accessor of its name (`provider_type_id()` answers the `TypeId`
itself). What only the boot runs has no reader any more, and no 7.0 spelling: the `run` thunk
of a `ScheduledMethod` or a `HealthIndicator`, a `ListenerMethod`'s `wire` and
`declaration_index`, and a `SocialProviderEntry`'s `build`, `provider_type_name` and `module`
(its `key()` and `config_namespace()` remain).

### A compile error, a boot refusal and a boot line name what the developer wrote

The identity audit read every refusal the framework says to a developer and every
line it says to an operator, and found them naming what the container holds rather
than what the app wrote or sets. Each now says it in the developer's words.

- **A compile error names a framework path as `nest_rs::<concern>::…`**, the path an
  app writes: each concern of the umbrella is a module over a glob of its crate
  (`pub mod graphql { pub use nest_rs_graphql::*; }`), so rustc no longer prints
  `nest_rs::nest_rs_graphql::async_graphql::Error`. No path an app or an expansion
  writes moves.
- **A handler parameter that reads nothing** — a DTO without `Json<…>`, a service —
  says "`Settings` is not a handler parameter", with what reads a body, a path, a
  query and a service, where rustc said `the trait bound … FromRequest<'_> is not
  satisfied`.
- **A `#[process]` method whose argument is not its queue's job** says "`#[process]`
  takes `String`, and its queue's job is `DemoCommand`", at the `queue = …` written,
  where rustc's E0271 blamed the queue's declaration.
- **A provider-hosted decorator, or `as dyn Trait`, on a host that is no singleton**
  says the one cause it has — "`#[hooks]` runs on a provider built once for the app,
  and `PerRequest` is `scope = request`, built per request: move these methods to a
  plain `#[injectable]` provider" — never one sentence listing every decorator, host
  and scope.
- **Two methods claiming one key under `#[cfg]`s that both hold** are refused in the
  decorator's sentence on every edge, where rustc printed a mangled marker name.
- **`#[crud]` in a build without its features** names itself and every feature in
  one error — "`#[crud]` expands into `nest_rs::authz` and `nest_rs::seaorm` … features
  = ["authz", "seaorm"]" — where three errors blamed an `#[authorize]` never written.
- **`#[derive(PrincipalIdentity)]`** refuses an actor or tenant that prints nothing,
  and an `Option` scope list, at the field, in its own words.
- **A misuse is one sentence, naming no expansion item.** A `#[crud]` op the service
  does not offer is said three times where it was five, each in the sentence above, since
  the parameters `#[crud]` generates are probed for no gate or shaper; a route under
  `#[authorize(A, bind = S)]` reads its entity off its `Authorized<A, E>` parameter and
  checks the service's once, so a swapped `bind = Read` is three errors in one sentence
  where it was five; `cannot`, `when` and `fields` on a capability, or on any type that
  is not an entity, say "`Exporting` has no rows to deny or narrow: `cannot`, `when` and
  `fields` take a SeaORM entity", where rustc asked for a SeaORM entity and pointed at
  the migration table's; a posture on a type that is neither an entity nor a
  `#[subject]` says so, then "`#[authorize]` arms no reply mask for this subject"; a
  `#[resource]` answer that is no body, an MCP operation that returns no `Result`, an
  `#[on_event]` method that returns neither `()` nor a `Result<(), E>` and a hand-written
  `Instance` each say what is expected, without the framework's own implementors; and
  `Authorize<A, S>`, which a handler does not write, is no longer offered among a wrong
  parameter's extractors. `Guard`'s rustdoc opens with an implementation writing
  `async fn check(&self, cx: &mut UnitContext<'_>) -> Result<(), Denial>`.
- **A boot refusal names its types as an app writes them** (`HttpConfig`, never
  `nest_rs_http::config::HttpConfig`) — a duplicate, a contest, a cycle, a failed or
  late factory, an unclaimed override, the key an override removed a provider through,
  a layer no module provides, two `#[meta]` values of one type; a type a program
  matches on stays whole in the error's field. **A `#[config]` that fails to read fails
  the boot with its own refusal**, the variable first — `invalid value for
  <PREFIX>_HTTP__PORT: …` — never "the factory for … failed", and a stop during it
  names "the read of `HttpConfig`". A validation failure lists each field with its
  variable — `client_id (<PREFIX>_ISSUER__CLIENT_ID): length` — and a field read whole
  as JSON (`#[config(json)]`) names its own for whatever failed inside it,
  `limits.max (<PREFIX>_KILN__LIMITS): range`. A stop during the boot
  names its signal (`stopped by SIGTERM`), an init hook its attribute and its method,
  and a scope violation no longer explains the kernel's "singleton map". The app-wide
  pool refusals name the module to list the pool in, and the wiring step names a pool
  by its declaration's type (`GlobalGuards`).
- **A guard reading the HTTP request in `check`** on a GraphQL field, a WebSocket
  message or an MCP operation is named, with the edit — `guard` and `hint` on "a guard
  read the HTTP request of a unit with no HTTP view".
- **A provider injecting `JwtService` or `OAuthClient`** with no module building it is
  told `nest_rs::authn::AuthnModule` or `nest_rs::oauth::client::OAuthClientModule`.
- **A health indicator the app reaches without `HealthModule`** is named at run, as an
  edge's host without its transport is: `host not served: the health probes are not
  mounted`, with the host, its module and the import, and no `edge`, since health is
  none.
- **Every unit an edge serves is one `mounted` line** with `host` and `method` —
  the struct and the Rust method — and its address in a field of its own: HTTP `verb`
  and `path`, GraphQL `field`, WS `path` and `event`, MCP one line per `tool`, `prompt`
  or `resource`, queue `queue`, schedule `trigger` (`every`, `after`, `cron`). The six
  messages (`mounted route`, `mounted operation`, `mounted message`, `mounted mcp
  host`, `registered queue processor`, `scheduled job (…)`) are gone, and the module
  line at register reads `module registered`, no longer "module dependencies
  initialized".

**Breaking:** `ConfigError::Validation` is `#[non_exhaustive]` and carries
`whole_fields`, the fields read whole from one variable; a match on it takes `..`, and
it is built with `ConfigError::validation`.

**Breaking:** `#[crud]`'s `service` names the service's type (`service = UsersService`),
never the field. A `#[controller]` or `#[resolver]` injecting one type in two fields is
refused at the second: the container holds one instance of it; two fields spelling it
two ways (a path, an alias) are refused by rustc's E0119 at the struct's decorator.
`AbilityBuilder::cannot`, `RuleSpec::when` and `RuleSpec::fields` are bounded on
`nest_rs::authz::RowSubject` (new), which every entity is, and `fields` takes its column
type as a parameter; a call naming an entity compiles as before.

What fails if you get it wrong: a log filter or dashboard keyed on one of the six old
messages, or on `controller`, `handler`, `resolver`, `gateway`, `processor`, the
schedule's `provider` or MCP's `kind` and `tools`, reads nothing — key it on `mounted`,
`host` and `method`. On HTTP's mount line `method` is the Rust method and the HTTP
method is `verb`, so a filter on `method="GET"` there reads nothing; the request's own
line keeps `method` for the HTTP method. Code downcasting a boot error to
`FactoryFailedError` to read a config failure finds the `ConfigError` itself.

### `nestrs g events`, every generator compiles, and the doctor reads what the app reads

- **Every crate is 7.0.0**, `nest-rs-cli` included. `nestrs new` and every
  `nestrs g` write `nest-rs = "7.0"`. **The framework requires itself at exactly
  its release**: every `nest-rs-*` crate depends on its siblings at `=7.0.0`,
  because a decorator's expansion calls `#[doc(hidden)]` seams — its own runtime
  crate's and, through the code `nest-rs-codegen` writes, up to eleven others' —
  that semver does not cover. An application requiring `nest-rs = "7.0"` sees no
  difference, and a partial `cargo update -p` can no longer pair one crate's
  expansion with another's seams. A dev-dependency on a sibling is a path and
  nothing else, so a published manifest carries no test-only edge.
- **The `compose.yml` `nestrs new` writes runs PostgreSQL 18**, where it ran 16,
  its volume mounted at `/var/lib/postgresql`, where the 18 image keeps its data
  under a directory per major. A project generated before keeps its file; one
  moving to 18 upgrades its data (`pg_upgrade`, or a dump and restore) or starts
  from an empty volume.
- **Fixed: a project `nestrs new` generates ran its test suites on the Redis database its
  development app drains.** Both `.env.development` and `.env.test` named
  `redis://localhost:6379`, database 0, so a job an e2e suite enqueued could be run by the app
  `nestrs run dev` was serving, and that app's jobs by a suite's worker. `.env.test` now names
  `redis://localhost:6379/1`, with a comment saying why; `.env.development` keeps 0. A project
  generated before keeps its files.
- **`nestrs g events <feature>`** writes an event listener adapter —
  `events/listener.rs` and `<Feature>EventsModule` — and the fact it listens for at
  the port as `event.rs`: the last edge of the closed vocabulary without a
  generator.
- **Fixed: `nestrs g graphql <feature>` over a `nestrs g resource` port wrote a
  resolver that did not compile**, in 6.0 and 6.1: it bound
  `#[use_guards(AuthnGuard, AuthzGuard)]` on a `#[resolver]`, and `AuthnGuard` has
  no GraphQL check. The generated resolver binds `AuthzGuard` alone, as the demo's
  do, and authentication runs per operation through the `AuthzGraphqlModule` bridge
  the adapter's module imports.
- **Fixed: the grant `nestrs g resource` and `nestrs g entity` print compiles
  where it is pasted.** It named `Action` and an `ab` that the scaffolded `define`
  neither imports nor declares (its builder is `_ab` until something is granted),
  so the paste failed twice. The grant is now one self-contained line,
  `ab.can(nest_rs::authz::Action::Manage, crate::<feature>::Entity);`, that can be
  pasted once per resource, and the step says `_ab` becomes `ab`. A scaffold test
  pastes what both generators print into `define` and compiles it.
- **Every adapter generator is compiled, not only read.** The CLI's e2e suite runs
  all seven edges over both port shapes from inside an app, the second app
  `nestrs new` adds to a workspace and `g migration`, and holds the result to the
  scaffold's own `clippy -D warnings`; a unit test joins its edge list to the edges
  the CLI knows, so a new edge cannot ship uncompiled. The text assertions that
  covered `g graphql` read a wrong import as readily as a right one.
- **`nestrs g queue` says how to push to the queue it declared** — inject
  `Arc<nest_rs::queue::Jobs>` and import `RedisQueueModule` in the pushing app — where it named only the worker's half.
  The generated processor notes `concurrency` (one attempt at a time per replica by
  default) and the retry budget, and the generated tick notes `replicas = "one"`,
  which needs `RedisScheduleModule` and the `redis-schedule` feature.
- **`nestrs doctor` follows the loader.** An empty shell variable hides both `.env`
  spellings, nothing is trimmed that the loader keeps, and `<PREFIX>_ENV=prod` sends
  it to `.env.production` rather than a `.env.prod` no app reads. Each variable is
  answered as the loader reads it — a differential test in `nest-rs-cli`'s own
  suite runs the two side by side on every shape a variable takes, the loader a
  path-only dev-dependency that `cargo install` never links: a `_FILE` naming a
  file that holds nothing is `not set`, and one naming a missing or unreadable
  file, or a variable given both inline and as a file, is reported as failing the
  app's boot and blocks. Its tests no longer read the developer's shell or working
  directory.
- `nestrs doctor -p <dir>` opens a relative `<NAME>_FILE` from `<dir>`, where the
  app started there opens it, rather than from doctor's own working directory.
- `nestrs doctor` fails, non-zero, on a `.env` naming `<PREFIX>_ENV` or
  `NESTRS_ENV_PREFIX`: every app started there aborts at its first config read,
  since the loader refuses such a cascade whole.
- **A new project's recipes are the framework's.** `nestrs run test` takes one
  kind per suite — `unit` (the `#[cfg(test)]` modules), `integration` (in
  process, no database), `e2e`, `doc`, `cov` — and runs every kind when bare,
  where it listed them. **Breaking for a habit:** `nestrs run test unit` ran the
  integration suites and the doctests too; it is now the unit tests alone, and
  `integration` is the no-database run. `nestrs run ci` runs the lint, then every
  test; the lint's clippy covers every feature; and each generated `lib.rs`
  denies warnings in its doc examples. An existing project keeps its own
  `Justfile`.
- **The scaffold follows 7.0**: `.env` explains the `_FILE` form and that a secret
  beside an EdDSA key fails the boot, every `main` — the app's, `migrate`'s and
  `seed`'s — is `#[nest_rs::main]`, with `tokio` a dev-dependency, the queue
  template pushes with `push(Q, job, None)`, every generated feature root declares
  its log target (`pub const TARGET: &str = "features::<feature>";`) and the WS
  template logs on it through `error_message`, and the `AGENTS.md` a new project
  carries states the rules as
  7.0 reads them — `events/` is an edge and never a plural folder, a family is a
  level of a config namespace, a namespace belongs to one `#[config]`, `Config`
  names a `#[config]` and nothing else, every type a `module.rs` declares shares
  its stem, the queue port owns a job's id and its retry budget, and an edge folder directly
  under a framework crate's `src/` takes the crate's subject
  (`nest-rs-x/src/http/controller.rs` is `XController`). It says how each edge is
  guarded — a controller and a gateway bind `AuthnGuard, AuthzGuard`, a resolver
  binds `AuthzGuard` behind `AuthzGraphqlModule`, MCP gates through
  `AuthzMcpModule` — names the paths a project depending on the umbrella can type
  (`nest_rs::config::var_name`, `nest_rs::core::EnvPrefix::var`), says
  `EphemeralDatabase` needs the `testing` and `seaorm` features, and lists all
  seven edge folders.

### The demo follows 7.0

- **The OAuth resource variables wear the family's namespace** in `.env` and the
  chart — `<PREFIX>_OAUTH_RESOURCE__*` was read by nothing, so the `assistant` app
  booted without its RFC 9728 identity and refused to start. The `.env` note now
  names `/.well-known/oauth-protected-resource`, the path RFC 9728 fixes.
- **The chart's KEDA triggers are `redis-streams` on
  `nestrs:queue:{audio}:jobs` and `nestrs:queue:{notifications}:jobs`**, by
  `streamLength`, and its README and install notes describe the 7.0 worker:
  leases, retries held back on Redis's clock, per-method concurrency, and why a
  deployment taking delayed jobs keeps `minReplicaCount: 1`. Every app's pod keeps the
  default 30-second grace period: the worker's drain now fits inside it, and the
  live app's sockets close at the signal. The chart is version 0.2.0, for app
  version 7.0.0.
- **The audio transcode runs four at a time**, `#[process(concurrency = 4)]`, and
  streams the object from storage into storage (`get_stream` → `put_stream`), so an
  attempt holds one multipart part rather than the whole file twice. Its KEDA
  trigger's length moves from 5 to 20 — the backlog one replica is sized for,
  now that a replica runs four — and the notifications method stays at one.
- **The worker purges notifications older than 30 days once an hour, on one
  replica**: `#[every("1h", replicas = "one")]` on a new
  `NotificationsScheduleModule`, wired beside `nest_rs::redis::RedisScheduleModule`
  under the umbrella's `redis-schedule` feature, each occurrence claimed under
  `nestrs:schedule:claims:features:NotificationsTasks:purge_expired:<instant>`. It
  deletes through `Repo`, at most a thousand rows a tick. The chart's README says
  scaling the worker does not multiply its schedule.
- **The api's synthetic transcode seed fires once per occurrence across its
  replicas**: `AudioTasks::enqueue_transcode` enqueues work every replica would
  otherwise repeat, so it declares `replicas = "one"`, and the api binds
  `RedisScheduleModule`; its heartbeat says each process is alive, so it declares
  `replicas = "each"`, written out.
- **Breaking for a copy of the demo: its issuer config is `SignInConfig`**, read from
  `<PREFIX>_SIGN_IN__*`, and its registered clients are the framework's
  `OAuthServerConfig`, under `<PREFIX>_OAUTH__SERVER__CLIENTS` — it was `IssuerConfig`,
  under `<PREFIX>_ISSUER__*`, a namespace its path did not name.
- **A publish whose transaction rolls back enqueues no notification**, now that
  events wait for the commit; an e2e test drives both outcomes against real
  Postgres and Redis.
- **The demo's log targets are constants its features declare**: each feature
  that logs declares `TARGET` at its module root and logs on
  `crate::<feature>::TARGET`; a literal target at a call site is a review
  finding.
- **Every error the demo logs goes through `error_message`**, at all eleven sites,
  so a wrapper such as `AudioError::Queue` names the cause beneath it.
- The posts MCP host reports itself as a `ServerConfig` read from `nest_rs::mcp`, the
  audio feature pushes through `push(AudioQueue, command, None)`, the OAuth
  config's `CLIENTS` is decoded through `ConfigService::json` — so a mistyped value
  is never quoted, a client secret included — and the e2e suites read their database and
  Redis URLs through `ConfigService`, so a `_FILE` spelling reaches them.
- The live app's e2e suite boots on a throwaway database, as every other suite
  over SeaORM does, and never opens the one `<PREFIX>_SEAORM__URL` names — one of its
  tests seeded that database directly, failed on an unmigrated one and could leave
  rows behind. `cargo check --locked` works in `demo/` again.
- **The demo's e2e suites no longer share Redis with a running app.** A committed
  `.env.test` puts the test profile on logical database 1, which nothing drains,
  and each test that starts a worker takes a database of its own, 2 to 8, from
  `features::testing::RedisDatabase`. The worker's e2e boots on a throwaway
  Postgres: it used to run whatever a developer's `api` or another suite had
  queued, against the main database, and a push-only suite's jobs were run by a
  developer's `nestrs run dev` worker against real data. Redis's logical
  databases are split between development and the two workspaces' suites — 0 the
  developer's, 1 to 8 the demo's, 9 to 16 the framework's, its shared tests on 16 —
  on every Valkey of the dev container, which declares thirty-two, and a database
  outside 9 to 31 declared by `nest-rs-redis`'s e2e suite does not compile.

### The demo's password hash never prints

The demo's `user` entity stored its hash as a bare `Option<String>`: `Model` printed it
redacted, but the `ActiveModel` and `ActiveModelEx` sea-orm derives `Debug` for printed it
whole once it was `Set`. The column is now `PasswordHash`, a value type whose `Debug` is
`<redacted>`; the service reads it through `as_str()`.

### A new project's committed `.env` names no plaintext URL

`nestrs new` wrote a `sslmode=disable` Postgres URL and a plaintext `redis://localhost` into the
committed `.env`, which production reads too: a deployment that forgot its own URL fell back to a
plaintext loopback connection. Both now live in a scaffolded `.env.development` and `.env.test`,
read only under those profiles; `db.just` declares the development profile, so `nestrs run db up`
still reaches `compose.yml`'s Postgres. Existing projects are not rewritten.

### `LogCapture::events_at` filters by level, and `CapturedEvent::quotes` says what a line holds

`LogCapture::events_at(tracing::Level::ERROR)` is every event filed at that
level, whatever its target, in emission order: the filter a suite asserting
that a failure is said once wrote by hand. `CapturedEvent::quotes(text)` is
whether a text appears in an event's message or in any field's value — what
a suite asserting that no line quotes a client's value or a secret read off
the fields' `Debug` by hand.

### The demo chart takes each pod out of its Service before it stops listening

Every rolling deploy of the demo sent some requests to a replica that had already closed
its listener: Kubernetes removes a terminating pod from its Service's endpoints while it
sends `SIGTERM`, kube-proxy and the ingress controllers learn of it seconds later, and the
HTTP transport stops accepting at the signal. Clients saw a refused connection or a `502`.

- **Each app's container sleeps `shutdownDelaySeconds` before the signal**, 5 by default,
  in a `preStop` hook's native `sleep` action — the distroless image has no `sleep`
  binary an `exec` hook could run. `0` renders no hook.
- **`terminationGracePeriodSeconds` is 35**, since Kubernetes counts the hook inside the
  grace: the 5-second delay plus the framework's 29.5-second way down at its defaults.
  A grace under the delay plus 29.5 seconds, rounded up, fails the render, naming the
  app, beside the chart's other render refusals.
- **Breaking (the demo chart only):** the chart needs Kubernetes 1.30
  (`kubeVersion: ">= 1.30.0-0"`), where the `sleep` action is on by default; it is
  version 0.3.0.
- The docs say the platform's half once, on the lifecycle page (*Leaving the load
  balancer first*): Kubernetes' `preStop` sleep and its grace rule, Amazon ECS's
  deregistration before `SIGTERM` within `stopTimeout`, Nomad's `shutdown_delay` with its
  5-second `kill_timeout` raised to 30, and Cloud Run, which routes elsewhere before the
  signal but kills ten seconds after it, fewer than the way down's bounds. `/health/ready`
  does not take a replica out — it answers `200` until the listener closes at the
  signal — so the platform's hook is the one supported way. Every page stating the grace
  rule now adds the `preStop` delay to it.

### One page says what every layer reads, and the docs tell one execution contract

Every layer — a guard, a pipe, an interceptor, an exception filter — is
handed one `UnitContext` on every edge, and the docs now say so in one
place: **Execution context** (`/fundamentals/execution-context/`) lists
what the context answers (`unit()`, `edge()`, `handler()`, `reflector()`,
`switch_to::<V>()`, `attach`, `ambient`, `peer()`, `deadline()`), each
edge's view and how a layer answers or settles a unit through it, what a
site declared and how `Reflector` reads it, and the ambient context with
the table of how far a value travels (`Continuation`).

- **The request lifecycle** is redrawn on the one pipeline: the seven
  stages every unit runs, and a table of which stages each unit runs — an
  HTTP route, a self-mount's request, the router's own answer, a GraphQL
  root field and a field resolver, a WebSocket message, an MCP request and
  its tool call, a job, a tick, a listener call.
- **Coming from NestJS** maps the execution context call by call
  (`ExecutionContext`, `getType`, `switchToHttp`, `getHandler`/`getClass`,
  `Reflector.get`/`getAllAndOverride`/`getAllAndMerge`), and adds the rows
  for `APP_INTERCEPTOR`, `APP_FILTER`/`APP_PIPE`, an error code on an
  `HttpException`, CLS and `registerPreRequestHook`. It no longer says a
  binary with no global guard pool fails its boot: it names the routes left
  without a guard in one `warn`.
- **Pages split along the topics they held**: *Lifecycle* keeps the hooks,
  *App state* (`/fundamentals/app-state/`) takes the run's sequence and
  `AppLifecycle`, *Shutdown* (`/fundamentals/shutdown/`) every bounded step
  from the signal to the exit and leaving the load balancer first; *Guards
  beyond HTTP* and *Pipes beyond HTTP* take the per-edge halves of the
  guards and pipes pages. Links to the moved anchors follow.
- **The decorator reference** lists the layer and posture decorators once,
  for every edge, with `#[no_pipes]`; the glossary defines unit, unit
  context, view, outcome and problem.
- **A create shown on the pipes page answers `201` with its `Location`**,
  as the responses page teaches.

### The HTTP, OpenAPI, tutorial and testing pages say what the code does

The pages that taught poem's vocabulary name nestrs's own since 7.0, and
their re-read against the code fixed what had drifted:

- **Routing**: the route-template table says a parameter never matches an
  empty segment and a root catch-all leaves `/` to a route of its own, and
  that a value is percent-decoded once; every route the controllers page
  shows declares its posture, as the one `nestrs new` writes does.
- **Creates answer `201` with their `Location`** on the extractors page and in
  the validation tutorial's output; the snippets that showed a create at `200`
  show the response a create owes.
- **HTTP configuration** lists every `HttpConfig` field — the TLS reload
  interval, the three versioning keys, the access log, the trusted proxies and
  the two event-stream bounds were missing — and no longer names a fail-secure
  boot switch 7.0 removed; a CORS policy without `max_age` is said to send no
  `Access-Control-Max-Age`, and its `403` to file the one `warn` every CORS
  refusal files.
- **Snippets that would not compile** compile: a `Valid` used and not
  imported, an `#[authorize]` naming an action that does not exist, a login
  returning a type the demo does not have, a module pinning TLS with a `let`
  at item level, a private module reached from outside.
- **Stale claims** are gone: an ability denial is a `Denial` the class gate
  answers, not an `Ok(403)`; a request runs in the unit of work
  `SeaOrmDatabaseModule` binds, not a transaction interceptor; a safe method
  runs on the pool, where each statement commits on its own, not on a
  read-only one; the tutorial no longer asks for manifest lines `nestrs new`
  wrote; the boot dials Postgres once before the pool opens, so wrong
  credentials stop it; a ranged
  `206` is never compressed; a `3xx` commits; fixtures sit in a suite's
  `main.rs`; `nestrs run test cov` needs no manual component install.

### A package author's guide, and one driver page per port, in one order

The docs now have a section for whoever writes a crate other apps import,
**Extending NestRS** (`/extending/`, moved from `/fundamentals/extending/`,
which redirects), and every page in it is built on code the framework
compiles and tests — each excerpt names the crate it is lifted from, line for
line:

- **Writing a package** (`/extending/package/`): the manifest and the naming
  rule (linked from the semver page), a module that owns a `#[config]` with
  its `for_root`, a resource it opens with its `Budget` and its
  `provide_close`, a registry it fills through `provide_wiring`, a value every
  unit carries, its tests on `TestApp` and `HeadlessApp`, and what its own
  users build on.
- **Writing a transport** (`/extending/transport/`): `Transport`'s four steps
  (`configure`, `listen`, `serve`, a finite `stop_bound`) and
  `provide_transport`; the three things a transport may do, since it adds no
  edge; its units with `unit!`, `operation_span!` and `operation_line!`;
  `HeadlessApp::spawn_transport`; and serving HTTP from a package — a
  self-mount with `declare_endpoint` and `HttpEndpointMeta`, its posture, the
  paths it may register, and an unguarded gated surface's boot `warn`.
- **Writing a decorator** (`/extending/decorator/`): a `proc-macro` crate on
  `nest-rs-codegen`'s kit — `Grammar`, `Refusals`, `reroot` and the two rules
  its expansion keeps — the registry entry it submits through
  `nest_rs::core::inventory`, and discovery gated on the app's imports with
  `ReachableProviders::of(container, what)?.reaches(provider)` and
  `report_inert_host!`. The decorator reference and the packages page link it.
- **Every driver page follows one order** — the contract, the binding and its
  `BACKEND_REMEDY`, the budget under the port's net, what a failure of the
  backend becomes, TLS, the proof at boot, health, and the test that holds
  it — on the reference binding's code:
  - **Writing a database driver** is rewritten on `nest-rs-seaorm`'s unit of
    work (`UnitOfWork`, `OpenUnit`, the settle on a tracked task), its
    binding through `provide_unit_of_work`, its ambient budgets, its
    `SafeLink`, its verified-only `sslmode`, its proof through `prove`, its
    close draining every settle before the pool, and the indicator its pool's
    module attaches. It no longer says a driver logs on `nest_rs::orm`: a
    driver logs on its own target.
  - **Writing a queue driver** gains the budget and the port's net
    (`BACKEND_TIMEOUT`), TLS through `ClientTls`, the proof at boot and
    health, installs through the umbrella alone, and its excerpts of the Redis
    binding match its code again.
  - **Writing a rate-limit store** (`/rate-limiting/writing-a-store/`) is
    split from the rate-limiting page: the `ThrottlerStore` contract, the
    pseudonyms a store outside the process is handed, the guard's fail-closed
    lines.
  - **Writing an occurrence lock** (`/schedule/writing-a-lock/`) is new: the
    `OccurrenceLock` contract — an atomic claim, the token verbatim, the hold,
    `LOCK_TIMEOUT` — and why a failure skips the occurrence.

### The demo's access tokens name their subject, a user's or a client's

The demo's `client_credentials` grant minted tokens with no `sub`, which RFC 9068 §2.2
requires — the client's own id when it acts on its own behalf — so its machine clients
carried no subject and could open no MCP session. Every token the auth app issues now names
it: `features::Subject` is `User(Uuid)` for the password login and the social callback, and
`Client(String)` for `client_credentials`, whose token also carries `client_id`. One string
on the wire, read back by its shape; the demo's sign-in refuses at boot a registered client id
a UUID parses, so no client passes for a user (RFC 9068 §5, RFC 9700 §4.15). A token
without `sub` is refused by the claims, before any route. A client still writes no post — a
post's author is a user — and is answered `403 FORBIDDEN` for it whatever its scope; a user
lacking `posts:write` keeps the `insufficient_scope` challenge naming it. The password
grant's refusal is filed once, by the account check that decides it: `login failed` at
`warn` on `features::users`, with its `reason` (`unknown_email`, `no_password_set` or
`bad_password`), naming neither the email nor the password; the token endpoint writes no
second line for it. The demo's user grants still name no `client_id`: they serve no OAuth
client.

### The demo's chat names its caller as a message's author, and transcoding requires `audio:transcode`

The demo's chat gateway sat behind `AuthnGuard` yet took a message's `author` from
its payload, so any user posted, and showed as typing, under anyone's name; its
`ModerationGuard` banned a name the client chose, reading the raw JSON to do it. A
message's author is now the subject of the token the upgrade verified, read from the
principal every message on the connection carries (`nest_rs::core::ambient::<Claims>()`).
`SendMessageDto` is an `#[input]` holding the text alone, so a payload naming an author
is refused as an unknown field, and a token naming no user neither posts nor types
(`403`, the [`Posting` capability](#a-guards-require-answers-by-cause-and-a-guard-writes-one-decision-for-every-edge)
it holds no grant of).
A client of the demo's chat sends `{"text": …}`.

The audio slice advertised `audio:transcode` in its resource metadata and granted it to
`demo-service`, yet nothing required it: an admin token narrowed to `posts:read` minted
upload URLs and enqueued transcodes. Transcoding is now a [subject no table
holds](#a-capability-no-table-holds-is-a-subject-gated-by-authorize-on-every-edge):
admins hold `Manage` on `Transcoding` behind `audio:transcode`, and every audio route
and the MCP `transcode_status` tool declare `#[authorize(Manage, Transcoding)]`, so each
answers RFC 6750's `insufficient_scope` challenge naming the scope, as the OAuth scopes
page says; a principal that is not scope-aware is gated on its role alone. The tool no
longer declares a `#[public]` it never was, and the OpenAPI document names the scope on
each audio route. The chat, MCP and scope pages follow.

### `nestrs g auth` writes claims whose subject is always present, a user's or a client's

The claims `nestrs g auth` scaffolded carried `sub: Option<Uuid>`, which cannot hold the
client id RFC 9068 §2.2 makes a machine token's subject, and which let a token naming no
subject through. The template now writes `Subject`, `User(Uuid)` or `Client(String)`, one
string on the wire read back by its shape, with `Subject::user_id` for a route writing a
user's row, and `Claims { sub: Subject, .. }`, whose `actor_id` always returns it; the
development token route mints a user's. `features` re-exports `Subject` beside `Claims` and
`Role`, and the ability policy's comment narrows a user's own rows under
`if let Some(user_id) = actor.sub.user_id()`, which a client never matches. A project
scaffolded earlier keeps its own claims: to follow, give `sub` the `Subject` type
(`nestrs g auth` in a scratch workspace prints it) and refuse any client id a UUID parses
wherever you register clients.

### Generators wire the app that serves their output from anywhere in the workspace, and the tutorial runs as written

A generator wired an app only when run from inside `apps/<x>/`, which the
tutorial told the reader not to do; even there, `g resource` wired nothing into
an app with no `SeaOrmDatabaseModule` — a freshly scaffolded one — and `g queue`
printed the queue backend for the developer to add. Followed to the letter, the
tutorial never reached a protected route: its whole-file `module.rs` blocks
dropped the `AuthnHttpModule` that serves `POST /auth/dev-token`, so the token
`curl` got a `404` and every guarded call a `401`.

- **The app a generator wires is resolved**: the one `--app <name>` names (a new
  flag on `g resource`, `g auth` and the seven adapter generators), else the one
  the working directory is in, else the workspace's only app. With several apps
  and none named, the generator writes the feature, wires nothing, and lists the
  apps and the imports to write; a name no app answers to is refused before
  anything is written. `nestrs info` says which app a generator run there wires
  into.
- **Every import the generated code needs lands in one edit of the app's
  `module.rs`**, unless the root already names it — bare, pinned or a named
  instance: the edge; `SeaOrmModule` and `SeaOrmDatabaseModule` under a
  `#[crud]` handler; `RedisQueueModule` under a processor, with `RedisModule`
  unless the root opens Redis's default instance, the one the bare binding
  reads — a root opening only `RedisModule::<Cache>` gains it;
  `AuthnHttpModule` when the run wrote the auth adapter.
- **`g resource` writes the resource's round-trip test** into the e2e suite of
  the app it wires — `tests/e2e/<name>.rs` over the auth adapter's claims and
  `TestApp::bearer`, the suite root's `boot` fixture over
  `ephemeral_database::<migrations::Migrator>()` and `ephemeral_signing_key()`, and
  the dev-dependencies they name.
- **`g entity` moves a lone `entity.rs` into `entities/`** beside the second
  entity, its `super::` paths deepened and every path naming it rewritten, in
  the same transaction; only a reference it cannot rewrite with certainty
  refuses the run, by file and line. A scaffold whose commit the system refuses
  midway is rolled back.
- **A failure names its file and its cause**: ``error: cannot read
  `apps/blog/src/module.rs`: Permission denied (os error 13)`` where the CLI
  printed the system's answer alone, and an unreadable `module.rs` fails the run
  instead of reading as an app with nothing wired.
- **A generated feature declares its log target only once it logs**: the
  `hello`, `g feature` and `g resource` ports declare none, and `g ws`, whose
  gateway logs, writes it.
- **`g graphql` over a resource leaves an entity `cargo fmt --check` accepts**:
  the `graphql` flag takes a line of its own in the resource's multi-line
  `#[expose(`, where it left a trailing space that failed `nestrs run lint`.
- **`nestrs new`'s `compose.yml` runs an S3 store** (RustFS) beside Postgres and
  Valkey, creating the bucket `.env.development` names and the suites' own that
  `.env.test` names.
- **The tutorial runs as written, from the workspace root**: page 2 runs
  `nestrs g resource posts --app blog` and edits one column, the HTTP and
  database pages read what it wrote, its `module.rs` included, and the testing
  page finishes the test it generated. `nest-rs-cli`'s new e2e suite replays the
  pages — it runs their `nestrs` commands, writes the files they edit and holds
  every file they show as generated to what the generator wrote — and runs the
  suite they end on against Postgres.

Nothing breaks: a project an earlier CLI scaffolded keeps its `TARGET`
constants and its hand-written wiring, which the generators now leave as they
are.

### The generators agree with each other, take the columns, and the tutorial replay covers every page that edits the blog

`g resource` wrote a `name` column, the migration `g migration` wrote created
none, and the round trip the resource wrote posted it: kept as generated, the
scaffold answered `500` on its first `POST` and its test failed. Every column
was typed four times in three files — the entity, the builder, the identifier
enum, the test's body — and the tutorial left that test red for four pages. `g
graphql|mcp` over a port with no entity wrote a `#[subject]` that `g entity`
then refused, handing back three steps over the CLI's own files.

- **`nestrs g resource posts title:string body:text`** takes the table's
  columns, `<name>:<type>`, the field grammar Rails' and Phoenix's generators
  share, or `<name>:<type>:nullable`; the types are `string`, `text`,
  `integer`, `bigint`, `float`, `boolean`, `uuid`, `date` and `datetime`. The
  one list writes the entity's fields, the table's migration — registered in
  `lib.rs` and `migrator.rs`, the crate bootstrapped where a workspace has none
  — and the round trip, which posts a value of each column and reads each
  back. With none, one `name:string` column runs through all three, so the
  unedited scaffold's `POST` and test pass. A name the grammar does not read, a
  type it does not know, a column named twice or one every table holds is
  refused before anything is written.
- **`nestrs g migration create_<table>` reads the entity declaring the table**:
  its `Model`'s columns go into the builder and the enum, with the default its
  key takes — an identity for an integer key SeaORM auto-increments,
  `uuidv7()` for the `Uuid` key of a table no `#[expose]` seeds, a key over
  several columns declared on the table — and a field whose type the builder
  cannot read off it is named for the developer. A migration name registered
  already is refused, naming its file, and so is a `create_<table>` whose
  table a registered one creates, however it is spelled (`create_post` after
  `create_posts`); `nestrs g resource` over such a table is refused with its
  own way through.
- **A name the generators write as a Rust identifier is never a keyword**: a
  column, a feature, a resource or an app named `type`, or `gen`, which
  edition 2024 reserves, is refused before anything is written, where a
  feature took it and wrote a `pub mod type;` that did not compile.
- **`nestrs g entity` retires the `#[subject]` the generators wrote** under the
  entity's name: `subject.rs` and its two lines in `mod.rs` go, and the
  operations `g graphql` and `g mcp` gated on it are gated on the entity, in
  the same transaction. The grant is printed, never written; a subject or an
  operation edited beyond the template is refused, naming the file.
- **`nestrs new` writes no doctest policy** into `apps/<app>/src/lib.rs` and
  `crates/features/src/lib.rs`, and its README passes the columns and
  `--app blog` to `g resource`. `AGENTS.md`'s path-to-type table leads with the
  row a product writes.
- **The tutorial's replay covers every page that edits `blog`**: the security
  page's policy and the suite it narrows, and the GraphQL page's
  `nestrs g graphql posts --app blog`, whose `{ posts { id title } }` answers
  — beside a resource of every column type, whose round trip runs against
  Postgres. Every demo excerpt on those pages carries a worded title.

One script line breaks, in either order: `nestrs g migration create_<name>`
after `nestrs g resource <name>` is refused, since the resource writes its
table's migration, and `nestrs g resource <name>` after it is refused, since
its table's migration exists — [Upgrading, step 1](https://nestrs.dev/upgrading/).

### A family's word names no product module, and the demo's sign-in takes its own

The demo's issuer feature was `oauth/`, the OAuth family's bare word: its
`<PREFIX>_OAUTH__CLIENTS` sat one letter from the framework member's
`<PREFIX>_OAUTH__CLIENT__*` — this app's own credentials as a client, the opposite role —
and a `NESTRS_OAUTH__` filter mixed product and framework settings. The architecture rule
held it up as the stem rule applied.

- **Breaking: `nestrs new` and `nestrs generate` refuse `oauth`** as a module, feature or
  instance name (`#[instance]` too), naming the family whose paths the word starts; the
  reserved vocabulary gains a `families` row. The rule says it: a product's stem never takes
  a family's word, whose first level is the members'.
- The demo's `features::oauth` is `features::sign_in` (`SignInModule`, `SignInHttpModule`,
  `SignInService`, `SignInConfig`, span target `features::sign_in`). Its registry moved to
  `<PREFIX>_OAUTH__SERVER__CLIENTS`; what the product decides stays under its own word,
  `<PREFIX>_SIGN_IN__RESOURCES` (was `<PREFIX>_OAUTH__SIGN_IN_RESOURCES`) and
  `<PREFIX>_SIGN_IN__DEFAULT_ORG_ID`. Its policy over the registry — a scope outside the set
  its rules gate on, a client id a UUID parses — refuses the boot from its service's
  module-init hook, in the config's words. The chart, `.env` and the docs follow; the
  tutorial's GraphQL page gives the real reason a resolver binds `AuthzGuard` alone:
  `/graphql` authenticates at its request, and `AuthnGuard` declares no GraphQL check.

### A suite boots on its database and signs its tokens in one line

`TestApp::bearer` signed with the app's own `JwtService`, which holds a private key only
on an issuer: on a resource server — every app of the demo but its issuer — it panicked,
so no suite called it, and the demo minted its tokens with a committed copy of the issuer's
private key and a role passed as a string. A database had two spellings, and the demo, the
code an app copies, kept the one the harness had replaced, re-pinned an `AuthnConfig` its
own `.env` held, and seeded rows through `DeriveIden` copies of its tables.

- **`TestAppBuilder::ephemeral_signing_key()`** (`authn`): the app verifies the tokens of
  an Ed25519 pair issued in process for that boot, as `TestAuthority` issues certificates,
  in place of the key material its configuration names — a secret, a key, an issuer's JWK
  Set — and signs with it only where that material signs, so a verify-only app stays one,
  and a configuration naming no key, the deployment's to set, verifies only. Its audience,
  its issuer and the resource it serves are its own, and whatever else its configuration
  refuses is refused. The boot refuses the key
  on an app that provides no `JwtService`, and on one whose `JwtService` was seeded or
  overridden past `AuthnModule`'s factory and refuses a token of the pair. The opt-in is
  one builder call: the workspace unifies the harness's features, so a key installed
  whenever `authn` is on would change a suite with the feature set.
- **`TestApp::bearer(&claims)`, and `WsApp::bearer(&claims)` for a socket's upgrade**: the
  `Authorization` value of a claims value signed with that key, for the app's audience and
  as its issuer (the resource it serves and the first authorization server it trusts, else
  its configured ones), `iat` and `jti` stamped and `exp` at the app's token lifetime when
  the claims carry none. A claim the claims carry is kept, so a
  test proving a refusal writes the `aud`, `iss` or `exp` it forges.
- **`ephemeral_database::<Migrator>()` is the one boot on a database**; a test seeding
  rows reads the connection back from the booted app, and `EphemeralDatabase::create`
  stays for a test that boots no app.
- **The demo**'s suites each boot in one line, sign with
  `app.bearer(&claims(org, Role::Admin, sub))`, seed through the features' own entities
  (`features::testing`'s `seed_org` and `seed_user`, where a row needs nothing more), and
  pin no `AuthnConfig` their app's `.env` layer holds — the api's and the live app's
  module tests prove the audience read from it, and the issuer's suite signs with the key
  its own file names. `features::testing` loses `DEV_PRIVATE_KEY`, `DEV_PUBLIC_KEY`,
  `ISSUER`, `signed_for`, `signed_by`, `token`, `token_with_scopes` and the stringly
  `token_for`, and its `ORG_ID` is a `Uuid`. The subscription test serves through
  `build_ws`, on a port the harness picks. The exemplar tests its own decisions — a
  transcode's retries, a task's replicas, the purge's period, the claims' mapping — and
  no longer a decorator's expansion, which the framework's suites prove.
- **`nestrs g resource`**'s e2e boot adds `.ephemeral_signing_key()`, and its round trip
  signs `app.bearer(&Claims { .. })`.
- **Breaking:** `TestApp::bearer` signs with that key, never the app's, and needs
  `.ephemeral_signing_key()` on the builder; `WsSocketBuilder::bearer(token)` is
  gone — `bearer` names one thing, a claims value signed into an `Authorization` value, set
  on an upgrade with `.header("authorization", app.bearer(&claims))`.

What fails: the compile, at `.socket(path).bearer(token)`. At run, `bearer` on an app booted without `.ephemeral_signing_key()` panics naming the
builder call; at boot, the key on an app with no `JwtService`, or on one whose
`JwtService` was seeded, refuses.

### Dependencies — the whole tree moved, and what your code can see is named here

Every third-party *requirement* now sits on its publisher's newest stable
release, with two exceptions named at the bottom. Most of the movement is
invisible: `cargo update` moved 73 crates in the framework's lockfile, 126 in
the demo's and 89 in the benchmark's, and before the release 69, 71 and 44 more
— among them the patch releases RUSTSEC-2026-0385, 0386, 0391, 0395 to 0406 and
0410 name (`ctutils` 0.4.6, `tokio` 1.54.1, `zstd` 0.14.3 with `zstd-safe` 8.2
and `zstd-sys` 2.1.2) — and the floors followed the lock: `rmcp` to 3.3, 3.5,
then 3.8, `tokio` to 1.54, `uuid` to 1.27, then 1.29, and `jsonwebtoken` to
11.1, since the minor a manifest states is the version we actually build
against. What a consumer can see is named here rather than left in a
lockfile diff.

#### `croner` 3.0 → 4.0

- **Step syntax is stricter.** The shortcut `5/5 * * * *` — "every five minutes
  from minute 5" — is rejected by default; the OCPS grammar wants the range,
  `5-59/5 * * * *`. croner's message names the fix, and `#[cron]` reports it at
  **compile time**, not at boot. No `CronExpression` preset used the shortcut
  and neither did anything in this repository. croner can be built lenient again
  (`CronParser::builder().sloppy_ranges(true)`) and `#[cron]` deliberately does
  not expose that: strict is OCPS, `sloppy_ranges` is the deviation, and the
  only place a per-site flag could sit is the attribute itself — a second
  grammar for one declaration. The compile error carries the rewrite.
- **Daylight-saving overlap iteration changed, and it changed for the better.**
  croner 3 dropped an occurrence inside the repeated hour. On a fall-back day —
  25 real hours — `EVERY_HOUR` now fires **25** times where it fired 24, and
  `EVERY_MINUTE` (1440 → 1500), `EVERY_5_MINUTES` (288 → 300) and
  `EVERY_30_MINUTES` (48 → 50) gain everywhere. `EVERY_2_HOURS` gains only where
  the repeated local hour is even — Paris 12 → 13, New York 12 → 12 — because
  `*/2` selects hours 0, 2, 4 and the repeat lands on 02:00 in one and 01:00 in
  the other. Daily and longer presets are unchanged, and so is the
  spring-forward gap.
- **A live hot spin is gone.** Inside that repeated hour croner 3 could return
  an occurrence *in the past* — the first pass over a time the clock is about to
  repeat, up to 59 minutes stale — which `Scheduler`'s
  `(next - now).to_std().unwrap_or(Duration::ZERO)` clamps to zero, so the job
  re-fires as fast as the executor can reschedule it until the hour is over,
  once a year, per zone. Sampled every second across the 2026 `America/New_York`
  fall-back, `EVERY_MINUTE` alone: **3,540 negative delays under croner 3, none
  under croner 4.** The delay is never exactly zero in either — the clamp is
  what turns a stale answer into a spin, which is why `next_delay`'s own comment
  says "clamp defensively rather than unwrap a negative span".
- The `chrono` backend is now an optional feature, still on by default.
  Occurrence methods became generic over a new `CronDateTime` trait, and the
  scheduler builds croner on its `jiff` backend instead (below).

Both halves of `#[cron]` now carry a trybuild snapshot. The expression half
never had one, so deleting the compile-time validation left the suite green
while the documentation quoted its diagnostic verbatim. `CronExpression`'s
presets are pinned to the instants they fire at, too: asserting only that each
parses could not see a preset change meaning, which is precisely what a cron
library's major release does.

#### `chrono-tz` → `jiff`: a `#[cron]` zone follows the IANA's current rules

- **Fixed: a `#[cron(tz = …)]` fired an hour off in a zone whose rules changed
  after March 2025.** chrono-tz 0.10.4, its last release, compiles in the IANA
  database 2025b. Morocco moved to permanent UTC on 2026-09-20, so a job in
  `Africa/Casablanca` has fired an hour early since; British Columbia, Alberta,
  Manitoba and Inuvik keep their summer offset after 2026-11-01, where
  `America/Vancouver`, `America/Edmonton`, `America/Winnipeg` and
  `America/Inuvik` would have fired an hour late. The zones are now read from
  the IANA's 2026e release.
- **One zone database, the build's.** `#[cron(tz)]` is checked at compile time
  and resolved at boot against the same copy, the IANA database bundled with
  jiff (`jiff-tzdb`), never the host's `/usr/share/zoneinfo`: the two verdicts
  cannot disagree, an image built `FROM scratch` needs nothing installed, and a
  link name such as `US/Eastern` resolves everywhere, where Debian 12 and later
  ship those in a separate `tzdata-legacy` package. The copy is your
  lockfile's, not nestrs's — `cargo update -p jiff-tzdb` brings the IANA's
  latest release — and a zoned job's boot line names the release it read,
  `tzdb="2026e"`.
- A zone name is matched without regard to case and logged as the database
  spells it, so `tz = "europe/paris"` compiles and boots as `Europe/Paris`. An
  unknown one is refused as *not a zone of the IANA time zone database bundled
  with jiff*.

#### `argon2` 0.5 → 0.6

- `password-hash` 0.6 drops the explicitly-constructed `SaltString` and has
  `hash_password` draw the PHC specification's recommended 16 bytes straight
  from the system CSPRNG. `hash_password` / `verify_password` keep their
  signatures, the pinned OWASP work factor still reaches the stored string, and
  the PHC output is unchanged.
- **Credentials hashed by the previous release still verify**, pinned as a test
  against two real 0.5.3-produced hashes rather than assumed — every other test
  in that file hashes and verifies with the same build and would miss a PHC or
  B64 change that locks a deployment out. Compatibility runs the other way too:
  a hash written by 0.6 parses under 0.5.3, so a rolling deploy is safe in both
  directions.
- **One panic left a hot path.** 0.5's `SaltString::generate(&mut OsRng)`
  panics if the system RNG fails; 0.6's `try_generate_salt()?` returns an error,
  which the code maps to `PasswordError::HashFailed`.
- **Salt bounds tightened.** `phc` 0.6 requires a decoded salt of 8–48 bytes and
  at least 11 B64 characters, where `password-hash` 0.5 accepted 4. Nothing this
  framework has ever written is affected — our salts are 16 bytes — but a
  credential *imported* from another system with a shorter salt now reports
  `PasswordError::InvalidHash` where it used to fail as a wrong password.

#### `rmcp` 3.1 → 3.8

- **`ServerInfo` is `ServerConfig` in 3.4**, left upstream as a deprecated alias that
  a `-D warnings` build refuses. The delegated `get_info` follows it, and
  `nest_rs::mcp` names `ServerConfig` at its root — see
  *WebSockets, GraphQL and MCP* above.
- **`ServerHandler::negotiate_initialize` is new, and this framework was not
  forwarding it.** `PropagatingHandler`, `CompositeHandler` and the object-safe
  `McpHost` view now all delegate it, which is what rmcp's own
  `impl_server_handler_for_wrapper!` does. Nothing misbehaved on the wire — the
  method is reachable through the `initialize` the wrapper already delegated —
  and that is exactly why every behavioural proof missed it: the integration
  suite's probe host records only the methods someone thought to add to it.
  **The real fix is `#[deny(clippy::missing_trait_methods)]`** on both
  `ServerHandler` impls, which turns the next method rmcp adds into a compile
  error naming it, and reaches `McpHost` too because `CompositeHandler`
  delegates through it. The module headers no longer claim a hand-written list
  is "exhaustive by construction"; the compiler says so instead.
  **`McpHost` gained a required method**, which is a breaking change for
  anything implementing that trait by hand. Almost nothing does — it is
  blanket-implemented for every `ServerHandler`, so a `#[mcp]` host is one
  without naming it — but a type implementing it directly now needs a
  `negotiate_initialize`.
- **An empty `#[tool_router]` is refused** — a host whose `impl` block serves no
  tools is a compile error upstream, which is the position `#[tools]` already
  took. If an empty router is what you want, rmcp takes
  `#[tool_router(allow_empty)]`, which is what this repo's own mount-only test
  fixtures use. Only hand-written rmcp hosts are affected; one written through
  the `#[mcp]` / `#[tools]` pair never could be empty, and `#[tools]` refuses an
  empty block with its own sentence before rmcp sees it.
- **rmcp's `LATEST` is `2026-07-28` in 3.5**, the stateless revision with no
  `initialize` (SEP-2567). `nest_rs::testing::mcp`'s driver opens a session, so
  `PROTOCOL_VERSION` stays `2025-11-25`, which rmcp now names
  `LATEST_WITH_INITIALIZE`; the endpoint serves both revisions as before. 3.8
  speaks the same five revisions, `2024-11-05` to `2026-07-28`.
- **3.6 keeps no tool schema**: it looks one up per request, so the cache a
  client's names grew is gone (*Security*), and with it the endpoint's gate in
  front of it — a call naming no declared tool reaches the handler at every
  revision, answered `-32602` `tool not found` under `NOT_FOUND`; one carrying
  no id is a notification, accepted `202`.
- **What a client reads from the transport moved in three places.** A request
  that is no `initialize` and names no session is `400 Bad Request: Session ID
  is required`, where 3.5 answered `422`; a JSON-RPC response or error sent
  statelessly is `400` with `-32600`, `stateless server does not accept
  JSON-RPC responses`, where 3.5 accepted it `202`; a message carrying a stray
  `result` or `error`, or a repeated `id`, is no message, `400` with `-32600`
  Invalid Request. An id written `1.0` reads as `1`.
- **A request the server sends the client is refused at `2026-07-28`**, which
  forbids one: `Peer::create_message`, `list_roots` and the `elicit` family
  return an error there, and an operation answers an `InputRequiredResult`
  instead. A session below that revision sends them as before.
- **A handler still running when its session's transport ends is cancelled**,
  and the session map's lock is released before a request waits on its
  session. rmcp's events on the server's paths are those of 3.5, so `rmcp=off`
  stays.

#### `opentelemetry` 0.32 → 0.33, `tracing-opentelemetry` 0.33 → 0.34

No `opentelemetry` type is in the API any more (*Metrics are recorded through
`Metrics`*), so an app that names `opentelemetry` itself — for an exporter of its
own — moves its own requirement independently; the framework built against 0.33
unchanged. The metrics provider still ignores
the timeout its shutdown is handed, so `FLUSH_TIMEOUT` stays enforced by the
framework. 0.33.1 files a failed metric export at `error` on `opentelemetry_sdk`
(`PeriodicReader.ExportError`), so a collector that does not answer shows
without `debug`; the OTLP HTTP exporter's failure names its status, never the
collector's URL, so no clamp is added. It drops a non-finite measurement before
recording it, and gives a span whose parent context is invalid a trace of its
own.

#### `handlebars` 6.4.3 → 6.4.4 — every `serde_json` map the demo emits reorders

handlebars dropped `preserve_json_order` from its default features, which
switches `serde_json::Map` from insertion order to alphabetical for everything
downstream of it. It reaches the demo through the exact-pinned `async-graphql`,
so `demo/apps/api/openapi.json` is rewritten — 1360 lines, **semantically
identical**: 18 paths and 18 schemas, none added, none removed. Almost all of it
is key order; four `parameters` arrays also swapped two elements, which OpenAPI
gives no meaning to.
The two workspaces had already drifted apart on this at 6.1.0: the framework's
lockfile was on 6.4.4 and the demo's on 6.4.3, so framework crates serialized
alphabetically and the demo's did not. They agree again. Nothing pins it, and a
future handlebars that restores the default flips it back.

#### `redis` 0.32 → 1.8

0.32 was past the freshness bar, and its types are in `nest-rs-redis`'s API, so
7.0 is the major that moves it. **Breaking for code naming `redis` types**:
1.x marks its enums non-exhaustive, builds a server error under
`ErrorKind::Server(ServerErrorKind::…)`, reads `ConnectionInfo` through getters
and parses an owned `Value` with `from_redis_value` (`from_redis_value_ref` for
a borrowed one).

- **No reply timeout of the client's.** 1.x answers a command after 500 ms
  and dials for one second by default; every connection the framework opens
  sets both from the connect budget instead, since `RedisConnection` bounds each
  command itself and a worker's read blocks for longer than 500 ms.
- **No `CLIENT SETINFO`.** Every connection skips it, so a Redis user confined
  to a binding's rule is refused nothing, and the ACL pages lose their
  exception.
- **The boot proves the connection it keeps.** A connection that sends nothing
  on opening meets a full `maxclients` only at its first command, so the boot's
  `PING` is asked of the kept connection too, on the manager that reopens it.
- **A refused TLS handshake is still named.** 1.x keeps the error it wraps
  behind an `Arc`, whose own `source` skips it; the diagnosis looks through it.
- **1.8 changes nothing the framework sends or reads**, and keeps the
  doc-hidden cluster seam the `=` pin is for. Its client configuration types
  print their secrets redacted in `Debug`, its own Sentinel client asks the next
  sentinel when one names no master (the framework discovers through its own
  `SENTINEL` commands), and it awaits every subscription confirmation (the
  framework subscribes to nothing). **An app naming `redis` in its manifest
  requires 1.8**: the framework's `=1.8` pin refuses `=1.7` beside it.

#### `parking_lot` 0.12 → 0.13

A private dependency: the framework's own locks move to 0.13, faster under
contention and without automatic eventual fairness, which no lock the framework
holds relies on. tokio, object_store and sqlx's futures-intrusive still require
0.12, so both sit in every lockfile until they move. An app using `parking_lot`
itself keeps its own requirement.

#### `schemars` 1.2.3

An internally tagged enum's schema now lists its tag first among its
properties, as `required` and serde's own output do: a committed OpenAPI
document holding one reorders on its next regeneration. The demo's holds none.

#### `async-graphql` is built without its default features

**Breaking:** `nest_rs::graphql::async_graphql` keeps what the framework uses —
the playground and dataloaders — and loses what it never did: the
`validator(email)` check, `Upload` and GraphQL multipart, `dynamic` schemas and
GraphiQL. An email is checked at the edge by `validator`'s `#[validate(email)]`,
as every other field is; a file reaches an app through an HTTP multipart route.
Two of the three MPL-2.0 exceptions in `deny.toml` (`fast_chemail`,
`ascii_utils`) leave with the email check, and `handlebars` and `tempfile` leave
the tree.

#### One crypto provider: `ring` leaves the tree

rustls, `jsonwebtoken` and `object_store` already ran on aws-lc-rs, and two
dependencies compiled ring beside it: `oauth2`'s bundled reqwest 0.12, which has
no other TLS backend, and sea-orm's `runtime-tokio-rustls`, which can select only
sqlx's ring backend. Both are gone: `oauth2` is built without its default
features and the token exchange runs on the reqwest every other client uses, and
sea-orm takes `runtime-tokio` with sqlx's `tls-rustls-aws-lc-rs` selected by
`nest-rs-seaorm`. `deny.toml` bans `ring`, so a dependency that brings it back
fails `just lint`.

- **The OAuth client trusts the platform's certificate store.** A provider's
  certificate is verified against the operating system's roots, as
  `object_store` and the OpenTelemetry exporter already were, instead of the
  roots bundled into the binary. An image without CA certificates now fails the
  code exchange as the provider being unavailable, naming its endpoint;
  verification itself is unchanged and never optional.
- **The two comparisons nestrs writes in constant time run on AWS-LC too**:
  an OAuth client's `state` and a registered client's credentials, through its
  `CRYPTO_memcmp`. `subtle` is no longer a requirement of ours, though rustls
  still brings it, and nothing changes for a caller.
- **An app naming sea-orm writes `runtime-tokio`.** `nestrs new` and the
  generators do; an existing app replaces `runtime-tokio-rustls` with
  `runtime-tokio` on its `sea-orm` and `sea-orm-migration` lines, or it compiles
  ring again for its own build. Postgres's TLS keeps its bundled roots.

#### The HTTP stack is nestrs's own: hyper in, poem and `async-graphql-poem` out

- **`hyper` 1.12 and `hyper-util` 0.1 serve every connection**, HTTP/1 and HTTP/2,
  on nestrs's own accept loop, with `tokio-rustls` 0.26 for TLS and `h2` 0.4, read
  to say how an HTTP/2 connection ended without the peer's `GOAWAY` debug data.
- **poem leaves every lockfile** — the framework's, the demo's, the benchmark's and
  the one `nestrs new` writes. The transport routes on its own segment tree,
  answers CORS itself, decodes queries and forms with `serde_urlencoded` 0.7
  (`form_urlencoded` 1.2 beneath it) and multipart bodies with `multer` 3.1, and
  encodes responses with `async-compression` 0.4 (brotli, deflate, gzip, zstd).
  `multer` and `serde_urlencoded` are past the 12-month bar and kept on the
  record: finished, without an advisory, already in every lockfile through
  async-graphql and object_store, and moving when those move. With poem go
  `rustls-pemfile` (RUSTSEC-2025-0134, unmaintained), which `deny.toml` no
  longer accepts, and the MPL-2.0 exception poem's `sse-codec` needed.
- **`async-graphql-poem` leaves**: `/graphql` is served on the transport's own
  types, and `nest_rs::graphql::async_graphql_poem` is gone with it.
- **`tokio-tungstenite` 0.30 is built with no default feature**: WebSockets open
  on nestrs's own upgrade, which needs only the handshake's accept key; a client
  (`connect`) is enabled where a suite drives one.
- **`tower` 0.4 leaves the framework's manifests**: MCP's endpoint mounts on the
  transport's own seam.
- **New, each for one job**: `headers` 0.4 (static files' conditional requests),
  `pin-project-lite` 0.2 (core's and GraphQL's futures), `log` 0.4 (a library's
  `log` records held at the console filter's level), `heck` 0.5 (`#[expose]`
  names an entity's `Column` variants as sea-orm-macros derives them; past the
  bar, kept on the record, moving with sea-orm-macros), `getrandom` 0.4 (`nestrs
  g auth` draws its signing secrets) and `criterion` 0.8 (the pipeline's
  micro-benchmarks, a dev-dependency of `nest-rs-core`, without its plots).
- **Features**: `async-graphql` adds `custom-error-conversion`, and
  `sea-orm-migration` drops its defaults — the framework links a migration and a
  migrator, never the migration command line.
- **Not added**: `tracing-appender`. The console's queue is the kernel's own
  writer, `ConsoleWriter`.

A third-party type in a public signature ties that library's next major — a `0.x`
library's next minor — to NestRS's. Each library in the public API is now classified once:

- **The public dependencies**, as Cargo calls them, are the libraries you write in your
  own code — their derives, their attributes, their types in your signatures:
  `async-graphql` 7, `rmcp` 3, `sea-orm` and `sea-orm-migration` 2, `validator` 0.21,
  `schemars` 1, `redis` 1, `rust-embed` 8, `inventory` 0.3, `futures-util` 0.3,
  `async-trait` 0.1, the foundations (`tokio`, `tokio-util`, `tracing`, `serde`,
  `serde_json`, `anyhow`, `uuid`, `chrono`, `http`, `http-body`, `bytes`), `syn` and
  `proc-macro2` for a decorator crate, and `tokio-rustls` and `rustls` in `nest-rs-testing`.
  No 7.x release moves their major, and you may name each in your manifest at a
  requirement compatible with the framework's. The list is on the
  [semver page](https://nestrs.dev/semver/#third-party-libraries).
- **Every other library is a private dependency, wrapped**: none of its types appears in
  a public signature, so its next major reaches you as a NestRS minor or patch. `poem` is
  no longer among the libraries whose majors are NestRS's.
- **A vulnerability fixed only in a public dependency's next major waits for NestRS's.**
  Until then the page you would reach the affected API from says what not to build:
  RUSTSEC-2026-0253 (`lru`'s `LruCache::pop`, unsound when a key's `Drop` panics) is
  reached only through async-graphql's `dataloader::LruCache`, which NestRS never builds
  and which `nest_rs::graphql::async_graphql` lets an app build — the
  [dataloaders page](https://nestrs.dev/database/dataloaders/) now says not to.

#### Not taken

- **`async-graphql` 8**, still a release candidate on 2026-10-27 (8.0.0-rc.5): 7.0
  keeps `=7.2.1` and the RUSTSEC-2026-0253 ignore (*Each capability builds
  alone*).
- **`cargo-chef` 0.1.78** in `demo/Dockerfile`, still pinned at 0.1.77. It is a
  `cargo install` build tool rather than a requirement, and no test walks it.

### Each capability builds alone, and the crates kept past the freshness bar are on the record

- **Every capability builds alone**: `just lint`, and so CI, builds the umbrella
  and `nest-rs-macro-hygiene` under each feature by itself with `cargo hack` —
  the install contract, `cargo add nest-rs --features <x>`. Every other gate
  builds one feature union, so a capability compiling only because another
  turned a feature on is invisible there.
- **Fixed: `nest-rs-seaorm` compiles with only its `graphql` feature.**
  `cargo add nest-rs-seaorm --no-default-features --features graphql` failed with
  `E0432`: its GraphQL refusals go through `nest-rs-authz`'s GraphQL binding, and
  only `http` forwarded its own; `graphql` now enables `nest-rs-authz/graphql`.
  A per-crate feature matrix found it. An application depending on the umbrella
  was never affected.
- **chrono-tz 0.10.4 (2025-07-11) and subtle 2.6.1 (2024-06-24) were past
  the bar, and are replaced** (*Dependencies*): jiff for the zones, whose rules
  chrono-tz had compiled in and aged with, and aws-lc-rs for the two
  constant-time comparisons.
- **figment 0.10.19 (2024-05-17) is past the bar too, and its pin says until
  when**: it serves only the framework's own tests, until 2027-01-31.
  parking_lot, which shared this bullet at 0.12.5, moved to 0.13 (2026-10-21)
  (*Dependencies*). The configuration pages no longer ask your app to add it: a
